The Names You Take

A Riarhin novel

***

Part One — The Agreement

***

Chapter 1 — The Room Eilis Has Given Them

She woke before the house did and lay listening to it settle.

The ceiling was low and whitewashed, crossed by one dark beam that someone had rubbed with oil until it held the light like a cheek. A rush mat had been laid beside the bed for her feet. She had not noticed it the night before. She noticed it now because her left foot was already on it, and the softness had told her in the same instant that she was not on a ship, or in a cell, or on a road, and that someone had come in while she slept and arranged for her to wake comfortably.

Eilis had done that. Eilis did things like that, and did not mention them.

Riarhin sat up. Her hauberk hung on a peg by the door with the cloak over it, cleaner than she had left it. Remorse lay across the chair, with Silence unstrung beside it. The Last Blade was under the pillow where she had put it; Toothpick was in her sleeve. She touched each knife before rising.

She dressed left-handed and without hurry, pulling the laces with a rough economy that Na’ar had once called the way you tie a boot when you don’t plan to argue with it. She had been braced for that sentence to come back at a bad moment. It came now, quite plainly, in his voice, with the little upward turn he gave to jokes he was not sure were funny. She finished the lace and let it be there.

Outside the light was the colour of a gull’s underwing. The tide had gone far out in the night. Beyond the low wall the strand lay dark and wet, pocked with the holes of things that lived beneath it, and the sea had drawn itself back to a thin bright line as if it meant to stand at a distance and see what she would do.

Nothing, at the moment. That was what she meant to do.

She rested her forearms on the wall. The stones were grey-green with lichen and set without mortar, and they were old in the way stones were old in Theona, which was to say that whoever had put them there was now a rumour. Every field she could see was divided by such walls. In the corner of the nearest a standing stone leaned at an angle, taller than a man, its face pitted and mossed. She had passed a dozen coming up from the beach. She had not yet found a road that did not go by one.

Veralyn must die.

She had thought it on the shingle with the sea in her boot. It returned now, whole, its edges finished. She had been afraid it would soften with sleep. Instead it was quiet.

She had known rage all her life. She knew it as heat, as a tightness in the throat, as a sudden preference for doing the next thing with her hands. This was not that. This was as cold as the wall under her arms, and as patient. It did not push her towards the sea or the queen. It stood beside her and waited for her to work out the rest.

How.

She took the question out and looked at it. On the third night at sea she had put the queen in rooms: a room with a window, a room without one, a chapel, a tent with the army asleep around it. She had put herself in each in every plausible disguise, and had found in each the same three or four ways in which it went wrong. She had not needed to invent them. She could see every guard, every cousin, every brazier where a brazier would be. She could see Veralyn at the end of a table with her hands folded and the green thing at her throat.

She could not see herself leaving the room.

Not from any of them. There was a moment after each that simply did not come, like the step on a familiar stair that the foot expects and does not find. She had spent so long looking at the missing step that she had begun to wonder whether it was information.

“You’re up,” said someone behind her.

It was the cook, broad and fifty-odd, with a basket on her hip and the pleasant blankness of a woman who has decided in advance not to be surprised. She stood a few paces off in the yard.

“There’s bread. Eilis said not to wake you, so I haven’t, and you needn’t say I did.”

“I wasn’t asleep.”

“No,” said the cook, considering her. “I don’t suppose you were.”

***

There were two long tables in the kitchen and a hearth as wide as a cart. Turf burned in it, cut in blocks the colour of dark chocolate, with a low orange steadiness that made no noise. Copper pans hung from hooks. Someone had scrubbed the flags within the day, and the water had dried in pale tide-marks along the joins.

Riarhin looked at the stools because she looked at everything. A healer she had disliked had told her once that her habit of noticing was a kind of hunger. She had said of course it is, and the healer had looked as though she had hoped for a different answer.

Nine stools stood about the nearer table.

The cook set down her basket, went round the end, lifted the ninth stool by one leg, carried it to the wall, and put it among the buckets with the seat turned inward. She did not look at it. She did not look at Riarhin. She wiped the place on the flags where it had stood and went to the hearth.

Riarhin sat on the bench. “Is there a reason?”

“For what?”

“The stool.”

“Which stool?”

“The one you moved.”

The cook gave her the whole of the blankness. “Oh, that,” she said, and cut bread.

There was butter beaten with salt until it was faintly grey, and a heel of loaf so dense it might have been dug. Riarhin had been hungry on the ship in a way that made everything taste of the ship. This tasted of itself. Halfway through the first slice she saw that her hand was shaking and the knife was ticking against the dish, and she put the knife down until the hand was still. You eat like a woman who has been told she’s about to be searched, Na’ar had said once, in a tavern on the coast road, watching her clean a bowl with a crust. She had told him to mind his own bowl.

In the wall beside the hearth, at about the height of a man’s knee, there was a niche, and in the niche a saucer of milk. A skin had formed on it. Nobody had broken the skin.

“Is that for the cat?”

“It’s for whoever comes.”

“Does someone come?”

“That’s what it’s for,” said the cook.

Riarhin finished the first slice and ate the second when it was put in front of her. She would not make it easy for a house to be kind to her. She would eat what she was given and say thank you, and go and find whatever came next.

***

Eilis came in at the end of it, wiping her hands on her skirt, still in the pinned-up kirtle she had worn on the beach. She looked younger than she had in the water, perhaps thirty-five, though something besides weather had gone into the care in her face. She dropped onto the bench across the table.

“You slept.”

“Some.”

“The Matron’s been to him. She says the leg is clean under the dressing and the fever won’t come back if nothing else does. That’s not a promise. It’s what she said.”

“I understand.”

“She’ll want to see you also. Don’t take it as an insult. With Eithne, civility is a kind of test, and the ones who mind it generally fail.”

“I won’t mind.”

“You will, but it’s kind of you to say so.”

It was said drily enough that Riarhin nearly smiled, and she let the muscle move. Eilis saw it and did not remark on it. That was the thing Riarhin had come to know her by, after the split thumbnail. She never remarked on what she had seen.

“I have a cart to return,” Eilis said. “It’s Sorley’s, from the far side of the hill. It carried your man up from the beach and stood in my yard three days, and he’d like it back before he hears from his wife what it cost him. And Cathal has been to the gate again to say that the men who carried the litter were owed a day’s pay by the landing, and Ruarc has said at the same gate that they were owed nothing because it was given.”

“I’ll pay the day.”

Eilis’s face went still. She looked at her a moment, and then laughed once through her nose, quite kindly. “You’ll do no such thing.”

“It’s a small sum.”

“It is. It would also make it a service, and a service can be argued before a Brehon. Given, it’s a courtesy, and nobody can bring a courtesy to a moot.” She turned her cup by the handle. “You think because I’ve done one thing for you I might do the next. It’s a natural thing to think. It’s the way in your country, I’m told.”

“I haven’t asked for the next.”

“You’re about to. I’ve watched you looking at my barn.”

Riarhin had been looking at the barn. It was long, dry and empty, with a good loft. She had been looking at it in the light of a great many people who might sit in it. She had not told herself so.

“I took people escaping a queen’s soldiers into my house,” said Eilis. “I did it for my sister’s sake and because I would not have liked myself if I hadn’t. I set four terms and I’ll keep them. No armed company on this land. My name not used in your business, at any moot or to the King. No letter out of my door that I haven’t read. And if a claim is made on your surrender, it is heard at my table first, with all of us present.” She said them flatly, without her fingers. “That’s what I promised. It’s all I promised. It isn’t a promise to be the household of whatever you do next.”

“No.”

“I’d like you to say you understand that.”

“I understand it,” said Riarhin. “I’d have made the same terms. I looked for the place where they were unfair to me and I couldn’t find it.”

“Good.” The set of Eilis’s shoulders eased, and Riarhin understood how much of the last three days the woman had spent holding them up. “Then we’ve no quarrel. The cousins will find one, but we haven’t.”

She rose, and stopped, looking at the stools.

“You’ve been counting,” she said.

“I always do.”

“Not here. Here you might leave it for a bit.” She did not say why. She lifted the ninth stool from among the buckets, looked at it, and put it back with its seat to the wall. “I mean it kindly.”

***

Berent had got as far as the yard.

She found him leaning on the wall with a stick Eamon had cut him, the bad leg held stiff before him and one hand flat against the stones. It was the first time he had been out of doors. His face was raw with wind and he stood in it like a man drinking. In the lane beyond the gate stood a small argument: Cathal, large and red, with his hands on his belt; and behind him Ruarc, with the thin patient face of a man who had learned to stand behind larger people; and a carter with his cap in both hands who wished to be anywhere else.

Berent had evidently decided that a knight of a minor branch of a minor house could settle it.

“It’s a matter of what was said at the time,” he was telling Cathal, in his best voice. “I was there. I was under the blanket, but I can say quite clearly—”

“You were raving,” said Cathal.

“I was—”

“You called the horse a countess.”

“That was a different day.” Berent shifted his weight to make a point of it, and Riarhin saw his face change. He put out the free hand and found the wall, and held on, and said in a slightly different tone, “Perhaps you’d give me a moment.”

Nobody moved. Ruarc looked at the sky. The carter looked at his cap.

Mara came out of the house with a bowl in her hands. She did not hurry. She crossed the yard, set the bowl on the wall beside Berent’s hand, and said, in the voice she might have used to a man reaching for the last of the bread, “You’ve been standing for the length of a psalm. Sit.”

“I’m mediating.”

“You’re swaying.”

“It’s the wind.”

“It’s your leg. Sit.” She took his elbow. He sat, on the low step by the door, with all the dignity of a felled tree, and she put the bowl in his hands, and turned to the gate as if nothing had happened. “Gentlemen. I’m told there’s a question of a day’s pay. Eilis says it isn’t owed. If your men want it, they may ask her for their dinner. It’s hot.”

Cathal opened his mouth and closed it. Ruarc, behind him, made a small noise that might have been a cough.

“There’s stew,” said Mara. “There’s always stew.”

They went in. Riarhin watched the carter put on his cap, take it off again, and follow them like a man who has been rescued at the last moment from being a man of importance.

She sat down on the step beside Berent. He was grey with effort and eating the porridge in Mara’s bowl with great attention.

“That was a triumph,” he said.

“It was.”

“I want it noted.”

“I’ve noted it.”

“Every word?”

“Every word,” said Riarhin. “You called the horse a countess.”

He looked at her, briefly outraged, and then put back his head and laughed, and the laugh turned in the middle into a cough, and she took the bowl before it spilled. When he had his breath he leaned against the doorframe with his eyes shut.

“I want to do something,” he said.

“I know.”

“I mean something.”

“I know that too.”

“Are you going to tell me I’m not ready?”

“You can’t stand up for a psalm.” She set the bowl on the step. “When your leg is ready you’ll be needed. Until then you’re to eat, and sit where Mara puts you, and let me do the arithmetic.”

“That’s what you always do. You do the arithmetic and I find out later what it was.”

“It’s an efficient system.”

“It’s a grim one.” He opened his eyes. He was looking at the yard, and the stone leaning in the corner of the field, and the grey water beyond. “I’ve something I ought to have given you.”

“What?”

“Wait.” He put a hand under the folded blanket on which he sat. It was a good blanket, wool, thick, the colour of oatmeal. He drew from beneath it, slowly, a short sword wrapped in oiled cloth.

She did not move.

“It was in the cart,” said Berent. “Under the folded coat. He must have put it down to lift me. Afterwards he had the pole.” His mouth worked. “Mara found it when they moved me into the shed. I asked her to keep it with my things. I couldn’t give it to you on the ship. You’d have had to say something, and so would I, and I wasn’t up to it.”

“It was in the boat.”

“Aye. And when the boatman gave it to Mara she thought it was mine, and I let her.” He held it out, on both hands, with the cloth across them. “It’s been clean since Tuesday. I did it myself. I wasn’t going to let it go to rust.”

She looked at the plain grip wound in worn brown cord. She looked at the nick near the point from a bad exchange on the coast road, and the small sign on the pommel that she had never asked him about.

She took it. The cloth slipped, and she caught the exposed blade across her palm.

“Thank you,” she said.

“Don’t.”

“I meant it.”

“I know.” He did not look at her. “That’s why.”

They sat, and the wind moved in the thatch. After some time she laid the sword across her knees, cloth and all, and put her left hand flat upon it. She was aware of the effort not to say his name. She did not know whether it was for his sake or her own.

***

Mara found her in the kitchen at noon, going through a satchel that did not need going through.

“I’d like to ask you something,” she said. “And I’d like you to hear the whole of it before you answer.”

“Ask.”

“Who is making arrangements for the people who’ll still live here when you’ve found a reason to leave?”

Riarhin put down the satchel.

Mara stood in the doorway with her arms folded. She had lost weight, and gained something else, and it sat oddly on her. On the ship she had been competent. Here she was only tired, in an ordinary, shoulder-bending way, but the tiredness did not soften her. She looked at Riarhin the way Riarhin had once seen her look at a cupboard door that would not close.

“I’m not asking whether you’ll be kind to them,” she said. “I know you would. I’m asking whether you’ve counted them. Eilis has cousins who think she’s harbouring the enemy. Eamon has a leg. The cook has been in this house since before Eilis was married, and nobody has ever asked her whether she’d like a war in the yard.” She drew a breath. “And there’s Maelis.”

“What about her?”

“She has work.”

“Work.”

“Paid. There’s a company of players at Coracle Harbor, they need someone who can dress a cast and make the dead look convincingly dead, and she told them yes.” The corner of Mara’s mouth moved. “She’s asked for wages in advance, and she’s got them. She says it’s the first honest employment she’s had in years and she’s not going to give it up because you need an introduction.”

“She said that?”

“In those words, near enough. She was frightened while she said it. I thought it was well done.” Mara looked at her steadily. “She isn’t going to be yours. Nor am I. I’m staying, if Eilis will have me, and I’ll work. I’m a widow and I want to be useful. But I want it written down that I chose it.”

“I don’t write things down.”

“Then say it aloud.”

Riarhin found that she had risen. She did not remember doing it.

“You’ve chosen it,” she said. “I heard you.”

“Thank you.”

“I wanted you—” She stopped. She had been about to say safe, and the word looked back at her, a small thin thing. “I wanted you to have somewhere to stand.”

“I know,” said Mara, more gently. “It’s a good place. Now go and find your own.”

***

The Matron came at the turn of the afternoon, on foot, with a boy behind her carrying a covered basket.

She was small and square and past sixty, in a dark green shawl worn so long that the colour had gone soft at the folds, with white hair cut short as a man’s. She stopped at the gate and put one hand flat on the post, as though asking it, and stood for the length of a short prayer. Then she came up the path. The dog in the yard rose and went to sit by the wall.

She saw to Berent first. She was an hour with him behind the shut door, and Riarhin, waiting below, heard a voice she did not know say hold that and Berent’s answering with one word she did. When Eithne came down, she wiped her hands on a cloth and looked Riarhin over, quickly, feet and hands and face, and stopped somewhere near the collar.

“You’re the tall one.”

“I’m not particularly tall.”

“Taller than you’d like.” She folded the cloth. “He’ll keep the leg. It closed over something dirty on the ship and the fever was the body arguing. The argument’s over. What’s left is a man learning to stand.”

“Thank you.”

“I haven’t done anything yet.” She looked at the satchel Riarhin still had in her hand. “I’d like the well for him. Not for the leg, for the rest. But he can’t be moved, and so I’ll take you. You’ll carry the water down.”

“Is there something I should know first?”

“It’s water,” said Eithne. “It’s in the ground. It asks your name at the rim, and I say it once so the water hears. You needn’t give the one you were born with.”

Riarhin looked at her sharply.

“I’m not asking which you’ve got. I’m saying it isn’t required.” The Matron took a piece of oatcake from the plate on the dresser and ate it with the placid thoroughness of someone who had never been rushed. “There are people who’ll tell you a name is only a sound. I find most of them have never had theirs used against them.”

They went along a path between two stone walls higher than her shoulder, past the last of Eilis’s fields. Ferns grew in every crack. Halfway along a boundary stone stood in the wall itself, upright in its own course of rubble, so that the two walls seemed to lean apart around it. It had a deep vertical groove down one side, as if a rope had been drawn through it for a very long time. She did not stop. She felt it watch her go.

At the end of the path the walls opened on a hollow. A hawthorn grew there, low and bent by weather, with a great many rags tied to it, faded to the colour of ash. Under the tree lay a ring of stones and inside the ring a round pool no wider than a table, lined with moss-green slabs. The water was so clear she could count the pebbles at the bottom. It made no sound at all.

Eithne dipped a wooden cup and knelt, with some difficulty, on the far side. “Come to the rim.”

Riarhin knelt. The wet came through at once, cold and immediate. In the water her reflection wavered, jaw, hair, the eyes examining whatever was examining them with an attention that had something hostile in it. It did not look like anyone she knew.

“What do I call you?” said Eithne.

The question was quiet and formal, and beneath it she heard a patience that was not for her, but for the water.

She could have said her own name. She could have said the shorter one she used with barmaids and ferrymen. She had half a dozen others kept ready, each with a small history attached, an inn or a road or a man to whom it had been said. She knew exactly which would serve.

She had decided on the path, without deciding, and it was none of those.

“Aine,” she said.

Eithne’s eyebrows rose a fraction and came down. “Aine.” She said it to the pool. “She says Aine.”

Nothing answered. A very fine ripple crossed from the far side to the near and stopped at Riarhin’s knees.

“That’s an old name,” Eithne said, still to the water. “A name for brightness. It’s not the one I’d have guessed.” She held out the cup. “Drink.”

The wood was smooth and very cold. The water hurt her teeth. It had no taste, not iron, not earth; it was only wet and clean and silent, and it went down into her chest and stayed, a small hard bright thing like a coin held in the palm.

Na’ar’s hand had closed on the front of her coat. She remembered the pull, the callus at the base of his thumb, the little white nick across the knuckle. She had been trying not to remember the moment his grip loosened.

She lowered the cup.

“You’ve gone grey,” said Eithne. “Put your head down.”

“I’m all right.”

“You’re a poor liar for someone who’s good at it.” The old woman took the cup gently. “Breathe. It doesn’t do anything. It only doesn’t let you pretend for a moment. That’s all a well is.”

Riarhin bent her head. The pool gave her back the top of her own hair and her knees in the wet grass, and after a while it passed.

“That’s the first,” Eithne said. “Not many are ready for a second.” She got to her feet with a hand in the small of her back, and the boy came forward to fill his jars. “Don’t ask me anything on the way up. You’ll want to and it’s good for you not to.”

At the boundary stone with the groove, the Matron paused and touched it with two fingers. She did not look at Riarhin. After a moment Riarhin did the same.

The stone was warm.

That was all. She noticed it the way she noticed everything, exactly, and set it down. It struck her, somewhere on the walk back to the house, that she had also stopped trying to make a list of what she could not explain. It had gone as easily as putting down a bag.

***

The letter she had been putting off was to Ailsa, and she wrote it that night at the kitchen table, under the good light.

There had been a beginning aboard the ship, on a strip of Berent’s bandage-linen in charcoal. Mara had folded it away. Riarhin took it out and read it. Ailsa, I have to tell you something and I have decided not to be clever about it. The lines wavered where the ship had moved. Under them was an inch of blank cloth.

She set it aside and took a sheet of Eilis’s heavy cream paper, and the pen, which someone had trimmed, and held it above the page until a drop formed and fell.

She could say that he had died quickly. She had seen his eyes stop following her, had felt beneath his jaw for a pulse. She could say that he had died well, a phrase she had never been able to bear. She could say that she had gone back for him. That was true, and it was also the thing she most wished to have written in her own favour.

So that I don’t keep looking for a better way it happened.

Mara had said that on the ship, with her hand on the unfinished letter. It had been the truest thing anyone had said to her in a month. She dipped the pen and wrote:

Ailsa. Na’ar is dead. I was there. I was not able to bring him out.

I will tell you how it happened and you may decide afterwards what to call it. I will not choose the words for you.

It took three hours and was not long. She put down what had occurred beside the gate, in order. His damaged leg had folded when he moved to avoid the bowman. She had gone back, and tried to lift him, and left him against the bank when she could find no pulse. She wrote his last instruction: The cart. She wrote that he had carried a lantern he did not need because he liked to see what he was walking into, and that on the night before the last job he had eaten a whole loaf sitting on a barrel and told her the crust was the best of it, and she had said it was the worst, and neither of them had meant it. She wrote that his sword had come out in the cart, and that Berent had kept it safe. She wrote: I do not know what I am going to do about the rest of it. I will not stop being here to be written to.

She signed with the name Ailsa would know and not the other, and read nothing over. She had it by heart already. She would have it by heart the rest of her life, whether she wished to or not.

She folded it in thirds. Then, because a letter to a family should not be the only one, she drew another sheet and wrote to Tavish. It was shorter. He talked about you more than he knew. She gave one instance, a market and a bad knife. I am sorry. It is all I have that is plain. I am not asking to be forgiven. She read that back and knew it was not enough, too careful, too aware of its own restraint. She left it.

Eilis was in the doorway. She had been there some time; Riarhin knew it from the draught.

“It’s done?”

“Yes.”

“You know what I have to say.”

Riarhin held out both sheets.

Eilis came and took them and did not sit. She held the first at a slight distance, the way a woman does who does not wish to be seen reading, and her lips did not move. Riarhin looked at the hearth. She had been read many times by people who wanted to know what she had written and would rather she had not watched. She had never been read by someone who would have preferred to be doing something else.

“Is there anything in these,” said Eilis, at last, “that could tell a man where you are?”

“No place. No date.”

“I can see that. That’s all I’m supposed to be reading for.” She did not lift her hand from the page. “I’ve read letters that said the price has gone up and your father’s dead and here is the bill. I’ve never read one where the writer said she doesn’t know what she’s going to do and meant it.”

Riarhin said nothing.

“I’ll send them. Eamon will take them to Coracle for the boat that doesn’t ask.” She hesitated, and went to the dresser and took down a small crock. A green-salt smell came out of it. She wrapped a pinch in each of two squares of linen and pressed one into each fold. “There’s a thing we do when someone writes to a dead man’s people. Salt with the letter, so it doesn’t arrive empty. I couldn’t tell you why. It’s only here.”

“I can’t find what you want for it,” said Riarhin. “The bed, the room. Your cousins’ hatred. Now this.”

“Nothing.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“It’s the one I’ve got.” Eilis sat, at last, across the table, with her chin on her joined hands. She looked very tired, and younger. “There’s an old rule. A stranger comes to your door, you feed them, and you ask nothing until the meal’s done. After the meal you may ask what you like. But the meal has to be real, and given for nothing.”

“The meal isn’t done.”

“No.”

“When it is, you’ll ask.”

“I might. Not everyone does.” Eilis stood, and gathered the letters. “I don’t know what I’ll want. I think it’s better if we both go on not knowing.”

She went out.

***

Riarhin stayed at the table until the light went red on the wall.

She had thought she might cry, and had made a small provision for it. It did not happen. What came instead was quieter and stranger: a sense that something had been taken out of her hands and put where it belonged, and that her hands, emptied, did not know what to do. She folded them. She unfolded them. There was ink on one finger where the pen had rested.

The cold thing stood beside her where it had stood since the shingle, and the letters had not moved it. She thought of a woman who fed strangers and would not ask, and a queen who let strangers walk out and had made sure they knew the shape of the door. Both were courteous. Only one was doing it for nothing.

She had served the Harrow at nineteen as a forward scout, with Ghere, and she had learned there what a company cost and how it was held. She knew how the trade was spoken of on the roads. The black banners, people said, with the sneer that goes to hired swords who win too reliably. She had never minded it. A good company crossed a field and finished what others could only prolong, and what made it good was not the banner. It was the person at the back who knew what each man in it wanted and what each was afraid of, and had counted before the first horse moved.

She did not have a company. She had a man who could not stand, a widow who had chosen a house, a young woman with a wage, and one door in Eilis’s barn that would not open.

She would have to pay for a room. That was the first thing, and it was small. It would have to be somewhere that was hers to explain: a loft, a boathouse, a place in Coracle Harbor where a woman might rent a table and not be asked why. She would not use Eilis’s name at any gate. She would stop letting a stranger’s kindness pass for her own doing, and put her own coin down for whatever she took.

She would make the enterprise an expense, with an address.

Aine, she thought, absurdly, testing the name against it. It fitted no better than a borrowed coat. It was still, for the moment, the only one that had been said to water.

She rose and went out to the yard. The tide had come back in, and the strand was gone under a wide grey shining. At the gate she stopped. She began, from habit, to count the lit windows behind her, and got as far as three, and left the rest, and went in.

***

Chapter 2 — The Captain and the Man Who Carried Him

The message came in the second week, and it came the long way.

Deirdre had given her word to pass on whatever was left with her carters, and had kept it, and the paper that reached the kitchen at Eilis’s had been folded three times and sealed with a plain wafer no one could have traced. It was addressed to no one. Inside, in a small, square, upright hand she had seen once on a sheet of vellum, someone had written: The wake at the O’Bran rath will end on the ninth night. I am there until it does. I should like to hear where the accounts part company.

She read it standing at the dresser. Eilis, watching from the table, said nothing at all, and pushed the bread across.

She went on foot, alone, because a woman with a knife was not a company. Eamon drew her a road on the back of a bill, with the limp of his pencil in every line, and told her the way was not difficult so long as she did not argue with it.

“With the way?”

“Aye.” He looked at a point beyond her shoulder. “If it looks like a road, it’s a road. If it stops looking like one, don’t stand about wondering. Walk on as if it did.”

It was two days’ walk for a man who lingered and a day and a half for her. The first afternoon she went through gorse and thin fields under a sky that came and went. Twice the road forked where Eamon’s drawing had no fork. Both times a standing stone stood at the split, leaning away from the left branch, and both times she took the right, and the second time she looked back and found she could not see the left. That night she slept in a herdsman’s shelter with a dog for company and gave the dog the heel of her bread. It asked for nothing else. In the small hours she woke to a low sound, very sweet, that came from the ditch by the door. It was a woman’s voice keening, slow and sorrowing, in a language she did not know, and it rose and fell at the ends of its phrases with such exactness of grief that she found she had put her hand out into the dark, palm open, the way one reaches to a child.

She drew it in.

She did not go out. In the morning the ditch was a ditch with water in it, and the dog was sitting at the threshold looking in the same direction as it had the night before. It looked at her once, as if to say that it had said nothing, and led her some way up the track.

***

The O’Bran rath was a great grassed ring of earth on a hill above a wood of oak and hazel. Ogham stones stood in the wood in ragged ranks, and she went up between them without reading, though her eyes tried. Somewhere to her left, parallel to her, keeping her pace, something light stepped among the trunks. When she stopped, it stopped. She did not look.

At the top the bank rose higher than a house with a single gap for a gate. Hawthorns grew along the crest hung with rags. Inside, on trodden turf, stood a long low house of stone with a roof of thatch and its door standing wide, and out of the door came the sound she had heard once before, at a funeral in a northern valley: a slow crooning of many women, rising and falling together.

Every window was open. Behind each, on the sill, a candle burned in the day. In the porch the clock had been stopped, and through the door she could see a mirror in the hall with a black cloth laid over its face.

A big ruddy man came across the grass turning a cap in his hands. His eyes were red. “You’re not of the family.”

“A friend of somebody’s.”

“Ah.” It seemed to answer. “You’ll eat. It’s the rule.” His mouth worked. “I’m Donnchadh O’Bran. It’s my mother’s house and my father we’re waking, that the sea kept. There’s no body. We wake the empty bed, and eight nights we’ve done it.” He looked over his shoulder at the house. “You’ll be wanting the foreigner.”

“I might.”

“He’s in the back room, and he’ll have the door locked. He doesn’t come out until he’s finished with it.” He twisted the cap. “He says he’s finishing it now. He’s been finishing it since morning.”

***

The door at the end of the hall was oak, banded with iron, and it did not open when she knocked.

“One moment,” said a voice within. It was dry and even and not at all surprised. “I’m closing a room. If you’re a friend of the family there’s stew. If you’re not, wait.”

She waited. There were small sounds, a scrape and a clink, a dry rattle like beads. Then silence, and a low continuous murmur in a language she did not know, forty breaths of it, ending. The bolt drew.

He looked exactly the same. That was almost the worst of it. The long grey coat, old and once very good, the narrow dark face, the close-cropped hair a little whiter at the temples than she remembered. There was chalk on his cheek. The coat had a new patch at the elbow, small and square and slightly crooked.

“Ah,” said Saikou Ira. “I wondered which of the rumours would be true.”

“Which?”

“That a woman of the northern march, left-handed, of middling height, had come by sea.” His eyes went briefly to her neck. “With a scar on the right. They’ve got it wrong.”

“I know.”

“Yes. Come in. Don’t touch anything on the table.”

The room had been stripped to the walls. There was no bed, no chest, no chair. On the stone floor stood a plain wooden table with a candle on it in a loop of red thread, a bowl of water, and a little heap of white powder, and where a mirror had hung the whitewash showed a pale rectangle.

“Sit on the floor,” he said. “I took the stools out on the second night. They obstructed the observation.” He sat opposite, cross-legged, with the ease of practice. “You’ll want to explain yourself. I’ll hear you. But not yet.”

“Why not?”

“Because I haven’t finished with the room.” He said it patiently, as one gives a rule of arithmetic. “I closed it three minutes ago. I don’t like leaving a room half secured. If we begin to talk my attention will be divided, and I would sooner finish entirely and give you the whole of it. Do you mind?”

“No.”

“Then watch, if you like. It’s instructive.”

She watched. He put the candle down in front of the door and tied the thread to a nail in the lintel. He drew a line of powder across the sill, a precise breadth, murmuring a word or two over it. He touched the water and marked the chalk above the door. He took a small dark stone from his pocket and set it in the north corner under a fold of cloth. Each thing was done in order and given the time it needed, and he did not once glance to see whether she was impressed.

“What is all this?”

“Attention.” He stood back and looked at the room. “People think an exorcist is a man who drives things out. I’m a man who notices what is happening in a room and changes the smallest thing and watches whether the thing changes with it. The rest is furniture.” He sat again. “If a thing repeats exactly, whatever I alter, it isn’t answering the room. It’s recurring. A mark, not a message.”

“And if it answers?”

“Then someone is present, and I would treat it very differently.”

The latch lifted. A very large man stooped under the lintel, and straightened, and stood there filling the door.

He was dark and heavy-featured, nearing forty, with a nose that had been broken and set by someone in a hurry. He wore a brown coat and carried a cup of tea in each hand, absurdly small in his fists. His eyes went across the room, to the sill, the stone in the corner, the powder. Then they came to her, and stopped, and she felt the whole considered weight of a man who had spent many years standing between one person and everything else.

“I didn’t interrupt,” he said.

“You’re interrupting now.”

“I waited until the bolt was done.” He came in and put a cup in front of Saikou and offered the other to her. “Amari,” he said.

“Riarhin.”

“I know.” He lowered himself to the floor with a slight grunt. “He said you’d come tomorrow.”

“I did say tomorrow,” said Saikou.

“It’s tomorrow in some countries.” Amari drank. “She’s early. I suppose she walked.”

She took the tea. It was hot and stewed and good. The coat, she saw, hung on the back of the door, where he must have flung it when he began; Saikou had put on a plain jerkin over his shirt. The sleeves of the coat had been shortened, plainly. Long ago, at the wrist, someone had cut them and turned them under and stitched them by hand. One cuff carried a square of darker cloth, patched on with large uneven stitches, in a thread that did not match.

“That’s a bad patch,” she said.

Saikou looked at it as if he had not seen it before. “It’s structurally sound.”

Amari put down his cup. He had begun, slightly, to smile.

“He’d not take it off,” he said. “On the beach in Dhahara. After Khemesh. There were a great many people who were glad to be alive, and one who was not, quite.” He glanced sidelong. “He wouldn’t take the coat off. Eleven days. It had been in the water, and in worse, and it was stiff with salt and rot. He said he’d catch cold. He wasn’t cold. He’d wrapped it round himself like a wall.”

“Amari.”

“On the eleventh day I cut the sleeves. It was that or he’d have gone on wearing it until it walked. I did the first patch. I’m not a tailor.” He smiled openly now. “He was furious for a minute. Then he looked at it and said, It’s structurally sound.”

Riarhin laughed. It broke out of her before she knew, short and startled.

“He’s mended it twice since,” Amari said. “Properly. He does a beautiful stitch. He could have taken mine out. He never has.”

“Because it’s structurally sound,” said Saikou, to the candle. Something had moved at the edge of his mouth. “I keep a great many things in order. That’s a fact about the coat.”

“It’s a fact about the coat,” Amari agreed.

She watched the two of them, the tall careful one and the broad one, and understood that she was looking at something very old. She did not know whether it was affection or long habit or a form of shared fear, and had the sense not to reach for the word. He had been Saikou’s subordinate once, that much had come through in the shape of the room, in where each man sat. She did not think either would have said so.

***

He gave her the wake first, because it was the work he was in the middle of, and because, as he said, he could not be sure of the rest of his attention until it had somewhere to sit.

He unrolled a plan of the hall in ink, its long table, its settle, its door, a row of small crosses down the west wall with a date beside each in tiny handwriting. A man named Diarmuid O’Bran, seventy-one, had gone down one spring morning to look at a boundary stone where his ground met the Mac Canas’, and had not come back. His cap was on the rocks. The household had begun the wake with no body, setting a bed and laying it with a sheet, and on the first night at about eleven a wet man had walked into the hall.

“Every night. Eight. He stands in the door, dripping from the shoulders down. He looks at the widow and at the bed. He says, in the speech of this island, ye’ll see it done. The candles turn blue.” Saikou’s pen tapped the plan. “The household has concluded that he is asking them to kill the Mac Canas. Donnchadh has been sharpening a billhook.”

“And you don’t believe it.”

“I don’t know it. I ask what would have to be true.” He turned the plan. “On the fourth night I moved the bed from the north wall to the south. I covered the window. I sat in the place where he usually stands so that he would have to come through me. He stood in the doorway and said it to the north wall, where the bed had been.”

She saw it before he said it. “He didn’t see the change.”

“He saw nothing. I altered the hour, the light, the number of people in the room. I put a cup of milk where his hand would fall. I said his name in the doorway and he did not turn his head.” His voice was very dry. “I cannot say that Diarmuid O’Bran is presently asking anything. It’s a recurrence: a copy of something meant, on a night, and left in the room. But I do not therefore say the wrong is not real.” He tapped a second mark. “The stone has been moved. A foot, perhaps. The old bed is visible under fresh turf. It was shifted within the last year. That is what I must tell the widow tomorrow, and neither half will please her.”

“It’s a good distinction,” said Riarhin.

“It’s the only one I’m entitled to.”

She had listened the way she always did to a man who was good at his work, with pleasure. She had heard it in a healer’s voice and a locksmith’s and, once, in a woman describing how a bridge had failed. It was the sound of someone who knew where the edge of his knowledge ran and stood exactly on it.

“You haven’t asked why I’m here,” she said.

“You’ll tell me.” He almost smiled. “Most people come to me with an explanation in their mouths, and I spend an hour finding out what it’s covering. You’ve come with a face like a shut door.”

She looked at the candle. She had planned in the wood to begin with the amulet, and saw now it was not the beginning. She began with the smaller thing.

“Na’ar is dead.”

Saikou did not move.

“He got us through the gate. His leg gave when he turned. I went back, but I couldn’t get him onto the cart.” She heard her voice and was faintly surprised how level it was. “I thought you should know before anything else.”

There was a very long silence. The candle burned straight.

“I’m sorry,” said Saikou. “I didn’t know him. I’d have liked to.” He looked at his hands. “I heard you say his name once in Adur-Gah, without meaning to, and I noticed you didn’t say anyone else’s that way.”

She had not known that. She sat with it, and Amari, without a word, moved the second cup an inch nearer to her hand.

“Please go on,” said Saikou.

***

It took the length of a candle. He ruled a sheet in three columns, and at the head of each wrote a word, and told her that it was not a court. What she saw herself; what someone else had seen and told her; what she had heard said.

She gave the first column from the place where everything was kept, and heard her own voice go level, and the pictures moved behind it. A green glass the size of a plum stone, cloudy at the centre, in a brass frame wrought like leaves; a hair-thin flaw; one leaf standing a fraction proud of the others. She had found it in a city and carried it a long way. She had put it into a queen’s hands at a table before witnesses, and the queen had smiled as though at a joke against herself that she liked.

“The glass changed colour,” she said. “It was bright when she put it on. In a hall it was darker. Against black cloth in a chamber it was very nearly black. It followed the gown.”

“Not the light.”

“I checked. I saw it in four rooms with four windows.” She met his eyes. “She wasn’t different, Saikou. I went through every word. She was kinder than she had been. More careful. Not strange.”

He wrote it. “I shall mark that one significant. It’s the one you would like to lose.”

“Why?”

“Because you are hoping the stone did it.”

She did not answer.

“I’d hope it in your place. A green thing, an old wrong thing with a will, and a queen who is only its prisoner, and you need only take it off her.” He kept his voice level. “But you’ve told me she was herself. I’d be a poor scholar to let you tuck that away.”

The second column was short. Berent, in fever, on the ship, had said the amulet was warm at a yard, like a hearth; two guards at the Mound had looked at her collar and away. The third was long: that the queen served Myrmis, that it granted wishes with a clause folded in, that it was a prince’s gift, or the prince himself, that the Mound had put it in her hand, that she no longer slept. She gave the last one and said where she had heard it. It had been a fishwife at the quay.

He put down the pen.

“I’ll say what I think in three parts, and be exact.” He held up a finger. “A glass that follows a gown is not evidence of harm. But it followed in four lights and I believe you, and so I accept, as an unwelcome fact, that it responds to something in its surroundings that I can’t account for. I have never seen it. I have read a description of a green stone in brass, and I have handled an object that wore its name. Neither is what you gave her. I will not certify it harmless. I have said so to two men who asked and was rude to both.”

He took a copy from the bottom of the satchel. Someone had drawn the frame larger than life, leaving room beside each leaf for a measurement. There was a number at the top in a second hand, and beneath it a price, crossed out. A strip bearing House Ira’s name had been pasted along the fold.

“You bought this?”

“The copy.” He put a finger over the price. “The description is older. I have not found the person who made it.”

She looked at the leaf that stood proud. The draughtsman had put it on the wrong side. Saikou watched her notice, took up his pen, and waited while she described the difference.

“Part two,” she said.

“Berent’s warmth. One voice, in fever. Beside the glass it is more than either. A stone that changes with its surroundings and gives off heat at a distance is a very small list.” He paused. “It does not make a monster. It doesn’t make a saint.”

“And three?”

“Three is the inference.” He looked at her steadily. “You would like me to say that it is why Lyra is in a cell and Elyas is dead and your friend lies with his leg in a sling. That if we take the thing off her the rest will stop.”

“I’d like to know if it’s true.”

“I don’t know. I won’t say it. It goes further than the paper will bear, and it is the kind of inference that gets people killed.” His hands lay very still. “She has an army and a council and a husband and a dozen reasons of her own. She may be doing exactly what she wishes. She may be doing what a stone wishes. She may be doing what a woman does who has been given a thing that lets her feel she is right. I can’t sort them. Neither can you.”

“Then what can you say?”

“That the object exists and is unexamined, and that it is worn by a woman you mean to kill.”

The candle stood very straight.

She had not said it. She had said I need, and I want to know, and I came for the accounts. Amari had not stirred, but she felt him hear it, the way one feels a large animal turn its head in the dark.

“I inferred it,” said Saikou. “I allow myself that sort. It’s what a person is doing when she asks a man in a strange room to help her think.”

“Yes.”

“I thought so.” He drew a fresh sheet. “Then I have a condition, and I’d like it in writing.”

***

He had written across the bottom of the ruled page, below a line, a single word. After.

“I don’t wish to prevent it,” he said. “That’s not my place. I have a strong view about what happens to an object that has been worn by someone who dies, and no view about the person.” The pen was steady. “When she is dead the thing remains. Someone will have to say who may approach it, and where it is kept, and what is done if the place where it is kept fails. I have seen what a room does that has been left with something and its keeper taken away. People come. They aren’t wicked. They are curious. They pick it up.”

“And?”

“A room that had been quiet becomes a room that has been listening.”

She thought of the keening in the ditch, and the hand she had put out into the dark.

“I’ll make provision.”

“I’d like to hear you say what it is.”

She said it. It took a quarter of an hour, and was not long. When she came to the last clause, there will be a person who knows what it is and who is not me, she heard it come out of her mouth without having known it was coming, and let it stand.

“Who?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then I’ll be that person until you do.” He drew the copy towards him. On its reverse was a plan of a chamber, with a narrow approach and a second wall inside the first. The notes round it were in several hands. One had marked the months when the upper road was closed by snow.

“That’s a strong-room,” she said.

“Possibly.”

“For keeping people out?”

He studied the small space enclosed by the walls.

“I should want to know which side they stood on when they shut it.”

“Where is it?”

“The man who sold the copy did not include a road I would trust.” He returned it to the satchel and wrote beneath After. “It’s method. I have told you I am not fond of keeping things in my head.”

“You keep a great deal there.”

“Some rooms are better kept than others.” He blotted the sheet, and offered her the pen. She took it left-handed and signed, and looked down, and it said Aine.

Saikou looked at it too. “That is not the name I asked for.”

“No.”

“It’s one you’ve said in front of me. That will do.”

He sat back. He was, she saw, tired: the fine lines beside his mouth had deepened, and there was ink on his cuff. When he spoke again it was in a different voice, plainer, like a man setting down a load.

“I have not come to Theona for the wake. I came because of a tale about a prince who could not be named, and because I wished to be somewhere I could be reached. That’s two years’ work, and I’m still following it. What I can say about the object is this. I think that the thing does, in the wearer’s neighbourhood, what a very great deal of success does to a house. First it draws everything into the one hand. Then it makes each part of the arrangement do exactly what it is for. Then everything is more and more brittle, until something that was never meant to break breaks.” He looked at his knuckles. “I don’t have a complete account of it, and I do not know how long it takes. I am telling you an approximation. I’m telling you because it changes what ‘after’ means.”

“It means it doesn’t end when she’s dead.”

“It means I don’t know when it ends.” He raised his eyes. “I’ll tell you what else is true. My mother is very rich. I have not told her what this is. I have asked her for roads and old descriptions. I have not told her where I mean to use them. She has put a sum at my disposal for the work, and I mean to spend it on this. I can pay for people. I can pay for a boat. I’d be a poor friend if I called that principle. It is simply that I can.”

“Nakato Ira.”

“You’ve read about my family.”

“I read a good many things in Adur-Gah.”

“I’ll take a room in Coracle Harbor,” said Saikou, “in my own name. It’s the sensible place. I can draw on her letters of credit there and the—”

“No,” said Amari.

They both looked at him.

“Your own name and her letters of credit,” said the big man, mildly. “In a town that has a Viterran clerk in every second house, and a port bank. She’ll have someone in that bank by Thursday.”

“She wouldn’t.”

“She’s your mother.” He set his cup down. “You’ve moved a very large sum into an unfamiliar country to a place that has no road. She’ll ask who is on the other end of it. It’s what I’d do.”

Saikou looked from Amari to the address he had begun writing. Riarhin watched him take the correction, without hurry, and without any performance of being wounded, and put it where it would be used.

“Then I’ll draw it through a carrier,” he said. “Deirdre’s. Slower. Less exact.”

“And not in your name.”

“Not in my name.” He inclined his head to Amari, a small bow of one man to another. “Thank you. I’d not have thought of Thursday.”

“You’d have thought of it on Friday,” said Amari. “I’d rather not wait.”

***

The wake drew on around them through the afternoon and into evening, and in the end she stayed for the ninth night.

There were eight in the hall when it came. She had been placed against the north wall, where a person could see and be seen by little. The widow sat by the fire, tiny and straight, with a face like a walnut. Donnchadh knelt by her chair. Two of the old women had refused to leave, and one sat by the empty bed; the other stood with her hands in her sleeves. The woman with the red arms had come in with a jug and been told to stay, and had set the jug down and not picked it up again. Saikou sat cross-legged with his paper and the candle, and Amari on his knees at the door with his hands upon his thighs.

A few minutes before midnight a man was standing in the doorway.

He was small and bearded, in a coat that hung in wet folds. Water ran from his hair and sleeves and pooled on the flags without a sound. His face was grey and perfectly composed. He was looking at something at the far end of the room at about the height of a person sitting.

The candles turned blue.

“Ye’ll see it done,” said Diarmuid O’Bran.

There was no menace in it. It was the voice of a tired old man saying at the end of a day the thing he had come to say. He said it to the north wall, where a bed had been.

Saikou did not blink. The bed had been carried to the south wall that afternoon under the widow’s eyes, and the dead man addressed the empty air. Riarhin’s stomach tightened. The room he was speaking to no longer existed.

“I ask whether you have any further word,” said Saikou, clearly. “I ask you to name the person you address.”

The candles wavered. The hair rose at the back of her neck. The widow leaned forward.

“Ye’ll see it done,” said Diarmuid O’Bran, in the same voice, to the same place, and was gone.

Nobody spoke. Saikou put a mark on his paper, and underlined it, and set down the pen. His hands, she saw, were shaking very slightly. Amari, without moving from the door, said quietly, “Nine. Same.”

“Identical,” said Saikou. His voice held. “I timed it. It is not addressed to anyone in this room.”

“It is addressed to me,” said the widow, through her fingers.

“It is addressed to the place where you sat.”

“It’s the same thing.”

“It is not.” It was gentle and quite inflexible. “I do not say it had no meaning. I say the meaning was fixed once, on a particular night, and left. It is a copy. It is a sad, exact copy and I honour it. But I cannot tell you that it is asking for anything now.”

The widow lifted her streaked face. “Then what am I to do?”

“That,” said Saikou, “is a question you can answer in daylight.”

She sat by the fire a long time after they had all gone quiet, and no one told her to go. When she rose at last and went out onto the grass under the bank, the night was clear and full of stars, and the hawthorns stirred their rags on the crest of the ring. Amari was standing in the gate. He did not look round.

“He doesn’t write it down,” she said. “Does he? The beach.”

“It’s not in the casebook.”

“No.”

“It’s in the coat,” said Amari, watching the wood. “I think that was the arrangement.”

She stood beside him. From inside the house came the crooning of the old women, taken up again, low. It had a sound in it now that she thought was rest.

“I’m going to need people,” she said. “Not this minute.”

“I know.”

“He’ll pay for them.”

“He will.” Amari turned his head. It was a broad, scarred, patient face, and there was no unkindness in it. “I’ll go where he goes, Riarhin. I’ve made no secret of it. I’ll go the way I went before.”

“And if it’s not a road?”

“Then I’ll carry him,” said Amari, as if it were a plain fact about weather. “I’ve done it.”

She nodded.

She did not say that she had not been able to. He did not say that he knew. They stood in the gap in the ring of earth until the stars turned, and the thin bright edge of the moon came up over the wood.

***

Chapter 3 — References

The upper room of the Cormorant smelled of smoked fish and wet wool, and Riarhin had taken it for the afternoon because it had a door that shut, a table long enough for six, and a window from which she could see everyone who climbed the outer stair.

She had paid for the room with her own money. She noted the modest sum, and the pleasure of putting it on the innkeeper’s counter without being asked whose.

The people had come to her by roads she could trace. Deirdre’s carters carried names as they carried salt. Amari had gone out three evenings running into the taverns of Coracle Harbor in his brown coat, a large silent man with a cup he did not drink from, and come back each time with a paper or a sentence. Saikou had said only that he wished to be present and would say nothing. He sat at the end of the table with his hands folded on a closed notebook, exactly as he sat in a candle-lit room.

“Four,” Riarhin said. “In the order they said they’d come.”

“And the fifth?”

“There is no fifth.” She counted the cups on the table without meaning to. “Berent will come when he can stand. With him, and the two of you, and myself, that makes eight.”

“Eight.” Saikou considered it. “I’m told it’s an easier number to be in, here.”

“I’m not building to a number.”

“No,” said Saikou, mildly. “It seems to have built itself.”

***

The first was late, and nobody noticed she had come.

She stood inside the door for a moment before Riarhin saw her, and only because the innkeeper’s girl, carrying a tray, walked round her as one walks round a chair. She was tall and lean in a man’s waxed coat, in her late thirties, with dark hair in which two broad streaks of white ran back from the temples like the wings of a bird. Her hands were chapped from the sea. She did not seem in the least put out.

“It’s a habit,” she said, before anyone spoke. “You’ll stop noticing in a while. Or you won’t, which I gather is your gift.” She had a low voice with a laugh underneath it. “Nessa Vey.”

“Say it again,” said Riarhin.

“Nessa Vey.”

“Sit down.”

She sat, and put a letter on the table, and folded her hands on it. The letter had been handled so much that the creases had gone furry. “This is my reference,” she said. “I’d not have brought it, but Amari was very civil and said you liked paper.”

Riarhin read it aloud, because it was short.

To whoever is asking. I cannot tell you her name. I have tried. I remember that a woman took my mother and me over the pass in the snow and sang the guards off us at the toll, and I remember the sound of her voice, and a coat, and that she was tall. I do not remember her face. I do not remember the word we called her. I wrote to the priest who heard our thanks and he could not tell me either. I only wish to say that she was the best help I ever had and that she went away before she had finished laying the hedge along our lower field, which she had promised, and it has been a gap ever since. If you find her, tell her the lambs are getting through.

Nessa put her hand over her mouth. Her shoulders shook.

“It’s a very fair complaint,” she said, when she could. “I meant to come back. It’s only that they forget I said so.”

“That’s the difficulty of it.”

“It is.” She wiped her eyes with the heel of her hand. “Aveh gets people free. I know the songs for slipping a watch and losing a pursuer. Sometimes the people we leave behind forget more than I meant them to.” She drew a white lock forward between her fingers. “The woman who wrote that letter let me sleep by her kitchen fire. When I went back, she asked who had sent me.”

“Did you tell her?”

“I said I’d come about the hedge.” She released the lock. “It needed doing.”

“What do you want?”

“To stay somewhere.” It came out quickly and slightly appalled. “That’s a foolish thing for a cantor to say. I’ve walked out of eleven towns in eleven years. I’ve never been in one long enough to be asked back. I’d like an undertaking that’s worth being remembered in, do you see? Something that has an end and a shape and a reason I can point at when I’m not there.”

“I’m not offering a home.”

“No. I can tell.” Her mouth crooked. “I’d like a job with an edge on it. Something I can finish.”

“What would you do for me?”

“Sing eight people through a door that’s watched, and have the watchers forget it. I’ve done more. I can put out the sense that a place is worth guarding.” She hesitated. “I can’t hold a thing shut. I can only make people forget it was open.”

Riarhin heard the last sentence, and turned it once, and set it beside the first. “What happens when magic doesn’t do what you meant?”

“It usually does.” Nessa’s face changed. She looked at Saikou, who had not spoken. “That’s the other thing. It does exactly what I meant. It’s only that the trouble was never that. I sing a man out of a locked room and he’s out of the locked room. And now he’s in a street.”

“Magic doesn’t solve problems,” said Saikou, from the end of the table. It was the first he had said, and he said it as a man quotes something he has heard a great deal. “It changes them.”

“Who said that?”

“A great many people, in a great many rooms, most of them after the fact.”

“Well.” Nessa gave him a long look. “They’re right.” She turned back to Riarhin. “I’ll tell you what I don’t say to anyone. I’ll be forgotten by the end of it. If we do this, I’ll be the one none of you can quite name in six months. And I’d like—” She stopped.

“Yes?”

“I’d like one of you to keep it. My name. Even if it’s only in your head.”

“It will be,” said Riarhin.

“Everyone says that.”

“I can’t not,” Riarhin said. “Nessa Vey. Tall, waxed coat, a white streak on either side, a laugh you don’t use enough. You said I’d like a job with an edge on it. You said it at half past two.”

The cantor looked at her a long time. The laugh came back into her eyes, and something else came behind it.

“You’re not joking.”

“I don’t.”

“Then I’ll come,” said Nessa Vey. “If it’s finite. I’ll come for the length of it.”

***

The second arrived with a bird, and everyone noticed.

He had a small jaunty way of coming up the stair, one boot at a time, whistling. A magpie sat on his shoulder, glossy and blue-black and with a tail longer than its body. It regarded the room as a customer regards a stall. It looked at the salt cellar with particular interest, and at Saikou’s watch-chain, and last of all at Riarhin. It bobbed its head twice.

“That’s Penny,” said the man. “She’ll not touch anything unless it’s already been given up. Good afternoon.” He was about thirty, dark and neat and slight, in good boots that had belonged to someone else, with a face that was not memorable and had been chosen not to be. “Tavin Rusk. Thief, mostly. The other thing, when it’s tidy.”

“You’re honest.”

“It’s cheaper in the end. I’m told I’m to say who sent me.” He sat, unhurried. “It was a woman at Dunmarrow who lends money at a dreadful rate. She says I’m to tell you she’s a friend, and that I have never once repaid her, and that she would very much like you to take me somewhere I can’t.”

“That’s your reference.”

“It’s a very warm one.” He turned to the innkeeper’s girl, who had come with the tray. “What’s in the stew?”

“Fish.”

“Bring me the largest of it. And the bread, and the good butter, not the salt. Put it on the account of the lady.” He smiled at Riarhin. “It’s a courtesy. It saves us both discussing it.”

“It’s an expense,” said Riarhin.

“It’s a very small one.”

She let the girl go and did not correct her. She saw Saikou’s pen move, quickly, once. She saw Amari in the doorway shift his weight.

“Tell me what you’d do,” she said.

“Open what’s shut. Shut what’s open. Take what wants to be taken and not be in the room when it’s missed.” He tore bread when it came. “I’ve a patron. It’s a small one. Lucky Jack.”

“I’ve heard of him.”

“Everyone has, and nobody prays to him properly. He isn’t fussy.” The magpie had descended to the table and was contemplating the butter. “I’ve his gift. That’s all. Not his rites, I couldn’t manage the rites. He’s never been the sort to ask.” He touched the bird with one finger. “Penny’s the rest. She finds what’s about to be found. It works better when I’m already standing in the right place, which is why I like to be the sort of person who stands in a great many.”

“What does the gift do?”

“It goes on a thing. A tool, a lock, a shoe. Makes a door less inclined to be noticed.” He grinned. “It isn’t permanent, and it isn’t free. It has to be passed on. You give it to the next person who needs it, or Jack gets restless, and a restless Jack is a great deal worse than a sad one.”

“And the other thing, when it’s tidy?”

His hand stopped on the bread. He looked at her.

“You want to know what I’ve done.”

“I want to know how you think of it.”

“Ah.” He thought about it properly, and she liked him a little for that. “I don’t enjoy it. I don’t mind it, which is different, and I’d rather you knew. A man wants a thing done and can’t do it himself, and there’s a payment, and I do it. I don’t ask about the man. I ask about the payment.” He shrugged. “It seems to me the only honest thing about the trade.”

She looked at his plate, which was already empty. She had not seen him eat it.

“Do you have anyone who’ll say you’re reliable?”

“The woman at Dunmarrow will say I’m predictable,” said Tavin. “Which is the same thing, if you’re paying.”

He left. The magpie went with him, but not before it had taken a spoon.

Saikou did not lift his eyes from the notebook. “He was charging you for the meal.”

“He was.”

“He’ll charge you for others.”

“Yes,” said Riarhin. “I could tell.”

“You’re going to take him.”

“He said what he was.” She took the spoon, which Penny had left, in fact, on the sill, and set it back on the table. “I’d rather have someone who wants a thing I can name.”

***

Amari had told her about the third one on the stair, in the low voice he used for things that could not be undone.

The household where the monk had served for two years was relieved that he no longer worked there. Amari had spent an evening with a man who had been the steward, and a young woman who had served the family’s table. Both had said the same things in different words. He was courteous. He was exact. He had never once raised his voice, or broken a rule, or been a moment late. And it was the servants who had begun, a little at a time, to prefer that he were not in the house. A girl would find her brother’s name at her ear on a morning she had not told anyone the brother had been ill. A cook would set down a dish and be thanked by her mother’s name.

“It’s never a threat,” Amari had said. “It’s a courtesy, and it lands. He says it kindly, and he watches what it does.”

“And you think he’s a danger.”

“I think he’s a man who’s found that being feared is a very restful way to live.” The broad face was shadowed. “And that he’s good. I’ll not lie to you. He’s good at what you’d need. I’d rather have him where I could see him.”

Riarhin had said she would think about it, and had done so for two nights, and come to the conclusion she had known she would.

He arrived exactly on the stroke. She heard the bell in the Abbey tower on the hill, and on the last note the door opened, and a man came in and closed it behind him with a hand that did not seem to touch it.

He was perhaps forty, tall and spare, in a plain grey habit belted with cord. His head was shaven and his face had been shaved to the same smoothness, a long unmarked face with deep-set eyes of no particular colour. He carried a small brass lantern, and in the lantern was a white candle, unlit, its wick untrimmed. The noise of the room seemed to draw back from him. She heard the clatter of the kitchen below become a distant thing, as though someone had drawn a curtain across a window.

He bowed to her, an inch.

“Brother Cadan,” he said. It was a soft, unhurried, perfectly level voice.

“Riarhin. Sit.”

“Thank you.” He sat, and set the lantern before him, exactly square with the edge of the table. “I understand you require someone to keep a threshold.”

“I require someone to be quiet when it matters.”

“That is the same thing.” He folded his hands. “I keep the Unstruck Bell. It’s the principal discipline of my order. It means that I do not make a sound until it is required, and that when it is required I make exactly the one that is wanted.” He looked at her steadily. “I have found that people relax in my presence. It is a service.”

“Is it?”

“It has been.”

The girl who had served the stew came up the stair with a jug. She stopped inside the door. Riarhin saw her face go still, the pleasant blankness of a person who has been told, without a word, to stand in a certain place.

“Thank you, Brid,” said Cadan.

The girl’s hand tightened on the handle. She set the jug down on the table without spilling a drop, and did not look at him, and backed out of the room with a small stiff bob. The door closed. Riarhin heard her run on the stair.

“She’d not given you her name,” said Riarhin.

“No. It was in the kitchen.” The eyes did not change. “I try to be polite.”

“And her brother’s?”

“I said nothing of a brother.”

“No,” said Riarhin. “You didn’t.”

She sat back. She was aware of Saikou at the end of the table, still, and of Amari in the doorway with his hand on the frame. She was aware, most of all, of what she felt, which was a small cold click of recognition. It was the sound a lock makes when it agrees with a key.

“What would you want from me?” she said.

“Instructions.” He did not hesitate. “Exact ones. I would like to know what discretion I retain, and where it ends. It is very much easier to serve when a thing is clearly given. It is the reason I left my last employer. He was not precise.”

“He was afraid of you.”

“He was not precise,” said Cadan, with the faintest pressure on the word. “I do not act beyond what is asked. I am not a disorderly man. I ask that the terms be plain, and I observe them.”

“And if the terms don’t cover something?”

“Then I shall come to you.”

“And if I’m not there?”

“I will wait.” The lantern sat between his hands. “I am very good at waiting.”

She looked at him. She knew what she was being told, and that all of it was true. She could not think of a single thing in it that she could point to and call a lie. He had done nothing except say a girl’s name kindly, in a room where he was a guest.

She thought of the roads, and the nights she had lain awake in the Harrow’s lines listening to the men on either side of her breathing, and known which of them was calculating how to reach the pay-chest. She had never once, in those years, been surprised by a man who told her plainly what he was.

She had hired Tavin because he said what he was. She would hire this one for a different reason. She needed a man who could be still for the length of a night, and did not need to be liked.

“I’ll think about it,” she said.

“Of course.”

“I’ll tell you tomorrow.”

“I shall be at the chandler’s on the quay.” He rose and took the lantern. He bowed, and moved to the door, and paused with his hand, apparently, a few inches short of the latch. “The woman who takes your money on the stair has a daughter who is unwell,” he said, gently. “I wished to mention it. I thought you would want to be kind.”

He was gone. Below, in the kitchen, the noise came back all at once, like a thing released.

Riarhin found that she was holding the edge of the table. She let go of it.

“You’re going to hire him,” said Saikou.

“Yes.”

“I’d like it noted that I object.”

“It’s noted.” She did not look up. “I’d like it noted that I know precisely what I’m doing, and that in a month I won’t be able to call it a surprise.”

“That is a very exact way of being wrong,” said Saikou.

“It’s the only kind I’ve ever cared for.”

***

The last of them was a woman, and she came without any fuss at all, and Riarhin knew at once that she was the best of the day.

She was perhaps thirty-five, small and brown, with cropped black hair and deep lines at the corners of her eyes, wearing a shabby linen dress the colour of dust. There was a long pale scar along the left forearm from wrist to elbow, neat as a hem. She carried a canvas satchel and set it on the floor between her feet. She did not touch anything on the table.

“Aurine,” she said. “Most call me the witch, and I let them.”

“Of which patron?”

“Isoka.” She said it without emphasis. “You’ll not care for it. Nobody in this country does. It’s the reason I came.” Her mouth twitched. “I’m told there are no snakes in Theona. It’s very restful. Nobody expects me to know anything about them.”

“Do you?”

“I know what they’re for.” She looked at Riarhin steadily. “I know poison. I’m told that’s what you want.”

“Who told you?”

“Nobody. I have eyes.” She glanced at Amari, at Saikou, at the untouched cup at Cadan’s empty place. “A woman hires a monk and a thief and a singer and a man with a large purse, and asks a witch of Isoka to come and sit in the back. There is one thing that a witch of Isoka does that the rest can’t.”

“I haven’t said what I want.”

“No. I’m waiting for you to.”

Riarhin had prepared the question with care. It was deliberately imprecise, a woman’s question, the sort a nervous client asks. She had used it before. She asked it now in the same easy tone. “If I needed a person incapacitated, for a while, and not harmed, how would you go about it?”

“I wouldn’t.”

“You wouldn’t?”

“You’ve told me two things that don’t sit together.” Aurine folded her hands. “A man is either to be dead or he isn’t. I can do both. I’ve done both. But I can’t do incapacitated, and not harmed, because it isn’t a thing that exists. Either you’re asking for a lie, or you’re asking me to help you tell one.” She said it without heat. “So I’ll wait until you say which.”

The room was quiet. On the sill the light had turned amber. Riarhin looked at the scarred forearm, and the plain hands, and the level eyes.

“I’d want someone dead,” she said. “Later. Not now.”

“Thank you.”

“Have you done it?”

“Yes.” It was said as one says I’ve crossed a river. “Six times that I’d call by name. It’s not a thing I’ve ever pretended was other than it is.” She hesitated. “I’d like to say something, if I may.”

“Say it.”

“I don’t work for a person I’m not allowed to know. I’d like to know who I’m serving. Not the reason. Only the person.”

“You’ll know.”

“And the patron helps when she’s minded. She doesn’t always. If you’re relying on her, I’d rather you didn’t. I’ll do it with what I know.” She paused. “It’s the only honest thing I’ve got.”

“It’s better than most.”

“Is it?” Aurine smiled faintly. “I’ve found honesty is mostly an inconvenience. But people prefer it after.”

She stood. She bowed slightly, an economical dip of the head, and went out with her satchel. Her step on the stair was light and even.

Riarhin sat for some time in the amber light.

“You liked her,” said Saikou.

“She’s clear.”

“She is.” He closed the notebook. He did not look pleased. “I found her very easy to hear. It’s a thing I distrust in myself.”

“You’d rather she’d been evasive?”

“I would rather have been less comfortable than I was.” He glanced at his hands, and up. “I keep coming back to the fact that she answered your question about incapacitation before you’d finished it. I’ve been asked the same by a dozen people and none has ever known the answer so quickly.”

“She’s experienced.”

“Yes.” He did not say more.

Riarhin stood at the window. Below in the street the lamplighter was going along the quay with his pole, and the harbour lay pewter and gold, and boats rocked at their moorings in a long uneven row. A little apart, on the slip, a black coracle lay upside down and someone had chalked a sign on its stern and scrubbed it out.

She counted. She had not intended to. There was herself, and Berent, and the man with the large purse, and the man beside him; a cantor, a thief, a monk, a witch. Eight, exactly.

She stopped at eight. It had not been designed. And she noticed that she was relieved to have stopped, and that the relief was of a kind she did not care for. It was the ease of a woman who has laid a hand on a wall in the dark and found that it was where she thought it would be.

***

Chapter 4 — Little Help

The loft she rented over the chandler’s smelled of tar and rope and mice, and by the end of the first week it also smelled of six people who had not yet learned where to put their boots.

It was long and low, with a floor of scrubbed planks and a window at each end. A bench ran down one wall that Amari had mended on the first morning without being asked, and along the other a row of hooks held nets nobody was going to use. Riarhin had paid the chandler three months in advance and told him they were surveyors. He had looked at Amari, and at Cadan’s lantern, and said in the flat tone of a man who has heard everything that it was a great country for surveying.

She had established rules by the third day. Nobody slept there but Amari, who preferred a door to a bed. Nobody came up the outer stair without knocking. Nobody spoke of what they were for except in the room itself, and never before the chandler’s boy had gone home.

Penny had a rule of her own.

“She’s to have nothing that isn’t already given,” said Tavin, from the doorway, in the tone of a man who had said it many times. “That’s the arrangement. She knows the difference.”

“She has Saikou’s watch-chain,” said Riarhin.

“She had.”

The chain was on the rafter above the bench, coiled like a small bright snake, with the magpie standing over it looking heavy with responsibility. Saikou kept his back to the room. Amari was studying the ceiling.

“I’ll give it to her,” said Saikou, without turning. “It’s not a good chain.”

“It’s a gift,” said Tavin, delighted. “That’s all right, then.” He held up a hand, and the bird went to it, and the chain came down into Saikou’s palm as if it had never been away. “She’s only exercising her professional interest.”

“I’m gratified.”

“Is that sarcasm? I can never tell with scholars.”

“It is a sentiment,” said Saikou, “that I hold at a distance.”

***

Aurine had taken the northeast corner and made it hers.

It was a small clean square of the loft where the light fell from the window at a good angle, with a table scrubbed to whiteness and a stool. Along the wall she set down her materials in a row of small stoppered jars and packets, each labelled in a neat round hand in a compact notation Riarhin could not decipher. She did not explain them. She did not need to. Riarhin looked at the labels once and knew that she had been given all the information she required, which was that the woman kept her things in order and would not be hurried.

On the fourth evening she found her on the stool, with a small wooden box open on her knee.

It was plain, oiled, the size of a loaf. Inside on a bed of pale cloth lay a length of something dry and translucent, the colour of a fingernail. It was coiled, patiently, with the care of a thing that had been handled for years. She looked at it and did not need to be told.
