“It’s a cast skin,” said Aurine. She did not look up. “She left it for me. I’ve carried it for eleven years. I ask, once a season, for something.”

“Ask what?”

“Whatever’s needed.” She lifted the skin with two fingers and laid it in her open palm. “I say what I want. I hold it. If she’s minded, it moves.”

“Does it?”

“On the mainland, always. A little. Enough to tell me she’s heard.” The skin lay flat in her hand and did not stir. “Not here. It hasn’t moved since I landed. I don’t think it’s that she’s refusing. I think there’s nobody at the other end who knows what to do with the request.”

“Because there are no snakes.”

“It’s a fine explanation.” Aurine put the skin back in its cloth and closed the lid. “It’s the one I give. I don’t know that it’s true.” She looked up, and her face had a rueful line in it that Riarhin had not seen before. “I’d like to be plain, since you asked me to be. What I can do here, I can do with what I know. I’ve a great many recipes and a good hand and an excellent memory. I’ve a little help, now and then, when she’s awake. I’ll not tell you it’s more.”

“Does it change what I can promise?”

“It changes what I can.” She rested her hands on the box. “Ask me to do a thing with what I know, and I’ll do it. Ask me to do it with what she gives, and you’re asking a stranger. You hired me. Not her.”

“I’d not thought of it as two.”

“Most don’t. It’s a very pleasant illusion.” She smiled, briefly. “I’ll tell you what the woman in the market said this morning. I asked for a pennyweight of salt. She looked at me and put down her scoop and said, we’ll have none of that here. She meant the trade. She had it from my hands. I don’t know how.” She shook her head. “It’s a country that watches. I’ve stopped taking it personally.”

“Did she sell it?”

“Eventually. Her son did.” Aurine shrugged. “It’s a great deal easier to be disliked when there’s salt.”

***

They rehearsed on the sixth day, and it went badly.

She had meant it as a small thing. She wanted to know what the eight of them, or the six who were in the room, could do in company: which door, which watcher, which hour, and in what order. The loft was long enough to lay out a corridor in chalk. Amari and Cadan were to be the guards. Tavin was to reach the far end and take a small tin box from a table. Nessa was to see him through.

“I’ll do a soft one,” said Nessa. She rolled her sleeves. “Not a deep one. Just enough that they’re looking at one another and don’t feel they should look at me.”

“Do it.”

“Very well.” She closed her eyes and began. It was the lowest music Riarhin had ever heard: a long descending line, a hum with no words in it, hardly louder than breath. She felt it go across the room like a hand smoothing a cloth. At the edges of it she found she could not be sure whether she had eaten that morning.

Tavin walked the corridor. He did not seem to hurry. Penny rode his shoulder with the air of a dowager at a fair. He passed between the two guards, who stood upright and looking at nothing, and picked up the tin box and put it in his coat.

Nobody moved.

“Now,” said Riarhin. “Cadan. Tell me what you saw.”

The monk turned his head. It was a tiny movement, like a lock being tried. “Nothing.”

“Amari?”

“I saw the wall,” said Amari, slowly. “I was looking at the wall.” He frowned. “I’d been meaning to look at it. It’s a very interesting wall.”

“And the door?”

“There was a door?”

Riarhin looked at Nessa. The cantor had opened her eyes. She was pale and smiling, with a small sweat at her hairline.

“That’s the soft one,” she said. “Would you like the other?”

“Not yet.” Riarhin walked the corridor. It was a plain length of planking with two men and a table at the end of it. She found she had been looking at the door for some time. “Amari. Where’s Tavin?”

“Tavin?”

“Tavin Rusk. The man with the bird.”

“Oh.” Amari’s face cleared, and then clouded. “He was here. He was here a moment ago. I’d have said he was in the room.” He looked at Cadan, who did not react. “Where did he go?”

“I’m standing in the room,” said Tavin, quite close to his ear.

Amari turned. It was a slow, honest, appalled movement. “That’s not a thing I like,” he said.

“It’s not meant to be liked.”

“I’d like it noted that I’d have let you go.”

“Yes,” said Tavin. “That’s why I’m alive.”

***

It was Saikou who put it into words.

He had been sitting on the bench with his papers on his knees, not watching, in the way that he did not watch. When Nessa had let the song go and Amari was blinking and shaking his head, he set down his pen.

“I would like to know what they’ll do now,” he said.

“Who?”

“The guards.” He looked at Amari, and at Cadan. “They’ve lost something. A minute, or a quarter of an hour, depending. They’ve been made to look at a wall and not to look at a man. Tonight, or tomorrow, one of them is going to be sitting at his supper and think, why was I looking at that wall? And then he is going to go and find out.”

“He’ll not know what to ask,” said Nessa.

“He’ll know he’s missing an hour.” Saikou’s voice was dry. “A missing hour is a very interesting thing. It doesn’t stay missing. A man who has lost one will go looking, and he’ll begin with the last thing he remembers, and the last person who was in the room. He will come to the table where the box was, and find it gone, and he’ll be very much more alarmed than if he had simply been shouted at.”

“It’s still better than being seen.”

“Is it?” He tilted his head. “You’ve turned a man who saw a thief into a man who suspects himself of having been robbed by nobody. That’s a very different problem. It’s not the same one you started with. It might be easier. It might be harder.” He looked at the cantor gravely. “I’m not criticising. I say only that the song worked, exactly as you meant, and that where it leaves us is not where we were.”

Nessa was quiet a moment. Then she gave a sort of choked laugh.

“That’s what I said. At the interview. It does exactly what I mean.”

“Then we should find out what he remembers tomorrow,” said Saikou.

“That’s no comfort.”

“It isn’t meant as comfort. It’s meant as a reminder of the shape of the thing.” He picked up the pen. “There is a phrase that they teach at the University to those who arrive there believing otherwise.”

“You went to the University?”

“I have lectured at it. I do not recommend it as an occupation.” The corner of his mouth moved. “It is very plain. Whatever you do with a working, the working is not the end. It is the start of the next difficulty. I’d give the same instruction to a child.”

Cadan, who had not spoken, inclined his head a quarter inch. “It is the discipline of the bell,” he said, in his soft dead voice. “What is struck once is heard until it stops.”

“It is a wretched metaphor,” said Saikou, “and I accept it.”

***

The rehearsal went on for another hour, and Riarhin took notes in her head.

Nessa had to be told twice that a soft song for the far end of the corridor meant not for the near. She took an instruction to “keep them looking away” so literally that both guards turned their backs and walked briskly into the wall. Cadan made a suggestion about the sequence of movements, which was excellent and no one wished to hear. Aurine, who had been called in to watch, said that she would not care to do her work at a table with two people standing over it, and it was a fair objection, and it was noted. Tavin took the tin box from the table four times and returned it three, and on the fourth he tried the small trick he had described: he drew out a pierced scrap of tin on a red thread, touched it to the lock and bent over his picks.

The last pin yielded on his first attempt. He opened the door without a sound.

“There,” he said. “That’s all it does.”

“It did that for the door,” said Riarhin. “Does it work on the box?”

“It works on the door I’ve touched. Not on the one I haven’t.”

“Give it to me.”

He looked at her. It was a very brief look, a shrewd, a little wary one. Then he laid the tin token on his palm. Its edges had been worn smooth; the red thread was dark with handling. He touched it to Toothpick’s sheath and tied it beneath the fastening.

She felt nothing. The blade lay cool against her wrist.

“That one’s yours, then,” said Tavin. “It’s not to be kept, mind. I told you. It goes on. You’ll know who.”

“And if I don’t?”

“You will. It generally isn’t difficult.” He smiled. “You’ll think, I’d rather this was in someone else’s pocket, and there it is.”

“That’s not comforting.”

“No. But you’ll notice you haven’t said no.”

“I haven’t.”

“No,” said Tavin, with a look of contentment, and gathered his bird.

***

They all went at last, in ones and twos, down the outer stair. She stood in the loft with the lamp lit and the chalk corridor faint on the boards, and listened to the boots fade. She thought she was alone until Saikou shifted on the bench beneath the far window.

His back was straight, the papers squared on his knee. He had the satchel open beside him. A folded cloth lay across his lap. He was holding, in both hands, a small round mirror in a plain silver frame.

He looked up.

“I don’t sleep here,” he said. “But I like to be sure of it.”

He laid the cloth over the mirror, folded the corners under, and set it face down on the bench beside him. It was neatly done. It was a thing done a thousand times.

“The custom in a house in mourning,” he said. “I’ve found that I do it now everywhere.”

She did not answer. She saw that he had laid out the rest of his papers on the plank beside him, in a row, each squared to the edge, and that in the row, between the tenth sheet and the eleventh, was a space the width of a page.

She had seen him leave such spaces before, among the papers in Adur-Gah and on the floor at the ringfort.

“It’s a gap,” she said, quietly.

“Yes.”

“You didn’t know you’d left it.”

“I did not.” He looked at it. There was no change in his face. “I know it’s there. I know I left it. I know a page belongs. I do not know what was on it.” He touched the edge of the eleventh sheet. “I traded for a name once. It was a small creature’s, under a hill, and it wished for a memory in return and I gave it one. I know I gave one. I know that I was glad to.”

“What was it?”

“I don’t know,” said Saikou. He said it as a man says the time. “I asked a man who was present, and he said he had no idea what I meant, and I believed him. I have not asked again.”

“Does it frighten you?”

“Yes.” He turned the cloth-wrapped mirror over once, and set it down again. “It does not stop me.”

“I know.”

“I had thought you might.” He gave her a small formal nod, as of one professional to another. “I’d be obliged if you didn’t mention it to anyone.”

“I won’t.”

“Thank you.”

She stood at the door with her hand on the latch. From the quay below, through the floor, she could hear the slow slap of water against stone. He gathered the papers, leaving the space until last. She waited at the door.

She blew out the lamp.

***

Chapter 5 — Eight Boats Without Names

Maelis sold her the introduction in a costume store behind the players’ hall, standing on a stool with a mouthful of pins.

“It’s a real one,” she said, round them. “I want you to understand that. I’ve not made it up, and I’ll not have you say afterwards that I promised more.” She was pinning the hem of a long black gown on a wicker figure that had been a dead queen the night before and would be a drowned one tomorrow. There was a colour in her face that Riarhin had not seen since the ship. “Muirenn Varr has her boats hauled up at Scarrow for the spring. The players go there on Thursday to give the crews a play. She always comes. She sits in the second row with her arms folded and hates it.”

“Does she know you?”

“She knows I dress the cast.” Maelis took a pin out of her mouth. “I’ve not told her a word about you. I’ve said that a foreign woman I know is looking for passage and protection and can pay. She said that was what everybody said. Then she said, has she got a face I’d remember?”

“What did you tell her?”

“That you’d take some doing.” She pushed the pin home. “That’s the introduction. It gets you into the row behind hers on Thursday, and a chance to say your own piece when the play’s done. It doesn’t get you a good word, and it doesn’t get you another, and if it goes badly I’ve never met you.”

“That’s very clear.”

“I meant it to be.” She turned on the stool. She was a small woman, precise, with hair pinned so tightly that it seemed to be a matter of principle, and there was in her face at that moment something that looked very much like fear held level. “I’ve been coerced by nearly everyone. You included. I’d like to be paid for something, once, and have it be my own.”

“You’re being paid.”

“I’m being paid to sew.” The corner of her mouth twitched. “And I’m selling you a row of seats. I find I don’t mind either.”

Riarhin thought of Mara’s level look in the kitchen doorway. She thought that she had not once, since the Mound, seen this woman without a small fear at her elbow, and that she had put it there herself.

“I’ll take the row,” she said.

“Good.” Maelis bent to the hem. “I’ll not give you the second. You’ll have to do your own.”

***

Scarrow was a harbour of grey stone at the head of a narrow bay, and its principal industry was the boats.

There were eight of them. She counted them from the cart as they came down the track, though nobody had told her to: eight great hide-skinned vessels, drawn up in a line on the shingle above the tideline like the bodies of enormous sleeping animals. They were not river coracles. They were the sea-going kind, twenty feet from stem to stern, with a keel-board and a high lip and room in each for a dozen men and cargo. Their skins had been tarred black and were bright with grease, and their oars were laid across them in neat rows.

None of them had a name.

She looked for it. There was the place on the stem where a name is generally painted, and on each it was bare. On the gunwale of the nearest boat, tied through the bow-ring and hanging down over the side in a small loop, was a length of grey thread. It was wet. The morning was dry.

“That’s the wash,” said the carter, who was one of Deirdre’s and had not said a word in three hours. “They put it on before they go out.”

“Every boat?”

“Every one.” He clicked his tongue at the horse. “You’ll not hear a man on that shore say the number of them out loud. They call them the fleet. They’d as soon call their own mothers by a number.”

She saw what he meant later, when she was standing among the crowd at the players’ platform with a cup of thin beer. A young man beside her, gawping at the boats, said, “Eight and—” and stopped, with his mouth open and no sound in it. A woman elbowed him. Neither looked at the shore. He shut his mouth and took a mouthful of beer and did not seem troubled, as a person is not troubled who has put his foot on a step and found it, and gone on.

The play was about a drowned king and his three daughters. It was performed with vigour on a platform of planks, and the crews roared at every entrance. Maelis’s dead queen came on in the third act and lay down on a bier of oars and had an unusually convincing pallor. Riarhin watched from the third row. She watched the back of the head in the second: broad, sun-browned, tied up in a rough knot with a strip of blue cloth. It did not move. It was a head that had made up its mind.

At the end, when the drowned king had been carried off and the crews had gone to the beer barrels, the woman turned round.

“You’re Maelis’s foreigner,” she said.

She was perhaps thirty-five, thick-shouldered, with a broad weathered face and a jaw she had inherited and had learned to use. Her hands were rope-scarred and her boots were good. She wore a leather jerkin over a fisherman’s smock and a knife on her hip. She looked at Riarhin, and it was the look of someone who has spent a life pricing things.

“Muirenn Varr,” she said. “Sit down. You’re blocking the light for my crew’s dinner.”

***

They talked on the stone quay, with their backs to the shingle and the eight boats behind them like a wall.

She had a way of listening that Riarhin recognised at once from the Harrow’s pay-tent: not attention so much as an assessment of what was being left out. She let Riarhin say it all, and asked no questions, and at the end said only, “So you want to cross.”

“With a small company. To the mainland. Not to a port a Viterran clerk can see from his window.”

“To Davra’s ship, I’d guess. She’s got a hull that goes where it’s told.” She nodded. “I can do that. I’ve crews that’ll take a party out to a rendezvous and not ask about the cargo, and put them off at the meeting with the mainland vessel. What happens after, I can’t promise. I don’t have a name on a wharf.”

“I don’t need a wharf. I need people who won’t be asked to say what they carried.”

“That’s no trouble.” She looked at the shore, at the tarred hulls. “You’ve seen the wash?”

“Yes.”

“Nobody who crews for me names a boat. It’s a thing you’ll hear about, it makes strangers laugh. There’s a story that if you name a boat in this country, something answers to it.” She said it without the smile. “I’ve found it cheaper not to test it. It’s not superstition. It’s an economy.”

“And the thread?”

“It’s the Gray Liturgy’s. It’s not mine. It’s the crews’.” She shrugged. “I’m told it helps.”

“Does it?”

“It helps them.” Muirenn turned her head, and the flat grey light showed the lines beside her mouth. “I’ll tell you what I don’t say to just anyone. I’ll take you across. I’ll take your six or seven or eight. That’s a matter of a day’s work and a bit of luck, and you’d pay me the usual. But it isn’t what I want.”

Riarhin waited.

“It’s only a passage,” said Muirenn. “The passage is small. I’m a woman with eight boats and a father, and my father wants the boats, and I’m not going to let him have them.” Her voice was level. “There’s a landing at Rossan. Brannoc’s. He’s a decent man, he isn’t clever, and he’s asked me to marry him. It’s a good marriage. It joins my work to a landing that’s never been raided and it ends a feud between his house and mine that’s cost eleven lives in my lifetime. It’s to be sealed at the High Hall at the autumn gathering, under the Bride-Charter, and it makes a peace that lasts a season, and after the season, if the water allows, another.”

“And your father objects.”

“My father doesn’t object. He improves.” She said it like a curse. “He’s very good at it. He says he’s delighted. He says it’s a fine match and he’ll add his own to it. He’ll fold the marriage into the compact he’s making with the mainland, with the queen’s men, whoever they are this month, and he’ll put me at the head of a larger fleet. Under him.” She drew a breath. “It’s the best offer I’ve ever had. It makes me a woman with eight boats and no say in where they go.”

“You’ve refused it.”

“I’ve refused it eleven times.” She looked Riarhin in the eye. “He’ll be eleven times as polite the twelfth. That’s what he does. He doesn’t force. He waits, and he’s kind, and he’s always got a better reason than you, and one morning you find you’ve agreed.”

“I see.”

“Do you?” It was flat. “Let me make sure. You came looking for a passage. What I’m offering is a second job, which is why I asked Maelis to bring you at all. The passage comes with it. The Abbey and the landing would pay the rest. You may buy the passage alone; I’ll not pretend otherwise.”

Riarhin had known for some time what it was. She had known it since the cart. It was in the way the woman held her hands, laced together in front of her, one thumb over the other, as one who has held a thing still for a very long while. She had wanted, absurdly, for it to be something else. She had wanted a threat that could be removed by negotiation, a rival who could be sent an offer. She had spent three weeks building a company that would, on the whole, be good at problems of that sort.

“Say it,” she said.

“I want my father dead.” Muirenn did not look away. “Before the autumn gathering. Before the Charter’s sealed, or as near as makes no difference. I’ve thought about it a long time. I’ve thought about doing it myself and I can’t. I’d have every man in his household against me and I’d lose the boats. I need it done by someone with no name in the harbour who can go away.”

The wind had begun to freshen. Behind them, the great black hulls creaked on their keels.

“You’re not asking to be forgiven,” said Riarhin.

“Should I?”

“It’s customary.”

“I’ve no use for it.” The jaw set. “He’s my father and I know what he is. He’s brave, and he’s generous, and he’s been very kind to me, and I’d rather see him dead than let him make me his daughter for another year.” A pause. “I’m telling you the truth because I think you’d know if I didn’t. I need you to understand what I’m asking before you agree.”

“I’d need to see him.”

“You’ll see him. I’ll bring you.”

“I’d need to speak to Brannoc. To the sponsors. I’d need to know what each of them will do when he’s dead.”

“Naturally.”

“I’m not saying yes.”

“I’d have thought less of you if you had.” Muirenn nodded, once. “You’ll want an hour. I’ll be at the fish shed.”

She rose and went along the quay with a rolling, unhurried stride. The crew’s dinner had begun; a ring of them squatted on the shingle round a fire, and one of them lifted a hand to her as she passed and she raised hers, without smiling, in reply.

Riarhin remained on the stone.

She looked at the boats. She looked at the wet grey thread on the nearest bow-ring, and the bare place on the stem, and the wide lip of the hull where a man had leaned and said eight and— and stopped.

I came for a passage.

She could still buy one. Saikou had offered enough for that. On the far shore there would be eight people to feed, information to buy, rooms to keep while they learned how to reach the queen. She could ask his mother to finance more of it through him. Or she could go to Whiterick and hear what this woman’s father was worth to the people who wanted him gone.

At the fish shed Muirenn had stopped to speak to a crewman. Riarhin watched her take a knife from him and test its edge against her thumbnail. She had not yet turned to see whether Riarhin was coming.

She thought of the round white face of the Mound’s window, and Lyra’s handwriting on a page of sworn statements, and a queen turning her head at supper. She thought of what she had said to Saikou in a stripped room, I want to know where the accounts part company, and how it had sounded, under the candle, like a decision that was still hers to make. Veralyn had been decided on the beach. This man had not.

She had hired a company for a purpose she could say aloud, in a room with a shut door, to men and women who wanted things she could name. She had not expected the first commission to walk up and hand itself to her, in daylight, under a play about a drowned king.

She got up. The wind was cold and had salt in it.

Behind her, without any sound she could name, the wet thread on the nearest boat lifted from the gunwale, hung a moment in the still air, and settled.

***

Chapter 6 — Whiterick’s Price

They crossed to Whiterick by night, in the fourth of Muirenn’s boats, and Riarhin sat on her hands.

She did it deliberately. She had noticed on the first mile, when a light came alongside the coracle low and gold on the black water, keeping pace a length off the port side, that her left hand had drifted over the gunwale. She had put it under her thigh and sat on it. The other followed in a little while. Nobody in the boat remarked on it.

The singing began as the point fell behind. It was a woman’s voice, slow and sweet, in a language she did not know, and it broke at the end of each phrase on a note so like grief that the back of her throat closed. It was the ditch by the herdsman’s shelter again, with the sea under it. The eight rowers pulled with their eyes on the bottom boards. The old man at the steering-oar hummed something tuneless and cheerful and sure, the way a man does who is walking past a door he knows.

“It’s somebody’s mother,” said Muirenn, from the bow, without turning. She sat with her back to the stern and her boots up on the ribs, as easy as a woman on her own hearth. “Somebody who’s been a while in the water. It’s only the sound they make when they’d like to be taken home.”

“Do you answer it?”

“You can’t. It’d be a great deal of trouble to you afterwards.” She looked at Riarhin then, for the first time since Scarrow, and the flat grey eyes held a plain and not unkind attention. “It follows anyone who’s been crying lately. I’d have thought you’d be used to it.”

“I haven’t cried.”

“No,” said Muirenn. “That’s what it follows.”

The light slid away astern. The song went on for a long time after the light had gone.

***

The Abbey of Whiterick stood on a green shoulder of ground above a small harbour, and when they came up to it in the grey of the morning there was a crowd at the gate.

She had expected a fortress. What she saw was a long, low, rambling collection of buildings in whitewashed stone, roofed in slate and bracken, set about a court like a farm that had grown by accretion. Outside the gate a queue had formed, fifty or sixty people, and she read it as she read every crowd. A woman with an infant tied to her back. Two old men with a cart of turnips. A man with his arm bound up in a bloody rag. A girl of eleven leading a blind goat. Every one held a bundle or a jar or a pot and every one, without exception, had brought something to give.

The gate was open. A sister in a black habit stood in it, receiving what was handed her with a small dry nod, and passing each person through. On the far side, in a hall whose double doors stood open to the court, she could see long tables laid for a meal.

“It runs late,” said Muirenn. “Every day. There’s always somebody who won’t come in.”

She was right. At the head of the queue, on the threshold itself, stood a very old woman in a shawl and clogs. She held a covered basket in both hands. She was not blocking the door; the sisters had stepped round her a dozen times. She had simply stopped, at the exact place where the flagstone changed colour, and would not go in and would not go away. Her lips were moving. A young sister stood beside her, still, with her hands folded, listening.

“What does she want?” Riarhin asked.

“Nobody knows. She’s been coming eleven years.” Muirenn shrugged. “She brings a loaf. She stands there. Somebody stands with her. At noon she goes home.”

“And they let her.”

“It’s an abbey,” said Muirenn. “They’d let the sea in if it asked politely.”

They waited among the others, in the queue, because the Abbess had sent a message that she would see them when the morning meal was done, and nobody in the country jumped a queue for a woman with a loaf. Riarhin stood with her hands in her sleeves and read the court.

There was a low arched doorway in the north range, and it stood open on a small, plain, whitewashed chapel. She could see into it from where she stood. On a slab of black stone before the altar burned a flame the size of a hand. It gave off no smoke. It was pale, almost white, and it did not flicker when the door swung. A brother sat beside it on a low stool with a jug of water, and did nothing. He looked as though he had done it for some years.

It looked, to her, like a thing that had never once been asked to be more than it was, and had found that restful.

She put her hands away again.

***

The Abbess received them in a small room behind the refectory, and the first thing she said was that she had no intention of keeping them standing.

“Sit. You’ve been on the water all night.” She was an old woman, seventy or so, small and upright, in a black habit with a white cowl. Her face was lined and calm, and her eyes were light brown and unhurried, and on her breast hung a plain round pendant of bright metal with a tiny hole at its centre. “Muirenn. Your father sends his regards.”

“Does he?”

“Through a man who sends me barrels. He’s a very courteous man, your father.” She lowered herself into a chair with a small sigh. “And you’re Aine.”

“Yes.”

“Eilis’s guest. I’ve heard a good deal, and most of it was generous.” The smile was faint and dry. “You’ll have tea. It’s dreadful. We make it in a pot the size of a font.”

It was dreadful. It was also very hot, and Riarhin drank it. The room was plain, with a table and four chairs and a window onto the herb garden, and a shelf of ledgers in brown leather that she did not look at.

“I’ll tell you what I know,” said Caelia, “and you’ll tell me what you like.” She folded her hands. “Donnan Varr is planning something. He isn’t hiding it. He is a man who has, for thirty years, raided the mainland coasts and sold the results to whoever would buy, and who has lately noticed that the mainland is going to be a very different place. There is a queen in Valora, and she has just taken a city that no one has taken in living memory. He wishes to be on the right side of that. He is selling her captains passage and crews. He has offered to put every boat in this country at their disposal, in return for a licence to hold the ports.”

“Whose ports?”

“Ours.”

The word fell in the small room with a flat sound. Muirenn, in the other chair, had not moved.

“I am an Ecktorian,” said Caelia gently. “The Everflame in this house has burned for four hundred years, and it is not a Viterran flame. The people who come to this gate are Ecktorian, and Theonan, and something older than either, and they come because the Abbey has never asked whose subjects they are. If Donnan makes his bargain, one of two things happens. Either the queen’s captains take Whiterick under their protection, which is another name for their thumb, or she decides the Abbey is a nuisance and begins to send her own clergy.” She spread her hands. “I do not want either. I have been Abbess for thirty-one years. I would like to die in the same country I was born in, with the same loaf on the same doorstep.”

“You’ve helped him before,” said Muirenn.

“I have.” No colour came into the old face. “Raiders have burned this island twice in my life. I found it cheaper to feed them. I have bought the peace of this Abbey with grain and lamps and a certain amount of looking away. I’m not proud of it. I did it because a hungry child is a hungry child.” She looked steadily at Riarhin. “I tell you because I want you to know exactly what sort of woman you are talking to. I have helped raiders, and I am now willing to pay to be rid of one. I don’t think you will find that surprising.”

“No.”

“And I know what you are.” The brown eyes did not leave hers. “I have known for three weeks, since a carter told me a foreign woman had taken a loft at Coracle Harbor and filled it with people who ask no questions. There is a witch among them who serves Isoka.”

Riarhin held her face still. “I don’t know that.”

“You do. I will not have Isoka invoked in this house.” Caelia moved the bread towards Riarhin. “Her hands and her learning are another matter. I know what I would be buying. She must not mistake that for a welcome to her patron.”

Nobody spoke. The window showed a strip of green, and a sister moving between rows of thyme with a hoe.

“What are you offering?” said Riarhin.

“Supplies. Silver. Something in the way of shelter, if it’s wanted.” Caelia inclined her head. “It goes to Muirenn in the first place, since it’s her marriage. I’d only ask that the Abbey stand at the sealing, as guarantor, and that any dispute arising from the Charter be brought to this house for arbitration.” She waited. “It is a customary request.”

“It is not,” said Muirenn.

The Abbess turned her head.

“It’s customary,” said Muirenn, “for the Abbey to attend. To bless the cloth. It’s not customary for it to hold the deed.” Her voice was quiet and level. “You’d be the court of appeal for every quarrel between me and my husband for as long as we’re married. Every argument about a boat, or a landing, or a cargo. You’d be sitting at my table for the rest of my life.”

“I would be a friend.”

“You’d be a friend with a claim.”

“I’m an Abbess,” said Caelia, patiently. “It’s the same thing.”

“No.” Muirenn set down her cup. “I’ll take your grain. I’ll take your silver, and I’ll pay it back with interest, and I’ll thank you in front of the whole of the Hall. I will not sign that. If the price of my father’s death is that I can never again sell a load without asking leave of the Abbey, I’d sooner keep my father.”

“He’ll make you his.”

“I know.”

“He will,” said Caelia, and for a moment something moved in her face, an old sad truth that was not a bargaining position. “I have watched it done. He is kind to you and you cannot refuse him. I don’t say it to move you. I say it because I would rather you were free, and the Abbey were safe, and I don’t see why both cannot be had.”

“They can.” Muirenn’s jaw set. “Not on those terms.”

The old woman was silent. Then she sighed, and did not seem displeased, and turned her light unhurried gaze on Riarhin.

“And you?”

“I’ve said nothing.”

“You’ve been very good at it. It’s the reason I asked you here.” She leaned back. “Will you take it?”

“No,” said Riarhin.

Muirenn looked round.

“Not today.” She put the tea down. She had been thinking about it since the boat, without having found the words, and she said the first plain ones that came. “I’ve been offered a man’s death by his daughter, and now a woman’s money by her Abbey. I’ve not yet seen the man. I haven’t met the groom. I don’t know what he will do the morning after, or what your sisters will do, or what the High Hall will make of a corpse at its gathering. I don’t know what any of you can deliver. Until I do, I’ll not say what I will.”

“That’s prudent,” said Caelia.

“It’s only method.”

“I said prudent.” The old woman’s eyes were bright. “You’ll forgive me. I’ve been offered a great many professions of moral unease by people who wanted me to pay them for it. Nobody has ever said to me, I need to check the pantry first.”

“It’s a good pantry.”

“It’s an excellent one.” She almost laughed. “Go and check it. I’ll keep the offer open till the Hall. There’s a fee, of course, and the Abbey will pay it, and I’ll make no fuss about the terms I wanted, which Muirenn will not give me.” She turned her head. “Not yet.”

“Not ever,” said Muirenn.

“We shall see.”

***

They ate in the refectory, at the long table, among the crowd from the gate. It was a plain meal, oatmeal and boiled cabbage and a hunk of black bread, and Riarhin had two helpings.

The old woman with the loaf had been brought in at last. She sat at the far end with her basket on her knee, and a sister sat beside her, and neither of them said a word. She held the loaf in her two hands and looked at it. She did not eat.

“What is it she wants?” Riarhin asked, of the girl beside her, who was eleven and had a blind goat under the bench.

“To be asked in,” said the girl, matter-of-fact.

“She’s been asked.”

“Not by the right person.”

“Who’s that?”

The girl considered her. She had a round grave face and enormous dark eyes, and the goat put its nose into her lap. “She says he’ll know her,” she said. “When he comes.”

“Who?”

“Whoever it is.” The girl shrugged. “She’s not been told.”

Riarhin looked down the table at the old woman’s hands, folded round a piece of bread that nobody had asked her to eat. She thought of a saucer of milk in a wall, and a stool turned to face the plaster, and a bell hung in a tower that could not ring a ninth note.

She was aware, quite suddenly, of a great many people sitting close together and none of them looking at her, and of what a quiet gift it was.

When they went out at last through the gate, the flame in the chapel was still burning in its dish. She looked in as she passed. The brother on the stool was asleep. The flame stood straight and pale and colourless, and she thought, without knowing why, of the way Saikou had tucked a cloth round the mirror. It did not seem to want to be looked at, either.

Muirenn was waiting on the road. She had a piece of bread in her hand and did not eat it.

“Well?”

“You were right,” said Riarhin. “She’s not asked for the deed to your boats.”

“She’ll ask again.”

“I know.”

“And you didn’t say yes.”

“I didn’t say no to the rest.” She fell in beside her, and they went down the road towards the harbour, where the boat that had brought them waited below the Abbey steps. “I’m not going to give either of you what you want because it would be convenient. I’d like to see him first.”

“You will.” Muirenn’s stride was long and quick. “My father dines at Scarrow on the Sunday. You may come as a stranger, and take a chair, and hear him.”

“As what?”

“What you are. A mainland veteran, come to look at the fleet.” She glanced sideways. There was, for a moment, something in the broad weathered face that Riarhin had not seen before, and did not at once know how to name. It was very like shame, and it was not for herself. “He’ll like you,” she said. “That’s the worst of him. He’ll like you at once.”

***

Part Two — What the Bride Owns

***

Chapter 7 — The Red Oar

Donnan Varr’s hall stood at the head of Scarrow with its gable to the sea, and by the time Riarhin came up to it on Sunday evening he had already, without her seeing it, made the room his.

She had walked from the quay with Berent on her arm and Muirenn a step ahead. Berent was thinner than he had been at Eilis’s and he had a stick, and he had insisted that morning, at some cost to Mara’s patience, on a clean shirt and a shave. He was to be a knight of a minor house, down on his luck and looking for an honest berth. It was very nearly true. Mara had cut his hair and told him that if he fell over she would not catch him.

The hall was long and smoky and loud. Along the north wall a fire ran the length of a stone trough, roaring, and the benches on either side were packed with crewmen in wet wool, eating. There were perhaps a hundred. Every head turned as Muirenn came in, then turned back to the meal. She had walked into that room ten thousand times.

At the far end, on a chair with a high carved back, sat the man they were all facing.

He was bigger than she had expected, and older. He was sixty, perhaps, or a hard fifty-eight: a great red-bearded man gone white about the jaw, with a barrel chest, thick forearms bare to the elbow, and hands like a pair of spades. His hair was thin and cropped. He wore an old leather jerkin, patched at the shoulder, over a good linen shirt, and a heavy ring on his thumb. He was pouring, with his own hand, a pot of ale into the cup of a boy who could not have been more than twelve, and he was telling the boy something that made him laugh.

“Muirenn!” He did not raise his voice. It carried. “You’ve brought me a stranger.”

“I’ve brought you two.”

“Two! Then I’m rich.” He put down the pot and rose, and a space cleared round him at once, without anyone moving. “Come here, girl. Let me look at you. You’re thin. Are they feeding you at Rossan?”

“I’m not at Rossan.”

“You will be.” He caught her by the shoulders and kissed her forehead, one great hand behind her head, and she stood for it with her arms at her sides. It was quick and warm and perfectly natural. Riarhin, watching, thought that in another life the woman would have liked it.

“And this is the friend.” His eyes came to her. They were a bright washed blue in a weathered face, and they took her in with a single unhurried sweep, hands and boots and the set of the shoulders. “Aine, is it? Muirenn says you’ve been about the mainland. Fought there.”

“A little.”

“A little.” He smiled. “That’s what I said to my first wife about the sea.”

***

The seat they gave her was at his right hand, one down from Muirenn. She noticed it. The place of honour at a Theonan table, she had been told, was the second from the head, not the first, because the first belonged to the host’s luck. She sat where she was put and looked at the room.

There was a great deal to see.

On Donnan’s other side sat a broad grey woman with a plain braid and a knife at her belt, watching the benches with the mild permanent attention of a sheepdog. Riarhin took her for Halla, the captain of his household. She had a way of moving through the room without seeming to. A man at the far bench had got to his feet, red in the face, and opened his mouth; Halla, without hurrying, had reached his shoulder before he could speak, put a hand on it, murmured something in his ear, and steered him back to his seat with a mug in his hand. Nobody had noticed but Riarhin, and the man himself, who looked, in the end, rather pleased.

At the foot of the table, among the old hands, a lean stooped man in a black coat was eating boiled mutton with an air of scientific curiosity. He had a long serious face and spectacles pushed up onto a high forehead. He kept a small brass box beside his plate and occasionally, between mouthfuls, opened it and made a note.

“That’s Heber,” said Muirenn, low. “He keeps my father alive.”

“How?”

“Mostly by being ignored.”

The meal came in waves. Fish, then mutton, then something with barley, then a pudding hot with spice. Donnan ate as he did everything, with the whole of himself. Between courses he worked the room. She watched him do it, and could not have said what exactly it was, except that no one who spoke to him went away without the sense of having been heard.

A grey old man came to the dais, his cap twisting in his hands. Donnan knew his name. He knew his son’s, who had gone down off the Skerries the winter before last, and the name of the son’s wife, and the fact that the wife had been ill in the spring. He gave the old man a purse without looking at it and told him a boat would be rigged for the family by the autumn fair, and the old man went out with his head high.

A woman came whose husband had been taken by a Viterran patrol off the Skerries and was, she believed, in a prison at Dun Lorne. Donnan asked her three sharp questions, sent for Halla, said a few words, and told her that a man would be at Dun Lorne within the week with a purse for the gaoler.

A very young man came, red-faced and rocking on his heels, and said that he had gone on the raid at Kilmarnan in the autumn and that his brother had not come back.

The hall quietened.

“Aye,” said Donnan. His voice did not change. “Fiachra, that was. Your mother’s boy.” He nodded slowly. “A good lad. I’d have taken him again.”

“He’d not been asked,” said the young man. “He went because you’d called for men.”

“He did.”

“And it was you who said the village would have no watch.”

“It was.” Donnan looked at him. The bright blue eyes did not flinch. “There was a watch. It was greater than I’d been told. That was my error, and your brother paid for it. I’ll not pretend it was otherwise.” He reached into his jerkin and laid a small heavy bag on the table. “That’s twenty in silver. It’s twice what I’d give for a hand lost. I’ll give your mother the same again at midwinter.”

The young man’s mouth worked. “It’s not—”

“It isn’t what he was worth. I know.” Donnan’s voice was very gentle. “Nothing is. It’s what I have. I’d not insult you by saying it’s enough.”

“You’re paying,” said the young man, with a sort of despair, “for a thing you’d do again.”

“I would.” It was said quite simply. “If the same night came again, and the same word, I’d take the same men. That’s why the silver’s not an apology. It’s the only honest thing I’ve got.” He nodded to the bag. “Take it to her. Say I’ll come at the next new moon and sit with her, and she may say what she likes.”

The young man stood a moment. Then he took the bag, and bowed, awkward and swaying, and went out with his back very straight.

Nobody spoke. The fire cracked in the trough.

Riarhin found that her hands had closed. She opened them. She thought of the Harrow’s pay-tent, and of a captain she had known who paid the widows of men he had sent to the wall on the day after, in coin, without a word, and was loved for it. He had provided for their grief before they knew to feel it.

He had done it perfectly. He had not said one word that was untrue.

And there, in the smoke and the noise and the good ale, was the boy who had asked. The boy would go home and his mother would take the silver, because there was a winter coming.

“He’s not lying,” she said, very low, to Muirenn.

“Not about the silver,” said Muirenn.

***

Heber caught him at the pudding.

It was done so quietly she nearly missed it. The lean doctor had risen from the foot of the table, wiped his fingers, and come up the hall with his brass box under his arm. He stopped at Donnan’s elbow, and for a moment said nothing at all, and Donnan, who had a spoon halfway to his mouth, lowered it.

“Not now.”

“I’ll only be a minute.”

“You said that on Thursday.”

“You said not now on Thursday.” Heber’s voice was dry and unhurried. “The hand.”

“It’s well enough.”

“It’s swollen. You’ve been favouring it since the Skerries.” He set the box down among the plates and opened it, and took out a small square of clean linen. “Give it to me.”

“I’ve a hall to feed.”

“And a hand to keep, if you want to go on feeding it.”

There was a small pause. At the far benches somebody laughed and was hushed. Halla, on the other side, stirred, and looked at nothing with great tact.

Donnan sighed. It was long and heartfelt. Then, with a gruff, rueful, entirely genuine smile, he laid his great red right hand on the table between the bread and the salt. The knuckles were thick and knotted, the second finger crooked, and along the back ran a white ridge of old scar. The base of the thumb was puffed and shiny.

“It’s an old wound,” he said, to Riarhin. “A man at Kilmarnan with an axe. I didn’t see him till he’d done it.”

“It’s not old,” said Heber, kneeling. “It’s old and re-opened. You’ve been using it to row.”

“A man’s got to row.”

“A man’s got to have a hand.” He turned it over, gently, and pressed with two fingers, and Donnan winced, and swore, and the doctor said “Yes,” with quiet satisfaction, and began to bind it. “You’ll not lift anything over a stone for ten days. I’ve said it before. You didn’t attend.”

“I attended.”

“You listened.” Heber tied the linen. “It’s not the same.”

“He’s right,” said Halla, unexpectedly, from the other side.

“You’re on his side.”

“I’m on the side of your hand.”

Donnan looked at the two of them, the thin doctor and the broad grey woman, and shook his head. It was fond, and a little helpless. He said, to the room in general, “Do you see what I’m surrounded by? Truth. Every day. It’s exhausting.” Then he lifted the bound hand and inspected it. “Very well. Ten days.”

“Twelve,” said Heber.

“Twelve.”

The hall roared. Somebody banged a mug on a bench. Heber packed his box and went back down the room with the same serious, faintly absent air, and Riarhin saw Halla lean over and say a word in Donnan’s ear, and saw Donnan laugh.

They tell him he’s wrong, she thought, and still wait to hear him.

It was not a thing she had known about a mark before. She put it away.

***

The offer came after the plates were cleared.

Muirenn had said it would. She had sat through the whole of the meal with her arms folded, saying little, eating less, and watching her father as one watches a weather-glass. Now the benches had emptied to the fire and the low talk of men with full bellies, and Donnan turned in his chair and looked at his daughter, and his face was kind.

“I’ve news,” he said. “I’ve been keeping it.”

“I thought you might.”

“Brannoc’s a good lad. I’ve been thinking about the match.” He leaned back. “I’ve been wrong to stand in your way. It’s a good marriage. It makes sense. I’m going to give you the eight boats outright, and I’ll put another eight beside them, and you’ll captain the lot.”

Muirenn did not move.

“Sixteen,” said Donnan. “A proper fleet. You’ll be the first woman in this country to command it. It’s only that it will be under the compact—the mainland business. The queen’s captains would like a single head at the water, and I’ve said that I’ll have my daughter in it. You’ll answer to me on the shore and to nobody at sea.” He smiled. “It’s more than I ever had at your age.”

“It’s a very handsome offer,” said Muirenn.

“It’s the best I’ve made.”

“I know.” She unfolded her arms. She put both hands flat on the table, and looked at them, and then at him. Her voice was very steady. “I’m not going to take it.”

“Muirenn.”

“It’s a very good offer. It’s a kinder one than the last, and the last was kinder than the one before. I’ve thought about each. I’d be a fool not to.” A pause. “I’d be your daughter with sixteen boats. I’d rather be a woman with eight.”

“You’d be the same woman.”

“I’d not.”

The hall had grown quiet around them. Nobody looked.

“I don’t say you’re wrong,” said Muirenn. “I say I’d have to spend the rest of my life proving to you that I’m not what you’ve made me. And I’m tired.” Her mouth twisted. “I’m only thirty-six. It’s a shame to be so tired.”

Donnan looked at her for a long time. The white-jawed face, the bright eyes. There was no anger in it. Riarhin, who had looked for anger, found nothing to take hold of, and understood that that was the whole of the difficulty.

“You’ll change your mind,” he said gently.

“I’ve said that to myself eleven times.”

“Then you know it.”

“Yes,” said Muirenn. “That’s why I’m saying no now.”

He nodded. He put out the bandaged hand, and covered hers, and held it a moment, and she let him.

“Well,” he said. “There’s time.”

***

Berent found her on the quay afterwards, in the dark, with the wind in her face.

The tide had turned. Out beyond the harbour mouth the sea was black and moving, and the eight great hulls lay on the shingle in a line, shadows on shadow. She had come out to breathe and had been standing at the end of the stone jetty for some time without thinking.

“I like him,” said Berent.

“Donnan?”

“No.” He came up beside her, slow, with the stick. “The old fellow with the one thumb. Cormac. He was at Fairport and at Hollin, on the other side.” He settled his weight with care. “We talked about the road to Sennet. He said the grey cob in the forward wagon was the only sound horse in the whole column and he’d given it a name, and I said I’d done the same with the mule. He wanted to know what I’d called it.”

“What had you called it?”

“Alderman.” He almost smiled. “He nearly choked. He said his was Bishop. We’d have gone on till dawn.”

She did not say anything.

“I know,” said Berent. He looked out at the dark. “I know what it is we’re here for. I just want it said that I like him. He’s good company, and he had a good war, and he’ll be very sorry when it’s done.” He rubbed the leg. “I shan’t forget I said it.”

“You’ll keep to it.”

“Aye. I’ll keep to it.” He glanced sideways. “Do you?”

“Do I what?”

“Like him.”

Riarhin looked at the black water. Behind them, through the open doors of the hall, came a great roar of voices and a burst of singing, and above it, quite clear, a big warm laugh.

She thought of a man kneeling on a hall floor with a boy’s brother’s name in his mouth. She thought of a bound hand on a table.

“Yes,” she said. “That’s the difficulty.”

***

Chapter 8 — The City Behind the Chains

The news of Thepyrgos came to Coracle Harbor in three pieces, on three days, and each was a different size.

The first was a notice. It was nailed to the post at the head of the quay on a Tuesday morning, on heavy cream paper with a broad green seal, and by nine o’clock there were two hundred people in front of it, reading with their lips. Riarhin read it from the back. It said in Viterran and Ecktorian that the city of Thepyrgos, called impregnable, had that month been taken by the arms of Her Majesty Veralyn, by the grace of the Light, Queen of Viterra, with small loss and no dishonour. It said that the harbour chains had been raised and had been lowered again. It said that the governance of the city was committed to a member of the Queen’s own household, of proven fidelity, whose name would be published on his arrival.

She noticed the pronoun. He. It was a formal he, the sort a clerk uses when he does not know and does not need to.

The second piece came on Thursday, in the mouth of a carter. He arrived at the loft at dusk with straw in his hair and a bundle under his arm and said that he was not going to carry the next parcel.

“Which parcel?”

“Any.” He put down the bundle. It was Saikou’s letters, sealed and corded, the third packet in a fortnight for the mainland. “There’s a Viterran squadron off the Dolmis. They’ve put men in every port from here to the Wick. Deirdre says she’ll take nothing across that hasn’t been through a clerk.” He looked at Saikou, and at the corded packet, and away. “She says she’s sorry.”

Saikou did not speak. He turned the packet over in his long dry hands and set it on the bench, and squared it to the edge.

“Well,” said Tavin, from the rafters. “Everyone’s suddenly interested in ships.”

The third piece was a boy.

***

He was fifteen and thin and stank of fish. He had been a chandler’s apprentice in the lower town of Thepyrgos, and he had come out of it in the well of a fishing boat, under a heap of nets, six days ago, and been passed hand to hand along the coast until a cousin of the harbourmaster’s had brought him to Coracle. Deirdre’s people had sent him to the loft because he asked in a strong Thepyrgian accent for the man who wants to know about the chains.

Saikou had not known he wanted to. He gave the boy a cup of hot tea and a chair, and sat opposite him, and did not write anything down.

“Tell me what you saw.”

The boy told it, and it was not what the notice said.

The chains had been raised on the fourth night of the siege. They lay across the harbour mouth in a single great catenary from tower to tower, each link the size of a man’s waist, and no ship in living memory had passed them. The fleet had come out of the Dolmis at dawn in three waves. The first, he said, was small, and slow, and clearly a bait. Every man on the lower town’s wall had run to look. The second was larger. The third was very large indeed.

“And then the chains went down,” he said.

“All at once?”

“Aye. The tower-winches. There was a—” His hands moved. “A smoke. Up in the north tower, and then the south. A great deal of shouting. And the chains just ran out. They went like a thing let go, and the whole of the second wave came through, and we could hear it, it was like a wall coming down.” He swallowed. “And then the third wave hit the wall of the inner harbour and it was all fire.”

“Fire.”

“The first two had their own. They’d burned the ships to make a road.” He stared at the tea. “I saw it from the roof of the chandler’s. They came in on it. They walked in on the burning boats. And the men on the harbour steps didn’t run, because there was nowhere to.”

Nobody in the loft made a sound.

“And the notice says small loss,” said Amari, after a while.

The boy laughed. It was a strange thin sound. “It says a lot of things.”

“What did the third wave burn?”

“The lower town. Where I lived.” He did not look up. “Master says it was an accident. It was the wind.”

“Do you believe him?”

“I don’t know.” The boy’s voice cracked. “I don’t know what to believe, sir. I only know that the winches went at once. It was too pat. Old Reeve the gatekeeper used to say that in forty years of chain-work he’d never known both towers to give way on the same morning. He said it couldn’t be done. He said the men who ran them hated each other.”

“And Reeve?”

“He was on the north tower.” The boy did not go on.

***

They talked it over long after he had gone to bed in the back of the chandler’s, in a blanket, with Aurine’s hot milk in him.

It was past midnight. The lamp was low. Saikou had put the boy’s account beside the notice and beside the two reports Deirdre’s carters had brought in the last week, and had laid the four sheets side by side on the bench, squared to the edge. He sat and looked at them with his long hands folded.

“It’s impressive,” said Amari.

“It is very much that.”

“I don’t mean the burning.” The broad man leaned on the wall with his arms folded. “I mean the timing. That report from the north shore puts a landing party at the winches before the fleet arrives, if the times are right. A first wave that draws the wall off. A second that comes through a gap it’s been told will be open. A third that follows on the flame. That’s not a battle, it’s a chain, and every link is a different officer.” He shook his head. “I’ve seen good plans. I’ve not seen one where nobody argues.”

“It was very well led,” said Riarhin.

“It was well led by people who did as they were told. It’s not the same.” He frowned. “I don’t say it’s wrong. I say it’s a great many men holding their breath at once. A single delay and the whole thing—” He opened his hands.

“Breaks,” said Saikou.

“I’d have said frays.”

“I would say breaks.” He was looking at the sheets. “It was my word in the ringfort. I am going to use it again, and I’d like you to notice that I’m doing so with some reluctance.” He lifted one of the sheets and set it down. “I said there is a pattern. That success draws everything into one hand, and then the parts begin to do precisely what they are for, and then everything is brittle. I said I could not tell you how long that took.”

“You did.”

“I am looking at a fleet that did not slip an hour. And I ask myself whether I am seeing it because it is there, or because I have brought it with me.” His voice was level. “I want to be careful of that. There are scholars who find their thesis in every stone. I would sooner be the other kind.”

“What are you going to do?”

“Revise.” He turned over the notice and the boy’s account and laid them on top of each other. “I said before that I suspected the object was connected to the shape of the queen’s successes. I now say that this one is consistent with it, and no more.” He looked up. “I need the fourth thing. I need the campaign that fails. I want to see her when she is not right.”

“She’s not been wrong yet.”

“That is why I have a small feeling in my stomach.”

Riarhin had been leaning on the frame of the window, looking at the black glass. She had not said anything for some time. The harbour outside was a patchwork of lamps and moving water, and on the slip a black coracle lay upside down beside the others.

“Saikou.”

“Yes?”

“She’s alive.”

He turned his head.

“I don’t care if it’s a stone or a coincidence or an officer who didn’t argue.” She kept her voice quite low. “I’ve listened. I’ve heard what you’ve said. But she is alive and she is gathering, and every week the notice on the post has a longer name in it. I can’t spend a month on a question.”

“I’m not asking you to.”

“You are, a little.”

He was silent. The lamp hissed.

“Yes,” he said, at last. “A little.” He folded his hands. “I don’t say you’re wrong. I say I would like a chance to be right before you’re obliged to be sorry.”

“I understand.”

“Do you?”

“Yes.” She turned from the window. “I’m not going to stop you looking. But I’m not going to wait for the answer either, and the Abbess won’t, and neither will a man who’s selling ports.”

“No,” said Saikou. “I think he will not.”

***

The change in the room came the next morning, in the form of a jar of honey.

It arrived at the chandler’s with a Whiterick lay-brother who did not wait, and it sat on the bench in the sun, a pale gold weight in earthenware sealed with wax. On the lid was a slip of paper in a small firm hand. It said: The pantry will keep till the Hall. It will not keep past the Queen.

She read it twice. It had the ease of a remark made over a fence about the weather.

An hour later there was another, from Scarrow, in Muirenn’s broad scrawl. Her father had been visited. A man had come off a Viterran cutter in a good coat and had sat three hours with him behind a shut door, and gone away in a very fair mood. There would be, said Muirenn, a rider from the mainland at the autumn Hall. She did not know his name. She knew the way her father had held his hand when he came out, the bandaged one, before he thought to put it in his pocket.

He was pleased, she wrote. I haven’t seen him pleased like it in years.

Riarhin folded the paper. She sat for a time on the stool by the window with the two notes on her knee.

She had thought of this as a season. It had been the way she thought of most things, in stretches, in seasons, with something like an edge at either end. There was the spring, in which one built the company. The summer, in which one placed it. The autumn, in which one went. She had, she now saw, been drawing it on the inside of her head like a strip of road with the towns marked, and she had drawn the queen, at the far end, standing still.

She was not standing still.

She was taking cities. She was giving governments to people whose names she would not print. She was, at this moment, across the Dolmis and a good deal more than a season off, and Riarhin sat on a stool in a chandler’s loft with a jar of honey, and understood that there was no such thing as time to spend.

Veralyn must die.

It was as quiet as ever. It was quieter. It did not push. It only stood beside her in the sunlight in the plain room with its faint smell of tar, as patient as a wall.

I’ll not be able to do it in a year. Or two. If she is not stopped by something other than me.

And I will still be here counting.

She put the honey on the shelf.

“Amari,” she said.

He looked up from the bench, where he was oiling a strap.

“Tell Muirenn that I’ll take the job,” she said. “If Brannoc is what she says. Tell her I’ll want an hour with him before the Hall, and a day with the man who keeps his books. I don’t say yes to the rest. I say the company’s at her disposal from the day I’m satisfied.” She rose. “And send to Whiterick. Tell the Abbess the pantry has been checked.”

“Have you checked it?”

“I’m going to.”

He grinned. It was slow and warm.

“That’s the Riarhin I know,” he said. “Say you’re certain and go and find out.”

“It’s the only way I’ve ever managed.”

She went down the stair into the noise of the quay. The gathering was still months away. She would need the time to learn what Brannoc could promise and whether he could keep it.

***

Interlude I — A Governorship

Jaennis · Early summer

The reports came up to the queen’s lodging together, in two boxes of the same green leather, and Jaennis Fenwood was the one who carried them in because nobody else had thought to ask what was in them.

The lodging at the fleet’s staging port had been a harbourmaster’s house. It stood on the rise above a burned warehouse quarter with its shutters open to the smoke, and the queen had taken the upper room because it had the widest window and the fewest people who wished to be seen in it. There was a peach on the sill. Jaennis had brought it herself, from the first market that had reopened, and it was the best thing she had eaten in a week. She ate half of it standing in the passage before she went in. It seemed a pity to give the day to paper on an empty stomach.

“Which first?” said Veralyn.

She sat at the table with her back to the window, so that the light came round her, and she was wearing the green. Jaennis noticed the green, and then the glass at her throat, and then, with the ease of long practice, stopped noticing. It was an old habit. She had not lied to anyone since she was nine years old, and she had found early that the trick of it was not to say false things but to leave certain rooms unlit.

“The Admiral’s,” she said. “Because he sent it first, and it will be sulking if it’s read second.”

Veralyn laughed. It was a real laugh, and the tiredness went out of her face for the length of it, and Jaennis felt what she always felt, a warmth so plain it had no business surviving all those years at court. She was very fond of her. That was the difficulty and had always been.

They read.

The Admiral’s report said that the harbour had fallen to arms. It said that the chains had been lowered on the third watch by the operation of the north and south winches, disabled by a landing party under the Admiral’s own captain, and that the fire in the lower town was the work of the garrison’s retreating stores, ignited by a defenders’ error. It gave the names of eleven officers who deserved mention. It gave, with some care, the names of three who did not, all of them from the intelligence service.

The Secretary’s report said that the harbour had fallen by arrangement. It said that the winch crews had been reached in advance, at a cost the Secretary set out to the coin, and that the fire in the lower town had been lit on the Admiral’s order without reference to the plan, to make the road the fleet wanted, and against the Secretary’s express advice. It gave the names of fourteen persons who deserved mention. It gave the Admiral’s captain as the only one who did not.

“They can’t both be right,” said Veralyn.

“They can, quite easily. It would take an afternoon.” Jaennis laid the two side by side. “The winches were bought, and the landing party went in anyway, because the Admiral didn’t trust the buying. Then the fire was lit because someone wanted the road, and the Secretary wasn’t consulted, because the Admiral didn’t trust him either. Nobody is lying. Each has told you the half of it that makes him look wise and the other man look rash.”

“And what did happen?”

“I don’t know. I could find out by Thursday.”

“Do.”

“I shall.” She tapped the Admiral’s page. “But look at how they’re written. Each one says the other’s people did it, and each is more careful about the other’s people than about his own. They’ve stopped reporting to you. They’re reporting to each other, and you’re the room they do it in.”

Veralyn looked for a while at the two boxes. The light lay along her shoulder. “It was well done,” she said. “Whichever way it was done. Twelve thousand men, and it went like a drawn breath.”

“It did.”

“You don’t say it was well done.”

“I agreed that it went like a breath.” Jaennis took the peach stone out of her sleeve and considered it. “ I don’t know that I’d call it luck. I don’t know that I’d call it anything.” She set the stone on the corner of the table. “I would be more comfortable if one of them had been late.”

“That’s a very odd thing to want.”

“I’m an odd woman.”

“You are,” said Veralyn, and reached out and covered her hand.

The touch was dry and warm. Jaennis sat still under it. She thought of a time in the old palace when the queen had been twenty-three and had wept in a linen cupboard for an hour over a dead hound, and Jaennis had sat on a basket outside the door and said nothing. It had been a long time, and it had been nearly the whole of what she had to give, and she had never resented it.

“I want you to have Thepyrgos,” said Veralyn.

For a moment the room was quite silent.

“Yes,” said Jaennis.

“I haven’t said what I mean.”

“You’ve said Thepyrgos. That’s enough of a sentence to be going on with.” She kept her face quite level. “A governorship.”

“Under my seal. With the customs, the harbour, the garrison, the courts, and the University if you want it.” Veralyn drew a breath. “I need someone there who will actually look. Every man I send will read the reports he’s given and be pleased. You’ll go through the drawers.”

“It’s very far away.”

“It’s a fortnight by fast ship.”

“It’s a fortnight by fast ship from the one person in the world who’s been in the room at every one of your quarrels since you were nineteen.” She said it lightly. She did not take her hand away. “I know what a governorship is. It’s an honour with a distance built in. I’m not offended. I would have done the same in your place, and I’d have wanted to be thanked.”

“Jaennis—”

“Thank you,” said Jaennis. “I’ll take it. I’ll do it well.” And then, because it was true, and because there was a very small ache below her ribs which did not care about either sentence: “I shall miss this room.”

Veralyn’s eyes were bright. She did not say anything. It was one of the things one had to forgive her, and it was easier than it used to be.

***

The commission came up an hour later in a clerk’s clean copy, with the ribbon still attached, and Jaennis read it at the harbourmaster’s desk with the peach stone at her elbow.

It was good. It was warm and formal and full of proven fidelity, and it gave her the revenues and the courts and the keys. It gave her the harbour. It gave her, on the third page, the command of the garrison and its stores under the general direction of the fleet.

She read the sentence again. Then she took up a pen.

“Tobin,” she said, to the boy at the door.

He came in.

“Take this back to Master Haldane and tell him I’m not going to sign it as written. He’s put the garrison under the Admiral’s direction. If I hold the city and the Admiral holds the garrison’s pay, I will be governor of a place in which I can’t buy a nail.” She wrote a line in the margin, in her small clear hand. “The paymaster is to be mine. Not the Admiral’s. He’ll be very cross, and it’s not personal. I’d be cross too.”

The boy looked at the line. “Is that all, my lady?”

“No. I want a list of every person from the University who’s left Thepyrgos in the last month. And a ship for the second week, if there is one, not the first. I have to find out who my husband’s friends were in that city, and I’d rather do it before they know I’m coming.”

“Yes, my lady.”

“And tell the cook I want the peaches.” She looked out at the burned rooftops. The wind had turned and the smoke leaned away over the water. “All that he has. I’m not leaving a fruit in this town for the Admiral.”

He went out. She sat a moment, alone, in the harbourmaster’s chair.

She was not angry. She looked for it, as one looks for a dropped coin, and found only the ache, and under it the sensible edge of the woman who had helped make this court and knew exactly what it looked like when one was moved to the side of the board. The commission lay open at the disputed clause. She sanded her correction before the ink could catch her sleeve.

She picked up the peach stone and turned it over. Then she put it in her sleeve again, for no reason, and went to find the paymaster.

Chapter 9 — One Named Death

Rossan was a landing before it was a village, and it had never quite decided to become one.

There was a stone jetty a hundred yards long, built by somebody who had known what the tide did to timber. There were six storehouses in a row above it, slate-roofed, each with its own iron-bound door, and a long hall of turf and black oak at the head of the shingle with smoke going up from every vent. The houses came afterwards, in the lee, as if they had been allowed. Riarhin saw all this from the back of a cart on the road down, with Saikou beside her and Amari walking at the tailboard, and what she noticed first was that the storehouse doors were all shut and all locked and that one of the locks was new.

She noticed, second, the reeds.

They lined the last mile of the road on the landward side, brown and high and rattling, and between the stems, at three in the afternoon, there were small lights. They were the colour of a candle seen through a thumb. They did not move as the cart moved. Every man who walked beside it kept to the sea side of the ruts, without looking, and the horse kept its ears flat and its eyes on the road.

“The lights,” said Saikou, softly.

“Yes.”

“I’m not going to ask.”

“No,” said Riarhin. “I’d think less of you if you did.”

He said nothing more. He looked at the reeds for a long time with his hands folded, and did not write anything down, and she liked him very much for it.

***

Brannoc met them in the hall, standing.

He was a big man of forty or so, going thick in the middle, with a brown beard trimmed close and a plain wool coat that had been mended at the elbow by somebody who cared about it. He stood between a long table and the fire with his hands open at his sides, as a man does who has been told to look at ease and is doing it as a job. Muirenn was already there. She sat on the end of the bench with her boots up, and she raised a hand and lowered it, and that was the whole of the introduction.

“Mistress,” said Brannoc. “Sirs. Sit down, please. There’s ale, and there’s bread, and there’s a fire that smokes.” He tried a smile. It was a good one, wide and slightly anxious. “I’ve been told it’s a poor host who makes you stand while he talks about murder.”

“I’d not put it that way,” said Riarhin.

“No? I’ve been trying to think how to.” He sat, heavily. “I keep coming back to the one word. It’s the only honest one.”

She looked at Muirenn. Muirenn looked at the fire.

“I want to be plain,” said Brannoc, “because I’m not clever, and I’d as soon nobody thought I was. I know what she’s asked. I’ve known since the winter. I told her I’d not have it done in my name and she told me it wasn’t going to be, and then we sat up all night, and in the end I said that if it was going to be done at all I’d sooner know when.” His hands lay flat on the table. “It’s not because of the marriage. I’d have you understand that. I said I’d marry her before I knew.”

“You mean you did.”

“I mean I asked her in the autumn, and it was a good idea then, and it is a good idea now.” He did not look at Muirenn, and there was a small hard colour in his face. “I want her. I want the boats. I want an end to the feud with her house, which is eleven of my people and about as many of hers, and I don’t say her father’s the cause of every one. But he’s been in the middle of most, and he’ll go on being in the middle. He’s not a man who lets a thing rest.”

“And you’d sooner he didn’t.”

“I would sooner,” said Brannoc, with a heavy careful honesty, “that he were in his bed at ninety. But I don’t see it happening.”

“Well, that’s a beginning,” said Saikou.

***

They went through it at the table, over four hours, with the fire slowly smoking down and a girl bringing in ale and taking it away, and Saikou did most of the talking because he was the only one who enjoyed it.

He wanted the numbers. He asked what the landing could actually put behind Muirenn’s crews, and Brannoc told him: forty-odd men of his own who could pull an oar or carry a load; six storehouses, of which four were full and two were being repaired; a road to the High Hall that a cart could use in any weather but the wettest; a name at the gathering that made people put down their cups.

“Two hundred barrels of grain and oil in the last four,” said Brannoc. “The cured fish is a separate purchase. Enough to feed the fleet through a season and the household through the winter. And there’s the smokehouse, and there’s—”

“There’s about a hundred and fifty,” said a voice.

Everyone turned.

She had been standing so quietly at the end of the hall by the door that Riarhin had taken her for part of the furniture: a heavy grey-haired woman of sixty or so in a stained apron, with a great ring of iron keys at her belt and a slate in one hand. She did not come forward. She had the look of somebody who had watched a great many bargains being made and had a lot of sympathy for the goods.

“Fenra,” said Brannoc, with a kind of resigned love. “I was going to say about.”

“You were going to say two hundred and I was going to let you, and then Master Saikou would have written two hundred on a paper, and then somebody would have asked for two hundred in October.” She came down the hall. “It’s a hundred and sixty, if you count the ones I’ve promised twice.”

“It’s—”

“It’s a hundred and fifty,” said Fenra, “and I’ll swear to it before whoever likes.” She set the slate on the table. “I’m the keeper here. I’ve kept it for thirty years, and I kept it for his father. When he says a number, it’s a number he’d like. When I say one, it’s a number.” She turned to Riarhin. “I’d as soon be the one to say it. If it’s put in the paper as two hundred, and I’m not asked, and we come up short, it’ll be my name and not his that people remember.”

“Thank you,” said Riarhin.

“I didn’t do it for you.”

“I know.” It was the second time in a week someone had said it to her. She was, she found, beginning to like it. “What would you put in the paper?”

“A hundred and fifty. And an honest note that the rest may be there and may not, depending what the weather does.”

“Does weather do that to barrels?”

The old woman looked at her a moment. Something went across her face too quickly to read; she looked, for the space of a breath, at the door to the fourth storehouse, and then not.

“It does to some,” said Fenra.

Riarhin saw it. She saw the look, and the door, and the new lock. She saw Brannoc stop with the ale halfway to his mouth, and set it down. And she was tired, and it was late, and she had been shown a plain, honest, oddly touching old woman who had just corrected a large man in front of strangers, and she put it in the place in her head where she put things that were probably nothing.

***

The terms were written on two sheets. Saikou drafted them, in his neat cramped hand, and read them aloud, and Riarhin listened, and thought that a man who wrote a murder contract so well ought perhaps to be alarmed at himself.

The company would be paid for two things. It would be paid for the death of Donnan Varr, before the Bride-Charter was sealed at the High Hall; and it would be paid for what Saikou, after some argument with himself, chose to call the work towards a settlement, meaning the company’s presence, advice and labour at the gathering, so that when the death had been done there was something for it to have made possible. It would not be paid, and it did not undertake, to kill any person but the one. If there were dissent from the Varr household or from Brannoc’s own relatives, that was a matter for the Hall and its hearing.

Caelia would pay a fee out of the Abbey’s treasury and provide the stores for the road. Muirenn would carry the company, and whatever it wished to carry, to the meeting with Davra’s ship at the end. Brannoc would give room in his storehouses, guides on his road, and the use of his name at the Hall.

The first payment was made there at the table: a small heavy leather bag that a Whiterick lay-brother had been carrying in his shirt for three days. It was silver, and Saikou counted it, and it was right.

The rest was secured. This was the part Riarhin had asked for, and the part she had not thought would be so easy. An agreed portion of the landing’s dues for the first season after the wedding would be held in Whiterick’s name, and the Abbey would advance the company’s fee against that sum. If Brannoc defaulted, the Abbey lost its money. Muirenn’s crossing was her own pledge; Brannoc could promise no part of her boats. If the Abbess herself failed, there was a long list of persons who would find her failure very interesting.

“You don’t propose to find us,” said Muirenn, dryly, “if we don’t pay.”

“No.”

“That’s unusual.”

“I’ve found it’s better that people should have something to lose besides their lives.” Riarhin folded her hands. “A man who’s afraid of me pays me once. A man who’s afraid of losing his landing pays me for as long as he has it.”

Nobody said anything. Muirenn’s mouth quirked, slightly, at one corner.

“It’s the company’s rule,” said Saikou. “I confess it was mine. I’d prefer that we finish this without an extended pursuit of anyone.”

“Then say what finishes it,” said Riarhin.

She had not meant to say it like that. She was looking at Muirenn.

***
