Saikou had put it, before that, as a question. It was the one he had been coming round to for the whole four hours, tapping the paper with one long finger, and when at last he got to it he did so mildly.

“I should like to be sure,” he said, “that this doesn’t grow.”

Muirenn raised her eyebrows.

“A man dies. He has a household. He has captains, and a practitioner, and a great many cousins with their own opinions. His daughter marries into a house with relatives of its own. Somebody in that tangle is going to think the death was a wrong, and it will need to be answered.” He turned the paper over. “In my experience a local murder becomes an indefinite war in about a season, if nobody says where it stops. I wish to know where you say it stops.”

“You’re asking me what I want after.”

“I’m asking what will count as done.”

Muirenn was silent. She looked, not at him, but at the long dark table and the crumbs of bread on it, and the girl’s cup, and her own big scarred hands. She had a habit of holding them still.

“He’s dead,” she said at last. “That’s the first. He doesn’t stand at the Hall on the day, and he doesn’t put his hand on the Charter. Then he’s in the ground. Then the Hall sits, and it decides what it decides about his captains and his cousins, and I stand up in front of it and say what I saw. If I’m asked whether I did it, I say I paid for it. That’s all.” She looked up. “I don’t want Halla killed. I don’t want Heber killed. I don’t want his wife’s people harried out of their houses. It stops when he’s dead and it’s been said in front of the people who ought to hear it. If somebody comes to my door after with a blade, that’s mine.”

“And if the Hall doesn’t take it?”

“Then it’s a season’s trouble instead of a year’s, and I’ll have my boats.” She said it flatly. “Not all of it comes clean. I’m not stupid. I’ve thought about what I’m paying for, and it’s one man.”

“One man,” said Saikou.

“I was told,” said Muirenn, “that you’d want it plain.”

Riarhin watched the fire. It had burned down in the hearth to an orange bed, and the smoke that had been troubling the hall had thinned to a thread that went straight up and out through the vent without any fuss at all.

Veralyn.

It was the plain fact of it. She would go to the High Hall, and at the High Hall there would be people, and among the people there would be the men and women of the mainland with ships and letters of credit, and a Viterran rider who had already been to see Donnan. She would be nearer than she had been. She would have a season to look and to listen.

She meant every reason. They would be there afterwards, sitting in the room with her, no help at all.

She looked across the table. Saikou was looking at her. He did not say you don’t have to, and he did not say think about it. He merely raised one dry eyebrow, and she thought he understood exactly what she was doing and would not offer to let her off.

“It doesn’t make it cleaner,” he said, quietly.

“No.”

“I only wished it said.”

“It’s said.” She turned to Muirenn. “I’ll do it. One man. Before the Charter. And then we’ll all stand in a room and see what it made possible.”

“Thank you,” said Muirenn.

“Don’t.”

“Very well.” The broad face did not change, but her eyes had gone bright and very tired. “I won’t.”

***

They stayed at Rossan for the night, in a loft over the fourth storehouse, and the smell of grain came up through the boards.

She lay on her back and did not sleep. Below her, in the dark, the floor was covered with barrels, and one of them, somewhere, was giving off a thin sweet ghost of a smell that she knew and could not for the moment name. It was like the inside of a bread oven the morning after. It was like something that had been left.

She sat up.

She listened. There was the wind, and the sea against the stones, and the settling of the house. There was nothing else. In the reeds, a mile off, the lights would be out on the road, going nowhere, in the dark, for reasons of their own.

It’s nothing, she thought. It’s a barrel.

She lay down again, and turned on her side, and after some time she slept.

Chapter 10 — Supper with the Company

Brannoc lent them a drover’s house a mile up the coast road, and by evening it was full of eight people who had never yet eaten together.

It had a kitchen the size of a barn and a table that had been made for shearing crews. Amari found the pot. Nessa found the salt, and put a pinch of it on the sill for whoever might come, and nobody remarked on that either. Berent, who had walked from the cart to the door on a stick and was now sitting with his leg on a stool and a face the colour of tallow, said that if anyone wanted him to peel anything he was available. He was given an onion. Aurine went out to the yard and came back with a fistful of green things that Cadan looked at with courteous interest and asked her the names of, one by one, and remembered.

That was the first thing Riarhin noticed about the supper. Cadan was good company. He asked after Nessa’s knees, which were bad on wet days. He said he had known a man in his monastery who could balance a bowl of soup on his head through the whole of the evening office and had never once spilled it, and that the man had been an idiot in all other respects. He laughed at the right places. He passed the bread.

She kept in her head, calmly and without letting it show, the three or four small things he had done in the Cormorant in the second week of April. She did not resent them. She simply did not put them down.

“Nothing to be said about a man having a pleasant evening,” Amari remarked, in her ear, sliding a bowl in front of her.

“No.”

“You’re counting the spoons.”

“I’m counting everything.”

“That’s all right,” said the broad man. “Somebody should.”

***

It was Tavin who brought out the expenses.

He did it after the stew, when the bowls were pushed back and the second jug was going round, and he did it the way he did everything, which was with no apparent effort and a look of sincere goodwill. He produced a folded sheet from inside his shirt. The magpie, sitting on the back of his chair, put her head on one side and looked at the sheet as a woman looks at a husband who is about to lie.

“I thought,” said Tavin, “that since we’re now a going concern, it would be well to have the costs of the first fortnight in front of us.” He smoothed it. “Rope, twenty feet, for the loft. A lantern. Oil, which I recommend we buy in the future by the cask. Bribes to one carter, one turnkey, and a lock—”

“A lock?” said Berent.

“A lock has to be persuaded. It’s not cheap.” He went on down the page. “A ferryman at the Ash, who wouldn’t take anything less than eleven marks and a hat. Eleven. And the hat, which I regret. Sundries. Ballast.”

“Ballast,” said Riarhin.

“For the boat.”

“We don’t have a boat.”

“We’ll have one. It’s a prudent expenditure.” He finished. “Sixty-one marks and some pence, all told. I’ve been out of pocket some time. I don’t mean to press.”

There was a silence. Riarhin looked at the sheet. It was in a beautiful, clear hand. It was also, she was fairly sure, a work of literature.

“The ferryman,” said Nessa.

Everyone looked at her.

“I was on the ferry at the Ash,” she said slowly. “When I came down for the interview. It’s a woman, not a man. She was very civil. She took two pence and wouldn’t have a hat.” She stopped. She had gone rather pink. “I was going to say that eleven marks sounded high. I was going to say I could ask her.” She looked at him with a sort of reluctant fascination. “I was going to back you up. I don’t know why.”

“It’s the way he says ballast,” said Aurine.

“It is a very good word,” said Tavin, with dignity.

Amari took the sheet out of his hand. He read it through with the patience of a man who has once run a commissariat, and then he laid it on the table and put one broad finger on the top line.

“Rope,” he said. “Did you buy it?”

“I did.”

“Show me.”

Tavin produced it, from somewhere, in a neat coil. Amari nodded, and moved his finger down.

“A lantern.”

“In the rafters.”

“Oil?”

“Yes.”

“The turnkey?”

There was a pause.

“I meant to,” said Tavin.

“The lock?”

“I thought about it very hard.”

“The ferryman?”

“It was—” Tavin looked at Nessa, and looked away. “I’d be prepared to say that it was a theoretical ferryman.”

Cadan laughed. It was low and warm and entirely real, and it went round the table, and after a moment even Berent was shaking, with one hand pressed flat on his bad leg and his eyes shut. Aurine put her forehead down on her folded arms. The magpie sidled along the chair-back and, unnoticed by anyone, lifted a spoon off the edge of the table and carried it up into the rafters with an expression of extreme virtue.

“It is,” said Amari, when he could, “a very fine document.” He took a pencil from behind his ear and drew a line through half the page. “Now. There’s money you spent, and there’s money you’d like to have spent, and they’re not the same. I’m going to have the first kind in the book by morning. The second you may keep for your memoirs.”

“You’re a hard man.”

“I’m a man who’s had a pay-tent.” He handed back the sheet. “Twenty-three marks. And I’ll give you the hat.”

Riarhin sat and looked at them. It was a long table, with a pot in the middle and smoke going up to the beams, and in the light of a single lamp there were seven people who had stopped talking about the thing she had brought them here to do. She had not planned it. She would not have known how. It was a small warm noise in the room, and somebody had made it by accident, and she felt it go through her like the first hot mouthful of something after a long cold day.

Na’ar would have liked this.

She let the thought stand a moment, and did not push it away, and after a while it went of its own accord.

***

They came to the job after the third jug, as she had known they would.

Nessa began. She had been quiet for some time, turning her cup on the board, and when she spoke she did it with her eyes on it. “I should say what I think I’ve agreed to,” she said. “So that nobody supposes I’ve agreed to more.”

“Please.”

“I’m here for an undertaking. It has an end. There’s Donnan, and the settlement. Then you go over the water to kill Veralyn. I know what that means; I haven’t promised to go with you.” She looked up. “I’ve been looking for a thing worth staying in for a good while. I’d not have said this was it. But it’s finite, and it’s got people in it I can stand, and I’d like to do it well.” She drew a breath. “I don’t belong to you. I want that quite clear. I’m not going to be a person who follows you after because it’s easier than going home.”

“I wouldn’t ask.”

“You wouldn’t have to.” Nessa smiled. “It’s a habit I’ve seen in people who lead. They don’t need to ask.”

Riarhin said nothing. It was, she thought, the kindest thing anyone had said to her in a month, and she was not sure she liked it.

“Agreed,” she said.

“Thank you.”

Cadan set down his cup. “May I say what I want?”

“Please.”

“I want the instructions to be exact.” He said it mildly, looking at the lamp. “Not the plan; I don’t care about the plan. The instructions. When I am told to do a thing I would like to know precisely what is meant, and precisely what is left to me. It is very much simpler for everyone if I don’t have to decide what you would have preferred.” His voice did not change. “I have found that people who say use your judgment are seldom thinking of mine.”

“That’s fair,” said Riarhin.

“Thank you.”

“I’ll be as exact as I can.”

“I should be grateful.” He inclined his head. “I’ll tell you if I think it is not enough.”

There was a small pause, of the kind that follows an honest statement. Riarhin found that she was, again, keeping her hands quiet on the table. Cadan was looking at them. He smiled with great kindness.

“You are not afraid of me,” he said. “That is good. It would be a waste.”

Nobody spoke. It was Aurine who broke it, in a low, even voice, from the end of the bench, where she had been sitting for some time with her long hands wrapped round her cup and her eyes on the fire.

“May I ask something of Saikou?”

He looked round. “Certainly.”

“You hold the After,” said Aurine. “The custody agreement you brought from the ringfort.”

“I do.”

“It seems to me that there is a great deal of planning in the first half of this and very little in the second.” She did not look up. “The fee here gets us to the mainland. Killing the queen is another undertaking. What if you manage it, and the money is spent, and there’s no silver left for the After?” She turned her head. “Are you keeping a separate reserve? For the recovery? Or do you suppose the assassination’s funds and its survivors will suffice?”

The table had gone still.

Riarhin looked at her, and then at Saikou. It was, she thought, a very good question. It was precisely the question that she herself would have asked, if she had been less tired. She felt, with a curious mixture of pride and something else that she did not examine, that she had chosen well.

“I am,” said Saikou. “I’m glad you asked.”

He said it to the table, in his level, dry, unhurried voice, so that no one could fail to hear.

“Four hundred and sixty marks in silver, apart from anything the Abbey or Brannoc or the Charter will make. It is kept in a sealed chest with Deirdre’s carrier at Coracle Harbor, and no one may open it but myself, with Riarhin’s word. I set it aside from the money I brought, before the company drew on it. It does not go into the fund for the killing. It is not to be touched for a wedding or a ship.” He put his long hands flat on the table. “If it is needed, it is enough for two crews and a bribe. If it is not, it goes to whomever we choose. I want everybody here to understand that I do not consider the After a thing that will take care of itself.”

“Thank you,” said Aurine.

“I am obliged to you for asking.”

She bent her head, and took up her cup. Riarhin, who had been watching Saikou, let her eyes rest on the girl’s face a moment and found it perfectly composed. It was a good face. It was not, she thought, at all unfriendly.

She turned her attention back to the room.

***

Later, when the others had gone up to the loft in ones and twos and the fire had sunk, Berent asked about Lyra.

He did it from his stool by the hearth, with his stick across his knees, not looking at her. She was scraping a pan. She knew from the set of his shoulders that it had been on his mind since the ship.

“What does this mean for her?”

“I don’t know.”

“We’re not going to be there.”

“No.” She put down the pan. “We’re not going to be anywhere near her. It will be the summer at the earliest and I’d not put it earlier than autumn. If the Charter takes, and we’re paid, and the rest follows, I could go and see what a prison in Valora is.” She looked at him. “I can’t tell you it’s a plan.”

“I didn’t ask for one.”

“Good.”

“I asked what it means.” His jaw was tight. “I want to know if I’m to be sitting on my backside in a farm kitchen while she’s—” He did not finish.

“No,” said Riarhin. “I’m going to write. Not a rescue. A question. Somebody in Valora who’ll take a personal letter to somebody in the prison, and bring back a line to say whether she’s living and what she’s been given to eat.” She stood a moment. “Saikou’s people can carry it. I’ll not try to do more than that till I’m nearer.”

“A question.”

“It’s all we’ve got the legs for.”

He was silent. Then he nodded, slowly, and the tightness went out of him, and he looked tired.

“It’s a good question,” he said.

“It’s the one we’ve got.”

They sat a while. When she took up the pan again, he put out a hand and took the cloth from her.

“I’ll do that.”

“You’ve one leg.”

“I have one and a half.” He was already at it, with the pan on his knee, awkward and thorough. “It’s Na’ar’s. It was always Na’ar’s. Every night on the road, whoever ate, he did the pot. He never said. I never thought of it.” His voice was rough. “I’d like it if somebody did.”

“Then I’ll walk the round.”

He glanced up.

“The doors,” she said. “The horses. The lamp.” Berent had kept that watch on their road south. She had not looked at the fact of it before. “You’re not fit for the yard yet. I’ll do it till you are.”

“That’s—”

“It’s only the doors.”

“Right.” He bent his head to the pan. “The doors.”

Neither of them said anything more. She took the lamp and went out through the back into the cold, and stood a moment in the yard, and looked at the black bulk of the hill and the faint low thread of the reed-lights along the shore.

She walked the round. She checked the byre and the gate and the small dark barn at the back where a cart stood. She counted the horses, and got to four, and stopped there, and stood with the lamp in her hand and her breath white.

Overhead, in the loft, someone laughed. It was Tavin, and it was quickly hushed, and it was the sound of a man who has found out that a thing is funny and cannot stop.

She went in and barred the door.

Chapter 11 — The Veil Before the Vows

The Bride-Charter was a length of undyed linen eleven feet long, and it lay across the refectory table at Whiterick like a body waiting to be dressed.

Nothing on it was written. It was stitched. Each clause was worked in a plain running stitch by the witness of the party that made it, in that party’s colour: brown for Brannoc’s house, black for Muirenn’s boats, white for the Abbey, a dull red for the High Hall’s own steward, who was a lean cheerful man named Eogan with ink on both cuffs. He carried King Conall’s authority to convene the hearing; each household still had to assent to what went on the cloth. Some clauses were finished. Others were pinned, with slips of paper, waiting. It was, Saikou had told her on the road, the oldest form of contract in the country, and its great advantage was that nobody could alter a stitch without the other stitchers watching.

“And its great disadvantage?”

“It takes a very long time.”

They stood at the back, she and he, with the air of people whose business is to be furniture. Muirenn had introduced her at the door as a mainland veteran, come to look at the fleet, and no one had asked anything else. It was the great convenience of a woman who looked, in a plain brown coat and boots, as if she might have carried a pike.

There were perhaps twenty in the room. She read them as she read every crowd.

Brannoc, in his mended coat, very red and very attentive, at the top of the table between two cousins. One of them, a narrow man of fifty with an air of permanent grievance, had been touching his cuff for ten minutes. Muirenn opposite, with her arms folded, and a black-thread stitcher at her elbow, a girl of about fifteen with a needle behind her ear. Caelia in a high-backed chair at the side, hands folded in her lap, with the pendant at her breast; behind her a middle-aged lay-brother holding a basket of white thread. Eogan, in red, at the foot.

And the two who mattered least to the cloth and most to the room.

The first was a woman of thirty or so in a saffron-yellow robe with a white sash, a round smooth face and a very straight back. She sat with a small carved sun-disc in her lap and she did not look at anything for longer than a count of two. Sister Cera, of the Temple of Light. She had come from the mainland with the Viterran squadron, or in its wake, and every person in the room was aware of it.

The second sat on a low bench against the wall and might have been a farmer’s wife. She was in her fifties, broad, in a grey wool dress with a grey cord bound about her left wrist in a neat knot, and from that wrist a small bronze bell hung on a length of grey thread. It made no sound when she moved. She held her hands still, and her face was pleasant and closed and not at all curious. That was Sile. Riarhin knew the type. She had seen it in a hundred villages, at the edge of a hundred gatherings: the person who is there because someone will need her, and who does not need to be told when.

“Mistress Muirenn,” said Eogan, brightly. “I’m told you have an objection to the fourth clause.”

“I have.”

“I thought you might.”

He read it aloud. It said, in the flat courtly language of the Hall’s stitchers, that upon the marriage of Muirenn Varr and Brannoc of Rossan, the flotilla of the bride shall be of the household of the groom.

It was very neat. It was stitched already, in brown, and the stitcher who had done it was a plump young man who looked at nobody. The narrow cousin was smiling.

“It’s a convenient form of words,” said Muirenn. “It’s in a good many old charters. It means that a wife brings her boats and her name to her husband’s house.”

“It does.”

“I’ve read them. It also means, if a quarrel comes, that my boats are Brannoc’s to lend, sell, or mortgage, and that if he dies they go to his heirs and not to me.” Her voice did not rise. “I’ve come here to be married. I’ve not come here to be added to somebody’s inventory. If the flotilla’s to be of the household, I’d like it of the household of Muirenn Varr, and in alliance with the household of Brannoc. And I’d like it stitched in black.”

“Cousin,” said the narrow man, gently.

“Lorcan.”

“I only say that it’s customary.” He spread his hands. “If every bride were to alter the form to suit her taste, we’d have a different charter for each wedding, and the Hall has enough to do. It’s a small matter. I’m sure Mistress Muirenn understands.”

“I understand,” said Muirenn, “that it’s not small.”

Brannoc had not moved.

He sat with his big hands flat on the linen, and she saw his eyes go to the cloth, and to Muirenn, and to the narrow man beside him. She saw the small hard colour rise in his face. She thought that he was going to say let it be, in his heavy kind way, and that the cloth would be finished, and that Muirenn would sign, and that in the spring somebody would very reasonably borrow a boat.

“Unpick it,” said Brannoc.

The room did not breathe.

“The fourth. Unpick it. Muirenn’s right. It’s a bad clause, and I ought to have said so when it was read to me.” His voice was steady. It had gone down and slow, the way it had at the table when he said the one word. “She’s bringing eight boats and I’m bringing a landing. It’s an alliance. It’s not a purchase.”

“Cousin—” said Lorcan.

“I said what I said.”

The plump stitcher looked at Eogan. Eogan looked at Caelia, and Caelia inclined her head by the smallest possible degree. He picked up a fine pair of scissors from the table and snipped the brown thread, one stitch at a time, and drew it out, and the linen lay bare and pale with a row of small holes where the words had been.

Lorcan sat back. He had a very white face. He said nothing else, and did not look at anyone, and Riarhin marked him with the small deliberate care with which she marked all people who had just been made ashamed in public by someone they had expected to agree.

He’s not doing it to trick her, she thought. He’s doing it because it is true.

It was a curious and not unpleasant discovery. She wondered how long it would last.

***

The next hour went to lists, and she stopped listening. She was aware of Saikou at her elbow, murmuring a running commentary on the clauses in a voice too soft to carry, and she heard the words reciprocal and usufruct go past like ducks. She watched the two women instead.

Cera and Sile did not look at each other. It was a thing she had seen before: two people who know precisely where the other is, and are courteous enough to stay on their own side of it.

Eogan had asked, tactfully, whether the Abbey would be prepared to stand as witness to the sealing. Caelia said that it would, and that it would prefer to hear first who else would stand. Eogan said that the Temple of Light had been good enough to offer its recognition of the marriage, which would carry weight with the mainland houses. Cera said that this was so. She said it with great composure, looking at the middle of the table. Caelia said that it was kind. She said that the Abbey did not recognise the Temple’s authority over the marriages of this country and would not stand in a place where it seemed to.

“It would be,” said Cera, “a witness. Not an authority.”

“Then it will need to say so.”

“I will ask that it be written.”

“Thank you.”

“And the Gray Liturgy,” said Eogan, and turned, with a little bow, to the wall.

Sile did not move. “It’ll be there,” she said. “It always is.”

“In what capacity?”

“Whatever’s wanted. I’m not asked to bless, and I don’t. I’m asked to be there if someone comes.” She said it comfortably, not at all defensively. “The Sister knows me. The Abbess knows me. I’ll not stand in front of the linen. I’ll stand at the back.”

“It would help,” said Caelia, dryly, “if the households that trust you were persuaded to come.”

“They’ll come if there’s something worth coming for.”

“There is.”

“Then there’s no difficulty,” said Sile.

It was the whole of the conversation. Riarhin found that she had been holding her breath, and let it out. It was like watching three people carry a heavy cupboard across a room, each holding a different corner and calling it something else.

Then a woman began to cry in the doorway.

***

She was perhaps twenty-six. She stood just inside the door with the hood of her cloak down and a child on her hip, a boy of two or three with fat cheeks and a grubby thumb, and she was weeping without sound, in the way of somebody who has been doing it for some time and has forgotten to stop. A young lay-sister had a hand on her arm. She was shaking it off.

“I’m sorry,” she said. “I’m sorry. I know this is—” She looked at the long linen and the twenty people. “I was told to come. I was told it was the place. I’m sorry.”

“Come and sit down,” said Caelia.

“I can’t. I’ve been sat down. I’ve been at the Abbey and I’ve been sent to the Temple and I’ve been sent to the Grays and I’ve been sent back to the Abbey.” The child, at the sound, put his thumb in his mouth. “It’s Senan. My man. He went over the side of a boat off the north point in the winter and they brought him in with the net. He’s been at the church a month. Nobody’ll bury him.”

“Why not?” said Eithne.

She had said it from the side of the room. Riarhin had not noticed her until then: a small brisk woman of sixty, in a good grey dress, with a ring of keys at her belt and the manner of someone whose whole life had been making sure that things were where they ought to be. Matron of the wells, Muirenn had said. She had a great many opinions about lists.

“He was on one of the Red Oar’s boats,” said the woman. “Under Halla. In a taking. It was a—they say it was a disputed taking. Somebody said the cargo was one thing and somebody else said another. So the Abbey says they can’t call it a lawful death till the Hall’s heard whose fault it was. And the Temple won’t give me the paper to say he’s dead unless it’s been called one. And the Grays will do the rites, but they’ll not do them till the church has let him go.” She wiped her face on her sleeve. “He’s been in a box. My mother’s got the house and I can’t claim it, because I’m not a widow. I’m not anything.”

Nobody spoke.

Then Cera rose. She did it without hurry, a straight-backed yellow figure in the silent room, and she walked the length of the table and stopped in front of the weeping woman.

“What is your name?”

“Orla.”

“Orla. I am Sister Cera.” Her voice was level and courteous and gave nothing away. “I can write you a recognition of death. It will say that a man named Senan, of this parish, is dead, and it will say how and when, and it will not say whose fault it was. It will say only that it is so. It will let your mother’s house be yours.” She paused. “It is not a judgment. It is a fact set down.”

“I—yes. Thank you.”

“Sit, please.”

She turned her head, and for the first time all afternoon she looked directly at the woman on the low bench.

“Confessor.”

“I heard,” said Sile.

She rose. She did it heavily, with a small grunt, and came across with the bell silent at her wrist, and she put her hand for a moment on Orla’s arm. It was a plain broad brown hand.

“I’ll come to the church tonight,” she said. “The tide turns at ten. I’ll say what’s to be said at the shore. It doesn’t need the church to let him go. It only needs somebody to walk him to the water.”

“Will it—will that be enough?”

“I’ll be there at ten.” Sile held her arm, and the woman’s face broke, and went on breaking, more quietly than before.

It had taken perhaps a minute. Riarhin sat and looked at it. The Temple’s sister and the Confessor of the Gray Liturgy did not look at each other, and did not speak to each other, and did not wait for the other to say that she was right about God. They had each simply done the piece that was theirs.

It isn’t agreement, she thought. It’s something better than nothing, and a great deal harder.

She felt Saikou beside her look at her sidelong. She did not turn her head.

***

Eithne spoke at the end, briskly, when the room had settled.

She had a list in her hand: the compensation list, the schedule of payments to be made by each side to the families of those who had died in the feud. It ran to thirty-one names. She had been reading it all afternoon with her lips pursed.

“Somebody’s left off Senan,” she said.

“He’s a disputed death,” said Eogan.

“He’s a dead man with a wife and a boy.”

“He’s reserved.” The steward’s pleasant face was thoughtful. “I take your point, Matron. It’s the difficulty in three or four of these. The old claim—the Tadhg claim, the one from the taking at Dunmarrow—it’s stopped every settlement in twenty years. Nobody can agree whether the man was killed in fair fight or out of it, and so nobody can agree on the price, and so nobody’s let the matter go.” He took up a pen. “I suggest we don’t try. We put it in the Charter that every death from a disputed taking, from any date until the Hall sits, is reserved for a hearing. Not paid, not forgiven, not forgotten. Held. And the feud does not open again because of it.”

“Any date,” said Muirenn.

“Any date before the Hall.” Eogan smiled. “It gives us all room. I find it’s a mercy to a truce, to have something in the drawer.”

“What’s the date, in words?” said Saikou, suddenly, at her ear.

“What?”

“The clause. What’s the phrase?”

She looked at Eogan’s pen. It moved along the slip. He was writing, in his neat court hand, all disputed deaths claimed between these households, past or to come, before the sealing.

To come.

She looked up. No one had looked at her. Brannoc was watching the scissors. Muirenn was staring at the linen. Caelia’s hands lay quiet in her lap, and her eyes were on the window, and if she had read the two small words she gave no sign of it.

It was one of the ways a truce worked. She knew it. She had seen it in a hundred old, tired treaties: you did not make the bad thing impossible, you made it survivable. You left a room in the house where it could be kept. Donnan’s death was not to be named as an exception to it. It would not have to be. The words stood there, plain and general and innocent, in the dull red of the Hall, and they held it as a jug holds water.

“It’ll do,” said Muirenn, gruffly.

“Then we’ll stitch it,” said Eogan.

***

Outside, at dusk, the wind had gone into the north and the sky over the sea was the colour of a mussel shell. She stood on the abbey road with her hands in her sleeves and watched Orla go down towards the church with the child asleep on her shoulder. At a bend in the road a small figure in grey was waiting, with a lantern.

They walked away together without speaking.

“Well,” said Saikou, at her elbow.

“It can hold,” said Riarhin.

“Yes.” He was looking at the road. “I did not expect that. I expected them to hold it together in the way that people hold a broken plate. But it is really made. It will stand, I think, for a season.”

“And then?”

“And then somebody will pick it up and see how it was done.” He shook his head. “I do not think that is a fault. I think that is what a truce is.”

She nodded.

It was quiet on the road. Far off, on the hill, a thin cold light stood among the stones and did not move, and she watched it for a long moment, and did not count it.

Interlude II — What Remains Hers

Aryka

The argument had been going on for an hour by the time Aryka Everblood came in from the yard, and it was in the shape it had been in at the beginning.

They stood on either side of a table on which a map of the river had been pinned flat with four knives and a mug. On one side was Sir Halvard Teague, who had been a captain of horse under her brother and a man of standing under her since the spring: broad, greying, with a beautiful voice and a great deal of experience of being right. On the other was a woman she had known for about five weeks, in a leather apron with tar on the cuffs, who had come up the river with the grain barges and had not yet been told to sit.

“It’s eleven miles by the road,” Teague was saying. “Eleven, to the ford. A force moving at pace can do it by dark on the second day, and they’ll not be expecting it. We put the stores on carts and the men on the road, and we’re under the hills by the third night. It is the only sensible way.”

“It’s the only quick one.”

“I said sensible.”

“The road goes through Marrow’s Cut,” said the woman. Her name was Bessa Marrow, no relation to the Cut; she had said so at the first meeting and been rather irritated when nobody laughed. “That’s a gully. It’s got a stream running down it in the wet months, and the wet months began Tuesday. You’ll have carts axle-deep by noon on the first day. You’ll have your baggage in a ditch, and the Viterrans a day behind you and gaining.” She did not raise her voice. She sounded as a woman does who has said a thing before, to men, and been patted. “The river’s slower and it’s safe. It’s three days by water. I can have the stores away by tomorrow’s dusk.”

“Barges,” said Teague. “Lumber. In a river three feet deep.”

“Four at the moment, and it’s rising.”

Aryka set down her gloves. She said, “Mistress Marrow. What’s the draught of the big grain-boats?”

“Two feet loaded, my lady. Two and a half if they’re tall.”

“And the weir at Cann?”

“There’s a lock beside it, that the millers use. It takes one boat at a time, and it’s slow. If it’s open, you can be through by midnight. If it’s shut, you can drag over the sill, but you lose a day.”

“Who has the key?”

“The miller. Dovie’s husband. He drinks with my uncle.” She said it flatly, and did not smile.

“And the ferry at the Three Stones?”

“The ferryman’s a Viterran sympathiser, or says he is. But he doesn’t run at night and he doesn’t like the cold, and there’s a channel round the back of the island that he’s never known was there.” She stopped. “I’d take the outside.”

Aryka took up a knife from the corner of the map. She turned it in her fingers. She asked eleven more questions, small and sharp and unrelated, about mooring rights, the cut of a bank, the number of men it took to warp a barge against the current, the price of pitch. She listened to the answers. She was not, particularly, listening to what they were: she was listening to the places where the woman paused to count, and where she didn’t, and where she said my lady and where she said I don’t know. It was an old habit of hers, from a life of hearing people report to her about what they had seen. She had found that what a person did not need to think about was nearly always what they knew.

Bessa Marrow did not need to think about the river.

“Sir Halvard,” said Aryka.

“My lady.”

“Take the cavalry by the road.”

He drew himself up. She saw the pleasure go across his face, and the caution follow it.

“Not the stores. The horse, and the light men. You’ll be under the hills by the third night, as you say, and you’ll draw the pursuit off.” She turned the knife over. “I want it thought that the whole army went that way. Mistress Marrow will take the stores and the foot down the river, in every barge that floats, and I will go with her.”

There was a small silence.

“Ma’am,” said Teague, carefully. “I’d thought I would have the—” He glanced at the map. “The conduct of the withdrawal.”

“I know you did.”

“It is customary that the—”

“It’s customary that the man who’s served longest be trusted with the thing that matters most. You’ve served longest.” She let it lie. “I’m trusting you with the decoy.”

He said nothing. He was a proud man and a good one, and she saw him take the word, and hold it, and decide, with an effort that did him credit, to be an officer about it. He bowed.

“As you order.”

“I do.”

He went out. The door closed quite gently behind him. Aryka did not look after him. She said, to the map: “He’ll be sulky by nightfall.”

“Will he?”

“Three days. Then he’ll see it was the right thing.” She put down the knife. “Or he’ll not, and he’ll be right too, in his way. I’m taking from him a thing he wanted and giving it to a woman he doesn’t know. It’s not a kindness.”

Bessa Marrow was looking at her. It was a wary, considering look, not at all afraid.

“Why me, my lady?”

“Because you know the river,” said Aryka. “And you’re the only person in this room who said I don’t know.”

***

She did the rest of the evening on foot.

It was a long day and she was tired. She went down through the yard where the grain was stacked, sixty tons of it under tarpaulins, bought with silver that Silkstrand had sent up the river in the winter and that she had spent without hurry on the things that would last: sacks, a boat’s worth of rope, carts, six months’ wages for a hundred and forty men who had followed her out of the south. There was a good deal there that she had not paid for. There was a promise to a widow in Cann that her son would be fed. There was a barge-master’s daughter, sixteen, who had come with her father’s word and a note, and was to be taught to keep the tallies. The girl was to start in the morning.

She stopped at the third stack. A man was sitting on it, a corporal, with his boots off, eating an onion.

“Corporal.”

He scrambled up. “My lady!”

“What’s in the sacks?”

“Barley, ma’am.”

“Whose?”

“It’s—” He blinked. “It’s the Cann barley, ma’am. The miller sold it.”

“At what?”

He told her. She frowned. It was too low by a third, and she asked him who had made the price, and it was somebody’s cousin, and she said in a voice that was quite pleasant that she would like to see the cousin before dark and would be charged a fair price for the barley and would pay it, and if the miller had sold it low out of fear then the miller was to be told he need not have been afraid. She said it plainly. She saw the corporal’s face change, and she thought he would tell the miller, and that it would be all over the yard by supper.

She gave him a silver bit for the onion. It was an odd thing to do, and he looked at it as if she had handed him a live bird.

“My lady, it’s only—”

“I know what it is.”

She went on. It occurred to her, as she crossed the yard in the last of the light, that she was being generous and possessive and abrupt in the same quarter of an hour, and that she was not at all troubled by it. She had been called a great many things. It had not, in the end, been very interesting.

The river was very wide at that hour. It lay grey under a grey sky, with the barges drawn up along the bank in a row, and on the far side the town of Cann was lighting its lamps. In a week, or two, or three, there would be a green banner on the tower, and a woman in green under it whom she knew slightly, and had once liked. The woman had a good army and a very great deal of luck. She would take the town. It was not going to be stopped.

Aryka stood at the water’s edge and let that be true. It hurt, and she let it. She had a hundred and forty men and sixty tons of grain and a woman who knew the Cut. She would lose a town. She would keep the rest.

“I’ll be back,” she said, quietly, to the water.

She went up the bank in the dusk to see whether the miller’s cousin had come.

Chapter 12 — Salt and Witnesses

By the fifth week at Rossan, Berent could reach the jetty with his stick. He had brought Na’ar’s sword out of the bag and spent an afternoon with it across his knees, looking at the worn cord of the grip. Riarhin had watched from the window, then gone back to the accounts. That evening he did the pot while she made her round of the doors.

The next morning he tried the stair to the loft, and then the ladder to the hay, and then, a little after noon, he took the cob out of the byre without telling anyone. He was a good rider. He got as far as the second gate. On the far side of it the ground fell away, and the horse, which had been stood for a month, put its foot in a rut and threw up its head, and Berent, who was thinking about something else, came off on the wrong side.

It was Amari who carried him in. He did it as if the man were a sack of onions and he a decent, reproachful farmer, and put him down on the kitchen bench, and looked at the thigh.

“It’s open,” he said. “Not badly. You’ve torn some of what Aurine sewed.”

“I’m fine.”

“You’re grey.” The broad man straightened. “It’s an afternoon. It’s a week if you’re lucky.”

“It’s an afternoon—”

“Berent,” said Riarhin, from the door.

He turned. His face was wet and set and furious, and under the fury there was a thing she had not seen since the stair at Eilis’s house, and she stayed in the doorway.

“You’re allowed to be angry,” she said. “I shouldn’t say much else.”

“I was doing well.”

“You were. It’s a thing that happens when you’re doing well.” She crossed to him and put her hand for a moment on his shoulder, which was hot, and rigid, and shaking very slightly. “Sit. Let her sew it.”

He sat. Aurine came in with her bag and her quiet face, and knelt, and went to work, and the kitchen was silent but for the sound of thread.

Nessa cleared the kitchen table while Aurine worked. She brought her letters to the shelf she had nailed above the pot-rail, beside a brass lamp of the Aveh kind. Her boots stood by the fire, newly resoled. Riarhin had stepped round them all week without wondering who had paid for the work.

“You’re staying,” said Riarhin.

“I’ve asked for a year.” Nessa did not look up from the letter she was sealing. “My cantor will want to know where to write.”

“And after?”

“Let him answer this one first.”

Nessa addressed the letter, then held it up.

“Is that legible?”

Riarhin read the place back to her.

“Good.” Nessa folded the sheet with the writing inside. “No name on the outside. He’ll know the hand.”

She set it on the new shelf, beneath the lamp.

***

On Saturday Aurine left Amari to keep Berent off the ladder and went to Dunmarrow for plants. She returned with thyme, a scarf wet from the road, and an account of Heber which Riarhin had not asked for.

Donnan’s practitioner had been at the thyme stall: a tall, stooped man of fifty with grey in his hair. Aurine had questioned him while the seller weighed their purchases. She sat down at the kitchen table and unwrapped the plants.

“I thought I’d catch him out,” said Aurine.

“About what?”

“The reeds. On the coast road. I asked him about the lights.” She did not look up. “He said he didn’t know what they were. He said he’d spent nineteen years trying to find out. He’d tried everything he had, and the only thing he’d learned was that they don’t like it when you stand between them and the sea.” She drank. “He said it plainly. He didn’t pretend. He said that a person who says he understands the Dolmis has been here a week.”

“And?”

“And I’d already been here months, and I’d been going to tell him it was a matter of vapours.” She turned the cup. “He was right about the lights. He was right about the thyme, too, and I’d have sworn nobody on this island grew it properly. I was going to say he was a hedge-witch with a good coat.”

“He’ll be a difficulty,” she said.

“I know. I’d rather have the difficulty be good.” Aurine set down the cup. “I was going to wait for Isoka to take care of him, you see. I’d have said so, in April.”

“And now?”

“Now I’ll have to think about it.”

It was said with such flat, unhurried honesty that Riarhin, after a moment, laughed.

***

After Aurine went up to bed, Riarhin cleared the thyme stalks off the table and brought out her account of Donnan’s household: who took his meat, who took his ale, who slept in the hall, who in the small chamber behind. She wrote what she thought and then what she had been told. She crossed out nine lines. She had thought he had a taster and he did not; he had a dog, a big yellow beast of no distinction, who was given the first mouthful of everything and had lived to be twelve. She had thought that Halla slept across his door. Halla did not. She slept, or did not sleep, in a small stone lodge by the gate, and the doorway of the Red Oar’s chamber was guarded by a boy of sixteen and a curtain.

It was an odd thing to see. She had built him, in her head, as a fortress. He was a man in a house, and the house was full of people who were fond of him.

She looked at the sheet for a long time. Then she folded it, and put it in her sleeve, and went and did the doors.

***

On Monday, at the beginning of the sixth week, Fenra sent for her. Riarhin left the household account folded beside Nessa’s letter and went down to the stores.

It was the fourth storehouse. The lock had been changed again; there was a new iron hasp, black and greasy, and a lad sat outside it on an upturned bucket. When Riarhin came he stood and looked at her uncertainly, and Fenra, who was standing in the door with her slate, said “Let her in, Ferg,” and he stepped aside.

Inside it was cold and dim and smelled of grain and something else. There were sixty or seventy barrels along the walls, tallied on their heads in chalk. Six had been dragged out into the middle of the floor. Their lids were off.

“Look,” said Fenra.

Riarhin looked. The fish lay packed in brine. In the first two barrels the top layer was pale and soft and stank a little; a cook might have cut round it. Under the top layer, where the fish had lain against the wood, the flesh was grey and slick and came away at a touch.

“It’s the salt,” said Fenra.

“It’s gone off.”

“Not the fish. The salt.” She poked the brine with a stick. It was a dull yellowish colour. “It was cured last autumn, in a shed at the north end of the landing. They used a cheap salt. A damp one. Half the weight they paid for was water. It made a weak brine, and nobody opened a barrel while the weather was cold.” Her mouth was very thin. “It’s not what it should be. It’s nothing like.”

“How many?”

“Of these? Two hundred barrels came in. I’ve opened a hundred and twelve.” She said it as she said all her numbers, quite gently. “Sixty-three are gone or going. The rest I don’t like the look of.”

“Sixty-three.”

“I told the house it was forty. That’s what I told them. I wanted them to hear it and not run.” She was looking at the barrels. “They said, that’s not so bad. They said it was a matter of the one shed. They said it was a limited loss, Mistress, and the replacement would be arranged.”

Riarhin waited.

“I’d not have you think he’s a liar.” Fenra’s voice was very quiet. “He’s not. He’s a good man who’s had a bad season and a bad steward, and he’s been very afraid of saying it. It’s the sort of thing that begins with limited, and it’s never limited.”

There was a step in the door. Tavin came in, with the magpie riding on his shoulder, and looked at the barrels, and at Fenra, and at the black hasp.

“Ah,” he said.

“You’ve been here before,” said Riarhin.

“I came round on the first day. I’ve a habit of looking in cellars.” He put a finger in the brine and sniffed it, delicately. “I thought there was something. The way the boy at the door talked. He said limited four times in the same breath. When a man wants you not to ask, he stays on one word.” He wiped his finger. “It’s a very old thing.”

“You didn’t mention it.”

“It wasn’t mine.” He turned round. He had a bright, easy, unbothered face, and for a moment it was not at all easy. “I’ll say what I see, if I’m asked. I’ll not go through another man’s books. I’ve been the man whose books were gone through, once or twice, and I’ve no wish to make more of that in the world.” He shrugged. “If you want to know how deep it goes, ask him. He’ll tell you. He’s not built for anything else.”

***

Brannoc told her, that evening, in the hall, with his hands flat on the table. He was very red. He said that there had been a loss, that Fenra had informed him, that the replacement stores would be found at the Rossan market by the first of the month. He said it in a firm, heavy, apologetic voice, and she saw that he believed it.

“How much?” she said.

“It’s a matter of some barrels.”

“How many?”

“A few. A dozen, perhaps.”

“Fenra says sixty.”

“She says what she’s found.” He looked away. “I don’t say she’s wrong. I say I’ve sent to the market. I’ll have it made good. It’s to be replaced, Mistress, not lost. I’d be very grateful if we didn’t make it more than it is before the Hall.”

“Is there more?”

“No,” said Brannoc.

He had called sixty-three spoiled barrels a dozen. She had heard men do the same with casualties. He met her look and waited.

“Then that’s all,” she said.

It was not all. She knew it as she said it. But he had shown her, twice in one month, that he would say the harder thing when he had to, and she wished to give him the chance to have said it of his own will.

I’m taking an enormous number of things on trust, she thought. It’s not a habit I’d have expected.

***

The gathering was fixed for the following Thursday. The steward’s letter came from the Hall that Monday, in a neat court hand, on the good paper, and said among other things that the keeper of the landing at Rossan is required to stand witness to the household’s obligations, in person, before the sealing. Fenra was to be there. It was to be her word, not Brannoc’s.

“Well,” said Fenra, reading it in the yard. “That’ll be a change.”

She was a woman who had never been asked to be believed, and she read it twice, and put it away without expression, and went in to see about the goats.

They were to leave at the end of the week with Brannoc’s provision train. On Tuesday Riarhin went out to inspect the cart and ponies. Two men waited with them in the light rain: a broad-faced man of forty-five in a black wool coat, and a lanky boy of eighteen with a whistle in his teeth.

“Daire,” said the man. “Drover. This is Ket. He’s a teamster and a nuisance. We’ll not be under your feet.”

“I’m sure you won’t.”

“It’s the pay I’m after.” He said it with a sort of pleasant candour, and she saw that he was arguing already, in his head, with somebody who was not there. “I’ve a sister at Killen with four children. I send her a third of what I make. Now, your Master Amari says an allowance for the road’s to be paid at the end, not the start, and I say that’s a fine idea for a man with no relations.”

“It’s the arrangement.”

“It’s an arrangement for a man who can’t be robbed.” He shifted the bundle under his arm. “I’d be grateful for a note, at the start, to be sent to Killen. So she’ll know it’s on its way.”

“Amari.”

The broad man said, from the cart, “I’ll write it.”

“Thank you,” said Daire. He put out his hand, and she took it, and it was rough and warm and hard as a board. His shirt-cuff showed beneath the frayed sleeve of his coat. She saw the darn in passing: a small neat square of grey wool, in a thread that did not match the linen, the stitches slanting a shade the wrong way, like her own. She did not attend to it. She was already looking at the pony behind him, which had a sore on its near foreleg, and she said so.

“I know,” said Daire, with a grin. “She’s a devil.”

***

On Wednesday the carter returned for Nessa’s letter. He brought one from Valora.

It was small, and it was unsigned, and it had come from Deirdre by way of three hands. It said in a plain script that a woman of the name asked after was alive, and not yet moved, and in the same wing, and that she was in good health for the place. It said that she had asked, through the person who carried her food, for a book.

It is the second volume of a Kahfagian grammar. She says it is in a brown cloth binding, with a torn spine, and it is in the hands of a Master Ridow at the University. She says he will know it. She says she was in the middle of a sentence.

Riarhin read it standing in the yard. She read it twice.

She was in the middle of a sentence.

She looked at the sea. It was very grey. There were three boats on it, far out, fishing, and one of them had a light.

She wrote to Master Ridow that evening. She wrote it with her left hand, in her small slanting script, on the back of a sheet of Saikou’s, and gave it to Deirdre’s carter in the morning. It went by the same narrow road as the first. She did not know whether it would arrive. There was no other way, and she had not the right to look for one.

She did not tell Berent until the request was out of her hands. Then she told him the whole of it, including the sentence.

“She would,” he said.

He sat on the kitchen bench with the sword across his knees, his thumb working at a frayed turn of cord on the grip.

***

They packed the linen on the last evening at Rossan.

Muirenn laid it along the kitchen table, moving the bread-board to make room. There were gaps where clauses still waited for witnesses, and loose ends of black thread which the stitcher had left long. Brannoc held the far corner while she folded. He kept getting ahead of her.

“Wait till I bring it over.”

“I am waiting.”

“You’re pulling.”

He let go altogether. The corner slipped, and Riarhin caught it before it reached the floor. For a moment the three of them stood holding different parts of the cloth. Muirenn looked at Brannoc. He looked back, and they laughed, briefly, with the relief of people who had managed to be foolish about something small.

“Again,” said Muirenn.

This time they folded it together. She wrapped the bundle in waxed cloth and passed it to him.

“It goes in your chest. The dry one.”

“I know.”

“I’ll ask you that again in the morning.”

“I know that too.”

Fenra stood in the doorway, waiting for the table. She put her slate down as soon as the linen was clear and began sorting the road warrants. Behind her Ket was trying to fit a blanket into a bag already full of oats. Daire took the blanket from him, rolled it tighter, and made him do it again. The kitchen smelled of the bread they were to carry. Someone had cut the heel off one loaf; Amari was concealing it very badly under his coat.

Muirenn sat on the cleared bench. She had been standing most of the day. When Riarhin handed her a cup she took it with both hands.

“At the Hall,” she said, “they’ll ask all the same questions.”

“Somebody new will ask them.”

“That’ll be a pleasure.”

Riarhin sat beside her. Through the open door she could see Brannoc carrying the bundle upstairs against his chest, careful of the latch. She wanted to remember him doing that when the questions began again.

Fenra tapped her slate.

“You can have another cup,” she said. “Then I want your bedrolls by the door. I’m not losing the daylight while you look for things.”

Riarhin put hers down and went to fetch them.

Interlude III — The University in the Family

Jaennis · Midsummer

The University of Thepyrgos had been founded four hundred years before the Viterrans, and it had not yet been told that it was now part of their empire. Jaennis thought this was a great strength. She thought she would have to break it of the habit gently.

In her fifth week as governor, she had gone to see the University’s Chancellor. She had a desk in the harbourmaster’s old counting-house, a great many drawers, and a habit of arriving at meetings before the men who were meant to be waiting for her. She had learned that the customs officers took a third of what they should. She had learned that the old garrison surgeon had been drinking, and was worth keeping. She had learned where the city’s remaining grain was, and who had hidden it, and that it was a matter of a very small number of people being frightened.

Now she sat in a panelled room above the great court with three Masters of the University and a fourth, a small hot man in a green gown, standing at the window with his back to her.

“It is a question of the tower,” said the Chancellor.

He was very old, and he spoke, she thought, as a man speaks who has done it in the same room for forty years and has stopped noticing the chairs. He had a long face and pale kind eyes, and he had asked her three questions in the first quarter of an hour, all of them good.

“The Astronomers’ Tower, Governor. It was built for observation. For sixty years it has been used, by arrangement, by Master Corvin”—the small man at the window did not turn—“for what he describes as private practice. He has had the key. He has had the exemption from harbour dues on his instruments. And he now proposes that both be made permanent, under your seal.”

“What does he practise?”

“I do not,” said Master Corvin, to the glass, “propose to explain my work to the government.”

“He would like to keep the door shut.”

“I would like,” said Corvin, and now he turned, “to be permitted to do what I was doing before your army arrived. It is very simple. This is an institution of learning. Learning requires that a man not be observed by every clerk in the empire.”

“It also requires,” said the Chancellor, mildly, “that a man be observed by his colleagues.”

“Which they have not done. Which they have been content not to do.”

“Which we have found difficult to enforce.”

It was a very old quarrel, and it was not the one she was there to settle. She could see that. She sat and watched the four faces and thought that the Chancellor wanted the work supervised, not because he thought Corvin was doing harm but because he could not tell, and that Corvin wanted it hidden, not because he was doing harm but because he could not bear to be asked. Somewhere between the two, there was a distinction. She could feel the shape of it as one feels a step in the dark.

“Master Corvin,” she said. “When you say private practice, do you mean workings that alter a thing? Or workings that alter what is asked of it?”

There was a pause.

It was, she thought, rather satisfying. The Chancellor’s eyes came round to her. Corvin stopped with his mouth a little open. The other two Masters, who had said nothing for half an hour, looked at each other.

“I beg your pardon?”

“I have read some of the Chancellor’s papers. And Markus Fenwood’s, which the family kept.” She said it evenly. “There’s a difference, I think. If I make a lamp burn longer than it would, I’ve altered the lamp. If I make a lamp so that the room is never dark, I’ve altered the question the lamp was answering. The first is a thing you can see and measure. The second is—” She stopped. “I’m not a scholar. I’m told the second is the kind that comes back on you.”

“Where were you told that?”

“By Markus. The fourth son.” She heard the dry, flat note come into her own voice, and did not correct it. “He said the trouble with the second kind isn’t that it fails. It’s that it works, and then the thing you’d asked for isn’t what you find.”

“Markus Fenwood,” said the Chancellor gently. “I knew the name.”

“Yes.”

“I taught him. Briefly.” He inclined his head. “A remarkable mind. He wrote to me, near the end. I have not been able to answer it.”

Nobody spoke. Corvin, at the window, had gone very white about the mouth, and it was not, she thought, from anger.

She said, to the Chancellor, “I should like the Tower open to the Masters. I should like a rota, and a book, in which he writes what he does, in whatever cipher he prefers. I don’t mean to read it. I only wish it to exist.” She turned. “Master Corvin. You may keep the key. I shall put my seal to the exemption for a year, and we’ll speak of it again. You’ll show the Chancellor’s people the work. Not because I’m frightened. Because it’s what a school is for.”

It was not, in the end, a great matter. Corvin bowed, stiffly, and went out. The Chancellor sat for a moment with his hands folded.

“Governor,” he said, “would you care to try something?”

***

She had not expected to say yes.

It was a small round room in the west wing, with a stone floor and no windows, and one of the Masters, a woman with iron-grey hair and a plain voice, stood at a table with a bowl of water on it and did not look at her. There was a candle on either side. There was a chalk circle on the floor. She had not, at any point, been given the impression that it was very dangerous.

“It is a supervised exercise,” said the Chancellor. “I will be in the doorway, and Master Hesse will be by you. You will do a very small thing, and you will tell us how it feels, and then we shall all go and have some tea. It is not an examination. It is not an appointment. I must say this clearly, since it is what people usually hear.”

“I’m not to be a prodigy.”

“You are to be a person who has stood in a room.” He smiled. “The University has had a great many prodigies. They mostly go out of the window.”

The exercise was simple. She was to look at the water in the bowl, and ask it to be still, and not ask for anything else. That was the instruction. Master Hesse repeated it twice, in a dry unhurried voice: ask it to be still. Only that.

She looked at the bowl.

The draught under the door rippled the surface. In her sleeve lay the peach stone she had carried for five weeks. She thought of Baldvyn’s hand resting on a table, and tried to keep it there.

The ripple stopped.

It did not stop as water stops when the wind dies. It stopped all at once, as a held breath stops, and the whole of the surface lay flat and black and level as a slab of slate, and in the middle of it the light from the candles stood upright like two small gold nails.

She said, very quietly, “Oh.”

“How does it feel?” said Master Hesse.

“Like—” It was extraordinarily hard to find. She looked for it, and there was only an odd bright pleasure, entire and unlooked for, going through her from the crown of her head to the soles of her feet. It was like being handed a key to a room in her own house that she had lived beside all her life. “I don’t know. Like a thing I could do all along.”

“Yes,” said Hesse. “That is what they say.”

“Can I do it again?”

The Chancellor had come in from the door. He stood at her shoulder, looking at the bowl, and his face was very grave.

“You may,” he said. “But not yet.”

“Why not?”

“Because you have asked a different question the second time.” He said it kindly. “The first time, you asked the water to be still. The second time, you will be asking whether you can. It’s not the same thing. And the water, I regret to say, will know the difference.”

She looked at him.

“I don’t say you’ll fail. I say you will have altered the problem by looking at it.” He touched the edge of the table, gently. “That is what we mean by discipline. It is only a very long habit of remembering that the question changes when you do.”

She was silent. The candles burned. The surface of the water lay quite flat, and then, as she watched, a small ring spread on it from the edge, and another, and it was the ordinary water of a bowl again.

She thought of Markus, who had wanted a thing and been given a great deal else. She thought of Lerris, wherever he was, with the University’s name on him like a scar and a knife in his hand. She thought that she had spent thirty years believing that this was a place other people went, and that she was very good at being a person to whom nothing of the kind would happen.

The room had been there all along. She had not known she was allowed in it. Markus had gone further; she need not follow him that far.

“May I come back on Thursday?” she said.

“You may come back on Thursday,” said the Chancellor, “and we will begin with the alphabet.”

She laughed. It was quite unlike her. It rang in the round stone room, and Hesse, at the table, permitted herself the smallest smile.

On the way out she stopped at the door, and looked at her own hands, and put them in her sleeves.

Part Three — What the Lights Leave

Chapter 13 — Off the Causeway

They quarrelled about the pony before they were out of Rossan.

It was Tavin who began it, and it was a reasonable quarrel. The grey pony with the sore on her near foreleg was the slowest animal in the string, and a slow animal in the middle of a train sets the pace for all of it. It was three days to the High Hall by the Whiterick road and one of them lay across the moss. The Hall’s steward had fixed the gathering for a particular Thursday, in writing, and every hour lost at the start would have to be found somewhere by the end.

“Leave her,” said Tavin. He was sitting the cart’s tail-board with the magpie on his knee, looking at the animal with the affection of a man who has never owned a horse. “Brannoc’s lad can bring her on after. She’ll be better for a week in a field.”

“She’s sound,” said Daire.

“She’s got a hole in her leg.”

“She’s got a sore. It’s not the same.” The drover had one hand on the pony’s neck. He said it mildly, in the voice of a man explaining to somebody young that the sea was wet. “I’ll not leave a sound beast behind for the sake of an hour. She’s carried this cart’s corn to Killen for eleven years. She’ll go where the rest go, and I’ll walk her.”

“At a walk.”

“At the pace she likes.”

“We’ve a date—”

“We have,” said Daire. “And I’ve a name. I’ve taken a third of the money in advance and I’ve promised I’d bring the stores through. If the stores don’t come, that’s my name.” He grinned suddenly, showing a broken tooth. “I’d not care to have it said in Killen that Daire left his mare in a ditch to keep an appointment with a lord.”

There was a small silence. Amari, walking at the horse’s head, said without looking round: “Take the two heavy barrels off the cart and put them on the other ponies. She’ll lighten by eight stone and she’ll go a little faster. Ket, you’ll walk on her off side.”

“It’s a good idea,” said Tavin, wounded.

“It’s an ordinary one.” The broad man clicked his tongue. “That’s the beauty of it.”

Riarhin, riding at the front on the bay that Brannoc had lent her, listened to it and did not intervene. It was not her quarrel, and it was being settled. She was aware, in a way she would have found hard to explain, that she liked it. She liked the small sound of it: the barrel-rope, the grumbling, the whistle of the boy Ket, who had begun some tuneless morning air and been told by Fenra to stop and had, plainly, no intention of doing so. It sounded like a march, and it was not one.

She looked back along the line. There were fourteen of them and seven animals.

***

They left the last farm at noon. Beyond it the road went down between banks of gorse into a country the colour of a wet cloak, and for the rest of the afternoon nothing else in the landscape had an edge.

Fenra rode beside her on a broad-backed cob, with the slate under her arm and a plain grey shawl over her head. She did not talk. She was a woman who had been a long time at a job and now found herself, for the first time in thirty years, in the road, and she rode as one carrying a jar of something fragile that she had not been asked to protect.

“You’ve not been to the Hall.”

“No.”

“It’s a fine place.” She looked straight ahead. “I’m told. I’ve heard the roof’s all whale.”

“I heard that.”

“There was one, in the old days. The only one the Dolmis ever gave up, and there’s people who’ll tell you it was two hundred feet, and people who’ll tell you it was a ship.” Fenra shifted the slate. “Whatever it was, they made the Hall’s beams of its ribs. I said to my mother it was a queer thing to build under. She said you take a roof where the sea gives you one, and don’t ask what else it was giving.” She sniffed. “She’d never seen it either.”

They rode on. A bird went up out of the heather ahead, and it was too big for a bird, and flew low and slow across the road with its legs hanging, and she did not see where it came down.

They were a mile from the ford when the light began to go.

***

It was a shallow ford, no more than knee-deep, with a scatter of flat stones across it and a bank of yellow flags on the far side. There was a woman washing at it.

She was kneeling on a flat rock a little way upstream, with her back to the road and a grey shawl over her head. She was doing what a woman does at a ford: wringing a shirt, and beating it on the stone, and wringing it again. She did not look up. The water was quite clear and quite cold and ran past her hands without a mark.

Riarhin saw her from a hundred yards, and then, as the bay went down into the stream, from thirty. She saw that the shirt was a plain man’s shirt of undyed linen, and that it was very white. It had been washed for a long time. It hung heavy from her hands and streamed water.

She saw that the cuff had been darned. It was a small square patch of grey wool, in a thread that did not match, and the stitches slanted a shade the wrong way, like her own.

She looked at it as she looked at everything: fully, and without any feeling that it required an answer. She thought, someone’s good with a needle. She thought, she’ll have a long walk home. Then the bay put its foot in a soft place and threw up its head, and she looked at the bay.

She heard the stream go on behind her.

“There’s a laundress,” said Cera, quietly, from the line. The Temple’s sister rode a small brown mare with a face very like her own, composed and slightly amazed. “At this hour. It’s odd.”

“It is.”

“Do you suppose she’s from the farm?”

“I don’t know.”

“I’ll ask her if she needs an escort.”

“Sister.” It was Sile, riding behind, with the little bronze bell silent on its grey thread. Her voice was pleasant and quite flat. “I wouldn’t.”

Cera turned in the saddle.

“It’s not a matter of danger.” The broad woman’s face did not change. “It’s only that if she’s from the farm, she’ll have a reason to be there, and if she isn’t, she’ll not be needing you.”

“That is—”

“It’s a good rule. It’s served me for forty years.” Sile’s hand rested on her bell. “Ride on, Sister.”

There was a brief pause. Then Cera, with a small, very correct movement of the head, rode on.

Daire led the grey mare through the ford last, whistling low. He did not look at the water. He had, she noticed, kept his eyes on the road from the moment the bank fell away, and he crossed like a man in a church. On the far side he looked up, and saw her watching, and touched his cap.

“Damp evening,” he said.

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

She did not look back at the ford. She did not, then, see any reason to.

***

The causeway began an hour after dark, and it was Fenra who told them to stop.

She had reined in at the head of it, on the last firm ground, with the cob’s head high and her face set. The road ran away in front of them, a low raised bank of black earth and stone about the width of two carts, going out across the moss into nothing at all. On either side lay the bog. It was flat, and vast, and very faintly luminous, as a plate is faintly luminous under a cloth.

Every hundred paces, on the right hand of the road, stood a pale stone. It was limed, so that it showed white. It was as tall as a man’s waist. She could count four before the dark took them.

“Three miles,” said Fenra. “The stones are on your right. You keep them on your right the whole way. If you can’t see one, you stop.” She turned the cob. “It’s best done in daylight. We’ll be at the hall tomorrow noon if we sleep here and cross at dawn.”

“We’ll lose a night.”

“We’ll lose some of a night.” Fenra said it without heat. “You’ll not lose your beasts.”

“There’s a moon coming,” said Tavin, behind her.

“It’s a wet moon,” said Fenra. “It’s no use to anyone.”

Riarhin sat and looked out over the bog. The light in it was pale and even, a sort of faint sourceless pearl, and she could see clearly the black line of the road and the white stones going out along it, four, five, six. It looked very simple. It looked like a bridge over a river that had been made by people who had crossed it before.

She thought of a strip of road on the inside of her head with the towns marked. She thought of a fixed date, and a notice on a post, and a man selling ports.

“It’s three miles,” she said. “The ponies have been at a walk since noon. We can do it in two hours. We’ll be at the far end before the moon’s up. There are stones.”

“Riarhin,” said Amari.

“I know.” She didn’t turn. “I know it’s not what she’s asked. I’d like the gathering to be a day early, so that whatever goes wrong in it can go wrong with a day to put right. That’s all.” She let out a breath. “I’ll take it. Stones on the right. Nobody out of line. Lanterns at the head and at the tail, and Ket on the last cart.”

“As you like,” said Fenra.

She did not say anything more. She swung down from the cob and took its head, and after a moment, one by one, the rest of them did the same.

***

The first light appeared at about the second mile.

It was to the left of the causeway, out in the moss, a little way off, small and gold, the colour of a lantern seen through a thumb. She saw it at the same moment as she saw the fourth stone go past on her right, and she thought, absurdly, that is a very steady lantern.

She looked to the rear of the train, where Ket walked with the last cart. The boy’s head had come up. He said something, and she heard the tone and not the words, glad, and then puzzled.

“That’s Daire’s,” he said.

“What?”

“The signal.” He was pointing. “Two swings and a hold. That’s a drover’s. That’s what he does when a beast’s gone off and he’s found it. Look—” The light rose in the dark, and dipped, and rose, and dipped, and held. It was an oil lantern. It might have been swung by a man’s hand. “It’s the mare. She’s got loose. He’s gone after her.”

“Daire’s at the front,” said Amari, sharply.

“He’s not.” The boy’s voice cracked. “He was behind me. I saw him drop back to check the strap. That’s his signal.”

“Ket, stay in line—”

“It’s only there.” He had let go of the cart. “I’ll be a minute.”

“Stay—”

He had gone. She heard his boots on the black earth, and then on something that was not black earth but only looked like it, and a splash, and his voice call Daire!, glad, once more.

At the same moment, from the head of the line, a man’s voice shouted “To the left!”

She turned.

It was Fergal, Brannoc’s messenger, a thin, anxious man in a leather cap who had been riding at the tail since Rossan and had said nothing to anyone. He was on the causeway with a lantern in his hand. He had seen the light too. He had seen the front of the train draw away to the right to avoid a hole in the road, and had seen the light go left, and had put the two together, in a moment of fear, and concluded that the party had divided.

“The left!” he shouted. “They’ve gone to the left! Follow the light!”

“No,” said Riarhin. “Stay on the—”

Behind her, in the dark, the second cart, the one with Berent and Aurine and the barrels, lurched. She heard the driver’s voice, a brief unpleasant oath, and the crack of a whip, and the long slow groan of a wheel turning off firm ground onto something softer.

***

It took perhaps two minutes.

She knew afterwards that it could not have taken longer. She was aware of a great deal of noise, and a spreading confusion of lanterns. She was aware of Amari’s voice, huge and level, saying halt, halt, halt. She was aware of a scream from the cart, which was Aurine, and a great slow sucking sound. And she was aware that the light in the moss to the left had gone out.

It did not fade. It simply was not there.

She stood on the causeway with her lantern held high and the bay’s reins wrapped round her wrist. She looked at the black bank. She looked at the white stone on her right. She counted the stones and could see four, and five, and then, when she turned, only three.

“Where’s the first light?”

“There,” said Ket’s voice, from a great way off.

“There,” said Fergal.

“There,” said somebody else, and she could not have said who.

They were pointing in three different directions.

She stood still. She had a perfect memory. It was one of the things that had made her useful, at nineteen, on the scouting line: she could see a road and know where every stone on it lay. She shut her eyes and looked for the light as she had seen it, and found it, small and gold, on the left and forward of the fourth stone. She opened her eyes and it was on the left of the causeway, and she was standing on the causeway, and the fourth stone was on her right. It should have been where she was looking.

There was nothing in the moss but the pale even glow, and, a long way off, the line of the hills.

Someone did this, she thought.

It was the first clear thought she had. It arrived with a sort of relief. If someone had done it, then somebody could be found, and questioned, and stopped. She thought of Donnan’s man Heber, with his scarf and his thyme; she thought of Isoka’s witch in a hedge-witch’s coat; she thought of the Viterran rider. She thought of the hand of a queen across the Dolmis. She held them up in the dark one after another like coins, and turned them over, and there was nothing on any of them.

She did not know. That was the whole of it. There was no name to give it, and no hand, and no reason.

“Amari.”

“Here.” He was beside her. He was breathing hard. “The second cart’s off. It’s in the moss to the axle. Berent’s out of it and holding the ponies. Aurine’s—” He shook his head. “I don’t know. She was on it. I can hear her.”

“Who’s missing?”

“Ket. Daire, I think. Fergal’s here, he’s all right. The Sister’s here.” He counted quickly, under his breath. “Fenra’s not.”

Fenra, thought Riarhin.

The old woman had been on the cob, at the head of the line, three horses ahead of her. She was a woman who kept the stones on her right.

“Nessa,” she called.

“Yes.” She was a small pale shape at the rear, kneeling beside a man who sat with his head in his hands. It was Fergal. He was shaking so hard she could hear his teeth. Nessa had one arm round his shoulders and was singing to him, very softly, on one low note, without words. Something about it, some drone in the throat, was like a hand laid on the back of a neck.

The man’s trembling slowed. It slowed, and slowed, and stopped, and he sat with his eyes shut and his mouth open and looked, for the first time since they had left Rossan, like a man who had come home.

“It won’t help with the rest,” said Nessa, without turning her head. She sounded very tired. “It’s not a charm against it. It only helps him.”

“It’s enough.”

“It’s all I’ve got.”

“Berent!”

“Here.” His voice came from the cart. He was standing in the road with his weight on his good leg and a rein in each hand and the bay’s rope round his waist, and his face in the lantern light was grey and wet and furious. “I’m holding the beasts. I can’t go off the road. I’ll pull the leg out and there’ll be two of you wanting fetching, and one of them’ll be me.”

“Then stay.”

“I am staying.”

“That’s good.” She swallowed. “That’s the right thing.”

“I know it is,” said Berent. He said it through his teeth. “Go and find them.”

***

She stood on the causeway with the lantern in her fist and the wet dark going away on every side.

It was nearly eleven. Far to the east, behind the hills, the moon was starting to come up, wet and white, and it was no use to anyone.
