Somewhere out in the moss a boy’s voice was calling for a man, glad and lost, and a long way beyond it something very like a woman’s voice was answering, in the same tone, from an entirely different place.

She could reach the far end of the causeway in an hour. She could be through, with the stores, and the ponies, and most of the company, and send back for the rest by daylight. She could keep the date. She knew exactly how it would be done, and who would be lost, and how it would sound in a report. She looked at the road ahead, the pale stones, the plain black bank going out across the nothing towards a Hall she had never seen, and something in her leaned towards it as a plant leans towards a window.

“I’ll not,” she said aloud.

“Not what?” said Cadan.

He was at her elbow. She had not heard him come. He stood with his hands folded in his sleeves, a lean shadow with a lantern at his feet, and he was looking at the moss with an expression of grave attention.

“Cross,” she said. “Not tonight. I’m going to stop. I’m going to find everyone who’s off the road, or I’m going to know why I can’t. The date can go.”

“Is that an instruction?”

“Yes.”

“Then I should like it exact.”

“Everyone in the company goes back to the fourth stone. Nobody leaves the causeway without a rope on their waist and a man at the other end of it. Nobody goes alone. Nobody calls back to a voice, or follows a light.” She drew a breath. “The Sister and the Confessor stay with the carts. Amari has the stone. Cadan, you’ll come with me.”

“Where?”

“Wherever the voice is.”

He was silent for a moment. Then he bowed, quite low, in the light of the lantern.

“That is very clear,” he said. “Thank you.”

She turned. Out in the moss, the boy’s voice had begun to call again, and the other voice answered it, and neither of them, she thought, was any nearer.

She took the rope. She tied it round her waist.

She did not count the stones.

Chapter 14 — Bringing Them Back

They went out roped, she and Cadan, with twenty yards of hemp between them and a lantern each, and for the first hour it was like walking on a table someone had laid with a green cloth.

The moss took the boot to the ankle and let it go with a small wet sound. It was not deep, near the road. It was only cold, and level, and pale, and in the pearl of the wet moon she could see her own hands. Every twenty paces she stopped and stood and listened. Behind her the causeway was a black line with a smear of lanterns on it, and Amari’s voice came across the moss at intervals, steady, calling the number of the stone. Fourth. Fourth. Fourth. It was a thing to keep hold of.

From ahead, the voice called back.

“Fenra?”

She did not know why she had said it. It was not Fenra’s voice. It was low, and slow, and a little hoarse, and it was saying here in a country accent. It was Ket’s, or nearly. It was Daire’s. It was a woman’s voice that she could not place and had known all her life.

“Here,” it said, from the left.

She turned and went. The rope came taut. Cadan, twenty yards behind, said quietly, “No.”

She stopped.

“I should prefer,” he said, “that we did not answer it.” His voice was mild. “I have counted eleven times that it has said here, and it has said it from eleven places. It is not moving. It is being somewhere else.”

“It might be one of them.”

“It might.” He came up the rope, hand over hand, without haste, and stood beside her, and in the lantern light his narrow face was interested and quite calm. “But when I call, it answers me, and when I am silent it is silent. It is a very obliging voice. I have known people who were that obliging, and they were seldom lost.”

She looked at him. She thought of a cold room in a monastery, and a bell, and a great many things she had been told about him that she had put in a drawer.

“So what do we do?”

“We stop calling.”

“That’s all?”

“It is a beginning.” He blew out his lantern. “I would also like to put out this. It is very bright. It suggests we are a thing that wishes to be found.”

She hesitated. Then she put out her own.

The dark came down at once, very close, soft as felt. The moss glowed faintly round her boots. She could hear her own breath and, a long way off, Amari’s number, and nothing else.

“Now we listen,” said Cadan.

They stood a quarter of an hour. Cadan waited at the other end of the rope with his hands in his sleeves like a man at prayer. After five minutes she had stopped shifting her feet.

“The voice,” he said, presently, very low. “It comes from every direction but one.”

“Which?”

“There.” He lifted his chin, a little to the right of the way they had been going. “Nothing has called from there. Not in an hour. It is the only place in the moss that has not once said here.”

“Then it’s empty.”

“Or it is a place where someone has been told not to call.”

She saw it. It was a small, hard, sensible thought, and it came to her quite as if it had been given her. Fenra. She thought of an old woman with a slate under her arm, in a grey shawl, who had kept a store for thirty years and had spent all that time learning what happens to people who say the number out loud.

“Go,” she said. “Slowly.”

They went.

***

Fenra had sat down.

She was on a low hummock, six feet across, with the cob standing knee-deep beside her, and she had her shawl over her head and her hands folded on the slate in her lap. She looked, in the wet white light, like a woman waiting for a ferry. Her lips were blue. There was a black ditch of peat-water a yard to her left, and an ash-coloured trickle down her boot where something had gone very wrong with her ankle.

“Fenra.”

“Don’t shout,” said the old woman. She did not open her eyes. “I said I’d not. I was told, when I was a girl. If you’re off the road at night, you sit down. You’re quiet. You keep your feet up and you don’t call. You wait.”

“How long?”

“I don’t know. I’ve been counting.”

“What?”

“The stones.” She opened her eyes. They were pale blue, clear and tired and quite sane. “I could see two from here, at the start. I kept them on my right. Then I could see one. Then I sat down.”

Riarhin knelt and took the old woman’s hands. They were like cold wood. Then she felt the flutter at the wrist. She’s alive. Relief rushed through her, so simple that it shamed her.

“Can you stand?”

“I don’t think so.”

“I’m going to lift you.”

“I’m a great weight.”

“I’m stronger than I look.” She got an arm under the shoulders and another under the knees. “Cadan. The rope.”

“I have it.”

“Was it you?” said Fenra.

“What?”

“The voice. I heard it. It called all the time.” The old face turned against her shoulder. “I thought it was my mother. It sounded like her the way she was at the end. I was very nearly—” She stopped. “I didn’t. I’d been told.”

“It wasn’t me.”

“No,” said Fenra. “It’s never anybody. It’s only something that wants to be answered.”

Riarhin stood, with the old woman in her arms. She weighed nothing at all. It was the strangest feeling, to carry somebody so light, and to know that everything that had been kept in that person for thirty years was in the arms with her.

They went back towards Amari’s voice, keeping the rope taut between them. Behind them, in the dark, the voice said here, once more, in Fenra’s own tone, and then did not say it again.

***

The dawn came up grey and cold, without any feeling of triumph, and showed her what they had done.

It showed the causeway first. It ran very plain and straight in the morning light, a low black bank across a brown moss, with a white stone every hundred paces on the right hand. It was quite dry. It was nothing at all. She looked along it, from the fourth stone to the far dark line of the hills, and it was as ordinary as a cart-track, and she could not for the life of her have said what had frightened her.

It showed the second cart. It stood axle-deep in the moss twenty yards to the left with its off wheel gone, and the two ponies that had drawn it standing in the traces, exhausted and steaming. It showed a great many footprints, going in all directions, and none of them explained anything.

It showed the company.

She stood on the bank and counted, and it was habit. Berent, upright at the head of the road, leaning on a rein, with Na’ar’s sword across his back and his bad leg held clear of the ground. He had got up when they brought Fenra onto the road. Amari, with a face like a shovel and both sleeves black to the shoulder, who had got its passengers clear. Saikou, cross-legged on a barrel, with three sheets of paper on his knee and a pencil behind his ear. Tavin, in the road, kneeling beside the grey mare, who had come out of the moss at first light by herself, walking very carefully, with her ears back and her saddle empty. Cera and Sile, side by side on a rug in the lee of the cart, not speaking. Fergal, asleep, with a blanket, in the crook of Nessa’s arm.

She saw Aurine last, on the far side of the fire.

The witch was kneeling. She had her sleeves rolled to the elbow and a leather bag open at her knee, and she was working on Ket. He lay on a stretcher of two cart-poles and a coat, grey-faced, with his left arm strapped across his chest and a splint on his right leg from the knee down. Somebody had put a blanket over him, and under it there was a pan of hot stones. His eyes were open. He was whistling, quietly, with no tune in it at all.

“It’s a broken collar-bone,” said Aurine, without looking up. “And the shin. It was in a peat-hole. It could have been worse.” She wound a bandage, quick and precise. “He’s cold. He’s very cold. I’ve been feeding him hot water and salt.”

“Did you try—”

“Yes.”

She said it flatly. She had a small, bare, unlovely bone charm in her lap, the sort of thing a witch of Isoka would carry, and she put it in the bag without looking at it.

“I sat with it in my hand for the first hour. I asked for warmth, and for the blood to keep. I asked as I always ask, in the ordinary way.” Her mouth was tight. “It didn’t say no. It didn’t say anything. It was like putting a coin in a well and waiting for it to hit the bottom. I could have gone on. I’d have been on my knees at midnight, asking, and he’d have died.”

“You stopped.”

“I had a kettle.”

It was, thought Riarhin, the best answer she had ever heard to that particular question. She put her hand for a moment on the girl’s shoulder, and Aurine flinched a little, and did not move away.

“Nessa stayed,” said Aurine. “When we found him. She could have gone back to the road. Tavin said he’d go faster without them. Ket said so, too.” She wound the last turn. “She sat down in the peat with his head in her lap and sang him something. I don’t know what it was. It was a good deal better than the hot stones.”

“It only helped him,” said Nessa, from across the fire. She had not raised her head. “I’d like that to be recorded.”

***

Saikou’s three sheets were divided into columns.

She read them over his shoulder, standing behind the barrel, with the old woman wrapped in every blanket in the company at her feet. He had not written down a conclusion. He had written down who had said what.

Fergal (messenger): the light was on the left, forward of the fourth stone, a lantern, at about the height of a man’s head; two swings and a hold. It was seen by everyone. He heard Ket say the signal.

Ket (teamster): the light was on the right of the road, low; it was a slow rising and falling, not a swing. He did not say the signal. He does not know what Fergal means.

Sister Cera: there was no light. She saw only that the party had grown agitated, and felt a strong wish to follow it.

Sile (Confessor): the light was on the left, and it was not a lantern.

Tavin: a light. He was at the rear and could not judge the direction. The ponies would not go toward it. He recommends they not be asked.

R.: left, forward of the fourth stone, gold. It was not there when she looked again.

There was a fourth column, at the side, that was blank. At the top he had written, in his small neat hand, what I should like to say. Below it nothing.

“It’s honest,” said Riarhin.

“It is the only thing I can be.” He did not look up. “I have lain awake for many years, and I have been very fond of my theory. I should like to be able to say that this is where it turns into evidence.” He touched the blank column with his pencil. “It is an interesting silence. It is at this moment the most interesting thing in my papers.”

“It’s not an answer.”

“No. It is somewhat better. It is a list of what I do not know.” He looked up at last. His long face was drawn and pale, and there was something in it that was not quite a smile. “I find I am sorry that it is not tidier. I find I would rather have it this way.”

She said nothing. She put her hand for a moment on the paper, and went to look for the last of them.

***

“The mare,” said Tavin.

He had not got up. He was still squatting by the animal, one hand on her flank, and the magpie sat on his shoulder in a heap, with her head under her wing.

“She came out on her own. Half an hour before it got light.” He stroked the neck. “She wouldn’t come straight. She kept turning her head, as if she were being led, and then—” He shook his own. “I’ve worked with beasts a good deal. It’s a trade. She’d had a bad night, and she knew where the road was, and she came straight to it. It’s the way they are. They find the firm ground.” He swallowed. “She didn’t bring him.”

“Where’s the saddle-cloth?”

“There’s no cloth.”

“He had it on.”

“He had it on her, when we left.” Tavin looked up. His face was pale and quite empty. “I don’t know where it is. There’s a bit of the halter still on, and it’s cut. Not broken. It’s cut, with a knife, near the ring, and I don’t think it was him.”

Nobody spoke. It was very cold. Away over the moss, in the growing light, a single curlew went up and called and went down again, and there was no other sound.

Riarhin stood on the black bank and looked out over the water-bright brown flats where the night had been. It was daylight. She could see for miles. She could see the whole of the country, edge and hummock and pool, and nothing in it moved.

“Amari.”

“Here.”

“How many are we?”

He counted. She watched his lips.

“Thirteen,” he said. “Counting Ket, on his stretcher, and Fenra in her blankets.”

“Out of?”

“Fourteen.” Amari did not look at her. “Daire.”

She looked at the mare. The animal stood with her head low and her sides going in and out, and the sore on her foreleg had begun to bleed again, a little, through the bandage. She did not look up. She had come out of the moss by herself, in the dark, on a night that would have driven any beast to madness, and she stood on the road as if she were waiting for a man who was late.

“We’re going to find him,” said Riarhin.

She said it aloud, in front of them all. The words carried over the moss in the thin clean air. Nothing answered; she had not expected anything to.

Chapter 15 — Daire’s Name

Tavin found him at a little after ten, a quarter of a mile off the causeway, in six inches of black water.

He did not shout. He stood on a hummock with the magpie on his shoulder and looked down, and when Riarhin came up the rope behind Amari he lifted one hand without turning his head, and she knew before she reached him. It was in the set of the hand. It was the small flat gesture of a man who has found what he was looking for and would very much rather he had not.

Daire lay on his face in the pool. It was not deep. It would scarcely have covered a man’s hand. He lay with his arms at his sides and his cap gone and his broad back in its black coat rising just clear of the water like a rock, and the peat-brown had stained his hair and the collar of his shirt. There was a lantern by his right hand, out, with the glass whole. Beside the pool on the moss, folded in four, neatly, with the corners squared, lay a grey saddle-cloth.

“It was there,” said Tavin, in a low voice. “I didn’t touch it.”

Nobody said anything. Amari went down on one knee. He turned the body over, gently, as one turns a sleeper, and the face was quite calm. The eyes were shut. There was no mark on him anywhere. The mouth was a little open and there was a crust of black at the corner, and that was all.

“Not a struggle,” said Amari.

“No.”

“There’s not enough water to drown in, near.” He put two fingers to the neck, although he knew, and took them away. “He’d have had to lie down.”

She did not answer. She had been looking, since they turned him, at the man’s wrist.

It was the left. The cuff of the coat had been pushed back a little, and under it the cuff of the shirt was showing, and it was a very white shirt. It was so white that it looked wrong in that place. It was wet through, as they all were, but it had none of the brown in it that the peat had put into everything else, none on the collar or the wrist or the hem. It was clean as a shirt is clean that has been beaten on a stone in running water for a very long time.

On the cuff was a darn. It was a small square patch of grey wool in a thread that did not match, and the stitches slanted a shade the wrong way, like her own.

She looked at it.

She had seen it before. She knew where. She held the knowledge still in front of her for the space of one breath, a small hard shape in a very bright light, and then it went as a thing goes when you look directly at it, and she was kneeling in the moss with her hand on a dead man’s sleeve.

“Riarhin,” said Amari.

“Yes.”

“Who’s going to tell him?”

“Who?”

“Ket.”

She got up. Her knees were wet. She said, “I will.”

***

They carried him across the causeway on the second cart’s tail-board, wrapped in Amari’s cloak, and in three-quarters of an hour they were at the far end.

It was as Fenra had said. There was a turf-cutters’ steading a little way above the road, a long low house with a thatched roof and a great stack of black bricks against the wall, and a woman at the door who had been watching them come across the moss for an hour. She was a big, red-armed, silent woman of fifty. She looked at the cart, and at the boy on the stretcher, and at the old woman in the blankets, and then she went inside and began to lay out the beds.

It was Cera who arranged it. She did it in her usual level way, standing in the yard with her hands folded, speaking to the woman in a low voice for about a quarter of an hour. She did not ask for kindness. She asked how many the house could hold, and what it would need, and she named a price for the fire and the bread and the space, and the woman named a higher one, and Cera said yes. It had the air of a transaction between two people who both knew what it was, and both preferred it. The Temple’s silver was counted into a red hand.

“It will be a fortnight for the boy,” said Cera, coming back across the yard. “Two for the old woman. She has a hip as well as the ankle. She’ll not be fit to sit a horse before winter.” She looked at Riarhin, and her round face was quite steady. “I have said the company will make up whatever it comes to. It seemed simplest.”

“Yes. Thank you.”

“I don’t require thanks.”

“No,” said Riarhin. “But it’s what I have.”

Cera was silent for a moment. Then she inclined her head, slightly, and something in the composed young face moved and was still.

“It is a plain house,” she said. “I think they will be looked after.”

***

Ket knew as soon as he saw her.

He lay in a box-bed in the back room with his arm strapped across his chest, and the whistle had stopped. Somebody had put a candle on the sill. He had a fat, freckled, bewildered young face, and it was white under the freckles, and he watched her come in and cross the floor and sit down on the stool by the bed.

“He’s dead,” said Ket.

“Yes.”

“Was it the mare?”

“No. She came out. She’s all right.” She put her hand flat on the blanket. “It was in the moss. He wasn’t hurt. I don’t know what it was.”

“I saw his light.” His voice cracked, and steadied. “I did. I saw it. It was his. I’d have known it anywhere, I’ve been with him five years. I only went to—”

“I know.”

“It wasn’t him,” said the boy, in a tone of great surprise. “Was it?”

“I don’t know.”

He turned his face to the wall.

She sat with him a long time. Nessa came in and sat on the other side of the bed with her lamp, and did not sing, and did not speak, and after a time the boy’s breathing changed. Riarhin looked at the candle. She was thinking of nothing at all, quite steadily. It was, she found, a great relief.

***

Before nightfall they had the questions.

They were not put by anyone. They were the questions that arise in a house with a dead man in it, and a dozen tired people, and nothing to do until dark. They came up in the kitchen over the bread, in small flat voices, like stones out of a field.

It was Fergal who said it first. He had not spoken all day. He sat by the fire with his knees drawn up and a blanket round his shoulders, and he said it to the fire.

“It’s her patron.”

Nobody looked round.

“She’s a witch of Isoka’s. Everyone knows. It’s Isoka’s, isn’t it, the water and the dark, and it was water, and the light.” His voice was very thin. “Somebody’s brought it on. I’m not saying it was her. But it’s hers. There’s not a person here would have gone off the road if she’d not been on the cart.”

“Fergal—” said Amari.

“I’m only saying.”

“It’s been like that in this country,” said the turf-cutter’s wife, from the door, “since before there was a road.”

They turned. She had not said a word until then. She stood with her big red arms folded, quite calm, and looked at the frightened man as if he were a child who had said a very silly thing.

“They’re not anybody’s,” she said. “The lights. There’s been one at that ford since my grandmother’s time. There’s been a man lost there every seven years or so, and nobody’s ever made a fuss. You go by the stones. You keep your head. If you don’t, you’ve only yourself to thank.” She shrugged. “I’ll not hear anyone put it on a lass with a bag of herbs.”

“It’s not a matter of thanks,” said Fergal.

“It’s a matter of what it is.”

“What is it?”

“It’s the moss,” said the turf-cutter’s wife, and went out to the hens.

Aurine sat at the end of the bench with a bowl between her hands. She looked at Fergal, then at the door, then the fire, as though she had dropped a coin into a well that morning and was still waiting for it to strike bottom.

Later, in the yard, Cera stopped Saikou.

“I must make a record,” she said. “It is required. The Temple has a register of the dead in its charge, and for a death of this kind there must be a statement of cause. I would be grateful, Master Ira, if you would put your name to the account. You have taken more notes than anyone.”

“I have.”

“Then you can say what it was.”

“No,” said Saikou.

She looked at him.

“I can say what happened,” he said, with great courtesy. “I can say that Daire of Killen was found dead in a pool on the moss at ten o’clock, and that no mark was on him, and that he had been missing since the night before. I will say that, and I will sign it. I will not say why.” He folded his hands. “It would be an assertion. I have no ground for it. I have three accounts of a light and none of them agrees.”

“That is not what is asked. It is only what is believed.”

“It is what I believe that I refuse.” His long face was tired. “I would like very much to be able to give you a reason. I am aware that a statement of no cause is a poor thing to write on a dead man’s paper. It will make his sister’s life worse. I am sorry.” He inclined his head. “But if I write that a thing was done by a witch, or by the moss, or by the queen, then it goes into a register and it stays there, and someone reads it in a hundred years and believes me.”

“I see,” said Cera.

She stood for a while in the dusk. Then she said, in a very quiet voice, “I shall write that he died on the moss on the Whiterick road, and that the cause is not known.”

“Thank you.”

“It is not a reassurance.”

“No.” Saikou looked at her. “It is a fact set down. I believe you said that, to the woman at the Abbey.”

Something went across her face. It might have been the very beginning of a smile.

***

The decision was Riarhin’s, and it was made in the small back kitchen, over a candle, with Amari and Saikou and a piece of paper.

It was simple. She could reach the Hall by Thursday if she left in the morning. She could take the company, and Berent on the cart, and the barrels. She could leave Fenra and Ket and the dead man in the hands of the turf-cutter’s wife and Cera and the Confessor, and she could arrive at the gathering with everything that was left. It was well within what she could do. She could see it laid out, complete, like a plan pinned on a table.

She could see, too, who would do the work she left. A red-armed woman and a girl of the Temple and a Confessor of fifty-odd who would do what was asked. Cera was already dividing her time between the two beds. Sile had the dead man to tend. The woman who had opened her house would have to find someone for the turf-cutting, and send to Killen, and feed the people Riarhin left with her.

She thought of Donnan. She thought that the delay would suit him very well. It would give him several days to say in the Hall that the witnesses were unreliable, that the messenger was late, that Brannoc’s landing was a rotten thing that could not keep its own stores. He would say it kindly. He would offer an amended settlement, and it would be a good one, and it would be very hard to refuse.

“I’m staying,” she said.

Nobody spoke.

“Not the company. Not all of it. Saikou, you’ll go on with the cart and the barrels tomorrow and take the Hall’s steward what we have. Fergal will go ahead of you with a letter. I’ll write it tonight. It says the witnesses are hurt and the date’s in danger and that the company asks a short adjournment.” She looked at the paper. “I’ll stay till Fenra can be moved. Or until it’s clear she can’t. I’ll follow.”

“It costs us several days,” said Amari.

“Yes.”

“Donnan will—”

“I know what he’ll do.” She heard her own voice. It was quite quiet. “It’s not a plan. It’s not a piece of cleverness. I’ve thought about it for an hour and I can’t find a reason that isn’t partly a reason, and partly the thing itself.” She drew a breath. “I need Fenra to stand in the Hall because there’s nobody else who can. And I can’t send her in the cart because she’s a person. It’s both. I’ve given up trying to make it one.”

“That’s a bad reason,” said Saikou, mildly.

“It’s the only one I have.”

He said nothing. He took up his pencil. After a moment he began to write, and she saw that it was the letter, in his clean small hand, and that he had begun it without being asked.

***

They laid Daire on the long table in the front room, with a candle at his head and one at his feet.

It was Sile who arranged it. She did it in the ordinary way, with her sleeves rolled, and washed him herself, and folded his hands, and put the grey cap on his chest. She did not look at the cuff. She did not seem to see it. Then she sat at the head of the table on a low stool with the little bell in her lap and did not say a word, and by nine o’clock the house had grown still.

“Ought there to be someone with him?” Riarhin asked.

“There is.”

“I mean a person.”

“Yes.” Sile looked at her. “Sit down.”

She sat. Amari sat beside her, and Nessa, and Berent, who had been helped in with his stick and set upon a bench by the fire. Cera stood in the doorway to the back room with her hands folded. The turf-cutter’s wife sat by the door. Nobody said anything. It was a house at a wake, and there is nothing to be said at a wake but that you have come.

The first knock came at ten.

Three light taps on the door, evenly spaced, such as a neighbour might give to avoid waking the house. They stopped. Nobody in the room moved.

She had known it would come. She had known since the ford, or since the boundary stones, or since the first night in the herdsman’s shelter, without ever having put a name to it. She felt the whole company go still around her. She felt Amari’s hand go flat on the table.

“Don’t answer,” said Sile.

“It might be—”

“It’s not a neighbour.” She said it very softly. “Nobody in this parish would knock at a house with the candles lit and a man on the table, and they’d not knock three. Don’t answer. Don’t look. It’s the custom. You’re not being rude.”

The second knock came.

Then the third, and the fourth. They were quite even. Between each, for perhaps a breath, there was silence, and a thin high draught came under the door and made the candle at Daire’s head lean and straighten. She sat with her hands under her thighs and counted them. She couldn’t not. She had counted all her life, one to eight, and had been told not to at the ford, and she had done it anyway.

The fifth. The sixth. The seventh.

The eighth.

There was a long pause.

It was, she thought, the longest she had known. She could hear the peat settling in the grate, and the candle-flame breathing, and Nessa’s slow breath at her elbow. She could hear the mare shifting in the byre. She could hear a very small sound in her own throat. She was waiting for the ninth. She was aware, with a cold quite lacking in surprise, that they all were.

“Sile,” she said, very low.

“Yes.”

“What does it take?”

“A name.” The Confessor’s voice was quite calm. She had not moved. The bell in her lap did not ring, and her broad brown hand lay over it as a hand lies over a sleeping animal. “The ninth is a name. It’s always somebody’s. It’s whoever’s in the house that hasn’t been said.”

“Said?”

“Said aloud. Given.” She lifted her eyes to the long white shape on the table. “He’s not been said. Not once since we brought him in. I didn’t want to, until it had come.”

“Why?”

“Because it’s a poor thing to take a man’s name when it’s only been used to call his beasts.” She rose. It was a slow, heavy movement, and the grey thread swung on her wrist. She went to the head of the table and stood. “Listen, now. All of you. Don’t count.”

The ninth knock came.

This time it came from the table: a dull rap beneath the deal boards on which he lay, just where his heart would be. A fist on the inside of a lid.

Sile put her hand on the dead man’s breast. She did it quite simply, like a woman feeling a child for a fever.

“Daire,” she said.

The candles stood up straight.

“Daire of Killen,” said Sile, in a clear plain voice, and it went out into the low room with no more force than a spoken word, and every one of them heard it. “Drover. Son of Ennis and Maire. Brother to Sorcha. He kept faith with a grey mare and walked her home. He took what was fair and no more. He’s been said.”

She lifted the bell. She rang it once.

It was a small bright note, and it rang on and on in the low room for a very long time. She saw the flames tremble. She saw Cera’s lips move in the doorway, silently, and the turf-cutter’s wife put a hand over her mouth. And then it was over, and there was nothing at all, and the draught under the door had stopped.

“That’s all,” said Sile, sitting down. Her face was grey. “It’s not a great deal.”

“Is he—”

“He’s his own.” She put the bell back in her lap. “It’s the most I know how to do.”

Nobody answered. In the dark of the byre the mare had begun to nicker, a small low anxious sound, over and over, as if at a door. Riarhin got up, and went out, and stood in the yard under the great cold stars with her hands hanging.

A wet stone at a ford. A shirt beaten white. I saw it. She let the thought go by.

She thought of a name she had given at the rim of a well, and of how easily it had come, and how little she had thought what it might have been used to call.

She went in and sat down by the dead man and did not move till dawn.

Interlude IV — Victories That Do Not Finish

Veralyn · Early autumn

The report from Marrenta was a good one, and Veralyn read it twice for that reason.

She read it standing, at the long table in the requisitioned tithe-barn where the court had lodged since the summer, with the autumn light coming in sideways through the slats and the sword lying in its plain sheath along the table’s edge beside her elbow. She had a habit of reading good news more slowly than bad. Bad news announced itself. Good news was where people put things.

It said that the hill-town of Marrenta had fallen on the eleventh, with the loss of nine men. It said that the Acasian field army, some ten thousand strong, had been broken at the pass and had not re-formed. It said that the prisoners were being humanely held. It said, in a paragraph on the second page, in the same even hand, that the greater part of the enemy’s infantry had withdrawn to their districts under the several obligations by which they had been raised.

She put her finger on the line.

“Colonel Venn.”

He came forward from the wall. He was a lean, neat man of forty, who had done well in three campaigns and looked as though he had never been surprised by anything, and he stopped at the correct distance and stood with his hands behind him.

“Majesty.”

“You’ve written that the men have withdrawn.”

“Yes, Majesty.”

“I read that we’ve won.”

“We have.” He did not hesitate. “The army’s broken. They can’t take the field again this year. But the men who made it up weren’t soldiers in your sense. They were levied. Each district supplies a certain number to its lord, and each lord to a greater, and they’re bound to go home when the term’s served, or the battle’s lost.” His voice was very level. “They’ve gone. They’ll not come back to the colours. But they’re not ours. They’re in three hundred villages, under three hundred headmen, each of whom must be asked separately whether he holds the peace.”

“I don’t follow. We’ve beaten them.”

“The lords, yes. The lords have yielded.” He paused. “The people who serve the lords haven’t been asked.”

“Then ask them.”

“It’s being done.”

“How long?”

Venn hesitated. The secretary’s hand stopped on his box; by the stove, both clerks looked up.

“A season, Majesty,” said Venn. “Perhaps two. It’s not a matter of arms. It’s a matter of going to each place and sitting down.”

She looked at the paper. His answer was sound. He had set it down with care on the second page, where she might have read past it.

“Thank you,” she said. “You may go.”

He bowed. He went out. Nobody in the barn moved until the door had shut, and then the secretary began, slightly, to stir his papers, and one of the clerks coughed. She watched them. She watched how the room rearranged itself round what she had not said.

They’ll write it differently next time, she thought. Not a lie. A different margin.

It was quiet. It struck her, without any particular feeling, that she would not have to tell them. They had learned it, in a moment, from the way she had stood.

***

Tylan found her a little later at the window.

He came up beside her with two cups of thin hot wine from the commissariat, and gave her one, and leaned on the sill. He was grey at the temples now, and thickened, and he had a good honest face that had never been made for concealment. She had been very fond of him for a very long time.

“You’re displeased,” he said.

“I’m not. It’s an excellent report.”

“You’ve been at it for an hour.”

“It’s a good report that says we’ve won a war and that it will take two seasons to find out.” She sipped. “I don’t understand why a man should be pleased to write that.”

“He wasn’t pleased. He was careful.”

“That’s a great deal worse.” She turned. “Tylan. If I’d been told in the summer that we should take Marrenta in eleven days with the loss of nine, I’d have said that was a war. And here it is. And it isn’t finished.”

“No.”

“It’s never finished. It’s always the next thing. It’s a headman in a village who’s not been asked, or a promise in a granary, or a cousin.” She heard her own voice, level and very clear. “I’m not a woman who’s afraid of a season’s work. I’d simply like to be told, when a thing is done, that it’s done.”

“It is done,” said Tylan gently. “Everything that could be done by an army.”

“And the rest is for whom?”

“Clerks.”

“Then I shall have to be more particular with my clerks.”

He laughed. It was warm and unforced, and she felt something in her loosen. She put her hand on his arm.

“You’re right about the grain,” he said. “At any rate. About the man Coll.”

***

That had been the morning’s other business, and she was rather proud of it.

A grain-factor named Coll had contracted in the autumn to deliver four hundred sacks of barley to the commissariat at a fixed price by the first of the year. In the month since, the price in the market had risen a third, on account of a shortage that was partly real and partly of his own arranging. He had not delivered. He had written, in a very courtly hand, to say that circumstances had altered.

She had sent for him. He had come, plump and plausible, in a fur-trimmed coat, and had spent twenty minutes explaining the difficulty. She had listened. She had asked him what the contract said. He had said it said four hundred sacks. She had asked him if he had the four hundred sacks. He had said that he had them. She had asked where.

He told her. It was a warehouse at Cor. She said that a party of her soldiers would be there by dusk, and that the sacks would be on the road to the barn by midnight at the old price, and that if any were short the factor would make it good from his own pocket, and that there would be a fine. She said it in an even voice, without any anger at all. She had not needed it.

There had also been the matter of the delayed pension. She had found it in the second box. A widow at Tarne, a sergeant’s wife, whose four months’ pay had been sitting in a clerk’s drawer since the autumn. It had cost her seven minutes. The clerk was dismissed, and the woman was paid in full and sent a note, which the secretary had drafted and Veralyn had altered with her own hand.

It had been a very good day’s work. She was aware of it, as one is aware of a well-sharpened blade. She saw the court see it. She saw the officers by the door look at each other, and look away, and thought it was the honest look of men who have been reminded who was in charge, and that they were the better for it.

This was what governing was. Not the wars; the small hard corrective motion, applied every day to a thousand places, until the whole machine ran clean.

“If I could only do it everywhere,” she said.

“You are.”

“Not fast enough.”

***

She drew up the order that evening, at the long table, by lamplight, with the secretary at her elbow.

It was short. It said that the districts of the Marrenta valley, and those adjacent, were to be given a fortnight from the day of publication to declare, by the mouth of each headman, that they held the peace of the Queen and would not raise men against her. It said that a district which did not declare within the term would be regarded as in arms. It said nothing of what that meant. She had not thought that it needed saying.

“Majesty,” said the secretary, hesitating. “Three hundred villages. A fortnight. Some of the valleys are—”

“They’ve had since the autumn.”

“Yes, Majesty.”

“If a headman can’t be found in a fortnight, that is an answer.”

“Yes.”

He wrote. She watched the pen. She had a strange, clear feeling of having come out of a thick room into an open one. A season had been a long time. A fortnight was very much better. It was simply a matter of not letting the thing slip.

Tylan read the order over her shoulder. He was silent for the space of a page.

“It’s reasonable,” he said, at last. “They had the winter. It’s only a term.”

“Yes.”

“I’d have said a month.”

“You’re kind.”

“I am,” said Tylan, cheerfully. “It’s my great fault.” He smoothed a strand of hair behind her ear. “But you’re right. It ought to be done.”

She signed it.

She moved Shadowsteel to make room for the sanded copies. In the yard a sentry was singing; the secretary shut the window to keep the papers still.

Chapter 16 — Borrowed Time

They buried him on the third day, in the little walled ground behind the turf-cutters’ steading, next to a woman who had died in the year of the great snow.

It was the turf-cutter’s wife who chose the place. She did it without discussion, with a spade over her shoulder, and paced out the length and marked it with her heel. Amari and Tavin dug. Sile stood at the head with her bell in her hand and her eyes on the wall, and when the box was down she tied a length of grey thread round the post of the small wooden marker and said his name again, all of it, in the same clear voice. She did not say anything else. The wind blew from the sea and the thread stood out straight.

“Sorcha will want to know,” said Amari, after.

“Yes.”

“I’ve written. It’s the same letter I wrote at the start, the one about the allowance. I put a line at the foot.” He looked at the mound. “I said he died on the road, and that it was quick, and that he wasn’t alone. I don’t know if that’s true.”

“It’s nearer than most.”

“It’s the best I could do.”

“It’s what she’ll keep,” said Riarhin. “Give her the money too. His whole pay, less what he’s already had. Mark the third for Sorcha.”

“It’s not what he earned.”

“It’s what he was promised.” She said it quite quietly. “It’s the only thing I can decide.”

The mare stood by the wall. She had been given a bucket of oats and had not touched it, and Tavin sat on the coping beside her with his elbows on his knees and the magpie on his boot, doing nothing. When the last of the earth went in the animal put her head down and breathed on the mound, once, warmly, and lifted it again.

Riarhin looked away. It was not that it was unbearable. It was that there was a limit to what a person could see and be of use.

***

She spent the afternoon with Fenra.

The old woman lay in the box-bed in the back room with her foot on a folded quilt and her slate on her chest. It was a bad break. Aurine had set it herself the first night and it had held, and she now sat by the bed with her eyes shut, upright, as Riarhin had once seen a heron sleep. She did not look ill. She looked as she always looked, like a woman who had been asked a question and had not yet answered it.

“I need to know what you can say,” said Riarhin.

“About what?”

“The Hall. If it comes to it. You’re to stand up before a steward and say what Brannoc’s landing can pledge.” She took the stool. “I want to hear it from you first, so that I know what I’m carrying.”

“A hundred and fifty barrels of grain and oil. That much stands.”

“And the fish?”

“Not what he promised.” Fenra did not move. “I’ve done a hundred and twelve. Sixty-three of those are fit for pigs. I told you the rest I didn’t like the look of. I’d not like to swear to it either way.”

“If you had to.”

“I’ll not.”

“Fenra—”

“I’ve been thirty years at that landing,” said the old woman. Her voice was low and steady and did not rise. “I’ve never yet set my name to a count I hadn’t made. I’ll say to the steward that I’ve counted a hundred and twelve, and what I found in them, and that eighty-eight I have not counted, and that I think they’re worse than the first lot and I couldn’t say by how much. That’s what I can attest. If he wants a number, he can send somebody to make it.”

“It might lose him the Hall.”

“I know it might.”

“Brannoc’s going to stand there and say two hundred.”

“Then he’ll have to say it and see who’s looking at me.” She opened her eyes. They were very pale, and the fear in them was old and quite composed, like a thing she had been carrying so long it no longer weighed. “I’m not afraid of Brannoc. He’s a good boy. He’s afraid himself. He wants it to be true. It’s not a crime to want it.” She shifted, and her mouth went tight. “But I’ll not tell a lie to save his date, and I’ll not have it said afterwards that I did, because everybody’s frightened of the dark.”

It was, thought Riarhin, the plainest piece of moral courage she had ever been shown. It was delivered from a bed by a woman with a broken hip and it did not want to be admired.

“Thank you,” she said.

“It’s nothing to thank.”

“I know. I’m thanking you anyway.”

The corner of the old mouth twitched. It was almost a smile.

***

Saikou had not gone on with the cart. The wheel that had gone into the moss was cracked through the felloe, and by the time Tavin and the turf-cutter’s wife had bound and mended it, the sensible course was plainly to wait for the Hall’s answer and go together.

Fergal came back on the fifth day, riding a foundered horse, with a leather satchel and a face the colour of putty.

He had gone ahead on the morning after the burial, as she had ordered. He had gone quickly, and had ridden through the night, and had got to the Hall before noon. He had done everything right. She saw that at once from the way he handed her the letters, without meeting her eyes, one after another, in the order in which he had received them.

The first was from Eogan. It was short and it was not friendly.

The Hall regrets to learn of the loss of the party’s member. It notes, however, that the date was fixed in writing, and that the witnesses named for the household of Rossan are required to stand. Master Donnan Varr has asked, in open session, whether the absence of the landing-keeper is to be understood as a withdrawal from the Charter. He has been good enough to say that he does not think so. He has also been good enough to offer, on behalf of his daughter’s house, an amended settlement, in which the disputed provisions shall be supplied by his own stores at the Hall, without prejudice. The offer is a courteous one and has been well received. The steward asks that the party attend on the appointed day, or petition in writing for a further adjournment, which he cannot promise.

She read it, and passed it to Amari.

“It’s very good,” he said, after a moment. “It’s very good indeed. He’s not accusing anybody. He’s offering to feed them.”

“Yes.”

“He knows we’re short. He knows.”

“He may only know we’re late.” She took up the second.

It was Caelia’s, on a plain sheet in a small firm hand, without ornament.

The Abbey is aware of the hindrance. It will petition the Hall for an extension of three days on the ground of common charity, and it will send to the Hall a lay-brother to speak to the condition of the roads. I write to say that I shall keep the door open at my end for as long as I can. I cannot keep it open for ever. There is a supply party on the road to you now. It has bread and oil for the households of the Charter who are in want. I ask that you receive it, and sign for it, and be kind to the boy.

“Which boy?” said Amari.

“I don’t know.”

The third was Muirenn’s. She had written it herself in her rough broad scrawl, and it had a thumbprint of tar on the corner.

I have been in front of the steward. I told him your people were hurt on the road and couldn’t walk. He said what he said, and I said it again. I’ve got you four days. I paid for them by giving him my word I’d be at the morning session in person and not send a deputy. It’ll mean I’m in the room when he speaks, and I’ll have to look at him.

I’m sending silver for the wounded. I’m not going to tell the steward they’re fit to travel because my father’s in the way. If they’re hurt, they’re hurt, and I’ll pay for it, and he can make what he likes of that.

He’s been very kind to me since I came. That’s what worries me.

She read it twice. She folded it. It seemed to her that she could hear the woman’s voice clearly across the leagues of moss: flat, gruff, tired, with the edge of something very near despair held down by main force.

“Four days,” said Amari.

“It’s enough. We can go in three if Fenra can be carried.” She put the letters in her sleeve. “Where’s Brannoc?”

“There’s a fourth.” Fergal had not moved. He held it out. His hand was shaking. “From the master. He said I was to give it to you last.”

I am at the Hall and all is well. Replacement provisions have been secured from a source I have confidence in. I ask that the party come as soon as it can, and that Fenra be spared any anxiety on the matter. I have every hope that all will be arranged before the sealing.

Brannoc.

It was the kindest possible letter. It was, she thought, a man holding his breath in the middle of a large empty room.

“What source?” she said, to the air.

Fergal looked at the floor.

“Fergal.”

“I don’t know, Mistress.”

“You were there.”

“I was.” His voice was very low. “I was told not to say.”

“By Brannoc?”

He did not answer. He stood with his cap in his hands, wretched, a thin frightened man who had lost his nerve on a causeway and had not yet found it, and she saw that whatever he knew was a thing he had been given to carry and had not been given leave to put down.

“All right,” she said. “Sit down. Eat something.”

“Mistress—”

“It’s not for you to say. I’ll ask him myself.”

***

The supply party came at dusk.

It was two carts and a string of donkeys and a half-dozen lay-brothers in undyed wool, led by a stout cheerful sister who said her name was Bridie and that she had been on the road since Tuesday. The carts were full. There were sacks of oatmeal, and barrels of oil, and a great many loaves in linen, and a crate of small round cheeses in salt. It was a very good load. It was the sort of thing that a house gives when it means to be thanked by nobody.

“It’s for the straitened,” said Bridie. “The households that’ll be at the Hall and can’t feed themselves. The Abbess said you’d know the ones.” She fumbled in her habit. “There’s a tally to sign. It’s only the Abbey’s habit.”

Riarhin took it. It was a long strip of paper with a column of numbers down one side, in a clear plain hand: so many measures of oatmeal, so many of oil, so many loaves. She checked them against the carts, and the sums were right. She was about to give it back when she saw the foot.

It was a line in a different ink. It said: charged against the landing at Rossan, on the guarantee of its master: the dues of the second season.

She read it again.

The second season. The first was already pledged towards the company’s fee; she had drawn up that agreement. Brannoc had now given Whiterick first claim on the following season as well. Somebody, at some moment in the last few weeks, had gone to Whiterick and asked for food, and had been given it, and had paid with a thing that was not yet made.

“Sister.”

“Yes?”

“Who signed this?”

“The Abbess had it drawn up.” Bridie did not seem troubled. “It’s for the replacement stores. The master of Rossan came to us himself. He was in a great state.” She lowered her voice, confidentially. “I don’t like to say. He’s a good man. But I understand he was very much afraid of the gathering.”

“When was this?”

“Four days ago. After the word came that you were delayed.”

Riarhin looked at the tally. It was a small neat piece of paper, and it said in the plainest possible words that the man who had shaken her hand at his own table and told her no had, gone to the Abbess in secret and pledged his landing’s next year’s money for barrels of oatmeal.

She thought of Fergal, standing on the flags with his cap in his hands.

Not yet.

She did not know where the words came from. It was quite clear and quite calm. It was only that there was too much on the table already, and that she had a dead man in a walled ground and a hurt woman in a bed and four days, and that she could not, without something breaking, pick up one more thing. She signed the tally. She gave it back.

“Thank you,” said Bridie, brightly. “It’s a great weight.”

“It is.”

She stood in the yard and watched the lay-brothers unload the carts by lantern light. She saw the loaves go in one by one through the door, and the oil, and the salt cheeses. She thought that she had now two prices in front of her. The one was written on a slip of paper in the Abbess’s clear hand and would be paid, in silver, at the sealing. The other was the price of what she had sent away in a hurry on the night of the causeway, in a letter with three names in it, over a road she knew nothing of. She did not know its sum.

She did not know why she had thought of it.

***

The last of the post was in a canvas bag that Bridie had carried under the seat, and she handed it over with a small apologetic smile.

“It’s a great muddle of things. Carriers all along the coast. It’s been three weeks in some of them. I’ve sorted it as well as I could.”

It was mostly Deirdre’s. There was a note from Eilis, four lines, which said only that Mara had been to the well and that the milk saucer had been found empty. There was a short letter from Ailsa in her broad, crowded hand, with a complaint about Tavish carried up the margin. There was a small square packet, sealed with black wax, addressed to Master Saikou Ira in a strong, upright, beautiful script.

She passed it across the table without looking at it.

He was sitting at the end with his papers. He took the packet in his long hands and turned it over. He looked at the seal. Then he broke it, gently, with the point of a knife, and unfolded three closely written sheets, and read the first.

She saw him stop.

He did not move. He did not put down the sheet. He sat with it held very steadily in front of him, and the candle lit the side of his long face, and for perhaps the space of ten breaths there was no sign in it of anything at all.

“Saikou?”

“It is from my mother.” His voice was perfectly level. “I should like to read it through. I should like, if it is not an inconvenience, to be alone.”

“Of course.”

He rose. He gathered his papers with his usual care and squared their edges, and put the letter in his coat. Then he stood for a moment with one hand on the table.

“It is a very good hand,” he said. “I had forgotten.”

He went out. She heard his steps go across the yard, and the door of the byre open and close.

She sat still. Nobody else spoke. It was Amari, on the far bench, who at last let out his breath in a long slow sigh, and put his big scarred hands over his face.

“Oh, Lady,” he said, into them. “Here it comes.”

Chapter 17 — A Detail in the Letter

He came back a little after midnight, with the letter in his hand, and sat down at the kitchen table as though he had been sent for.

She had been waiting. She had not meant to, but she had found herself on the settle by the dead fire with a cold cup, and Amari on the bench across from her with his boots off, and neither of them had gone to bed. The house was silent. Somewhere in the back Berent was snoring, and somewhere nearer the old woman’s breath went in and out like a saw. The candle on the table had burned down to a stub.

Saikou put the three sheets flat and squared them. He did it with great deliberation. Then he sat with his hands on either side of them and said, in a tone so level that it was almost a courtesy:

“My mother writes a very fine letter.”

“Yes,” said Amari. “She always did.”

“I should like to read parts of it aloud. I do not think I can bear to read all of it.” He looked at the top sheet. “It begins as they do. She is well. The house is well. My uncle has been ill in the spring, and is better. She has had the roof seen to. She hopes I am eating.” He paused. “I am not eating. I have been remiss.”

“Saikou.”

“The third paragraph.” He lifted the page. “I have had occasion to learn that you have made a provision apart from the funds you had of me, which I approve. You are right that a thing of this kind should not depend on the fortunes of an assassination. I confess that I had not thought you would think of it so soon. Four hundred and sixty marks is a very reasonable sum. I should have made it six. I understand that it is held in a sealed chest and that you regard it as not to be touched for a wedding or a ship.”

He put it down.

Nobody spoke.

“It is,” said Saikou, “a very close paraphrase.” He moved the sheet aside. There was a short paragraph at the foot of the next, in smaller writing.

I have had the western cabinet emptied. Your uncle’s seal remains sufficient for its use, and the men who carried the old pieces are still with us. There need be no valuation, nor any visitors, until you are satisfied. If you are delayed, send the case ahead. I would rather have it here than learn that you had entrusted it to another house.

Beneath it, in the same hand:

Should your inquiries take you into Ecktoria, you need not spend your first week finding a trustworthy house. I have instructed our western office to select someone who can attend you there. Passage can be arranged as soon as I have a port. You may give that person your papers and any case you are unwilling to entrust to a carrier. There is no need to settle a fee; the service is mine.

“Do you know who she means?” Riarhin asked.

“The office has several people.” Saikou ran a finger down the fold. “She has not given a name.”

“Then they haven’t left.”

“She says she wants a port. That is what I know.”

“Would you recognise them?”

He looked at Amari. Amari shook his head.

“Some,” Saikou said. “They would know me.”

Riarhin looked at the next line, where Nakato asked whether his coat was warm enough.

“Have you asked for a cabinet?”

“No.” Saikou put the first page back on top.

She had not moved. She felt, very clearly and quite without alarm, something inside her go quiet and grow attentive, in the way it had done on the scouting line when the ground ahead had changed its sound.

“Go on.”

“There is a good deal more, but it is mostly of that kind. She is glad I have chosen so wisely.” His mouth twisted. “She says that she has never doubted my judgment in the matter of an unfortunate cause. She is pleased, I think, that I am doing something so plainly unwise in so orderly a way.” He turned the page. “Then the fifth. I should like, as a favour, that you arrange with some person in that country a means by which you might be recalled. I do not say that I expect it to be needed. I say only that a man in your position ought to have a door he did not have to make in a hurry. I would bear the cost. I ask that you write me a name.”

He stopped.

“Recalled,” said Amari, very softly.

“It is a courteous word.”

“It’s a good deal else.”

“Yes.” He folded the letter. “It is the word for a thing that is done to a man with a servant and a cart, in the night, by people who love him. I have, I think, been rather more of an embarrassment than I had understood.”

There was a silence. Riarhin thought that she had never seen a face so completely give up trying to be anything at all.

“When was it written?”

“It is dated the first of the month.” He unfolded it again. “Three weeks and four days before the causeway. She could not have known of it. There is nothing here of Daire, or the lights, or my being alive.” He looked up. “She is quite right that I am. It is the only thing she does not say.”

***

They took it in order, because that was how Saikou took everything, and because it was easier than any other way.

“First,” he said, “the innocent explanation. I prefer to begin there.” He steepled his long fingers. “My mother has correspondents. She is very rich, and very old, and very well served. It is not only probable but certain that she has people in every bank between here and Khemesh who will tell her, within the week, where a portion of her money has gone. If a chest of silver were placed in the yard of a carting-woman at Coracle Harbor, and if I had drawn from my account in order to place it, someone would say so.”

“Then that explains it,” said Amari.

“It explains the sum. It explains the chest. It explains, if she has been well informed, the place.” He tapped the sheet. “It does not explain the words.”

“What words?”

“Not to be touched for a wedding or a ship. It is from the supper. I said it aloud at the drover’s house, at that table, to eight persons. I did not write it. I did not say it to Deirdre, or to a clerk, or to any banker. I said it to you.” His voice was very even. “And it is a very particular way of putting it. It was not the way I would have put it in a letter. It is the way I said it because there was an argument.”

Riarhin nodded slowly.

“The argument,” she said.

“Aurine’s question. Whether the assassination would leave anything for the recovery. I answered her.” He looked down. “My mother says, in the next paragraph, that she is glad I did not simply assume the survivors would suffice. That is not my phrase. It is hers. But it is Aurine’s, very nearly. It is the shape of the objection.”

“You said it to all of us.”

“Yes.”

“So it might have come from anyone.”

“It might.” He bowed his head. “I have been trying, for the past hour, to think of a way in which it would not.”

She sat still. She had heard the argument at the table herself; she remembered every word. She could see the room, clearly, as she saw every room: the lamp, the smoke, the jug going round. She could see where each of them had sat. She could hear the girl’s voice, low and even, from the end of the bench. Are you keeping a separate reserve? Or do you suppose the assassination’s funds and its survivors will suffice?

She turned it over. She felt in it, as she always did, the temptation to be a person who saw. She could feel the shape of it: a small pause in a level voice, an eye that went to a hand, a face perfectly composed. It was all there. She had been very careful of it since. She had let it lie, all these weeks, in the place where she put things that were probably nothing.

And she could not honestly say it was anything.

“It was Aurine’s question,” she said. “And it was in front of eight of us. And I can’t tell you anything from her face, because I didn’t look at it for any reason. I looked at it because I was pleased.” She took a breath. “I’d be making it up.”

“Yes,” said Saikou.

“I don’t want to make it up.”

“No,” he said. “I would think less of you.”

***

It was Amari, in the end, who put the harder question.

He did it without looking at anyone, with his eyes on the boards between his boots, in the voice of a man who has been thinking about it for a good while and has hoped that somebody else would say it first.

“It’s not only what she says. It’s that she knows what to say.”

“Meaning?”

“A door he did not have to make in a hurry.” The broad man rubbed his face. “That’s not her. I’ve known her thirty years. That’s somebody who’s been told what’s going on and is deciding, with great kindness, how much of it to allow. She’s not asking. She’s—”

“Managing,” said Saikou.

“Aye.”

“It is what she does.”

“I know it.” Amari lifted his head. “And I know what you’re going to ask, so I’ll say it. I write to her.”

There was a small hard silence.

“I do,” he said. “Twice in the year. When you were ill at Khemesh, I wrote every week. She’s a right to it. She paid for the surgeon. She’s paid for a great deal.” His voice was quite steady. “I didn’t write about the supper. I didn’t write about the reserve. I’ve not written since April, and then it was to say you were alive and eating.”

“You may be mistaken,” said Riarhin.

“I may.”

“I don’t say you are.”

“I know you don’t.” He looked at her. He had a broad, weathered, honest face, scored with all the years of holding one man’s weight, and there was nothing in it that she could read as anything but what it was. “But you’ll ask, and so I’ll say. You’ll ask if I’d tell you, if I had. And I’d say yes, and it would be no use, because a man who was doing it would say the same.”

“Yes.”

“So I’ll say what’s true and you can put it wherever you like.” He spread his hands. “If I were the one, I’d not have been so slow to tell him. He’s been in a room with that letter for two hours and I’ve been sitting in a chair. I’d have been out of the door.”

“Amari,” said Saikou, quietly.

“I’m only saying.”

“I know what you are saying.” Saikou did not look up. “You are the only person I would not have to be told about. I should sooner doubt my own hands.” He put a finger to the corner of his eye, briefly, and took it away. “I do not think it is you. I say so in front of Riarhin because I would not like her to have to wonder. I do not think it is you, and I know that it is not evidence.”

“No,” said Amari. “But it’ll do.”

***

She put the question after that, very carefully, as she would have put a knife on a table.

“Saikou. Who would think it was allowed?”

He turned his head.

“A thing like this. Reporting.” She spoke slowly. “Somebody at that table told your mother what you said. It might have been a spy. But it might also have been someone who thought it was harmless, or thought she had a right to it. Somebody who knew that the money was hers. Who did?”

“You did.”

“I did.”

“And Amari. And I.” He looked at the sheet. “And by the supper, I think, all of them. I said the funds I had of my mother, in the ringfort, and I said it again to the Abbess. It was not a secret. It was a fact of my circumstances.”

“Then anyone might have thought she was entitled to news.”

“Of the reserve. Not of the reasons. Not of the—” He stopped. “It is a fine distinction. I have spent a good deal of my life on it. There is a great difference between telling a person that a thing has been done, and telling her why. The first may be a courtesy. The second is a report.”

“And it was the second.”

“It was very much the second.”

She sat and looked at the candle, which had begun to gutter. There was a moth in the room. It went round and round the flame, and touched it, and fell, and got up again.

She thought of the eight of them, gathered round a table with a pot in the middle, and a magpie in the rafters, and a lamp. She thought of Nessa saying I don’t belong to you, and Cadan saying I should like the instructions to be exact. She thought of Berent’s hand on a pan. She thought that somebody at that table, on that evening, had heard a distinction being made in the presence of all, and had understood exactly what it was worth to a woman a thousand miles away, and had said nothing, and had passed the bread.

It was not, she found, a thought she could hold. It kept sliding off, as a wet fish slides, and she kept picking it up.

“It’s one of them,” she said.

“Or someone who had it from one.”

“It’s one of them.” She heard her own voice, dull. “I’ve had a whole company at my table for months. I’ve been pleased. I’ve been very pleased. I thought—” She stopped. “It doesn’t matter what I thought.”

“It does,” said Saikou. “It is the only thing that will help.”

“Then I thought it was going to be all right.”

He said nothing. It was a good silence. It was the silence of a man who has very little to give, and does not pretend to have more.

“I’ll not ask you to leave it,” said Riarhin. “I’ll not ask you to say what you’d like done. I’ll say what I want.” She looked at him. “I want to know which of them it is. I want to know why. And I want it done without any of us doing something we can’t take back.”

“That is more than one thing.”

“I know.”

“It is also,” said Saikou, “extremely reasonable.”

***

She did not sleep.

Towards dawn she went out into the yard. It was grey and very cold. The frost had come down in the night, and every hummock in the field beyond the wall stood out white against the dark, like a great number of small sleeping animals. The mare was in the byre. There was smoke from the turf-cutter’s chimney.

Aurine was at the pump.

She was drawing water, slowly, in the half-dark, with her sleeves pushed up and her cropped hair wet with mist. She had a jug at her feet and her back to the house. She did not hear the door. Riarhin stood on the step and looked at her, at the narrow back and the bent head and the bare arms, at the small competent hands on the handle.

She was not going to say anything. She was quite clear about that.

The girl turned, with the full jug. She saw her. She stopped, and there was, for the space of a moment, a very slight change in her face, and then it was gone.

“Good morning.”

“Good morning.”

“It’s cold.”

“Yes.”

“I’m taking this to Ket.” She lifted the jug. “He’s very thirsty. It’s the fever.” She waited, courteous and composed, with her eyes on Riarhin’s face. “Is there something?”

“No.”

“Then I’ll go in.”

She went past, quite close, and Riarhin smelled the cold water and the herbs on her, and the faint dark smell of cast skin that had never yet, in this country, moved. She thought: I don’t know. She thought it steadily, in the frost, and it was a very odd and lonely thing, to look at a person in the dawn and know that you did not know, and that somebody in the house did.

The door shut behind her.

She stood a while. A little wind had come up from the sea. It carried the smell of turf-smoke, and salt, and something very faint and sweet, like the inside of a bread oven the morning after.

She went in to wake the company.

Chapter 18 — Aurine’s Second Employer

It was Amari who asked the sister, and he did it in the ordinary way, over a mug of buttermilk, without anyone knowing he was doing anything.

Riarhin watched him from the kitchen window. He had gone out into the yard at first light and found Bridie among the carts, busy with a donkey’s girth, and he stood beside her with his broad back to the house and his hand on the animal’s neck, and talked. It took a quarter of an hour. He was very good at it. He asked about the roads and the price of oil, and whether the lay-brothers ever carried private letters, since a man at the steading had a sister at Ash. He laughed at something. Once he took out a pencil and wrote on the back of his hand.

When he came in he did not sit down. He took a cup of tea from the pot, and drank half of it, and put it on the table.

“Well?”

“It’s not the carter,” he said. “It’s a boy of theirs. Brother Cillian. He takes the Abbey’s post from Rossan to Whiterick twice a month, and he carries a little for the herb-women, and for the men on the jetty who can’t write.” He kept his voice low. “He took two packets from the lass at the drover’s house, the month before last. He remembers because she paid him in dried mint and said he was to give them to the Abbey clerk, and not to say.”

“Not to say to whom?”

“She didn’t say.” He looked at the floor. “The clerk sent them on. That’s all Bridie knows. It’s a plain bag. It goes to the coast, and from the coast it goes anywhere.”

Riarhin did not answer. She was looking at the yard. It was bright and cold, with the frost going off in smoke, and the lay-brothers were harnessing the donkeys. The sun had come up behind the turf-stack.

She had told herself for months that there were things she did not need to be told. She had told herself that a person who asks a question at a supper is a person who wishes to know the answer. She saw now that it had been a kind of laziness, or a kind of hope, and she thought that they had probably been the same.

“Where is she?”

“In the loft with the herbs.”

“I’ll go.”

“Do you want—”

“No.” She put down the cup. “I’d sooner it was only me.”

***

The turf-cutter’s loft ran the whole length of the house, under the thatch, and smelled of dried thyme. Aurine had made it her own in four days. There were bundles hung from every rafter, and trays on trestles, and a small charcoal brazier with a pan on it, and at the far end, under the gable window, a table where she sat with her back to the door and a pestle in her hand.

She did not turn. She said, quite calmly, “I wondered when.”

Riarhin shut the door.

“I’ll say what I know. I don’t say what I think.” She crossed the boards and stood at the other side of the table, and the light from the window lay on the girl’s dark hands and the heap of grey leaf. “You told me, on the way to the Cormorant, that your letters went through Deirdre. All of them. That you’d sent one to your teacher, and one to a sister, and that was all.”

“That’s so.”

“Deirdre’s carter remembers those two. I asked him when I checked everyone’s arrangements. I asked him again last week.” She kept her voice level. “There’s another. Two, in fact, the month before last, carried by a lay-brother named Cillian, to the Abbey clerk, with an instruction not to say where they went.”

The pestle went on for two more turns. Then it stopped.

Aurine set it down. She did it with great care, as one puts a thing down that one does not wish to break, and folded her hands on the table. Her face, in the light of the gable, was quite unmarked, and it was for that reason, Riarhin thought, that it was terrible.

“I did not lie to you,” she said. “I said what I was asked. I was asked where my letters went. I said two had gone through Deirdre, which is so. I did not say the others.”

“No.”

“I should like to say that it was a reasonable distinction.”

“Would it help?”

“No.” A faint dry smile. “I think it would not.”

She looked at the wall. There was a long bundle of rue hung there, and she seemed to study it.

“I have another employer,” she said.

***

She told it plainly, and did not once look away.

It was a woman named Nakato Ira. She had known the name for eleven years, since a winter in Khemesh when she was twenty-four and a poisoner’s apprentice with no master, and a rich old lady had sent for her through a factor. The old lady had wanted a thing done. Aurine had not asked what. It was quick and quiet and she had been paid three times what it was worth, and afterwards, in a very courteous letter, she had been asked whether she would care to be of use again.

“She never asked me to kill anyone,” said Aurine. “I want you to know that. She is not a woman who does. She’s a woman who arranges. She asked me, from time to time, to look at a thing and tell her what was in it. A medicine. A cup. Once a man who had been ill for a very long time, that she wished to know whether to forgive.” She was quite calm. “I did. It was well paid. It was not, I thought, a very great harm.”

“And Saikou.”

“In the winter, she wrote. She said her son was going to a dangerous country on a dangerous business, with people she did not know. She said she should be very grateful for a person who could be about him. She didn’t say watch. She said be of use.” Aurine’s mouth twisted. “It was such a lovely letter.”

“You took it.”

“I did. I’ve never been anything but honest about what I am. I’d been offered a place with you by a man who’d never met me, at a fee that wasn’t bad, and it happened that the place was near a person she cared about. I saw no conflict.” She drew a breath. “I saw none for a month.”

“And then?”

“And then I began to like him.”

The words came out of her with a small flat sound, like a stone dropping in a jar. She did not seem to have meant to say them. She picked up the pestle again, and turned it, and did not grind.

“I sent what she asked. It was mostly small. That he was well. That he was working. That he was keeping the company’s accounts himself and had a very neat hand.” She spoke to the table. “I sent the reserve. I sent his answer at the supper, because it was what she would want to know: that he was sensible. I sent that he’d used your name on a paper and that you’d signed it Aine.” A breath. “And I sent the mirror.”

“The mirror.”

“That he covers it. At night, with a cloth. And that there’s a gap in his papers, in the things he wrote after Khemesh, of about a page and a half, and that he doesn’t like to be asked.”

Riarhin said nothing.

She had seen the cloth herself. She had seen it in the loft at Coracle Harbor, a plain grey cloth over a small round glass on a nail, and had thought it a quirk, a private and reasonable oddity, like a man who will not sit with his back to a door. She had thought it was Saikou’s. She had not for a moment thought it was a fact.

“Why?” she said.

“Because she asked how he slept.” Aurine looked up. “She’s his mother. She said he had not been well since Khemesh, and that she did not know what it was. She wanted me to tell her if he was eating and if there was a thing he did not say.” She laughed, very shortly. “I told her what I saw. It seemed what I was paid for.”

“What did she say?”

“Nothing. She wrote me back a week later, and it was two lines. She said thank you, that is exactly what I wished to know, and there was a draft on a Kahfagian bank.”

The thatch above them creaked in the wind. A very small sound of dripping came from somewhere in the eaves.

“And the last letter,” said Riarhin.

Aurine was still.

“It came when we were at the drover’s. I’d been on the road, so I read it after. I have it here.” She reached into the front of her dress and drew out a folded scrap, quite small, in a brown envelope, and laid it on the table between them. “She asks me to make arrangements. She wants, if he persists, a way of bringing him out. A person he trusts, or a drug, or a ship. She doesn’t say which. She says she leaves it to my judgment.”

“And have you?”

“No.”

She said it very simply.

“I’ve thought of it. I’ve lain awake and thought of three ways. I’m a poisoner. It is the sort of thing I’m for.” She looked at the scrap. “I have not done it, and I don’t know why. It might be that I like him. It might be that I don’t like being told, by a woman I’ve met four times, that she will leave my judgment to me.” Her voice dropped. “She has a way of doing that. She makes the job larger in the letter and smaller on the day, and you’re left holding the difference. I’ve seen it done. I thought, if I’m to be the one she leaves it to, I had better know what the job is before I say yes.”

“That’s not innocence.”

“No.” Aurine met her eyes. “I don’t say it is. I say I haven’t done it, and I’m not sure that I would, and that I have been keeping a very careful watch on myself for a fortnight. It may be a thing I’m ashamed of. I don’t know yet.” She let out a long breath. “I’m not going to say I’m sorry. It would be too easy. I’ll say I know what I did.”

Riarhin stood a long time.

She thought that she had expected anger. She had gone up the stair, she found, with a great deal of it ready, and it had run out of her like water through a colander, and left only a stiff cold desolation, and, quite absurdly, a sort of admiration. It was a thing she would not have believed if she had been told it. The girl had said it all. She had said it in order, without asking to be excused, and had kept back nothing that was not hers.

“Come down,” she said. “I want it said to the rest.”

“Yes.”

“All of it. The mirror as well.”

“Yes,” said Aurine. She stood, and wiped her hands on her apron. “I’d thought you would.”

***

They were in the kitchen by ten.

It was a hard hour. She had thought it might be worse. Aurine stood at the end of the table with her hands folded in front of her, in her plain dark dress, and told it from the beginning, as she had told it in the loft, and did not once change her voice. She told it to Amari, who sat with his fists on his knees and did not look at her. She told it to Berent, who had been helped in and set on the bench and said nothing at all. She told it to Nessa, and to Tavin with the magpie on his shoulder, and to Cadan, who stood by the wall with his hands in his sleeves and his head slightly tilted, as one attends a very well-made speech.

And she told it to Saikou.

He sat at the head of the table. He had not slept, and the skin under his eyes was the colour of ash. He listened to all of it without moving, and only when she came to the mirror did he draw one long breath through his nose, and let it out.

“Thank you,” he said, when she had done.

“Master—”

“It is very clear. It is a good account.” His voice was quite level and quite dead. “I would like to say that I am not angry, and I find I cannot. I am angry with my mother. I am angry with you.
