It is a very great deal of anger, and it has nowhere to go, and I will not settle it by hitting anyone.” He looked down at his hands. “I should like the room to know that I am aware of it, and that it will not be allowed to decide anything.”

“It is a family quarrel,” said Aurine, very quietly.

“It is not.” It was Riarhin who said it. She had not known she would. “It isn’t only that. That’s the thing I don’t want us to do. I don’t want it turned into a quarrel between a man and his mother, with a girl as the go-between. It’s not.” She looked at them. “Whatever went out went out of all our doors. It’s not Saikou’s exposure. It’s every one of us.”

“I gave my name,” said Nessa.

She had spoken from the end of the bench. She was very pale. Her hands were in her lap, one over the other, still.

“I gave it at the Cormorant. And my cantor’s. And I told a story, about a hedge I hadn’t finished.” She looked at Aurine. “Did you send that?”

“I sent your name, and that you were of Aveh.” Aurine’s voice was steady. “I did not send the hedge. It wasn’t what she asked.”

“Does she know the name of my cantor?”

“No.”

“Are you certain?”

“I wrote it, and I know what I wrote.”

“That’s not the same,” said Nessa. “But it will do.”

“What did she know about my gift?” said Tavin. He had come round on the bench. His bright easy face was not at all easy. “That’s the thing I’d want to be told. The little matter of a luck that I’d not care to have generally known. Did it go?”

“That there was a magpie. That you were a thief of some skill.”

“That’s nothing. That’s known in every port.” He looked down at the bird, who looked back with one eye. “It’s an expensive thing, my luck. I’d not like to hear it had been spent in a letter. I’ll say it plainly. Before anybody does anything, I’d like it measured. I don’t care to see a good capability smashed because somebody’s hurt.”

“That’s not a kind way to put it,” said Berent.

“It’s not meant to be kind.” Tavin did not look at him. “It’s meant to be useful. If she’s cut off, we’ve lost what she’s got. If she’s kept, we’ve to know what she’ll do. If she’s—” He stopped, and swallowed. “I’ve done it both ways in my time. It’s never the sum you think.”

“And me?” said Cadan.

They turned. He had not moved. He stood with his head on one side and his hands in his sleeves, and his narrow face was interested and quite calm.

“I should like to know,” he said, “what the lady in Khemesh has been told of me.”

Aurine hesitated.

“That you were a monk of Ikasha. That you had been asked to leave, for what were called pedagogical severities. That your references were cruel and that the cruelty was not disorderly.”

“She has the references, then.”

“She has what Saikou had.”

“Then she has read them,” said Cadan, with great satisfaction. “I should like to have seen her face.”

Nobody laughed. It was quiet. Riarhin found that she was looking at him, at the smooth gentle unlined face, and that her hands had gone under the table.

***

It was Amari who said the last of it, when the rest were silent.

He had not said a word. He sat with his forearms on his knees and his big head lowered and looked at the boards.

“It’s gone,” he said. “That’s the thing. It’s in her hands. We can’t call it back. All we can do is decide what to do with the one who sent it.”

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

“And she’s here.” He raised his eyes at last, and they were bloodshot and quite steady. “She’s here, and she told us. I don’t know what that’s worth. I’d like somebody to say.”

“I will,” said Riarhin.

“When?”

“Tonight. When I’ve thought.”

She stood. All those tired faces turned towards her, including the woman at the end of the table. What I do now is what they will remember.

She went out into the yard to be alone. The mare stood at the byre door with her ears forward. She put her hand on the warm neck. It was a very long time since she had felt so entirely as if there were two of her: one that stood in the cold and stroked a horse, and one that sat in the kitchen with a lit lamp, being asked for a verdict, and was very careful, and very quick, and very small.

Chapter 19 — The Reprieve

She said it in the kitchen after dark, with the door shut and the candle lit. Aurine sat at the foot of the bench. The others had left that end of the table to her.

“Here is what I’ve decided,” she said. “Aurine stays.”

Nobody spoke. Aurine, at the foot of the bench, did not move. She had been sitting all day with her hands folded, like a woman in a waiting room, and she raised her eyes now and lowered them.

“We’ve one job. It’s three days’ road to the Hall, and after that only days before the sealing. I can’t do it without a poisoner. I can’t do it without somebody who knows what she knows.” She kept her voice level. “So she comes. She works. She does what she’s hired for, and does it well, and when it’s done we’ll sit down and decide what’s to be done with her.”

“Decide,” said Aurine.

“Yes.”

“And if the job goes wrong?”

“Then we’ll talk about that.”

“And if I don’t wish to go on?”

“We’ll talk about that too.”

She had said it the way one says a thing that is obviously reasonable. It was a kind of a fence. She had put it round the girl quietly, without a word about what lay on the far side, and she had let the words we’ll talk stand where the words you’re safe might have been. It was a trick of the Harrow’s pay-tent. She had watched a paymaster do it, when she was nineteen, to a sergeant he did not trust. You did not say if you run I’ll hang you. You held back the last third of the money, and said nothing, and let the man do the sum.

Aurine had done it already. She looked across the table with her dark eyes very steady and tired.

“I understand,” she said.

“I hope so.”

“I do.” A pause. “Thank you.”

There was a small dry sound from the wall.

It was Cadan. He was standing as he always stood, with his hands in his sleeves, and his head a little on one side, and there was on his narrow face an expression she had not seen there before. It was delight. It was quite pure.

“That is very fine,” he said.

She turned.

“You have left her to invent the punishment.” He inclined his head towards Aurine. “She’ll work harder than if you named one.”

“Brother,” said Amari.

“I mean it kindly.”

“I know you do.”

“It is the highest form of instruction.” Cadan smiled at her.

She did not answer.

She pressed her left palm against the table to stop it shaking. Cadan was still smiling.

He means it.

She had meant to leave Aurine uncertain. She had even been pleased with how little she needed to say.

She saw Aurine’s face. It had not changed. But the girl was looking at Cadan, and then, quite steadily, at her.

Riarhin drew her hands off the table.

“I’d like to say that again,” she said.

“Pardon?”

“What I said. I’d like it back.” She heard her own voice. It was quite steady, and rather low. “I’m sorry. I said something that I meant as a decision and it was something else.” She turned to the foot of the table. “Aurine. I was wrong.”

“About what?”

“The last part.” She stopped. She found the words, one by one, like stones. “I said we’d talk. I let it stand as if there were something behind it. There isn’t. I’ve thought of every way that it might be, and I’m not going to do any of them, and I don’t want you to spend the rest of the job thinking that I might.” She drew a breath. “That’s not how I want to keep anyone. It’s just a habit. It works. That’s the whole of my excuse for it.”

“It is very effective,” said Cadan.

“Yes.”

“You will be giving it up?”

“Yes.”

“She may leave you without a poisoner.”

“I heard her.”

He looked at her, quite kindly. Then he shrugged, slightly, in his sleeves, as one does who has been shown a fine tool put aside for reasons of taste.

***

She put it in order. She did it standing, with her hands behind her.

“Here’s what I’ll say. It’s said once. If any of you wants it changed, say so now.

“One. Aurine will give a full account of what she has sent, with dates, to whom, in whose hands. It’s to be written. Saikou has it. It doesn’t leave the house.

“Two. No more reports. Not to Nakato Ira, not to her factor, not to anyone else who pays her. If the lady writes to her son, she’s welcome to. If Aurine writes to the lady, Saikou reads it first, and it’s a thing he could send himself.

“Three. She’ll not take the mother’s commission to bring Saikou out. Not in any form. If she thinks he ought to be brought out, she says so to us, at this table, and we’ll say yes or no. It’s not for her to settle on somebody’s behalf and I’ll not have anyone who works for me do it to anyone else.

“Four.” She looked at the girl. “At the end of the job, when the Charter’s sealed and it’s over one way or the other, she chooses. She goes on with us or she doesn’t. If she doesn’t, she’s paid what she’s owed to that day, and she leaves, and nobody follows her. I want that heard. She doesn’t owe the company the rest of her life for the thing she’s done.”

“What she has done,” said Tavin, from the wall, “is expensive.”

“I know.”

“I only say it’s a good deal to be forgiven for.”

“I’m not forgiving her.” It came out flat. “I’m not saying it doesn’t matter. It’s cost us a secret. It’s cost Saikou something I don’t know the shape of. It’s not going to be given back, and nobody at this table is under any obligation to like her.” She met his eye. “But I’m not going to keep a woman alive by keeping her afraid. It’s a poor foundation for a company. It’s a worse one for a job like this.”

“And if she runs?”

“Then she runs. And I hope she takes the road for Ash and has a good life.”

Tavin was silent. Then he nodded, once, shortly, and looked down at the bird.

“It’s not what I’d have done,” he said. “But I’ll not say it’s wrong.”

***

It was Saikou who spoke next.

He had been sitting at the head of the table the whole time, with his long hands in his lap. He had not said a word since the morning. He looked, she thought, like a man who had gone very far into the cold and been told he might return.

“I agree,” he said.

“You’re sure.”

“I am. It is very reasonable.” He turned his head towards Aurine, slowly. “I would like to add something, if I may. I should not like it to be thought that I say this only because it is my mother’s money that has kept the company fed. That is a thing that could be said. It is even true.” He folded his hands. “I do not consider that it gives me the right to settle it. I have had a great deal of practice, in my life, in settling things that were not mine. I should like very much to stop.”

“Saikou—”

“I shall write to her. I will say that we know, and that we have taken account of it, and that there will be no arrangement for my recall.” His voice caught, and steadied. “I shall say it in my own hand. I shall not say it unkindly. And I shall say that if she wishes to speak to me, she has only to write, and that I shall answer.”

“She’ll be furious,” said Amari.

“She will.”

“She’ll say you’ve been led astray.”

“She will say that I have been misled by an unfortunate cause.” The ghost of a smile touched the long face. “I believe I have heard it before.”

“She’s not our master,” said Amari, flatly.

“No.”

“I’m not saying she’s wrong to be afraid.” The broad man rubbed his eyes. “She’s a right to be. It’s not a small thing. He’s been in a room with the worst of it since Khemesh, and I’ve seen what it’s done. If it were my son I’d have sent a ship.” He lifted his head. “But it’s not her hand on the tiller. Whatever she’s paid for, it’s not that.”

“Thank you,” said Saikou, very quietly.

“It’s only true.”

Aurine had not moved. She sat at the foot of the bench with her hands in her lap, and for a moment Riarhin thought that she was going to speak, and that it would be a long thing, and that it would break. She did not. She looked at each of them in turn, without haste, and at the last she looked at Saikou.

“I will do it,” she said. “All four.”

“You’re quite certain,” said Riarhin.

“I’m not certain of anything. But I’ll do them.” She drew a breath. “I would like it said that I do not ask to be liked.”

“No one would think you did.”

“Good.” The dry smile came and went. “I’d hate to be misunderstood.”

***

It was not a merry evening.

They ate, since it was supper. It was oatmeal and cold mutton and a dish of boiled turnips that the turf-cutter’s wife had sent in, and they ate it in almost complete silence, eight people at a table that left their elbows touching. Nessa moved her bowl a few inches to the left when Aurine sat down, and then, seeing what she had done, moved it back. Berent ate with his eyes on his plate. Tavin made a joke about the mutton, and nobody laughed, and he did not seem to mind. He sat for a while with his hands round his cup, and then said quietly, to no one, “Well, that’s a thing,” and was silent.

She had made the decision. Now Nessa was watching where she put her bowl.

Aurine ate quickly and quietly and rose. She went to the door, and stopped there, and said without looking round: “Thank you for the fourth.”

“The choice?”

“Yes.”

“It’s yours.”

“I know.” A little pause. “I had not expected it to be.”

She went out.

Riarhin finished her turnip and cupped her hands round her drink. Conversation had begun again at the other end of the table. She could still use what she had given up; that was the trouble. She sat listening without taking in the words.

“May I say something?”

It was Cadan. He had come round the table and was standing beside her chair, with his hands in his sleeves, and there was on his face an expression of earnest and gentle perplexity.

“Yes.”

“I am sorry if I was clumsy. I did not intend to give offence.” He spoke quite low. “I thought it a very fine thing. I have thought about it all evening. I do not understand why you put it down.”

“I know you don’t.”

“Was it because I said it?”

“Partly.”

“Then I have made you less effective.” He said it with real regret. “I should not like that. I said that I should tell you if I thought a thing was not enough. I find I do not think it was a mistake to say so. But I think it may have been a mistake to be right.”

She looked up at him. He was quite serious.

She thought that she should be angry, and could not find where. He was not a man one could be angry with in the ordinary way; it would have been like being angry with a well. He was courteous, and attentive, and honest, and perfectly happy to see her put it down, provided that she understood how good it was. He stood there at her elbow with his mild, clever, unlined face, and she saw that he would never be a danger to her by intention, and that she would never be safe from him by any other means.

“Goodnight, Cadan,” she said.

“Goodnight.” He bowed. “I am glad to be here. I hope it is not impertinent to say so.”

He went to his blanket by the fire. She heard him settle, and after a few moments his breathing slowed.

She took up the lamp and did the round. The doors, the byre, the yard, the gate. She counted the horses and got to five, and stood for a moment with her hand on the grey mare’s neck, in the dark, under the cold enormous sky.

Somewhere in the house, at the top of the stair, a single light was burning in the loft where the herbs hung. It burned late. After a while it went out.

Interlude V — A Result of Her Own

Jaennis

The pattern took her eleven Thursdays, and on the eleventh it worked.

It was not much to look at. It was a ring of grey thread pegged to a stone slab with seven brass tacks, and inside the ring a candle in a plain dish, and around the outside a border of chalk marks that Master Hesse had once described as a sentence in a very dull language. The effect it was meant to produce was small and specific. A flame inside the ring would stand upright in a draught. Nothing more. It was what first-year students did in the west wing, on wet afternoons, and it was generally done badly.

Jaennis had done it badly for ten weeks. She had not minded. It was pleasant to be bad at something. She had lived for thirty years among people who were good at things, and it gave her a curious, holiday feeling to kneel on a cold floor with chalk on her sleeves and be told, in a level voice, that the fourth mark was a hair’s breadth to the left.

On the eleventh Thursday she pegged the thread, and drew the border, and set down the candle, and opened the shutter of the little window so that the sea-wind came in over the sill. She looked at the flame.

She did not ask it for anything. That was the whole of the lesson, when one came to it. She thought only of the shape of the thing, the stiff small upright of a wick that had no reason to bend, and let the pattern do what patterns did.

The flame stood up.

The wind came in. It blew the shutter against the wall and set the papers dancing on the desk, and the candle in its ring did not move. It burned tall and yellow and quite straight. It burned as a flame burns in a room that has no air in it.

“Yes,” said Hesse, from the doorway.

Jaennis did not turn. She sat back on her heels and let out a long breath.

“It is sufficient,” said Hesse. “It is not brilliant, and I should not want you to think so. But it is done correctly, and it will hold. You may have tea.”

“It leans.”

There was a pause.

“I beg your pardon?”

“At the edge.” She lifted a hand and pointed, without touching. “At the north-west, where the border meets the ring. The flame’s quite upright. But the light at that side isn’t. It’s just perceptibly out. If you look at the shadow of the tack.” She frowned. “It’s as though there were a very small draught that the pattern hadn’t stopped.”

Hesse crossed the room. She was a spare woman with a face like a plank, and she stooped, and looked, and stayed there for a good while. She did not say anything. She was still.

“It’s a fault in the drawing,” said Jaennis.

“It is not.”

“Then what?”

“I don’t know.” Hesse straightened. “I will tell you that I have seen that lean before. In perhaps one pattern in forty, done by the best of students, and by the worst. It’s in the books. It is called the edge and it is ignored.” She turned her plain grey eyes on her. “There is a reason that it is ignored. I should like you to hear it now, from me, and not later from a book.”

“Go on.”

“A pattern that works is a solution. You wished for a steady flame, and you have one. That is all it was built to do.” Her voice was dry. “The edge is what the pattern does in addition. It is the place where the problem, having been solved in one direction, has gone elsewhere. It is very small. It is nearly always harmless. But if you are the kind of person who wishes to follow it, you will find that it is not a matter of drawing the pattern better. It is a different operation. It is not this one, done more carefully. It is another thing, and it has its own edges.”

“I understand.”

“Do you?”

“I think I do. It’s the same as the water.” Jaennis rose, and dusted her knees. “It changes the question. And then the new one has an edge of its own.”

“Very good,” said Hesse, and there was, in the plank face, the smallest possible flicker of something like alarm. “I’d thought it would take you a year.”

“What is the work you don’t give the students?”

“I beg your pardon?”

“There is some. There always is.” She said it quite pleasantly. “You’ve been very careful, for ten weeks, to teach me nothing that touches the edge. That’s reasonable. I’m an amateur, and I’ve a city to govern. But I should like to see it.”

“You would not understand it.”

“Then it will teach me something.”

“It is not,” said Hesse, “a matter of understanding.” She looked at the candle, upright in its ring, with its tall gold flame and its small offending shadow. “I will speak to the Chancellor. I will not promise.”

“Thank you.”

“Don’t,” said Hesse, and went to see about the tea.

***

The rest of the day was the governorship, and she gave it, as she had given every day since her arrival, the whole of her attention.

It was an ugly dispute. It had begun in the fish market at six that morning, over a drowned pilot. His widow said that he had held a share in a boat, and that the boat’s owner had sold it two days before his death, without paying her. The owner said the widow’s late husband had signed his share away in the winter. There were witnesses. There were four of them, and each had a different date, and by nine o’clock two families and a good part of the quay were in the square before the counting-house, in a state that could be called angry only by a person who had not seen a crowd.

She went down to them herself. She took the paymaster and a clerk, and no guard, and she stood on the steps in her plain dark coat and heard the whole of it. It took two hours. She asked for the papers. She had them read aloud, in the square, in front of everyone, by the clerk, in turn: the widow’s, the owner’s, the four witnesses’. She did not say what she thought of any. She made them read it into the air, in full hearing, so that nobody in the crowd could afterwards alter a word.

It was the dates, in the end. There had been three, and a fourth that did not fit. It was not a lie. It was a man who had been mistaken about a Tuesday. The owner went white. The widow began to cry. She sat on the step, in the sun, with the widow’s hand in hers, and said, quietly, that the owner would pay a fair price for the share by the end of the month, and that she would see it done.

It was a good piece of work. She was aware of that. She was aware also that the whole of the quay was looking at her, in the bright noon, with the expression of people who have been given something they did not know they wanted.

She went home at dusk. She was tired. She ate a plain dinner at the counting-house desk, and answered four letters, and signed two commissions, and read a report on the winter’s grain. At nine she took a lamp and went down through the dark court to the west wing.

The little room was empty. The pattern still lay on the slab, pegged and chalked, with the candle burned to a stub in its dish. She knelt and lit it.

It stood up. She watched it. The shadow of the north-west tack lay along the stone as before, a small dark line, slightly out.

She sat for an hour with her chin on her hand.

She thought that she had come to this city because the queen had sent her. She thought that for a fortnight she had been quite sure that this was the whole of it: that she had been moved to the side of the board, and that the work she did was what a person did when she had been moved, to keep from thinking. She had done a great deal. She had drawn the city’s drawers. She had fed the hungry and fined the fat. She had met a widow in the square. She had done it well, and she had been proud of it, and she had also, all the while, been listening for a footstep on the stair.

It had stopped. She noticed it now as one notices that a clock has stopped. She sat in a stone room on the far side of the sea with a candle, and she was not waiting for anyone. She was looking at a small dark line, and wanting, with her whole mind, to know why it was there.

She thought of Markus. She knew where that road went. She had read his letters in the winter, with the hand of a person who reads what she does not wish to be true, and she knew what had happened to a man who wished to know why. She knew it was a real thing. It did not frighten her as it ought. It only made the question sharper, as a cold night makes a star.

I will be careful, she thought.

She blew out the candle. Then, quite deliberately, she lit it again, and drew a fresh sheet of paper towards her, and began to copy the pattern.

Part Four — Under the Whale Ribs

Chapter 20 — The Hall Already at Work

The roof was a ribcage, and it was very much older than the people under it.

She saw it from the last rise of the road, on the following afternoon, with the cart creaking behind her and the old woman lying in it on a heap of blankets, saying nothing. The High Hall lay in a hollow of green ground above a reed-fringed lake, a long grey building with a steep thatch and a great many smaller roofs huddled round it like chickens, and along its ridge, through the thatch, there showed a row of pale curved shapes. They rose out of the straw like the ribs of a wrecked ship. She counted eleven before the road dropped, and could not count the rest.

Below the buildings, boats lay at a timber jetty. The lake narrowed beyond them into a river that wound away through the reeds.

“They come up from the eastern landing,” Fenra said. “When the water’s high enough. We’d have been hauling the loaded boats over the shallows this week.” She lifted her head to look at the roof. “That’s him.”

“Who?”

“The whale. What’s left.” The old voice was thin and quite pleased. “My mother was right. It’s the only one the sea’s ever coughed up on this coast, and they’ve been living under it four hundred years. There’s people say it’s not a whale at all. There’s people say it’s the roof of the drowned king’s house, come ashore.” A pause. “I’d not like to say.”

“Is it in the play?”

“It’s in every play.”

***

It was, when they came under it, the loudest room she had ever entered.

She had expected a hall. What she found was a market held indoors by a small country. The nave was two hundred feet long, and the floor was beaten earth strewn with rushes, and the ribs of the beast went up on either side and met above in a vault of pale bone gone the colour of old butter. It was oiled. It shone. In the smoke and the shifting light the curves overhead seemed to breathe. Below, in every bay, in the aisles and the side-chapels and against the walls, people had set up whatever they had come to do. There were cheese-women with their boards. There was a man selling ribbons and another selling whistles. A tinker had lit a fire in a bucket. There was a bench of brehons at the north end, in grey, hearing a dispute about a cow, and a Temple table with a green cloth and a clerk writing, and, in a corner by the door, four lay-brothers in undyed wool setting out long trestles for what would plainly be a great meal.

And they were all doing it at once. A woman was crying at the cow. A drover was arguing with a priest. Somewhere a child had got hold of a whistle.

“It’s not stopped,” said Amari, at her elbow.

“No.”

“I thought it would. For us.”

“Why?”

“I don’t know.” He rubbed his neck. “It felt as though we were coming to something.”

She looked at him. The broad, honest, tired face was quite serious.

“We’re coming to a hearing about a cow,” she said. “It’ll be over by supper.”

It was. As they stood in the door, the chief brehon, a very old, very small man with a face like a walnut, gave his judgment in six words and rapped his rod on the bench. The woman stopped crying. The drover, who had lost, said something short and went off to the beer. A pair of boys ran in with a cauldron between them on a pole, and the smell of stewed mutton went through the hall like a wave.

Nobody had waited. The gathering had not been suspended because her company was late, or because a man was dead on the moss, or because a hurt old woman was being carried in on a hurdle. It had gone on doing what it did. She had, she realised, been carrying a picture in her head for three days, of a great room turned to face the door. It had never for a moment been true.

Good, she thought. It surprised her. That’s good.

***

Eogan met them at the west door with a clerk and a slate, and did not apologise.

“You’re late,” he said. He was cheerful and inky and very tired, and he said it with an air of great relief. “Not by much. Not in the way that matters. The Abbess’s people have been standing for you since Tuesday. I’ve put you in the north bay, by the second rib. It’s a good place. It’s dry.” He glanced at the cart. “Is that the landing-keeper?”

“Yes.”

“She’ll want a bed. There’s one behind the Everflame’s table. It’s warm.” He lowered his voice. “I’m sorry about the man. It’s a bad business. I’ve put something in the roll.”

“Thank you.”

“It’s nothing.” He hesitated. “I should say that the matter of the adjournment was not easy. The Red Oar was very polite. He asked, in open session, if the delay was to be taken as a withdrawal. He’s not been at all difficult.” He looked, for a moment, faintly troubled. “I do not know when I have been so well treated by a man I did not care to see.”

“That’s what he does,” said Riarhin.

“Is it?”

“Yes.”

He nodded slowly. Then he shook it off, and put on his bright face again, and led them through the crowd.

***

Cadan reported at dusk. He did it standing in the north bay, with his hands in his sleeves, in the manner of a man giving a lesson.

He had spent the afternoon in the Hall. He had gone in a grey coat, without a name, and had walked the nave and the aisles and the two galleries, and had bought a hot pie at a stall and eaten it under the third rib. He had looked at doors. He had looked at the little stone lodge by the west gate, where a woman in a leather jerkin sat on a bench with a sword across her knees. He had watched a great many people go past.

“Master Donnan is not where we thought,” he said.

“Meaning?”

“He is not in the guest-gallery at the north end, where the Red Oar’s household has lodged for thirty years. He moved on Monday. I have been told it was a matter of the chimney.” He paused. “He is in the old scriptorium, at the back of the Everflame’s wing. It is a stone chamber with a single door, and one small window that looks onto the abbey garden. It has a stair to the roof on one side. It has a very solid lock.”

Riarhin was silent.

She had a sheet in her sleeve. She had written it in the kitchen at Rossan, a month ago, in her small slanting hand. It was a plan of the guest-gallery, with the doors and the beds and the boy at the curtain. It was an excellent plan. She had spent a fortnight on it.

“It is not a great change,” said Cadan. “But it is a change of kind. He will be in the same room every night, in stone, with a door that opens inward, and Halla has put a bench outside it. The boy is gone. I do not think it is anything more than the chimney. But I should have liked to tell you before we came.”

“How could you?”

“I could have been here.”

“You were at Rossan.” She said it quite gently. “It’s not a failure. It’s a fact. I gave you a picture of a place and it’s stopped being true.” She drew out the sheet and looked at it for a moment. Then she folded it in four and put it in the fire. “I’d rather have the new one.”

He watched the paper burn.

“It is very generous,” he said, at last.

“It isn’t. It’s practical.”

“I did not say the two were different,” said Cadan, with a small courteous smile.

***

Aurine came in a little later, from the west aisle, with a covered basket on her arm and a slight frown.

She had been to see Heber. She said it plainly, at the trestle, taking off her shawl. She had gone to look, as she had said she would, and had found him at his corner by the Confessors’ bench, in his scarf, with a line of people waiting. There was a small girl on his knee. She had a fever and a sore throat, and her mother was crying. He was bathing the child’s forehead with a wet cloth and talking to her in a very low voice about a dog.

“He’s been there since noon,” said Aurine. “He’s not at his master’s door. He’s not where he was before. Halla came to fetch him twice, and he sent her away.” She put the basket down. “I don’t think it’s a stratagem. I think the child was ill and he was the only person in the Hall who knew what to do.”

“Is it a good sign or a bad one?”

“I’ve not decided.” She looked, for a moment, very tired. “It’s inconvenient. It means he’s a person. I’d have liked to be able to put him in a box.”

“Did he see you?”

“He saw me.” A short dry pause. “He nodded. He was very polite.”

***

The stores came in with the evening.

It was the Abbey’s supply party, at last, with its carts and its donkeys, drawn up in a line along the nave with the bread on the trestles and the oil in its casks. She saw the sisters carry them in. She saw the mark on every sack, a small round white seal, and on every cask a bit of white cord, tied through the bung. Even the knots in the white cord matched.

Brannoc’s household was at the north bay. There were nine of them: his mother, an old and stately woman in a black shawl; his cousin Lorcan, narrow and silent; two nephews; a steward; three others whose names she did not know. They stood in a row and received the sacks as they came, with a bowing of heads. They said, each of them, in turn, to the lay-brother who brought them, thank you. It was gratitude. It was also very like shame.

“It’s a great kindness,” said Lorcan, to nobody.

“It is.”

“The Abbey’s a good friend to us. It’s a great thing.” He said it stiffly. He looked at his boots. “And now that it’s settled, we may be easy.”

He delivered it towards his boots, as though repeating an instruction. She doubted it was meant for her.

But Muirenn, at the end of the bay, had turned.

She had come in an hour before, with her black-thread stitcher and two of her crew, and was standing still with a cup in her hand. Her face was very calm. She looked at Lorcan, and then at the sacks, and then at the round white seal, and she said, quite lightly, in her gruff level voice:

“Settled?”

“I only—”

“What’s settled?”

“The Abbey’s interest,” said Lorcan. He had gone a little white. “In the landing. It’s only that—it’s a matter of the supplies. A friendly matter. I’d not have said it if I’d thought.”

“Nor would I have asked if I’d not been curious.” Muirenn set down her cup. “Brannoc.”

He had come up behind them. He was very red, and his brown coat was buttoned wrong.

“I’d like you to tell me,” she said, “when you’re ready, what the Abbey’s interest in your landing is. I’ll not hear it here. I’ll hear it tomorrow. In a room with a door.” She smiled. It was a hard, brief, level thing, quite without cruelty. “Till then I’ll not discuss the Charter with anyone. It seems to me that I’ve been talking to the wrong end.”

“Muirenn—”

“Tomorrow.”

She went out. She went across the nave, through the smoke and the noise, and no one in the whole Hall looked round.

Brannoc stood a moment where she had left him. He said nothing. His hands hung at his sides. Then he turned, and saw Riarhin watching, and the colour went out of his face all at once, as it goes out of a cheek that has been slapped.

***

The meal for the poor households was held under the fourth rib, at long trestles, at nine.

There were two hundred at it. Sile and Cera sat at opposite ends. The sisters went along the benches with the oatmeal and the bread, and Caelia, who had come in at last on a mule, at the end of a long road, sat at the head of the table with her hands folded and ate very little. She looked very old. She looked as though she had been carrying something up a hill.

Riarhin was at the second table, with the company. She watched. She was sitting opposite Aurine.

It was Aurine who saw it. She did it calmly, over a bowl of stew, without turning her head. It was a small thing. A pair of Brannoc’s people were carrying a platter down the line: a long dish of salt fish, in strips, pale and very neat, with a sprig of something green laid on top. It was set before a family with three small children. A brother in white stood over it, hands raised, and said a blessing.

“Riarhin,” said Aurine, quietly.

“Yes.”

“That’s from the fourth storehouse.”

“How do you know?”

“Because I’ve smelled it.” She kept her eyes on her bowl. “It’s been washed. It’s been washed in vinegar and salted again and dressed with sorrel. It looks quite decent. It’s not. It’s the same fish.”

“Is it dangerous?”

“Not to a well man. Not once.” Her voice was very level. “To a child of three, or to a woman who’s been ill, or to someone who eats it four nights in succession because it’s been blessed, I should say it was a very poor idea.”

“It’s been blessed.”

“The blessing’s a fine thing. I’d not say a word against it.” She looked up. It was the plain grave look she wore when she gave a dose. “But it doesn’t change what’s in the barrel. It’s not that sort of blessing. A person who says that it is, is not being kind.”

“You’ll have to tell somebody.”

“I know.” She pushed back her bowl. “That’s the trouble with being useful. It’s never for a good reason.”

She got up. She walked the length of the table, small and upright in her dark dress, and she went up to the brother in white, and said something in his ear.

He looked at her. He looked at the fish, and back. His face changed; it was the face of a man who has been asked by a stranger to do a thing that he knew perfectly well he ought to have done. He did not argue. He took the platter from the table in both hands and carried it away, and the mother of the three small children looked after it, bewildered, and did not ask.

Caelia had seen. She sat at the head of the table with her light unhurried eyes on the retreating dish. She did not move. She looked, for the space of a breath, at Aurine, who was walking back to her seat with her eyes on the floor, in a country that did not want her patron. And she inclined her head, slightly, in the manner of one who acknowledges a fair blow.

Riarhin looked away.

The meal went on. The beast’s pale ribs went up into the smoke, and the lamps burned, and somewhere among the tables a child had found her whistle again. She sat with her hands in her lap. She thought of a hall she had been asked to come to, a marriage she had been asked to make possible, and a man she had been asked to kill.

She had signed for the stores at the steading. Brannoc had pledged the dues without telling Muirenn; Riarhin had known, and let the carts go on. Tomorrow she would have to say when she had known.

Muirenn had left. Brannoc had not come to the meal. And in the dark at the far end of the Hall, in a stone room behind the Everflame’s wing, a lamp burned in a small high window, and the Red Oar was alone with his books and his dog, and, she supposed, was quite happy.

Chapter 21 — The Price of Salt

Muirenn chose the weighing-house, because nobody in the Hall would go there unless they had something to sell.

It stood behind the kitchens, a low stone shed with a great iron beam hung from the roof and a pair of scales on it as wide as cartwheels. It smelled of wet wool and old tallow. On a bench against the wall a clerk had left his lunch, half an onion and a heel of bread, and no one had moved it. There was one high window. There was a lamp, and a table, and six stools.

They were seven, in the end. Fenra had asked to be brought and had been carried in on her hurdle by Amari and Tavin, and set down in the corner with her broken leg on a stool and her slate in her lap. Nobody had argued. It was the first time in thirty years, Riarhin thought, that anyone had been ready to let her be there.

Muirenn stood by the scales. She had not sat. She had put on a clean jerkin and tied her hair back with the blue cloth, and her big scarred hands hung loose at her sides.

“It was a very good supper,” she said. “I am told the fish was blessed.”

“Muirenn,” said Brannoc.

“I’d like you to say it in the order it happened. From the beginning. And I’d like Fenra to say if you leave anything out.” She did not look at him. “I’m not asking you to be kind to yourself.”

He sat down. He put his big hands on the table and looked at them. He had not slept, and the beard round his mouth looked grey.

“In the autumn,” he said, “I bought two hundred barrels of cured fish from a salter at Dunmarrow.”

“Through Sheil.”

“Through Eamon Sheil. My purchaser. He’s dealt for me eleven years.” His voice was low and even. “The price was good. Better than I’d had. I thought it was luck. I put them in the storehouse and forgot them, and in the winter Fenra asked to see the tally.”

“I asked twice,” said Fenra, from the corner.

“You did.”

“He said the books were at Dunmarrow.”

“He did.” Brannoc did not look up. “I believed him. I’d no reason not to. I’d had other things. The wedding. The Charter. I was thinking of a good many other things.” He drew a breath. “In the spring you opened the first barrels, and told me they were not what they should be. And I went to Sheil, and he said it was a small matter. He said the salter had used a cheap salt, a damp one, and that the top layer was sound and the bottom was not. He said it was in about a quarter.”

“How did he know?”

“He’d known since October.”

Nobody spoke. Fenra’s slate scratched.

“It was the salter’s trick,” said the old woman. She did not raise her voice. “I found it when I had the tally sent for. Sheil paid for prime, in prime salt, and was given a barrel where the top three inches were prime and the rest was old fish in wet brine. He knew it by the first opened cask. He said nothing. He’d already taken his fee from the salter for not looking.” She tapped the slate. “I have the chandler’s receipts here. And the salter’s own note. It came yesterday, by Mistress Muirenn’s clerk.”

“It was a purchaser’s fraud,” said Saikou, very softly.

“It was,” said Brannoc. “I say so. It doesn’t help.”

“Why not?”

“Because I knew.” He lifted his head. “I’m not going to say I didn’t. I knew in April that a quarter of it was spoiled. I told myself it was Sheil’s fault, and it was. I told myself I’d make it good by the summer. And I knew, from that day, that what I’d promised for the Charter was not what I had.”

Muirenn said, “What had you promised?”

“The landing’s provision. Enough for your crews, for a year, and the household, and the hall.” His hands moved. “It was written. It’s in brown thread on the third clause. Two hundred barrels of fish, and grain, and oil. I’d stitched it myself.”

“And the fish was not there.”

“Not fit to give them.”

“Fenra.”

“A hundred and twelve I’d opened,” said Fenra. “Sixty-three of them bad. I’ve since had the rest done by the boy Ferg, and a lay-brother. Ninety barrels I’ll put my name to. Forty-seven are doubtful; I won’t promise those to the crews. Sixty-three are spoiled.”

Muirenn nodded slowly.

“That’s the loss,” she said. “I’d like the rest.”

***

He told it without adornment. Riarhin listened as she had listened to a hundred sergeants explain what had gone wrong with a supply-train. He did not make it worse than it was. He did not, she saw, make it better.

He had written to Whiterick in April. He had not sent it. He had held it a month, and the barrels had gone on being what they were, and he had gone on being a man with a wedding. When the witnesses were stopped on the moss, and Fenra was hurt, and the date was in danger, he had understood in one clear cold hour that the whole of it was about to fall in. He would stand up in the Hall with fewer than half the promised barrels fit for use and an injured landing-keeper and be asked to say what he had. He had gone to the Abbess.

“I went alone,” he said. “I rode all night. I said I’d a shortage. I said I’d need stores to last through the gathering, and that I’d not the money to buy them.” He swallowed. “She gave them to me. She asked nothing. She said it was the Abbey’s duty to feed a hungry country. And I said I’d pay.”

“How?”

“I pledged the dues of the second season.” His voice dropped. “Not the first. The first is for the company. I’d not have touched that. The second was mine. I said that, after the marriage, the Abbey should have first claim on the landing’s income for a season, before the boats or the crews or the hall. It was to be paid out of what the wedding brought.”

“And it’s written.”

“It’s written in the Abbess’s hand, and I signed it.”

“When did you mean to tell me?”

There was a long silence.

“I meant to tell you,” said Brannoc. “After.”

“After.”

“After the Hall. When the money was coming in.” Brannoc rubbed at a mark on the table. “I thought I could put it right before—”

“Before I found it?”

“Before it cost you anything.”

“The dues.”

“Yes, but I meant to replace them. I had Sheil’s word. There were still the market receipts, and if the first season—”

“Look at me.”

He did, briefly. His hand went back to the mark.

“I couldn’t send the carts away empty.”

“You could send them to me.”

He began to answer. The sound stopped in his throat. Fenra set her slate on the table, close enough for him to read what he had signed.

“The dues,” said Muirenn. “Say what that means.”

“It means—”

“I’d like Fenra.”

The old woman shifted. She looked at Muirenn, and the look was quite unafraid.

“It means the landing’s takings, after the wedding, go first to the Abbey,” she said. “Everything that comes in on the jetty. Every barrel, every crate, every boat’s fee. Before anything is paid to a crew. Before there’s a penny for oars or for pitch.” She tapped the slate. “Your boats are your own. They’re in the Charter in black. He’d never have given them up. But they work through the landing. They land at its jetty and sell at its stalls and their crews eat from its stores. If the landing’s first silver goes to Whiterick, then the first silver of your boats goes to Whiterick too, on the way through.”

“I’d be working for the Abbey.”

“In name, no. In fact.” Fenra shrugged. “I’ve seen it done to a smaller house than yours. It’s not a theft. It’s a slow lien.”

“A hold,” said Saikou, dryly.

“If you like.”

Muirenn turned round.

“You said you’d never sell my boats,” she said to Brannoc.

“I never would.”

“You didn’t have to.” Her voice had gone very quiet. “You only had to put a rope on the door.”

Riarhin put her cup down.

“I knew about the pledge at the steading. Before we brought the carts on.”

Muirenn turned to her. “How long before I knew?”

Riarhin gave her the day.

“And you signed for it.”

“For the stores. Yes.”

“You could have sent word.”

“I could.”

“Why didn’t you?”

“I wanted the people fed and the carts moving. I let telling you become something for afterwards.”

Muirenn looked at the cup Riarhin had set beside the scales.

“You’ve been asking me whether I understand what I’m paying for.”

“Yes.”

“Next time you’re told something about my boats, you bring it to me before you help anyone spend it.” She drew the slate towards herself. “Fenra. Put down the day she’s given us.”

***

They sent for Caelia at midnight. She came on foot, in her black habit and white cowl, with one lay-brother carrying a lantern, and stood in the doorway of the weighing-house with the cold coming off her, very old and upright and quite unafraid.

“I had thought you would send,” she said.

“Then you know what for.”

“I know that Brannoc has told you.” The light brown eyes went round the room, and stopped for a moment on the old woman in the corner, and moved on. “I should say first that I did not spoil the fish.”

“No one said you did.”

“Somebody will.” She came in and stood by the table, with her hands folded. “I supplied sound provisions. Oatmeal and oil and bread. Enough to feed the people stranded on the road and supply the Hall while replacement stocks were bought. It cost the Abbey a great deal. It was given in good faith to a man who came to me in real need, and I want that understood.” She turned her head. “I did not ask him to pledge the dues. He offered. I accepted, since it seemed to me just. I expect a real return.”

“Of course,” said Muirenn.

“Of course?”

“You’re an Abbess. You’ve a house to feed.” She said it flatly. “I don’t begrudge it. I don’t say you’ve done wrong. I say that you’ve put your hand on my boats without asking, by way of a man’s frightened promise, and I’d like you to take it off.”

“I’ll take it off,” said Caelia, “when it’s been paid.”

“I’ll pay it. I’ll pay it out of my own share of the first season and not out of the landing’s.” Muirenn leaned on the table. “You’ll have your grain and your oil and a fair rate for the risk. You won’t have the landing’s first silver. And you’ll not stand at the sealing as arbiter.”

“That is not part of the pledge.”

“It’s part of what you wanted at Whiterick. It’s in your letters.”

“It is a proposal.” The old woman’s voice did not change. “I made it in the spring. It was my hope that, with the pledge, and the Abbey’s support, and your marriage, you would see the sense of it. That the Abbey should hear disputes arising under the Charter. That it should be the court of the observances, where the Gray and the Temple and the Everflame quarrel over rites and burial, and the households have nowhere else to go.” She paused. “It is not a term. It is a thing I would like.”

“It’s a thing you’ve been arranging.”

“I have been arranging a great many things. For thirty-one years.” She said it quite calmly. “It is what I am for.”

“No,” said Muirenn.

“No?”

“I refuse it. I refused it at Whiterick and I refuse it now.” Her voice was steady. “I won’t be the woman whose marriage gave the Abbey a court. I’ll not sit for the rest of my life with every quarrel between me and my husband going to a bench of nuns. I’ll pay you every bit of it. I’ll pay you twice it. I’ll not do that.”

“The Abbey is not in the habit of being refused.”

“It’s been refused twice.” Muirenn looked at her. “By me.”

For a moment the two women faced each other across the table in the yellow light, the tall broad tar-stained one in her leather jerkin and the small upright old one in her black. Neither moved. It was the look of two people who have known for a long time exactly what the other is, and are not much surprised.

Caelia bowed her head, slightly.

“I see,” she said. “I would only say, Mistress, that a woman who is beholden to no one is a very lonely thing.”

“I know.”

“I hope you shall be happy.”

“So do I,” said Muirenn. “I’d like to be tried.”

***

It was Brannoc who said the last of it, and it was the worst.

He said it as they were going, in the doorway, with the lamp guttering and the old woman already on her hurdle. He said it eagerly, in a low, half-shamed voice, like a man clutching at a rope.

“There’s Donnan’s estate.”

She turned.

“When it’s—when it’s done. He’s a rich man. He has the Dunmarrow houses, and four hulls at Ash, and the stores at the Hall.” He wiped his mouth. “If it’s divided, and you’ve the greater part, then it’s money already ours. It’s only a matter of time. We might settle the Abbey out of that. It’d be no burden at all. It’s as good as in the chest.”

Nobody spoke. Riarhin felt the room go quite cold.

“Say that again,” said Muirenn.

“I only—”

“As good as in the chest.” She spoke very slowly. “You’ll count on my father’s death. In my hearing. In front of these people, who’ve done nothing yet.” A small hard breath. “It’s not money. It’s not ours. It’s my father’s house. It’s a thing I’m paying somebody to make it possible for me not to need.”

“I didn’t mean—”

“I know you didn’t.”

He was crying. It was a quiet, hopeless, open weeping, the tears going down into the beard, and he did not seem to notice it. She had seen a great many men cry. It was not an easy thing to watch in a man who was very large.

Muirenn looked at him for some time.

Then she turned to Riarhin.

“I want it stopped.”

Riarhin met her eyes.

“Not cancelled. I’m not saying cancelled.” The broad face was grey and very tired. “I’m saying stopped. Till it’s a marriage I’d want. If he dies now, and I’m bound to the Abbey and my husband’s a frightened man and the fleet’s working for Whiterick, then I’ve bought nothing. I’ve killed my father for a different cage.” She drew a long breath. “I’ll pay for the days. I’ll pay for the whole of the company, from today, at the rate we said.”

“You needn’t.”

“I know I needn’t. I want to.”

There was, in Riarhin, a small, hard, brief refusal. It rose in her without asking, quick as a spark, and she felt it go through her like a hunger. She had leaned so far. She had spent months getting these people to the Hall. She had counted on this crossing, this payment. She had been all but at the end.

And underneath it, more quietly, something else was glad.

She did not like either. She stood and looked at them both, one after the other, the angry thing and the relieved one, and thought that she would be a fool to trust a word either said.

“Stopped,” she said. “Not cancelled.”

“Yes.”

“Till a revised bargain exists. In writing, in front of a witness.”

“Yes.”

“Then I’ll say yes,” said Riarhin. “And I’ll say one thing more. If the price of a marriage is that a man must be killed, it seems to me it ought to be a marriage worth killing for. I’m not sure that yet. I’d like to be.”

Muirenn gave her a long look. Then, absurdly, she laughed. It was a short cracked sound, more breath than voice.

“That’s the most honest thing anyone’s said to me in a week,” she said.

“It’s not a comfortable one.”

“No,” said Muirenn. “I’d have been suspicious if it were.”

Chapter 22 — The Groom and the Father

Brannoc said it in public, at nine in the morning, standing on a bench under the third rib with his mother beside him.

Muirenn had asked for that and nothing else. She had not asked him to be brave, or to be sorry. She had said, at the door of the weighing-house, in a gruff level voice, that a thing done in the open could be answered in the open, and that if he wanted the Charter he would have to stand where the households could hear him. Then she had gone to bed. He had not.

By nine the north bay was full. Word goes through a fair like water through sand, and there were two hundred people round the bench in the smoky slanting light, with the ribs going up over them into the thatch and a man selling hot pies at the back who had stopped calling his wares. Eogan stood at one side with his slate. Caelia sat on a stool, quite upright, with her hands in her sleeves. Sile was against a pillar. Cera was beside her, a yard away, not looking.

He was very pale. He was wearing the mended coat.

“I’ve something to say about the landing at Rossan,” he said.

His voice shook once, and then held.

“I bought two hundred barrels of cured fish in the autumn from a salter at Dunmarrow. Sixty-three are spoiled, forty-seven are doubtful, and ninety are sound. I knew there was spoilage before we set out. I did not say so. When the party was delayed, I went to the Abbess and asked her for stores, and she gave them, and I pledged the landing’s dues for the second season after the wedding as her security. I did not tell Muirenn Varr. I meant to tell her after the Charter was sealed.” He drew a breath. “I want it known that this is so. I want it known that it was done by me, for reasons that seemed good to me and were not. Fenra of Rossan told me before we left that the stores were bad, and I did not listen. She is here. She is the reason you are hearing this. If you have any doubt of the landing’s word, you may ask her.”

Nobody spoke.

“It’s not a secret purpose,” said Brannoc. “There’s no plot in it. I was afraid, and I hid a thing, and I hoped that if it all went well, it wouldn’t matter.” He looked down at his hands. “That’s all it was. I’m sorry.”

He got down off the bench. He did it stiffly, like a big man who has been sitting a long time. His mother took his arm. She was a small stately woman in a black shawl, and she did not look at him. She looked straight ahead, at nothing, with her chin up, and her hand on his sleeve did not shake.

Lorcan had turned his back.

It was the only sound in the bay, the scrape of his boot on the floor. It went very slowly across the earth, and stopped, and he stood with his shoulders rigid and his face to the ribs.

“Now the rest,” said Muirenn.

She had come in from the aisle. She stood at the front of the crowd with her arms folded, in the leather jerkin, with the blue cloth in her hair.

“He’s said what he did. I’ll say what I want. The debt is the landing’s. It’s his and his house’s. Any part my crews pay must be agreed with me, in front of the Hall.” Her voice carried without being raised. “My boats are not security for it. They are not the pledge behind the pledge. If it’s ever said that Muirenn Varr’s fleet stands behind the landing at Rossan, it’s said falsely, and I’ll have the man who says it in front of the Hall.” She looked round. “I’ll marry him. I’ve not decided against that. I’ll say it when it’s a marriage between equals, and not before.”

“Is that a condition?” said Eogan.

“It’s a fact.” She uncrossed her arms. “Put it in the roll.”

***

She saw Donnan an hour later, at the gate.

He had come out of the scriptorium wing into the noon sun with Halla at his elbow, and he stood on the step with his hand on the head of a yellow dog, looking at nothing in particular. His barrel chest filled the coat, and the red of his close-cut beard still showed above the white at his jaw, and he wore a plain blue coat with a silver clasp. He had a way of standing that made a space round him. It was not that people were afraid. It was that they wished to be at the right distance to be looked at.

He turned his head and saw her, and smiled.

It was a very good smile. It went up to the eyes. It made him look ten years younger and ridiculously kind, and it was quite real, and she knew that it was, and she felt every muscle in her back go tight.

“Mistress Aine,” he said. “Muirenn’s friend. I’d hoped we should meet again before the Hall.”

“So had I.”

“I hear you’ve been walking my wedding.”

“It’s not yours.”

“It is in a sense. I’ve been to a great many.” He came down the step. The dog followed, grey-muzzled and very slow. “I’ve been told that there’s been a difficulty over some fish. I’m sorry. It’s a bad business. Poor Brannoc. I like the boy. He’s a fool, but I like him.” He shook his head. “To do it in front of everyone. That took something.”

“It did.”

“She’ll be hard on him.”

“She’s been fair.”

“That’s what I mean.” He laughed, softly. “It’s a great mercy in a daughter, and very hard on a man.” He looked at her with bright, quick, gentle eyes. “I wonder, would you walk with me a minute? I’ll not keep you. I’d only like to say something that I think you ought to hear, and I’d as soon it wasn’t in front of the boy.”

Halla did not move.

She stood two paces behind him with her thumbs in her belt. She was a square, weather-tanned woman of fifty with a nose that had been broken twice, and her eyes were on Riarhin’s hands.

“It’s all right,” said Donnan.

“Aye.”

“I’m an old man in a crowd. I’ll be quite safe.” He offered his arm. It was quite steady. “Mistress?”

She took it.

***

They went along the side of the Hall in the sun, where the wall was warm and a row of pigeons sat on the eaves. He walked slowly. He talked slowly, and pleasantly, in a voice like a good fireside.

“I’m going to make her an offer,” he said. “I’d like you to know it, since you’re her friend, and I’d be grateful if you’d tell her it’s meant kindly.”

“What offer?”

“The Abbess is a fine woman. I’ve known her forty years. I’d not say a word against her. But she’s a hard bargainer, and she’s got my girl in a corner, and I’ll not have it.” He tapped the dog’s head. “I’ll pay Whiterick. All of it, this week. I’ll send the money from Dunmarrow tomorrow and Muirenn will owe the Abbess nothing. Nor Brannoc. Nor the landing.”

“That’s generous.”

“It’s nothing. I’ve a great deal. I’ve nobody else.” A shadow of something crossed his face. “She’ll owe me nothing either. I want that clear. I’d not have her bought.”

“What do you ask?”

“Only what I asked before.” He said it lightly. “That her boats should sail under my mark on the mainland compact. Four years. She’ll be at their head, in the west, where she’s wanted. She’ll have her own command. She’ll have her own hull, and her crews, and a share of every prize.” He turned his fine old beaked face to her. “She’s the best I’ve ever had. She’s the best sailor I’ve ever known, and it’s a scandal she should be tied to a stone jetty by an Abbess. I’d give her the sea.”

“As much of it as she could carry.”

“Yes.” He looked pleased. “That’s the phrase. As much as she could carry.”

“And who decides how much?”

He did not answer at once.

They had come to the end of the wall. Beyond it the ground fell away to the lake, and the lake was blue beyond the reeds, and the wind smelled of wet leaves. He stood for a while with his hand on the stone.

“I do, I suppose,” he said. “For a time. Not for ever.” He said it very simply. “She’s young. She’s not seen the mainland. She doesn’t know what the queen’s people are like, or what they want, or how to talk to a Viterran governor who’s got his eye on a port. I do. I’ve been at it thirty years.” He looked at her, and it was, absurdly, an appeal. “I’ll tell her when it’s too much. That’s all. I’d be a bad father if I didn’t. She’ll come to see it.”

“She may not.”

“She will.” He said it with quiet, unshakable tenderness. “She’ll see it in her own time, and she’ll come to me, and it’ll be no shame. I’ve waited for it eleven years. I can wait for another.” His voice did not change. “I love her, you know. It’s the great fact of my life. It’s the only thing I’ve never had to arrange.”

She stood a moment. There was a pigeon on the eave, and the sun on the grey stone, and the old man’s hand on the yellow head. He means every word.

She thought of the man in the black-stitched jerkin at the head of the table in his own hall, paying a grieving family in front of a hundred men, and being loved for it. She thought that she was going to have to stand in front of that face, and do what she had been asked, and that there was no version of it in which he was not what he was.

“I’ll tell her,” she said.

“Thank you.”

“I’ll not say what she should do.”

“I’d not ask.” He smiled again, warm and quick. “You’re a good woman. I said so to Halla. I said she looks at a thing like someone who’s going to mind what happens to it. It’s rarer than you’d think.” He patted her hand. “Come and dine with me. I’ll have a fire lit.”

***

Muirenn heard it standing up, in the weighing-house, with her back to the great scales and her jaw set.

Riarhin told it plainly. She did not soften it. She said what Donnan had said, in his own order, and did not add a single opinion. She saw the broad face change as she spoke, very slowly, as if a hand were smoothing a piece of cloth. It went from anger to something else, and stayed there.

“That’s it,” said Muirenn, when she had done. “That’s the whole of it.”

“Yes.”

“He’ll pay the Abbess.”

“So he says.”

“He will. He’s never lied to me in his life.” She stared at the beam. “It’s the best offer I’ve ever had. It’s better than the last three. He’s—” She stopped. “He’s made it better. Every time. He’s made it so that I’ve nothing to say against it except that I don’t want it.”

“That’s not nothing.”

“It’s not a reason. It’s what I’d say if I were a child.” She turned. Her eyes were very bright. “Is it what you’d say?”

“I don’t know.”

“Do you want me to?”

“No.”

“You do,” said Muirenn. “It would be very much easier. He’d be dead in a week and I’d have the boats and the debt would be Whiterick’s to argue with a corpse. You’d be paid. You’d have your gathering. You’d be a step nearer whoever you’re nearer to.” Her mouth twisted. “I’m not so stupid as to think you hadn’t thought of it.”

“I thought of it.”

“And?”

“And I’d rather you decided it for another reason.”

“What reason?”

“Yours.”

Muirenn said nothing. She looked at the beam, and at her hands, and at the clerk’s bit of onion on the bench.

“I’ll say no,” she said, at last.

“Why?”

“Because he’s improved it.” It came out quite quietly. “That’s the whole of it. It’s the thing I said the first day, on the shingle. It’s not a better offer. It’s the same cage, with the bars gilded. I’d have the sea, and he’d tell me when it was too much. And I’d go on being his daughter, in the west, on his ships, under his mark, for four years, and then for ten. And he’d be so kind.” She drew a long breath. “I’d never be able to tell. I’d never know whether I’d been let or whether I’d done it. I’d not know what I was.”

“You’d be alive. And he’d be alive.”

“Yes.”

“That’s a great deal.”

“It is,” said Muirenn, gravely. “It’s the whole of the argument. I’m not going to pretend it isn’t.”

She went out. She did it quite slowly, with her big boots ringing on the flags, and did not look back. Riarhin stood alone in the shed, in the half-dark, among the great scales.

Either way, someone will pay for it.

She thought that she had known it, and that it was one thing to know it and another to stand in the middle.

***

Muirenn went, that afternoon, to the north bay.

She did not go to her father. She went to Brannoc, who was sitting on an upturned barrel by the second rib with his mother’s shawl over his knees, and she stood in front of him for a moment with her hands hanging.

“I’ve told him no,” she said.

“Told—”

“My father. I’ve told him no to the fleet and no to the mark. I’ve told him I’d take the Abbess’s terms, and pay them my own way, and be married when I’m ready.” Her voice was quite flat. “I don’t say I’ll marry you. I say I’ll go on with you. I’ll sit at a table and say what we’re to do.”

He looked up.

He had, she saw, the face of a man who has been given back something he had not dared to expect and cannot think how to hold it. He did not say anything for some time.

“I’ll not have the boats,” he said.

“No.”

“I thought I would. In a way.” He turned his big hands over. “I thought, if it were all done, it’d be a thing we had together. I’d not want to be a man who took. But I wanted it to be ours.” He swallowed. “It won’t be.”

“No.”

“It’ll be yours.”

“Yes,” said Muirenn. “And the landing’s yours. That’s what an alliance is.”

He looked at the ground for a while, then nodded.

“All right,” said Brannoc.

“All right.”

Riarhin watched from the shadow of the rib, her tea cooling in her hands. For a full minute they sat together while the fair went on around them. Then Muirenn went away.

***

Halla stopped her in the aisle at dusk.

She stood in the way. She did not raise her voice. She only planted herself, square and broad, in the middle of the flags, with her thumbs in her belt and her nose bent to one side, and looked at Riarhin for perhaps the space of ten breaths.

“He’d like you to dine.”

“I know.”

“He said it kindly.”

“I know that too.”

“I’ve been with him twenty-four years.” The voice was gruff and quite calm. “I’ve buried a good many men on his account. I’ve done things. I’ve no illusions.” She did not blink. “He’s not a good man. He’s a great one, in his way. It’s not the same.”

“No.”

“I don’t know who you are.” She tilted her head. “You’ve been at the girl’s elbow since spring, and you’ve a look I’ve seen. I don’t say what it is. I say I’ve known a good many soldiers.” She let it lie. “Nobody in this country would refuse what he’s offered her. Not one. It’s the best chance she’ll get. If she takes it, everybody’s safe. If she doesn’t, it’s a quarrel, and quarrels in this country are bad.” She drew a breath. “Your friend has said no.”

“Yes.”

“I’d as soon you told me that it was a good reason.”

“It’s hers.”

“Is it a good one?”

Riarhin looked at her. She was a hard tired woman with a broken nose, who had spent twenty-four years standing at a door.

“I think so,” she said.

“Then I’ll say this.” Halla stepped aside. “If he comes to any harm on my watch, I’ll not sleep again. I want it known.”

She went. She did not look back. Riarhin stood in the aisle among the drifting smoke, and the crowd went round her, and overhead in the dark the pale curved ribs of the beast rose into the thatch and were lost.

She thought that she had been asked, in one day, by three people, to say whether a thing was good. She had said I think so. It was not a phrase that she had used for a long time, and she found it was a good deal heavier than she remembered.

Chapter 23 — The Man Cadan Has Been Questioning

Tavin found the note in a laundry basket, and he brought it to her folded very small between two fingers, like a man carrying a moth.

“It’s not much,” he said. “I’d not have looked, only the boy was clumsy.”

It was the third evening since their arrival at the Hall. She was in the north bay, in the last of the light, with a sheet of Saikou’s figures on her knee. She took the paper and smoothed it flat. It was a torn strip of good cream stock, the sort the Hall’s clerks used, and it was written in a cramped hand that she knew: a hand she had seen on four letters that had come to her from Rossan and one from the Hall.

The plan is stopped. The bride will not have the Abbess’s terms nor the Red Oar’s. The Charter is not sealed. The company is still at the north bay. The mainland woman is her friend and is called Aine. There is talk of a hearing in three days. F.

Anyone in the Hall might have said as much beside the third rib. Every line was true.

“Where?”

“The scriptorium kitchen. There’s a yard behind, by the midden.” Tavin sat down on the edge of the trestle. “He was putting it in a basket. A boy, I’d say twelve, a scullion, with a dish-clout on his shoulder. I saw the hand go across. It was neat. He’s done it before.” He looked at the bird on his knee. “I’d not have taken it, but I’d recognised the coat. I’ve been watching that coat for a week.”

“Fergal.”

“Aye.”

“How long?”

“It’s not the first. He’d a way of coming in from the road with mud on his boots. I put it down to the causeway.” His face was pale and quite empty. “I didn’t say. It wasn’t mine.”

“No.”

“I’m saying it now.”

She sat with the strip in her fingers. She tried to find anger. Instead she read the familiar hand again, fitting it to the man who had carried her letters.

“Bring him,” she said. “Quietly. Amari will help. I don’t want it seen.”

***

They put him in the old smokehouse behind the kitchens.

It was a stone hut with a slate roof and a door that shut, cold and dark, with a floor of packed earth and a rack of long-empty hooks. Amari sat with him the first hour and brought him a blanket. Fergal said nothing. He sat in a corner with his knees drawn up and his cap in his hands, a thin grey-faced man of thirty-five with a bad chin, and when she came in he did not lift his head.

“I want to know what you’ve said,” she said.

He did not answer.

“Not why. I don’t need the why. I need what, and to whom, and how often.” She spoke quietly. “I need it exact. It’s the thing I need before I decide.”

“I’ve said nothing.”

“You’ve said what’s on that paper. It’s in your hand.”

He put his head on his knees.

She stood a moment. Then she turned and went out into the yard, where Cadan was waiting, with his hands in his sleeves and his head a little on one side.

“Brother,” she said. “I want you to find out from him what he’s passed on. All of it. Everything he’s told Donnan’s household, and everything they’ve told him, and who took it. I want it as complete as you can make it.”

“That is an instruction.”

“Yes.”

“May I ask how exact?”

“As exact as you like,” said Riarhin, and then, because she was tired and her mind was on the causeway and Muirenn and a marriage that was not a marriage: “Keep him till I’ve heard.”

She went in to supper.

***

She did not go back until midnight.

She had not meant to be so long. There had been Saikou, and a letter, and an argument with Berent about the sword. There had been a message from Eogan about the hearing. It was after eleven when she remembered, and went out across the yard, in the cold, with a lantern, and found the smokehouse door ajar and a candle burning inside.

It was very warm.

That was the first thing. There was a brazier in the middle of the floor, and someone had put a blanket round the man in the corner, and set a stool beside him, and a jug. The air was thick and close and smelled of charcoal. Fergal was sitting where she had left him. But he had changed. He sat quite upright, with the blanket to his chin, and his face was wet and open and quite empty, like the face of a child that has cried itself out. His eyes went to the door as she came in. They did not fix on her. They went past her, to the man on the stool.

Cadan sat with his legs crossed and his hands in his lap. He was pouring water from the jug into a horn cup. He did it slowly, carefully, without spilling.

“Thank you,” said Fergal.

“Not at all.”

“It’s very kind.”

“You are very cold.” Cadan held the cup out. “Drink it slowly.”

Fergal drank. His hands shook so that the water ran down his chin. When he had done he gave the cup back, and looked at the man before him with an expression of absolute trust.

“It’s been a long time,” he said. “Hasn’t it, Brother?”

“It has been six hours.”

“It felt longer.”

“That is often so.” Cadan set the cup down. “Shall we go on?”

“Yes. Please.”

“You were telling me about the kitchen boy. His name.”

“Cob. His name’s Cob. He’s Piran’s nephew. Piran’s the steward. It was—it was two marks each time. Four times. I told him about the stores, and the delay, and the witnesses. I told him Fenra was hurt. I—” His voice broke. “I didn’t know about the other. I swear. I didn’t know. Nobody said.”

“I know you did not.”

“It’s the truth.”

“I believe it.”

She had not moved from the door. She stood with the lantern in her hand and watched it.

It was so quiet. There was nothing in it. Cadan sat with his narrow face composed and his voice soft and level as a hand on a fevered forehead, and did not raise it, and did not threaten. She saw the blanket, and the brazier, and the jug. She saw that all three of them had been given, not before, but after: she saw the way the man’s eyes went to the cup as it was filled, and the way the light went out of his face when it was set down. She saw that in six hours Fergal had learned exactly one thing, and that it was where the warmth came from.

“I gave him the blanket at nine,” said Cadan, without turning. “He had not answered the second question. He answered the third.” He turned his head. “I gave him water at ten. He is a good deal more comfortable. I think we have very nearly all of it. It will be complete by morning.”

“What have you asked?”

“The same question. Every half hour. It seems to be very effective.”

“Has he been hurt?”

“No.” He seemed faintly surprised. “I have not touched him.”

“Has he been allowed to leave?”

“You said to keep him.”

“Has he been given any water except from you?”

“He has not asked for any.” Cadan said it mildly. “It is the beauty of the thing. He has no wish to go anywhere. He would not, I think, leave if the door were open.” He smiled, gently, at the man in the blanket. “It is a very sound method. It takes some patience, but it is all in the arrangement. The warmth arrives through one person. The water. The prospect of being let go. He will tell me anything, and he will be quite well afterwards, and he will not remember that he was ever afraid.”

“Get out,” said Riarhin.

He looked up.

“Now. Go and stand in the yard.” She heard her voice. It was quite quiet. “I’ll speak to you when I’ve finished.”

“I have not finished.”

“You have.”

He studied her. There was on his face nothing but a kind of courteous, attentive puzzlement, and after a moment he inclined his head, and rose, and went out through the door without touching her. He shut it carefully behind him.

She was alone with Fergal.

***

She put the lantern on the floor. She knelt.

He was shaking again. She saw it start at his hands, and go up the arms, and into the jaw, and she saw his eyes come round to her, wide and swimming, and fill with something that was not trust.

He was afraid of her.

She knew it, all at once, with an entire clarity, in the smoke-smelling dark. He had been afraid of Cadan for an hour and had loved him for five. And he was afraid of her. It was in the way he flinched from her hand, and drew the blanket up, and did not once look at her face. She was the person who had said keep him. She was the person who had turned and left. She was the source of the whole arrangement, and he was quite right.

“Fergal,” she said.

“I’ve told him.”

“I know.”

“I’ve told him everything. I’ll tell you. I’ll tell anyone.” The words came in a rush. “I’ll not say a word more about the—I’ll say what you like. I’ll say it was the Abbess. I’ll say it was Brannoc. Please. I’ve a wife at Rossan. I’ve two.”

“Fergal.”

“Please.”

“I’m not going to hurt you.”

He stared.

“I’m not,” she said. “I want you to hear it. Nobody is. I’ve not asked for any of this, and I’ll not have it done. I’m going to give you some water. It’s from the same jug, and it is only water.” She took the cup. Her hands were steady. She filled it, and drank a mouthful herself, slowly, and gave it to him. “You can have as much as you like. You can have the blanket. You don’t need to say anything.”

He held the cup in both hands. He did not drink.

“Do you want to know what I need?”

“Yes.”

“I need to know what you’ve told Donnan’s household, so that I know what they know. That’s all. I need it accurate. If you tell me a thing to please me it’ll be no use, and I’ll find out, and it’ll be worse.” She said it quite flatly. “I can’t make you. I could make it frightening not to. I know how it’s done. I’ve seen it, in another place, and I know what it does.” She stopped. She had not meant to say that. “I’m not going to. I’m going to ask, and you’ll tell me what you like, and I’ll check it. If it doesn’t check, I’ll know. That’s all.”

“And then?”

“Then you’ll go somewhere safe.”

“Where?”

“I don’t know yet.” It was the truth. “Somewhere you’ll be looked after. I’ll not have you where he can reach you.”

“Who?”

“Cadan. Halla. Me.”

He looked at her for a long time. He was very white. Then, in a small hoarse voice, as if the words were being drawn out of a well, he began to say what he had done.

***

It was not a great deal.

It came out in an hour, quietly, and it did not change. He had sold four small things to a scullion named Cob at the back door of the scriptorium kitchen. He had done it because he had a wife at Rossan, and another at Killen he had not told the first about, and two children, and the master’s wages were not what they had been. He had begun at the Hall. It was the first night, after the stores came in. A man in a blue coat had been at the yard, in the dusk, and had asked him if he wanted a drink. It was not Donnan. It was not Halla. It was somebody who called himself Piran’s friend.

He had told them what he saw. The stores. The Abbess. The quarrel. That the company was stopped. He did not know why. He had not known that there was an other, that they had come to do anything but escort a bride’s baggage. He had thought they were the Abbey’s people. He had thought it was money.

“You didn’t know about the death.”

“I swear.”

“Who told them my name?”

“I did.” He shut his eyes. “I said Aine. It’s what she was called. I didn’t know it was—”

“It’s not.”

He looked at her.

“It’s not my name,” said Riarhin. “It’s a thing I said at a well. I didn’t say it to you.” She sat back on her heels. “Where’s the man in the blue coat?”

“At the second table. The north end. He has a scar.”

“I’ll look.”

“It’s the truth.”

“I know. It’s checkable.” She rose. “That’s the best sort.”

Tavin was waiting outside. She sent him, in a low voice, to find the scullion, and a man with a scar in a blue coat, and the linen store where the note was to be left. He was gone in three minutes. She did not ask him how he would do it.

Amari came out of the dark.

“He’ll not be safe here,” he said.

“No.”

“If the boy talks, Halla’ll know. If Halla knows, she’ll ask where he’s gone. If she asks, Donnan’ll hear the messenger was taken.” He rubbed his neck. “It’ll cost us. We’ve had a quiet week. We’ll not have it after.”

“I know.”

“Then why—”
