“Because I’m not going to keep him.” She spoke without heat. “I’ll not keep a man in a smokehouse. I’ll not have it said that we did. I’d rather it cost.”

“Aye,” said Amari. He seemed to think about it. “I’d have said the same, if I’d been quick enough.”

***

It was Sile who took him in.

She did it at two in the morning, at the door of the Gray Liturgy’s lodging, a low stone shed behind the pillars with a lamp in the window and grey thread hung over the lintel. She came to the door in her night-shift with a shawl over it and her hair in a plait, and looked at the man in the blanket, and at Riarhin, and at the dark yard behind, and did not ask a single thing.

“He needs somewhere,” said Riarhin. “For a few days. Where nobody’ll touch him. He’s done a foolish thing, and he’s afraid.”

“Is he in danger?”

“From me, a little. From some others, more.”

“From you.” Sile’s broad flat face did not change. “That’s honest.”

“It’s what I have.”

“And you’d like me to keep him.”

“If you’ll agree.”

“It’s a Confessor’s guest-right. It’s not a thing I’m asked for often.” She stood a moment. “It’ll be known. There’s nobody on the Hall’s floor who doesn’t know when I’ve someone under the thread. It’ll be said that the company took a man and put him with the Grays.” She looked at Riarhin. “It’ll cost you your quiet.”

“I know.”

“Do you?”

“Yes.”

“Then I’ll do it.” She stepped back, and held the door. “Bring him in. Don’t tell me anything I’ll have to hold. I’m not the person for it. He’ll sleep, and be fed, and I’ll say the office over him if he wants.” She put her hand on the man’s arm, quite briefly, as she had done to Orla. “And he’ll go where he likes. I’ll not keep him past his wish.”

Fergal went in. He did it without looking back. The door closed, and the lamp in the window burned on.

“Thank you,” said Riarhin.

“Don’t.” Sile drew her shawl round her. “You’d have done it yourself, in the end. You’d only have done it slower.” She paused. “The other one. The Brother.”

“Yes?”

“Is he to be kept?”

“I don’t know.”

“You’ll want to be sure,” said Sile, “what you’re keeping him for.”

***

She found Cadan in the yard, where she had left him, standing still in the frost with his hands in his sleeves.

He had not moved. She thought that he might have been standing there since midnight, as one stands at the door of a room, without impatience, prepared to be called. His breath went up in a small white plume. He looked round as she came and bowed, courteously, from the neck.

“I am sorry,” he said. “I do not think that I have done what you wished.”

“No.”

“I should like to understand it.”

“I know you would.” She stopped in front of him. She was aware that she had never before spoken to him without wanting something. “I told you to keep him. I didn’t say how. I didn’t say what I meant by it. That’s mine. I want you to hear it. You chose how to do it. I gave you leave and walked away. I’m not putting my part on you.”

“It is a good method.”

“He offered me Brannoc’s name. Then the Abbess’s. He was trying to find one I would accept.”

“One checks the answers.”

“You made relief depend on pleasing you. I sent you in for what he knew.”

“I could have corrected that.”

“After he’d learned it?”

Cadan considered her. “It would take longer.”

“Yes,” she said. “It would.”

“He was afraid of me, Cadan.” She heard her own voice go quiet. “Not of you. He loved you. He’d have said whatever you wanted. And he was afraid of me, because I’m the reason for it. I wanted an accurate account, and I knew what I was asking, and I didn’t say. I let you do it. I’d not thought about what it meant. I’d only thought that it was quick.” She paused. “I’d like to say that it’s not what I wanted. But I’d not be telling you the truth. It was quite near.”

“You did not ask for a demonstration.”

“No. I didn’t ask for anything. That’s the difficulty.”

He was silent. The frost glittered on the stones between them. He looked, in the lantern-light, not at all displeased, and not at all convinced.

“So you will not use it,” he said.

“I’ll not have anyone held. Not by you. Not by anyone in the company, unless it’s Amari or myself, and said aloud, in words, at a table, so that everyone knows how.” She met his eyes. “Not water. Not warmth. Not the door. If a man’s to be kept, he’s to be kept as a man, and he’s to be told what’ll happen. It’s not a method. It’s a rule.”

“That is exact.”

“Yes.”

“Thank you.” He bowed. “I shall be glad to have it. I said that I should like the instructions to be clear.” He tilted his head. “I am to understand that I am not to have charge of captives.”

“Not any. Not ever.”

“And the rest?”

“The rest stays. The Hall’s watched. I need you on the doors.”

“I see.” He smiled, with real warmth, and it was not a smile of surrender or of shame. It was the smile of a man who had been given a plain fence, and was pleased to know where it stood. “I am sorry that you are troubled. I will do what is asked.”

She stood in the frosted yard, and felt no better at all.

In the north bay, Tavin whistled softly from the shadow of the second rib. He had found the man in the blue coat.

Chapter 24 — Nobody Else’s Death

The man in the blue coat was a Viterran clerk in a rented colour, and he confessed to nothing, because nobody asked him.

Tavin had followed him for a day and reported it in the north bay in a low voice, with the bird on his knee and his eyes on the roof. The clerk had bought his supper. He had gone to the Temple table and spoken to Sister Cera for ten minutes about the register of the dead. He had gone back to the scriptorium yard and stood a while under the wall. He had a scar on his jaw and a pleasant tired face, and he had at no time looked at the north bay or at anyone in it. It was Piran’s doing, then, or the Red Oar’s, or nobody’s; a small man buying small facts at a fair, because that was what a fair was for.

“It’s nothing,” said Tavin. “It’s ordinary. Everybody’s selling something to everyone.”

“Fergal wasn’t.”

“Fergal was selling to the wrong person.” He shrugged. “It’s the same in my trade. You learn who’s listening. It’s a poor thing when the answer is everyone.”

“And Cob?”

“Back at the dishes. Piran’s had him carrying things since he could reach the latch. A basket goes out, a penny comes back.” Tavin turned the bird’s foot gently to free a caught feather. “He asked if he was in trouble.”

“What did you say?”

“That I wasn’t his uncle.”

“Leave him to his dishes. No fetching him out to answer for the men who paid him.”

Tavin nodded. “He’ll like you better than his uncle.”

That was the state of the Hall when she called them together on the fourth night, after the Charter’s hearing had been adjourned to the seventh, and Muirenn’s revised terms had gone to the steward under Whiterick’s seal and Eogan’s pleased signature. The marriage was still under discussion, and a man in a blue coat was buying pies. She had five days before the eve of the sealing. She had eight people, and a great stone room with a single door, and a man in it with a yellow dog.

They sat in the north bay, on trestles, in a ring, with a lamp between them and the beast’s pale ribs going up into the dark. She had asked Berent to sit at the entrance to the aisle. He had laid Na’ar’s sword across his knees. He had not said why. Nobody asked.

“I’ll say what we have,” said Riarhin. “And then I want each of you to say what you think. All of it.”

“Even me?” said Tavin.

“Especially.”

***

She laid it out plainly. It took a quarter of an hour, and she did not embellish.

Donnan had moved. He was in the old scriptorium, at the back of the Everflame’s wing, in a stone room with a single inward-opening door and a window onto the garden. Halla had put a bench outside the door and slept on it. The dog lay inside. There was a stair to the roof on one side and no way from the roof into the room. The household ate together in the Hall at nine each evening, under the fourth rib, and Donnan ate with them, and was seen. He drank nothing but small ale and the occasional cup of hot wine, and Heber, who was not a fool, tasted neither. He went to bed at eleven. He was alone at that hour for perhaps five minutes, between Halla’s last look at the door and her first at the bench.

The time was narrower than it had been. There was the eve of the sealing, five days off, when the households would dine together in the nave for the last time before the Charter. It was the one night of the week on which the doors of the wing stood open and no one thought it strange to see a stranger in the passage. After that there was only the morning of the sealing, in front of the Hall, where he would stand on the steps in a blue coat, and be at his most protected, and most seen.

“Aurine,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Say what you can.”

The girl did not move. She sat upright at the end of the trestle with her hands round a cup.

“I’ve been buying thyme,” she said. “From Heber’s stall. Since we arrived.” Her tone did not change. “He’s begun to say good morning. The dog is fond of me. It has no taste at all.” She looked at the lamp. “I’ll say no more than that. I don’t propose to tell you how. I’ve said I’ll do it and I will. But it’s not a thing I can do by design. It’s a matter of a small door I’ve been keeping open, and it may close, and it may not open on the day. I’m not a woman who can promise a night.”

“That’s honest.”

“Ask me after I’ve seen him tomorrow.” Aurine set the cup down.

“Cadan,” Riarhin said. “You had something to say.”

***

“I did,” said Cadan. “I hope I may say it quite freely.”

“Please.”

“It seems to me that the difficulty is one of aftermath.” He had risen. He stood with his hands folded, in the lamp’s circle, in the attitude of a man giving a lecture. “We have a man in a room, with a dog and a captain and a practitioner. If he dies, they will know. Halla will be at the door in a moment. Heber will be at his side. They will say what they saw, and the household will be very much in earnest, and there will be an inquiry. The Hall will hold it. It will be unpleasant.” He inclined his head. “I should like it not to be.”

“How?”

“By making it simpler. If the captain and the practitioner were not there, there would be no one to say what was seen. The household would be without its memory. They would know that he had died. They would have no captain to press the claim and no practitioner to say what had happened. We could leave before anyone thought to look.” He said it earnestly. “I do not propose it lightly. I have thought about it since we arrived. It seems to me that a cleaner death would be a smaller one. Three, perhaps. It is a very small increase.”

“Three.”

“Halla. Heber. And the boy on the curtain, if there is one. I understand there is not.”

Nobody spoke.

She had known Halla’s name since Donnan’s supper at Scarrow. She had heard it said in a gruff level voice in an aisle, with the smoke drifting between them. If he comes to any harm on my watch, I’ll not sleep again. She saw the flat weathered face and the nose broken twice and the thumbs in the belt. She had, she realised, been given a picture of a person, and it did not go away.

“No,” she said.

“May I ask why?”

“Because I know her name.”

“It is a consideration,” said Cadan. “I do not see that it is a reason.”

“I know.”

“Then I will say what I think.” He turned, quite politely, to the ring. “I do not mean to be unkind. But the aim is to complete a commission with the least disturbance. If we accept one death, we have accepted that we are people who cause them. It is a matter of scale.”

“That’s not all of it,” said Berent.

“Let him finish,” said Riarhin.

***

It was Tavin who spoke first, and it was not what she expected.

“It’s a bad idea,” he said. He had put the bird on the trestle. He sat with his hands between his knees, looking at the lamp, and his face had none of its usual brightness. “I’ll say it as a business. I’ve no pity in me, I’d like that understood. I’m not made that way. I don’t care whether Halla’s got a mother.”

“Then what?”

“I care what happens after.” He held up one finger. “One man in a room, a private matter. It’s a death in the night, a sad thing, the old man’s heart. He was near sixty. He’d had a hard year. Nobody’s surprised. There’s an inquest by a brehon, and a burial, and a daughter.” A second finger. “Three in a room. It’s a massacre. It’s a matter for the Hall. There’s a hue and cry, and a Temple inquest, and every soul in the place with a boot on a door. They’ll not stop at the north bay. They’ll go through every cart in the Hall, and the roads, and the coast.” He lowered his hand. “I’ve done it both ways, Brother. It’s a poor bargain. It takes a week of your life for every extra body.”

“You do not think it could be arranged?”

“I think it could be arranged very neatly, and I’d be found in a ditch in Ash in a month.”

There was a small dry sound. It was Aurine, at the end of the trestle, and she was laughing, silently, with her mouth pressed shut.

“I beg your pardon,” she said. “It’s just that I’ve been sitting here a quarter of an hour trying to think of a way to say it, and you’ve said it in a manner I didn’t expect.”

“Say the rest.”

“It isn’t only that.” She set down her cup. “Brother Cadan says it would be selective. That a thing done quietly, in the dark, in a cup or a dish, would spare the innocent. I’d like it noted that I don’t accept it.” Her voice was even. “A poison is not more precise because it’s subtle. It’s only more difficult to stop. If I’m to put a thing in a household’s food, I’m putting it in a household’s food. I don’t control who eats what. A child at the table. A guest. A woman who has a cup she’s not supposed to have.” She did not look at him. “I’ll not call a thing selective because it’s quiet. It’s the loudest thing I know.”

“You are being very scrupulous,” said Cadan, with interest.

“I’m being accurate.”

“It is not,” he said, “a great difference.”

“It is to the child.”

He considered. He appeared genuinely to do so. Then he nodded, once, with a small courteous bow of the head, as one who has been shown a flaw in a design.

***

The stick scraped at the mouth of the aisle.

“There’s something he hasn’t said,” Berent said. “About leaving her.”

Riarhin turned. He had drawn the stick back between his feet; Na’ar’s sword still lay across his knees.

“In Istovar we escorted a miller’s cart through a quarrel that had lasted longer than I’d been alive. Six men for a cart of meal. His son sat on the sacks with a bow. The boy couldn’t draw it.” He worked his thumb beneath the sword-belt, freeing a fold caught under the buckle. “One house had a dead man. The other had paid what the hearing ordered. It hadn’t bought the dead man’s brother. He waited three years and took a shepherd. The shepherd’s people had a claim then.”

“Halla knows who killed whom,” said Tavin. “So would her brother, if she has one.”

“I know. I’m saying she can put men in boats. She can keep them at it. If we leave her with that and a dead lord, whose children do they fetch off a beach ten years from now?”

“Their lord is still alive,” said Riarhin.

“Aye.” He looked at her. “We mean to change that.”

Cadan inclined his head towards him.

“You understand the difficulty.”

“Don’t thank me, Brother. I’ve sat across from her too.” Berent shifted the blade on his knees. “Heber could give her the finding she needs. Halla could raise the men. If killing them ended it, instead of leaving it to people who haven’t been born yet—I’d have to count that. I think you would too.”

“Who says it ends there?” said Amari.

“Nobody. Nobody can.” His voice had risen; he lowered it. “But there’s nobody here to speak for the shepherd either. We get into a boat and go.”

Riarhin had a question ready: whether he would do it himself. She kept it. He could scarcely rise from the bench, and the question would let her hide behind that.

“It’s still asking Aurine to kill them,” she said.

“Yes.”

He waited. She had wanted him to take something back.

***

Saikou had been sitting at the far side of the ring. He had not spoken all evening. Now he unfolded his long hands.

“I oppose it,” he said. “I should like that plainly on the record. I oppose the expansion, on all grounds. I do not think that it is practical, which is Tavin’s ground; and I do not think it is accurate, which is Aurine’s.” He paused. “I think it is exactly the kind of proposal that arises when one has agreed to a small thing, and begins to see the size of it.”

“Saikou—”

“I have been in this business a good while. I have advised on several campaigns. I know the thing. It is the drift.” His voice was dry and tired. “You begin with a man. You find that there is a wall. You say, if there were no wall. And then there is a captain, and a physician, and a cook. It is very reasonable at every step.” He looked at the lamp. “I would not have it here.”

“It’ll make it harder,” said Amari.

“It will.”

“It’s a good deal harder. If we’re to leave the captain and the old man alone, it’s one night and five minutes. It’s a door that may not open.”

“I know.”

“You’re sure?”

“I am aware that I cannot hire away every inconvenience,” said Saikou, “and then say a word against those who must endure it.” He turned his head, and for a moment the tired long face was quite open. “I have paid for this company. I have sat in a warm room and said that a man should die. I should like, at the least, to be the person who says it must be hard.”

There was a silence.

Amari let out a breath. “All right,” he said. “I’ll say what I think, then. I’ll not pretend I think it’s all the same.” He looked at Riarhin. “I was with a captain once who’d have liked to make it simpler. He always did. I’d say that a plan that can be done by one person on one night is worth six that need to be done by ten.” He rubbed his eyes. “And I’d say the risk’s not the same for all. It’s greater on her side. I’m not going to say it isn’t. But it’s the plan I can carry out.”

“Thank you.”

“It’s only honest.”

Berent had been listening from the mouth of the aisle. He got up, with an effort, and came to the edge of the circle, on the stick, with the sword in the crook of his arm.

“I’ll say what I can do,” he said.

“Berent—”

“I’ll say it.” His face was drawn. “I can sit at a door. I can stand at the head of the passage on the night and look as though I’ve a right to be there, with a sword, and say no to a man with a tray. I can do that for as long as it’s wanted.” He drew a breath. “I can’t do the stair. I can’t go up a roof. I’ll not pretend I can. If there’s a thing that wants a running man, it’s not me.”

“That’s what I needed.”

“I know it is.”

He sat down again. He did it heavily, like a very old man, and laid the blade across his knees, and did not look at anyone.

***

She said it standing, when they had all done.

She had thought about it for four days. She had thought that she would want a great deal to say, and found now that there was very little. She stood in the lamplight with her hands at her sides and looked at them: at Amari’s tired square face, and Aurine’s narrow dark one, and Tavin’s, and Saikou’s, and Nessa’s, sitting quiet with her hands in her lap. She looked at Cadan.

“Halla and Heber are not to be touched,” she said. “Nor the boy, if there is one. Nor the dog. They’re in the way, and we’ll go round them. If we can’t, we don’t do it.”

“That is very clear,” said Cadan.

“It’s not a principle. I’m not saying it’s better.” She shook her head. “I don’t know that it’s better. It’s only what I’m going to do.”

“And the plan?” said Amari.

“One man. One night. The eve of the sealing.” She turned to Aurine. “You’ve a door. It may open. I can’t make it, and I’ll not ask you to hurry it.” She hesitated. “If it doesn’t, we’ll say so, and we’ll take the morning. I’ll not do a worse thing to save a night.”

“And if that fails?”

“Then I’ll go to Muirenn and tell her I’ve not done it.”

“She’ll be angry.”

“She’ll be alive.” She let out a breath. “I’m not going to be a person who can’t say that.”

Cadan bowed.

“Then I shall walk the doors,” he said.

***

They dispersed slowly, in ones and twos, with a shuffle of feet and the small noises of people who did not know what else to say. She sat on with the lamp for some time. Berent had gone to his blanket. Amari had gone to the yard, and Saikou to his papers. It grew quiet.

There was a sound from the shadow of the second rib.

It was a voice, low and true and a little husky, singing without words. She knew it at once. It was Nessa’s, in the dark, alone, a plain slow tune that went up and turned and came down, very simply, like a cradle-song, or a thing one sings at a well. It went on for perhaps a minute. She had not heard it before.

Then it stopped.

It did not end. It stopped, in the middle of a phrase, as one stops on a stair. There was a silence. Then a small puzzled sound, the sound of a person who has put her hand out for a thing that ought to be there.

“Nessa?”

“Yes.”

“I didn’t mean to listen.”

“No, it’s quite all right.” She came out of the shadow. She had a shawl over her shoulders, and her hair, in the lamplight, showed white at the temple in two long streaks. She smiled. It was a small tired smile, quite unembarrassed. “I’ve lost the last line. I’ve been trying to find it for an hour.”

“What is it?”

“It’s a song for a door. My mother sang it. I’d it whole till a year ago.” She sat on the trestle, and drew up her knees, and looked at her hands. “It’s not so bad. It’s only that when you’re at it long enough, you find that things have gone. It’s not always the big ones.” She said it lightly. “I’d a whole street, once. I could tell you every house. I went to look for it in the spring, and it wasn’t there.”

Riarhin said nothing. She thought of a girl at the Cormorant with a hedge she had not finished.

“The road I left by was the one I needed,” said Nessa. She picked at a loose thread in the shawl. “I’d take it again. I didn’t know the song would go with it.”

Riarhin waited.

“It would be a comfort to have somebody who kept it.”

“Kept what?”

“Whatever it was.” She laughed, a little. “You’ve a great memory. Amari says so. You never lose a thing, do you?”

“No.”

“Would you—” She stopped. “No. It’s foolish.”

“Say it.”

“Would you remember my name?” She said it lightly, not looking up. “Not the one I sing under. The one my mother used. Nessa Vey goes round with the work. This one I’ve kept to myself. It’s a thing I’d like to know was in one place.”

“What is it?”

She told her. It was a short plain name, of two syllables. Riarhin kept it with the sound of Nessa saying it, and waited.

“There,” said Nessa. “Thank you.”

“It isn’t a great thing.”

“It is to me.” She rose. She drew the shawl round her, and smiled, and there was in her face a quiet, unfrightened weariness that Riarhin knew, very well, from mirrors. “I’ll finish it in the morning. It’s usually easier by daylight.”

She went out through the aisle, into the drifting smoke. Riarhin stood alone by the lamp, with the name in her head like a stone she had been handed to keep.

“Only when I ask,” Nessa said from the aisle.

Riarhin nodded. She could still hear Na’ar telling her to move the cart. She would keep this too, and wait to be asked.

Interlude VI — The Conventional Answer

Aryka · Autumn

The bridge at Orsk had cost her eleven months, and she was going to give it away in an afternoon.

She stood on the wall above it in the flat light with her gloves in her hand and looked at the river. It ran wide and brown under seven arches, carrying the rain of three provinces down to the sea, and on the low green ridge above the approach road, where there had been nothing on Monday, the Viterran camp was already smoking. She could count the fires. It was not the small army that had taken Cann. It was the other one, the one that had been coming since the spring, with wagons and a hundred and forty engines, and a queen in the middle of it who did not yet know how much of the country she was standing in.

“It is the whole of the main force,” said Bessa Marrow, at her elbow. She said it flatly. She had grown thinner through the campaign and her hands were raw, and she had a way of speaking now that was not deference and not quite anything else. “I’ve had it from three sides. There’s a river-scout who counted the standards.”

“How many?”

“Sixty-one.”

“Say what you want to say.”

“I want to say that we can’t hold it.”

She had known, and she was quite sure that Bessa had known she had known. They stood in the wind for a little. The bridge below them was very fine. She had had it strengthened at the piers and the parapets, and there were four hundred men on it and behind it and in the tower, and a good third of what remained of her stores in the vaults of the toll-house. It was the only bridge for forty miles. She had promised herself, the previous November, that she would keep it if she had to burn the country behind.

“Tell me what you have,” she said.

Bessa told her. It took four minutes, and she did not waste a word. There were sixty-one standards, and a hundred engines on the ridge by tomorrow noon. The ford at Karre, three miles up, was passable if the rain held off, and would carry the foot and the wagons. If the whole force went by the ford tonight, it could be on the road to the hills by dawn, with the stores and the three hundred families who had come in from the villages. The barges would leave separately, downriver under the far bank, while the wagons crossed. If it stayed to hold the bridge, it would be battered from the ridge in the morning, and the ford would be under fire by dusk, and the last of the reserve would be lost with it.

“You’re saying give it up.”

“I’m saying that if you keep it, you’ll keep it a day, and lose four thousand people. If you give it up, you lose the bridge.”

“And the bridge matters.”

“It’s the only one.” Bessa did not look away. “I know what it is to you. I’d not say it was small.”

Aryka did not answer at once. She looked at the seven arches and the brown water. She had walked every yard of that span, in the January frost, before the invasion, with a mason and a lamp. She had put her own hand on the keystone of the third. She thought it a very ordinary piece of engineering, and she loved it like a child.

She weighed it again. The same answer.

“Do it,” she said. “Tonight. All of it. Put hooded lanterns on the far bank to mark the ford. No fires on the approach. Break the third and fifth arch at dusk, so they’re slow.” She turned. “I’ll say the rest in the yard.”

***

She said it in the yard, to eleven officers, in the rain.

They had come in from the walls with wet cloaks and hard faces, and stood in a ring on the cobbles under the tower with the wind whipping the torches. They were good men. She had picked most of them. She said it plainly: the bridge was to be given up, the ford was to be used, and the whole of the force would be over it by first light. Eleven people drew a long breath.

“Ma’am,” said Sir Halvard Teague.

He had come up from the ditch, where he had been for three days with the horse, and he stood in the light of the nearest torch with his beautiful voice a little roughened and his grey moustache dripping. He was, she thought, the only man in the yard who could have said it. He had lost a good deal in the spring, and taken it, and he had grown very solid in the summer, in a way she rather admired.

“I should like to say a word.”

“Say it.”

“It is a fine thing,” said Teague. He looked round, and his voice went out to the ring. “It is the finest I have seen. We have drawn the whole of her army to the river. She is at Orsk with sixty-one standards and a hundred engines and no bridge, and we have the ford. When she comes over it, we shall be in the hills. It is the oldest trick in the book.” He drew himself up. “She will follow. She must. And on the third day she will find that she has been drawn out beyond her lines, across broken ground, in the wet, with her stores forty miles behind. I say that we have not lost a bridge. We have laid a snare.”

There was a murmur. It was a hopeful sound, and it ran round the yard.

“Sir Halvard,” said Aryka.

“Ma’am?”

“No.”

He stopped.

“I am very grateful.” She said it quite clearly, without heat. “I know what you are doing. It’s kind, and it’s generous, and it would make the night a good deal easier. But it’s not what this is.” She looked at the ring. “We are giving up a bridge because we cannot hold it. We’re losing a river because she is stronger than we are. I’ve not laid a snare. I’ve not thought of one. There is no trap in the hills.”

“Ma’am—”

“If I let it be said that there was, then by Thursday there would be a hundred men who believed it and looked for the trap, and it would not be there, and they would begin to wonder what else I had not told them.” Her voice did not rise. “It’s a loss. It’s a very great one. I’ll not pay myself that compliment.”

Nobody spoke.

“But I’ll say what is true,” said Aryka. “We have four thousand people, sixty barges, and the greater part of our stores. The people and wagons are going over the ford tonight. The barges will go downriver. By morning I want an officer’s account from each. That’s not a victory. It’s not a small thing either. It’s what we have left, and it will be enough to be going on with.” She looked at Bessa. “Mistress Marrow will have the ford. You will do what she says.”

There was a pause. Teague bowed, quite stiffly, and stepped back into the ring. She saw him look at Bessa. It was a hard look, without hostility, the look of a man who has been very much hurt and is deciding, again, to be an officer.

“As you order,” he said.

***

She went up on the wall at midnight, alone, with a lantern, to say goodbye to it.

It was raining hard. The river ran black below, and on the ridge above the approach a hundred fires burned like a town. She could hear the drovers on the far side of the tower getting the wagons down the ramp. She could hear Bessa’s voice, sharp and plain, saying left, left, left. Upstream, the hooded lamps showed briefly as men passed in front of them. She could hear many people moving through water. Below her, the broken ends of the third and fifth arches stood clear of the current; an hour earlier she had felt their fall through the soles of her boots.

She had been told she would lose it, in a great many ways, and had built it anyway.

She was not a clever woman, she thought. She was a woman who knew, on the whole, what was going to happen, and who could tell when somebody had said a true thing. She had a great deal of talent for choosing people, and it had not stopped her from losing a bridge. She thought that she would go on losing them for some time. She thought that the way to go on was to do it in order, with a sensible number of people alive at the end.

She thought of her niece under the green banner. She had once been glad to see that face at a family table. She did not think of her in hatred. She thought of her as one thinks of weather.

I’ll have it back, she thought.

She did not say it aloud. She had said it once, on the bank at Cann, and the river had not answered. She stood in the rain until the last wagon was over. Then she went down, and put out her lantern, and left the bridge to the dark.

Chapter 25 — Other People’s Departures

The letters from Eilis’s coast came on the fifth morning, in Deirdre’s canvas bag, along with a pair of boots and a jar of pickled walnuts that nobody had ordered.

She read them in the north bay standing up, with the smoke drifting between the ribs and a boy going by with a tray of hot rolls. There were three. The first was in Eilis’s square black hand and was very short. It said that Mara had spoken to her on the Sunday, in the kitchen, in front of the fire, and that they had come to an arrangement. Mara would stay at the house through the season. She would be paid for it, at a rate she and Eilis had agreed. She would do the visits she was trained for: the women near their time, the children with fevers, an old man at the top of the lane who had been bedridden for a year. She did not wish to be spoken of as anyone’s nurse. She says to tell you that she is not staying for you. And then, below, in the same hand, quite without punctuation: I have put salt in the bag.

The second was from Mara herself, on a torn sheet, in a plain slanting script.

I am well. Eilis is well. The cook’s youngest has learned to whistle and is impossible. I said I would stay and I have. I don’t know why I am writing except that you will worry.

I’ll want the money, and I’ll want it said what it is for. I don’t want anything else. Don’t send me a present. Don’t write to say that you are sorry. I’ll say when I am ready to have that said.

Tell Berent that the leg is not to be tested with a horse. I know how he is.

The third was from Maelis, and it was on good paper, with a pressed flower.

The players have offered me the workroom. It has a window and a door that locks. I have a commission for eleven gowns for the spring cycle. They have asked if I will be there through the following season and I have said I will consider it. I have not said yes. I should like to be a woman who has not said yes.

She read them twice. Then she folded them and put them in her sleeve.

She did not write to say that she was sorry. She wrote, that afternoon, in the small hard hand she used for accounts, a brief letter of instruction to Deirdre’s clerk, releasing the wages Mara had agreed and the outstanding cost of the cart and bedding at Eilis’s. She began a third line for Maelis, then crossed it out. The players were paying for their own gowns. She wrote to wish her well with the workroom.

“Do you want to add something?” said Saikou, who was sealing it.

“No.”

“It is very brief.”

“It is what she asked.”

“Ah,” said Saikou. He set the wax. “That is a great rarity.”

When she took the empty bag back, Deirdre’s man caught her by the sleeve. There was another slip tucked into the seam, folded round a bit of waxed thread. He waited while she opened it.

The woman is still in the same wing. The person who carried her food no longer goes in alone. Questions have been asked about who sends the books. There is no answer yet from the University. Please send nothing further through this hand until you hear again.

Below, in a narrower script:

She asked whether the second volume had been found. I could not stay to hear the rest.

Riarhin turned it over. No date. The thread had bitten a groove through the fold.

“How long?”

“It’s been in my bag since the coast. Before that I couldn’t say.”

“Who gave—”

He took his hand off her sleeve.

She stopped. “No. You needn’t tell me.”

The boy with the rolls came through again. This time she bought two. She gave one to the carrier and stood with the other going cold in her hand while he checked the bag for anything else.

She could write a question now and wait weeks for it to reach a person who had asked her to stop. She could offer money. She knew how quickly money made a frightened servant noticeable.

“Is there an answer?” he asked.

“Only that it’s understood.”

After he had gone she broke the roll. Steam still rose from the middle. She had eaten breakfast, and somebody had stood over Lyra while her food was brought in. The slip gave her no sentence of Lyra’s to hold against it.

She put it inside the earlier request for the grammar, where the two folds met. Then she went to find Berent.

***

The hearing on their seventh day at the Hall was held in the nave under the whale, with three brehons on a bench and two hundred people standing, and it took most of the day.

A very old brehon with a walnut face said yes and no and continue. Each time a clause was laid aside, the stitcher put her needle down. Brannoc kept looking at the unfinished places in the linen.

Fenra was called in the afternoon.

They brought her in on a chair carried by two lay-brothers, with her leg on a stool and her slate on her knee and her grey shawl over her head. She sat in front of the bench, very small and very upright. She had been ill since the causeway, and looked it. She looked, thought Riarhin, like a woman who had been waiting all her life for somebody to ask her a question.

“Fenra of Rossan,” said Eogan. “You are the keeper of the landing.”

“I am.”

“You have kept it thirty years.”

“Thirty-one in the spring.”

“Will you say what the landing owes?”

“I will.” She did not consult the slate. “Under the Charter as first stitched, Rossan was to supply the household and the boats of the bride with two hundred barrels of fish, sixty of grain, and forty of oil, in the first year, and to hold the jetty and its stalls at a fixed rent for four. That was the promise.” She spoke in the plain dry voice of a person reading from a page she had written herself. “It could not be kept. We have now examined all two hundred. Ninety are sound, forty-seven doubtful, and sixty-three spoiled. I say so under oath. I have the receipts.”

“And the new pledge?”

“The master pledged the dues of the second season to the Abbey, as security for its stores. That’s a matter of record. I’ve seen the paper. It was fair, and the Abbey’s to have.” She stopped. “I was not asked to witness it. I should have been.”

“You have heard the proposal,” said Eogan, carefully, “that the shortage might be made good from the unassigned property of the late—of Master Donnan Varr’s house, in the event of any change in its circumstances.”

There was a small stir. It was quiet. It ran through the bay like a breath.

“I’ve heard it.”

“Can you attest that it can be so made up?”

“No.”

“Can you say it can’t?”

“I can say that I do not know what Master Donnan has, and that the landing has never had any claim on it.” Fenra folded her hands. “It isn’t assigned. It isn’t ours. A man who is alive has his own property, and a man who isn’t has his heirs. I’ll not say a word about what they’ll have.” She turned her old pale face towards the bench. “I’ll not certify what I can’t count. It’s my whole trade.”

There was a silence.

Then Donnan, who was sitting in the front row on a bench with his yellow dog at his feet, said quite gently, in his warm fireside voice, “She’s right.”

Everyone looked round.

“It’s not assigned,” said the Red Oar. “I’ve not made a will. I’ve nothing to make one of. It’d be a great presumption in anyone to say what I’d leave.” He smiled, at Fenra, kindly. “I’d thank the Hall to write it down. I’m not dead yet.”

There was a ripple of laughter. It went round the bay and died. Muirenn, at the side, did not laugh. She was looking at the floor. She looked, for the first time in a week, as though she were quite alone in a very large room.

Riarhin watched her.

She thought that she had never seen a man so plainly enjoy being the reason for someone’s pain. She thought, in the same instant, that he had not meant it so. That was the trouble with him. It was always exactly what he meant, and it was always kind.

***

They brought up Ket and Daire at the end of the afternoon, and it was Riarhin who did it.

She stood. She had not meant to. She rose from her place at the back, with the company round her, and said in a clear plain voice that the company had a matter to put.

“Mistress?” said Eogan.

“There was a man died on the causeway. Daire of Killen. A drover, in Brannoc’s service. There’s a boy who was hurt, a teamster called Ket, with a broken shoulder and a leg. They were doing the household’s work when it happened.” She heard her voice go out into the nave. “I want them in the Charter.”

“They are in the roll,” said Eogan. “I entered the death myself.”

“I know. It’s not what I mean.”

“What do you mean?”

“I mean that Daire had a sister at Killen with four children, and a third of what he earned went to her. That’s stopped.” She looked at the bench. “I mean that Ket’s not going to be lifting a barrel till the spring. They’re not a name in a roll. They’re a household. They need provision. It should be made, in the Charter, out of the landing’s first season, before anything else. I’d like it said.”

There was a short pause.

“Is that a term?” said the walnut-faced brehon. It was the first thing he had said in an hour.

“It is if you make it one.”

“Ah.” He seemed to consider. “Who’s to pay it?”

“The landing.” It was Muirenn. She had got to her feet, at the side, with her arms folded. “And the boats. We’ll split it. It’s a debt.” She looked at Brannoc. “Isn’t it?”

He had risen, too. He looked at her with dumb astonished gratitude.

“It is,” he said.

“Then say what.”

“A half of Daire’s wages for as long as the sister’s children are under twelve. And Ket kept on the roll at full pay till he’s fit.” His voice was rough. “It’s no more than we ought.”

“Write it,” said the old man.

Eogan wrote.

It took ten minutes. When it was done, Caelia, who had sat throughout in the second row with her hands folded, said in her level unhurried voice that the Abbey would add a sack of oatmeal a month to the household at Killen for a year, out of its own stores, and would ask for nothing in return. She said it without looking at anyone. Riarhin thought that it was the kindest and least political thing the Abbess had done since they met, and that the old woman knew it, and was rather embarrassed.

She sat down.

She found that her hands were shaking. She put them between her knees.

“That was well done,” said Amari, beside her.

“It’s only what was owed.”

“Aye.” He didn’t look at her. “That’s the whole of it. Most people don’t get it.”

***

The revised bargain was read at dusk.

The revised agreement was smaller than the first, and plainer. The Abbey’s claim was acknowledged. There was no dispute about it. Whiterick had sent bread and oil in a hard week, at some cost, in good faith, and would be repaid, over three seasons, out of the landing’s dues beginning in the second season, in a fixed proportion that was written down and could not exceed a stated sum. The Abbey would hold no lien on the boats. It would have no place at the sealing but that of a guest. It would arbitrate no dispute under the Charter and have no voice in the observances of the households.

“The Abbey declines,” said Caelia, when she was asked, “to insist.”

“You’re content?”

“I am not content. I am solvent.” She said it with dry composure. “I have thought about it a good deal. A landing that cannot pay its crews cannot pay me. A marriage that falls on the sealing-day harms the Abbey more than one which stands on less. I asked for a court. I had not expected to be asked for a receipt.” She looked at Muirenn, and there was, in the old light-brown eyes, a very faint gleam. “You are a great deal more expensive than I had hoped.”

“I’m told it’s a family trait.”

“It appears to be.”

Brannoc surrendered a fixed portion of three seasons’ landing income, beginning with the second. It was a large portion, and neither of them said anything about it. The crews’ subsistence was to come first, before any payment to the Abbey or the landing. And Saikou, when the clerk had finished reading, rose from the bench at the back of the bay and said that he would like, if it were not an intrusion, to put in a small thing.

“Master Ira?”

“The crossing.” He looked, in the smoky light, very tired and very courteous. “It is understood that the company is to be taken, at the end, by Mistress Muirenn’s boats to a mainland vessel. It is a fee that was to have been paid out of the commission. I should like, since the commission is at the moment in a state of some uncertainty, to advance a portion of it. The half. Out of my travelling credit from home.” He paused. “I do not propose to buy the marriage. I only propose that the boats should be paid for the work they will do.”

Muirenn stared at him.

“You’ve no need.”

“I have every need. I have a great deal of money that is not mine and a very strong wish to have spent some of it on something honest.” He sat down. “It is a small thing. I hope you will accept it.”

“I’ll accept it,” said Muirenn. Her voice was gruff. “I’ll write you a receipt.”

“I should like that,” said Saikou, “above all.”

***

She gave the gift away that evening, in the yard behind the Everflame’s wing, in the last of the light.

She had thought about it for a month. It had sat on the sheath of the Toothpick since the loft at Coracle Harbor: a small red thread knotted round a bit of tin, a very small thing, easily overlooked. It had not done anything she could name. Tavin had said once, in passing, that it was not a thing that did. It was a thing that was passed on. It came from a man who was said to be the luckiest in the world, and it would stay lucky only for as long as it did not stay.

The mare stood at the hitching-rail with her head low. She had been brought up from the steading that morning, with the boy Ket, who sat on a bench by the wall with his arm in a sling and his face very white. He was watching Riarhin. He did not know why.

“Ket.”

“Yes, Mistress.”

“You’ll be going home, I think. To Killen. When the Hall’s done.”

“Yes. To my mam’s. I’ll take her.” He nodded at the mare. “Sorcha’s to have her. It’s what he’d’ve wanted.”

“Yes.”

She drew the small thing from the sheath. It came away quite easily. She held it in her palm for a moment, red and small and warm from her own body, and she looked over her shoulder.

Tavin was standing at the corner of the wing. He had not been asked. He stood with the magpie on his shoulder and his hands in his pockets, and he watched her, and after a moment he nodded.

She tied it to the mare’s halter.

She did it with her left hand, slowly, in a plain knot, at the ring where the strap went under the jaw. The mare turned her head and breathed on her wrist. It was a warm, damp, very ordinary breath. It smelt of oats.

“It’s a gift,” said Riarhin. “It’s not a thing that’ll save her. It’s a traveller’s token. You can refuse it.”

“Is it lucky?”

“I’m told so.”

“Who from?”

“From a man I never met.” She stepped back. “It’s not to be kept. If you meet somebody who’s going a long way, and hasn’t much, you pass it on. That’s the only rule.”

He looked at the red thread. He was eighteen, and thin, and very pale, and he touched it with the tips of two fingers, as a boy touches a sleeping animal.

“I’ll take it,” he said. “And I’ll remember.”

Riarhin stepped back from the halter.

The magpie flew down. She landed on the halter, on the ring, on the very knot, and put her head on one side, and looked at the bit of tin with one bright black eye. She did not take it. She sat there for the space of three breaths, and then flew back to Tavin’s shoulder, and settled, and shut her eye.

“Well,” said Tavin, in a curious, cracked voice. “That’s that, then.”

***

Muirenn came to her at dark.

She had no lantern. She stood in the yard with her arms at her sides, in the leather jerkin, with the blue cloth tied in her hair, and the last of the western light lay along her jaw.

“It’s poorer,” she said.

“Yes.”

“It’s a good deal poorer than it was. I’ve given up a third of what I’d hoped, and the Abbey’s got its grain, and Brannoc has lost a stone in a week.” She let out a breath. “I’m not the woman I was on the quay. She wanted a great deal. I’ve found I can do with less.”

“That’s not a bad thing.”

“It’s not a good one.” She laughed shortly. “I’ll not say I’m glad.”

“No.”

“But it’s written. It’s in front of the Hall.” She looked at Riarhin. “It’s the marriage I’ll have, and I’d as soon know what it is. I’d as soon be able to see the walls.”

“Yes.”

“Then I’ll say it.” She drew herself up. “It’s begun again. All of it. I’d like it done as we said.”

“The same?”

“The same.” Her voice was steady. “The one man. Before the sealing. I’ll name it to you tomorrow, in a room, in front of the others. I’ll not hide behind the Abbess. I’ll say it plainly.”

“I’ll want it plain.”

“You’ll have it.”

She turned and walked away across the yard, into the dusk, with her big boots ringing on the stones. And Riarhin stood a while where she was, with the token gone from her sleeve and the smell of oats in the air, and thought that she had never felt so light, or so entirely at a loss for what she was going to do next.

Chapter 26 — The Commission Renewed

Muirenn said it in the weighing-house the next evening, two evenings before the sealing, with the great scales hanging over her, and she did not lower her voice.

There were seven of them, as before, and one more. Riarhin had asked for the room, and Muirenn had said yes without asking why. The clerk’s onion had gone. Someone had swept the floor. The lamp burned on the table and beyond its ring the corners were dark, and in the silence between the words a fly went round the beam.

“My father,” said Muirenn. “Donnan Varr. I want him dead before the Charter’s sealed. I’ll pay for it. I’ve said what for, and I’ll say it again to anyone who asks.” She stood with her feet apart and her arms at her sides. “I’ve not put it on the Abbess. I’ve not put it on Brannoc. It’s mine.”

“It is a very clear statement,” said Saikou.

“It was meant to be.”

Brannoc rose from his stool. He looked, she thought, a stone lighter, and a great deal older. He did not look at Muirenn.

“I take my share,” he said. “I was told the price of the marriage. I knew it before I asked. I’ll not say I didn’t.” He drew a breath. “I’ll not say I’m a man who hasn’t thought about what it means. I’ve thought of nothing else. But I know what I asked, and she’s not to be left alone with it.”

“You needn’t.”

“I want to.”

“Then that’s two,” said Muirenn. She did not smile.

Caelia had come. She sat in a corner on a stool with her habit gathered round her and her hands in her lap, and she had not spoken until now. She looked older by the hour, and quite composed.

“No writ of the Abbey will name this undertaking,” she said. “I say it for the record. I will say it again, if I am asked, before any court in the country.” She turned her face towards the dark end of the room, where Aurine sat by the wall with her hands folded. “I say also that I know what has been arranged, and by whom, and that it is not a thing that I approve. I have no quarrel with the person who does it. I have given her bread.” Her voice was dry. “I have contributed to the company as I said. I will not retract it.”

“Your money is here,” said Riarhin. “You know what it buys.”

“Yes.” Caelia looked at the lay-brother waiting beyond the door. “My writ is not.”

Riarhin left the place for the Abbey’s name unfilled. Beside it she wrote the amount Caelia had contributed.

Saikou was the last.

He sat with his long hands folded and his eyes on the lamp. He had grown thin in the fortnight, and the skin under his eyes was violet. He had said the most against it, in the north bay, and paid the most to make it hard, and he was the only person in the room who looked, as she looked at him, as if he would rather have been somewhere else.

“I agree,” he said. “I should like that written.”

“You needn’t be here.”

“I know.” He looked up. “I shall be uneasy. I think that I should like to be uneasy in the room.”

“That’s what I thought,” said Riarhin.

***

Aurine told her afterwards, in the empty nave, under the third rib, with the last of the hall’s lamps guttering in their brackets.

It was very late. The households had gone to their beds. There was a lay-brother asleep at the foot of a pillar with a broom across his knees. She stood beside the girl in the shadow of the beast’s ribs, with her arms crossed, and did not look at her.

“Something’s changed.”

“What?”

“Heber.” Aurine’s voice was low. “He came to the stall this morning. He’s asked me to help.”

“Help.”

“There’s a woman in the Temple’s tent, near her time. Her last child came badly. He wants a second pair of trained hands close by, and the Temple’s sister has no midwifery.” Her hands were quite still. “He said he’d heard I was a person who knew what to do with a difficult night. He’s asked me to take tomorrow evening with him, from the hour of the household’s supper. We may be there all night for nothing. We may not.”

“Tomorrow is the eve.”

“Yes.”

“That’s the night.”

“Yes.” She looked up. “It’s not a thing I arranged. I want you to know that. I did not ask for it. It’s an honest request, from an honest man, and I think that if I said no he’d think the less of me.” She paused. “It’s the door I’ve kept for a week. It has opened. And it opens in a place I didn’t expect, and I cannot say where it leads.”

“Meaning?”

“He keeps his case in the room next to Donnan’s. He wants me to fetch it before I come to the tent. He’s told Piran to admit me and give me whatever linen I need.”

Riarhin remembered the room from the day she had waited for Halla: the case beside a tray, a little blue cup turned upside down on its cloth. Donnan had come in for the cup himself, dismissing the feast’s heavy silver with a complaint about its taste. He had carried it back towards his bed.

“The household will know you.”

“They already do. Tomorrow they’ll have been told to let me work.” Aurine looked towards the sleeping lay-brother. “Your people have kept the passage clear for our visits all week. I can go back through it on his business. Whether I get more than that, I won’t know until I’m there.” She drew a breath. “I don’t know. I can tell you where I’ll go. I can’t promise what I’ll find when the door opens.”

“That’s all?”

“If the household sends someone with me, I fetch his case and leave. The woman in the tent still needs us.”

Riarhin stood a long time.

Heber had brought his own case when they first met him. Riarhin remembered the care with which he had shut it after showing Aurine something inside. He would be waiting in the tent while she used his name at the household door.

“If they send someone with you, leave it,” she said.

Aurine nodded.

“And if they don’t?”

Riarhin looked at the lay-brother asleep beside his broom.

“Go on.”

Aurine drew her scarf closer and went towards the lodging. Riarhin stayed until the lay-brother woke and asked whether he had missed the bell.

***

The next day, on the eve of the intended sealing, the last gathering before the marriage was held in the nave under the whale, at dusk, and Donnan Varr was the life of it.

She had not expected that. She had expected, if she had thought at all, to sit through it as one sits through a long grey wait before a door, watching the clock. It did not go so. The tables ran the whole length of the hall, and the lamps were lit in every bracket, and a hundred and fifty people sat down together under the pale vault. There were the households of the Charter, and the brehons, and the Temple’s green cloth and the Abbey’s white. There were three harpers. There was a roast ox.

Berent had found a place at the end of a bench where he could stretch his leg. Beside him a grizzled man with a scar running into his collar was disputing the age of a horse.

“Ask Cormac,” someone called from farther down. “He’s had the Bishop twelve years and the beast’s still nine.”

“Eleven,” Cormac said.

“Which?”

“The horse. Mind your plate.”

Berent laughed. Riarhin had nearly passed them when Cormac shifted along to let him rest his heel on the bench’s crosspiece. He did it without interrupting his account of the Bishop’s soundness.

“Off fore too?” Berent asked.

“Sound as the rest of him.”

“I had a grey mare with a swelling there. You’d never have known it to watch her walk.”

“Then you should have watched her trot.”

“I did.”

Cormac looked at him again. He pushed the jug within reach and asked whose farrier Berent had used. Riarhin left them comparing men neither would trust near a hoof.

Donnan sat at the head of the second table with his dog under his chair. He had the blue coat on. He was in extraordinary form. He told the story of how he had lost a boat to a very small bull. He did it with his hands, with his broad weathered face alive and his red-and-white beard shaking, and the table roared. He asked after the old brehon’s knees. He said something to Cera about the sun that made her, to her evident dismay, laugh out loud.

It was Muirenn who caught her eye. She was sitting three places along, at the end, with Brannoc at her elbow, and she had not eaten. She looked, in the yellow light, tired. She sat with her big hands flat on the board and watched her father with an expression that Riarhin knew, that she had seen on many faces, and could not put a name to.

“Muirenn,” said Donnan, across the table.

“Da.”

“Do you know what I found in the byre this morning?”

“What?”

“The hen you named.”

“Which?”

“The one you named for the Abbess.” He beamed, and put his hands round an imaginary neck. “It’s been sitting on a clutch of duck-eggs for a fortnight. It’s very serious. It hasn’t said anything. But it has the look of a woman who’s been told a thing is a marriage, and has decided to see it through.”

There was a great burst of laughter. It ran up and down the table, and Caelia, at the far end, put her hand over her mouth. Brannoc turned pink. And Muirenn, who had been sitting like a rock, put her head down on her arm and laughed until she cried.

It went on for a good while. It was a helpless, shaken, cracked sound, more breath than voice, and it stopped, and started again. She lifted her face at last. It was wet. She was looking at her father, and he was looking at her, and for a moment there was in the whole of the great smoky hall no one but the two of them, a big weathered woman and an old man in a blue coat, sharing a thing that neither had expected to find funny.

“That’s a good one,” she said, unsteadily.

“It’s true.”

“It’s not.”

“It’s a little bit true.” He reached across, and wiped her cheek with his thumb. It was a very old, plain, tender gesture. “I’d not have you cry, girl. Not tonight.”

Riarhin looked away.

Riarhin looked at her plate. Muirenn was wiping her face with the heel of her hand, still laughing. Across the room Aurine waited for Heber to rise. Riarhin could call her back. She watched Muirenn take her father’s hand off her cheek and hold it for a moment before letting go.

***

They went about their work.

She watched them do it. She did not have to look. Berent had gone to the head of the wing passage with the stick and Na’ar’s sword, and stood there very upright, in a plain brown coat, with the look of a man who had a right to be. He said no to a boy with a tray. He said it kindly, and the boy went away. Cadan walked the doors. He had a way of walking that made him look like a monk who has lost his way to chapel. Amari sat in the corner of the kitchen yard with a mug of ale and a wooden spoon, and looked like the sort of person no one sees. Tavin was in the pantry passage with Penny on his shoulder, arguing amiably with a cook about the price of pepper. Nessa sang at the head of the hall. She had been asked by the harpers to sing a verse, and she gave it, low and true, a plain slow song about a door. At the turn where she had lost the words, she held the note and let the harper take the tune. He brought it to a close. She thanked him with a nod before sitting.

Saikou sat beside Caelia, with his hands folded, and talked to her about barley. It was the bravest thing she had seen him do.

And the household went on living. It was that which she could not get past. The steward laughed at something Halla said. Halla, at the door of the wing, had put her thumbs in her belt and was looking round at the roast ox with the cheerful, ruminative air of a woman who has come to the end of a long watch. A little boy under a table fed a bone to the dog. Cob, the scullion, ran across the flags with a jug and slid and caught himself and grinned. Heber, at the end of the second table, said something quietly to Aurine, who was standing beside him, with her sleeves rolled and her plain dark hair covered by a scarf. He put his hand on her arm. They went out together, through the north door, towards the Temple’s tents, and the door swung shut behind them.

She did not follow. It was not hers to follow.

***

It was past midnight when Aurine came back.

She came in through the north door with her hands quite clean and her sleeves down, and the cold on her, and her face very white and set. She stood a moment in the hall with the lamps burning low, and then she came across the flags to the north bay, where Riarhin was sitting on a trestle with a cup gone cold in her hand.

“The woman’s alive,” she said. “The child’s alive. It was a breech. It was a bad hour. Heber did a great deal. I did what I could.” Her voice was steady and very low. “He’s asleep on a bench. He’ll sleep till noon.”

“And the other?”

“It is done. Piran let me into the rooms. He sent the boy for linen and went back to the supper.”

She sat on the trestle a little away, with her hands in her lap. “I brought Heber his case. He thanked me. Then the child began to come.”

“I don’t know how it will go,” she said. “I said I wouldn’t. It might be quick. It might be a week. It might be nothing. I’ve told you that it isn’t a thing I can promise.” She was silent. “I’ll say only that it was done as a person does a thing she’s good at, in a place where she was trusted. It’s not a rite. There was nothing from Her.”

“From Isoka.”

“She’s not sent a word.” Aurine turned the little bone charm over in her fingers. It lay in her palm like a piece of grey grit. “I asked, afterwards. Not for a sign. Only to be told if there was a thing I ought to know. I’d have taken a warning.” She gave a short, dry laugh. “She’s a very fine goddess. She’s not a great one for a fuss.”

“No.”

“It’s mine, then.”

“It’s ours.”

“It’s mine,” said Aurine. “I’d like it said. I’ll not have it carried by a person who didn’t do it.” She put the charm back in her bag. She sat a moment with her head bent. “But I’ll take the company, if you offer it.”

“I offer it.”

“Then thank you.”

She rose. She walked away through the north bay towards her blanket, in the dim light, a small straight figure with her scarf in her hand.

Riarhin sat where she was.

The hall was nearly silent. Somewhere at the far end a harp was being put away in a case. The lamps burned low, and above her the great pale ribs went up and up into the smoke. Donnan’s daughter was asleep a hundred feet away, with Brannoc’s hand in hers. The Red Oar was asleep in a stone room, with a yellow dog on the floor by his bed, and Halla on a bench outside the door.

Aurine had waited for her answer in the nave. Riarhin could hear how promptly she had given it.

She sat on until the lay-brother came to put out the lamps.

Part Five — One Season

Chapter 27 — Donnan’s Last Illness

Halla woke her at six, by standing at the foot of her blanket with a lantern and saying nothing until she opened her eyes.

She knew before the woman spoke. She had known, she thought, since she lay down. She sat up slowly in the grey of the north bay with the dew still cold on the beams and the beast’s ribs going up dark above her, and looked at the broken nose and the leather jerkin and the two square hands hanging at her sides.

“He’s ill,” said Halla.

“Ill?”

“He woke at four. He’d a pain, and a sweat, and he couldn’t keep a cup down.” She said it flatly. “Heber’s with him. He says it’s not the belly. He doesn’t say what it is. I’ve never seen him not say.” A pause. “Will you come?”

“Why me?”

“Because the girl’s asked for you. Muirenn.” She turned the lantern. “She said, get Aine. I don’t know why. She’s not said a word besides.”

Riarhin got up. She put on her boots. She did it slowly, with great care, one lace and then the other, and found that her hands did not shake. It was the strangest thing. She had thought they would.

***

The old scriptorium was warm and very small, and it smelled of sickness and lamp-oil and dog.

They had lit every candle. They stood on the sills, and on a table with a jug, and on a shelf of books that nobody had moved in years. Donnan lay on a narrow bed against the far wall with the blue coat still on a chair, and his broad weathered face gone the colour of tallow. He had a wet cloth on his forehead. His hands lay outside the blanket, the fingers moving slightly, as if they were counting.

Heber sat beside him. He had his scarf off. Without it he looked older, and his long grey face was set and quiet and quite unlike the thyme-seller’s. He had a bowl of water on his knee and a set of small worn instruments on the coverlet, and he was looking at the old man’s eyes.

Muirenn was on the other side. She sat on the floor with her back to the wall and her father’s hand in both of hers. She did not look up when Riarhin came in. She looked, thought Riarhin, like a woman who had been sitting in the same position for a very long time and had forgotten there was any other.

“He’s asking for the dog,” she said, in a low flat voice.

“The dog’s here.”

“I know.”

The yellow beast lay on the floor against the door, with her head on her paws, and did not raise it. Her eyes were open. She did not seem to be watching anything.

Riarhin stood in the doorway. She did not go in. There was room for six people in the chamber and there were already four, and Halla behind her, and it seemed to her that any more would be a kind of theft. She stood with her back against the jamb and her hands at her sides, and watched a man try to save another man’s life.

***

Heber worked. He did it quietly, in small exact motions, without haste and without display. He held the old man’s wrist. He looked in his mouth. He put his ear to his chest and listened for a long time, and sat back, and frowned. He asked questions, in a low even voice, of Halla, of the boy, of a serving-woman who had come in with fresh water. What had he eaten? What had he drunk? Who had been with him? When did he first feel it? He wrote nothing down. He had a very good memory.

“It’s the same as the household’s,” said Halla. “We all had the ox. We had the hot wine. He’d nothing I didn’t.”

“Nothing else?”

“Bread. A bit of cheese. He’d a cup of ale before bed, from the jug on the sill.”

“I drank that jug at ten,” said Heber. “It was quite sound.”

His own cup stood beside the jug. The little blue one was on the floor by Donnan’s bed, empty, its rim against the stone. Riarhin could see the pale ring where it had stood on the bedside board.

Heber went on looking at the old man’s face. “There’s a pain, and a coldness, and a thing in the pulse that I don’t like. It is not a fever. It is not the heart, or not the heart alone. It has a kind of—” He stopped. “I can’t put my hand on it.”

“What can you do?”

“Warm him. Keep him drinking, if he can. Watch.” He said it very evenly. “I have done what I know. I have a draught that helps in some cases and does harm in others, and I do not know which this is. I will not give it in the dark.”

“Is he going to die?”

He did not answer at once. He looked, for the first time, up at the woman in the door, and then, briefly, at the two people on the other side of the bed, and away.

“I do not know,” he said. “I would say that he may.”

There was a silence. Muirenn’s head lifted. She said nothing. She held her father’s hand.

Riarhin stood still.

She saw the hands on the coverlet, moving. She saw the small worn knife lying on the wool, and Heber’s long capable fingers, and the tired grey scarf on the chair. She saw that he was doing the whole of what could be done. She had not known that a thing could be so plainly good and still be of no use.

I could stop it. She did not know what she meant. There was no knowledge in her of what she would have to do. There was only the small clear fact of the idea, standing in the room like a stranger. She looked at it, and it looked at her, and she let it stand.

Halla had not moved. She said, in a low voice, to nobody:

“Get me Sile.”

***

Sile came at eight.

She came in without a word, in her plain grey dress, with her bell on its thread, and stood at the foot of the bed with her hands folded. She looked at the old man. She looked at the girl on the floor. She did not look at the door, or at the woman in it. She stayed there a little while, and then she turned to Halla and said, in her comfortable flat voice, “I’ve not come to say anything. I’ve come because you asked.”

“It’s enough.”

“Is it?”

“It’s a good deal.”

“Then I’ll sit.”

She sat. She drew a stool up by the head of the bed, on the side that Heber had left, and put the little bronze bell in her lap and her broad brown hands over it. She did not speak. She did not pray, that Riarhin could see. She only sat, as she had sat at the table in the steading, with the air of a woman who has been fetched to a place she knows very well.

Cera arrived an hour later. She was in her saffron robe with a basket, and she went, without asking, to the household. She had brought word that Eogan would hold the sealing over; no one had argued for keeping it that morning. There was a great deal to be done. There was a boy running errands, and two of the Red Oar’s captains weeping in the passage, and the steward, Piran, grey and helpless, who had never seen the old man ill. She sent for broth. She sent for hot water. She found a place for the captains to sit. She did not ask anyone anything. She did a hundred small, useful, unthanked things, one after another, in a level voice, and a good part of the house went quiet under her hands.

She passed Riarhin twice with bowls in her hands. The third time she asked her to move the bench clear of the door.

***

He was awake, on and off, all day.

She did not go in again. She stayed in the passage, on a bench against the wall, with her hands between her knees, and heard it through the open door. She heard the small sounds a room makes when a man is dying in it: the creak of a stool, the drip of the cloth in the bowl, the low voices going on and stopping. She heard Muirenn’s, quiet, and his.

He talked, when he could. It was not what she would have thought.

He asked about the hen. He asked, with some concern, whether the duck-eggs had hatched. He asked Halla to feed the dog, and she said it had been fed, and he said feed it again. He asked whether Fenra was comfortable and whether anyone had seen to her leg. He said something about a man at Dunmarrow who owed him for a boat, and that it was not to be pressed. He said, at noon, that there was a Viterran gentleman who was expecting an answer before winter, and that Muirenn was to tell him that the Red Oar was a great deal obliged and that the matter would be looked into. He said you’ll manage. He said it three times.

He did not confess. She listened for it. She had not known that she was listening until she noticed it: the great slow grinding wish that he would say something, anything, that would let it be one kind of thing and not another. He would say that he was afraid, or that he was sorry, or that he knew. He would say a word about the men he had killed, or the villages, or the coast. He would say something that would settle it.

He said none of it.

He was uncomfortable, and he was tired, and he was sometimes very much frightened, and he said so, in simple words, with no shame. It’s a bad business, Muirenn. It’s a poor way to go. And then, a little after, in a cracked voice that made her hands go cold: I’d have liked to see you married.

“You will,” said Muirenn.

“I’ll not.”

“You’ll be there.”

“No, girl.” He sighed. “I’ll not be lying to you. I never have.”

There was a long silence.

“I know,” said Muirenn. Her voice was quite steady. “I know you haven’t.”

Riarhin looked at her knees.

She sat in the passage, in the smell of lamp-oil and wet wool, and thought of a stone shelter on a ridge, and a keening in a ditch, and a man in a ringfort saying ye’ll see it done. She thought that there had been a great many deaths in her life, and that she had not been in the room for most of them. She had been on the scouting line. Others she had held until they stopped breathing. Na’ar’s hand had gone slack against her coat; she had still tried to lift him.

She had never been in the passage.

***

The dog began to howl at dusk.

It was a low, steady, dreadful sound, not loud, going up and down like a pump-handle. It came from the chamber, and it went out through the open door and along the passage and out into the yard, and every person in that end of the Hall stopped what they were doing. Halla stood up. She went to the door and stood in it, still, and her big hands hung open.

Riarhin went to the window at the passage’s end and looked out.

There was the abbey garden, dim and blue in the dusk, with its rows of thyme and its bean-poles and its low grey wall. Beyond the wall the ground fell away to the lake. It was quiet. The light had gone out of the sky, and the first stars were showing, and a thin cold wind had come in off the water.

There was a light on the garden wall.

Gold, the colour of a candle seen through a thumb. It sat on the wall’s coping twenty yards from the window, without flickering. They do not come to the Hall. Someone had said so. She could not remember who.

She turned her head. Halla was standing beside her. She had come up without a sound, and was looking out with her jaw set and her thumbs in her belt.

“Aye,” said Halla. “I see it.”

“Does it come for—”

“It comes,” said Halla. “That’s all I know.” She was silent a moment. “My mother said it was for the ones who’d been very much loved.”

She turned and went back to the door.

***

He died a little before dawn.

It was quiet. She had fallen asleep on the bench with her head against the wall, and she woke to the sound of the dog. It had stopped. It was a silence so complete that she heard, before she saw anything, the small dry clink of the bell in Sile’s lap as the Confessor rose.

She stood. She went to the door.

They were all there. Halla was at the foot of the bed, with her face like a flat stone. The steward Piran had come in, and stood weeping quite openly in the corner with his hand over his mouth. Cera stood behind him with her head bowed. Sile had gone to the head of the bed and put her hand on the old man’s brow, briefly, as she had done in the steading, and taken it away.

Muirenn had not moved. She sat on the floor with her father’s hand between hers and her head against the blanket. She did not seem to be crying. She sat still, and looked at nothing, and her big shoulders rose and fell.

Heber closed the old man’s eyes.

He did it very gently, with two long fingers. He straightened the blanket. He wiped the hands with the cloth, and folded them, and laid the small worn instruments back in their leather case, one by one, and shut it. Then he sat on the stool by the bed, and put his elbows on his knees, and his face in his hands, and did not move.

“It wasn’t his heart,” he said.

He said it quietly, into his palms. He did not say it to anyone.

Nobody answered. It was quite dark in the room. The candles had burned down to stubs. On the floor by the door the yellow dog lay with her muzzle on her paws, and did not stir, and did not seem to be breathing either.

Riarhin stood in the doorway.

She had thought that she would feel something. She had waited for it, through a day and a night, in a passage: some great cold flood of certainty, or the release of a long-held breath. There was nothing. There was a small room with an old man in it, and a woman on the floor, and the smell of candles. The thing she had commissioned was quite complete, and the people around the bed had spent all night trying to prevent it.

She thought of nothing at all. She stood there, and the light grew slowly at the window, grey and thin, and out in the abbey garden there was no lamp on the wall.

Chapter 28 — His Daughter’s Grief

Muirenn gave instructions all that morning, in the same level voice she used at the jetty, and by noon there was not a person in the wing who had not been told what to do.

She did it standing at the door of the scriptorium with her back to the room. The body was to be washed by the household and no one else. The Red Oar’s captains were to keep their boats at the Hall and their people in their lodgings until the burial. The steward was to send to Dunmarrow and to Ash. The Abbey’s lay-brothers were to bring lime and linen. Nobody was to touch the papers on the shelf. Nobody was to carry any word beyond the Hall until the brehons had been told, and then only what the brehons asked. She gave each order once. She did not repeat any of them.

It was very orderly. It was so orderly that Riarhin, standing at the end of the passage, felt the hair rise on her arms.

She saw the moment it went.

It was about eleven. Piran, the grey helpless steward, had come up to her with his cap in his hands and asked, in a small cracked voice, which cloth the master was to be laid in: the blue, or the white, or the old brown one from the chest. He said it very carefully, as one asks a question one has been rehearsing. He said the master had liked the blue.

Muirenn opened her mouth.

She stood with it open. Nothing came. She looked at the old man, and at the cap in his hands, and at the door of the room, and her big face went quite blank, as a slate goes when it is wiped. She did not seem to have heard him. She did not seem to be there.

“Mistress?” said Piran.

“I—” She stopped. She put a hand out, and found the jamb, and held it. “I don’t know.”

“Shall I—”

“I don’t know.” It came out cracked and small. “I’m sorry. I can’t. Ask Halla.”

“Yes, Mistress.”

“She’ll know.” She stood a moment longer, quite rigid. Then she turned and walked into the scriptorium and shut the door behind her, and Riarhin heard, through the wood, a sound she had not heard from her before, low and dreadful and quite unlike the laugh.

She stood there.

She did not go in. She thought of what she would have done in another life, with another person, and it was to put her hand on the door. She did not. She stayed in the passage with her back to the wall, and listened, and understood with cold entire clarity that she was hearing the thing she had been paid to bring about.

She did not go away. She stayed where she was for as long as the sound lasted. Nobody had asked her to. It seemed to her a very small thing to give, and the only one that she had.

***

Later she found the blue coat.

It hung on the chair, as it had hung for three days. It was a plain, good, old coat, with a silver clasp at the throat, and someone had folded the collar. She did not touch it. She stood in the empty chamber, where they had taken the body to be washed, and looked at it for perhaps a minute.

Muirenn came in behind her.

She had washed her face. She stood in the door with her hair tied back and her eyes red-rimmed and quite dry. She looked at the coat. She crossed the room, quite slowly, and put out her hand, and took the silver clasp between two fingers.

“He had this from my mother,” she said.

“I didn’t know.”

“He’d not tell you.” She turned it over. “She gave it him the year I was born. She said he’d never keep his coat shut, and he’d catch his death.” Her voice was flat and quite steady. “He never did keep it shut. He wore the clasp all my life and never once fastened it.”

She stood holding it.

“I’ll have it,” she said. “I’ll put it on the Charter, in black, where the clause is. It’s a small thing.” She lifted her head. “Brannoc says he’ll speak to his people. He says he’ll not ask me anything.”

“That’s kind.”

“It’s the first time he’s not.” She almost smiled. “He’s a decent man. It’s a great nuisance.” She shut her hand. “Go and be somewhere, Aine. I can’t have you in the room. I don’t mean it unkindly.”

“I know.”

“It’s that you’re the only one who’s not sorry.”
