Riarhin went.

***

Caelia found her in the garden at dusk.

It was the abbey garden, behind the scriptorium wing, with its low grey wall and its rows of thyme. The wall had a small wooden door in it, on the side facing the bay, and beyond the door there was a path down to the shore. It was quiet. The light was going. Somewhere below the wall water lapped against the landing posts, and the smell of the herbs rose thick and green from the beds, and in the corner by the bean-poles the coping was bare.

She sat on a bench with her hands between her knees. She had been there for an hour. She did not look up when the old woman came through the gate.

“May I sit?”

“It’s your garden.”

“It is the Hall’s.” Caelia lowered herself onto the bench, with a small sigh, at the other end. She wore the white cowl. She had the pendant at her breast, and she held her hands in her lap, folded, and looked out over the beds. “I have wanted to speak to you since yesterday. I did not think it would be kind before.”

“Say it.”

“I want to thank you.”

Riarhin did not move.

“I have buried men from every house at this gathering.” Caelia kept her voice low. “I have watched a great many men die. Most of them not in their beds. Most of them at the hands of one another, in some quarrel, in a field. I have buried the greater part of them.” She paused. “I have never seen a death so clean. I mean that. I do not say it lightly. There has been no fighting. There has been no burning. There is a house in mourning and a marriage to be made and the Hall is standing. There is nothing here that could not be borne.”

“Yes.”

“He would have sold us.” She said it gently. “Not from malice. He would have sold the ports and the Abbey and the household’s good name, for a licence and a Viterran gentleman’s good opinion. It would have been the ruin of this coast. He was a great man, and a kind one, and it would have been done kindly. I have known it for years.” She turned her light brown eyes to Riarhin. “You have delivered us. I do not say it is a good thing. I say it was a necessary one, and that it has been done as well as such a thing can be, and that I am grateful.”

She waited.

Riarhin pressed her palms against the rough bench. The old woman had come to be kind to her. She could see the care in the tired face, the patience with which Caelia waited for her to receive it. She pressed harder, until the wood hurt.

“He’s dead,” she said.

“Yes.”

“That’s all that’s happened.”

“It is a great deal.”

“Halla’s standing in a passage with a knife and a claim. Ask her whether you’ve made peace.”

“I understand.” Caelia inclined her head. Her voice was patient and kind. “I have seen it before. It is the scruple of a person who has done necessary work and cannot bear to be praised for it. I have felt it myself, in my time. It does you credit.” A small smile. “You will find that it wears off.”

Riarhin stood up.

She did it slowly. She was aware of her hands, which had gone quite cold. She was aware of the smell of thyme, and of a small bird that had begun somewhere in the dusk, and of the old woman looking up at her with a benevolent enquiry. She thought that it would be very easy to say something. She thought that she would not say it.

“You have the fee.”

“I have. I was going to speak of that.”

“I’ll take it.”

“Of course.” Caelia reached into her habit. She brought out a small round leather bag, and held it out. “The second half. It is all there. I should like you to count it.”

“I’ll count it.”

“And there is a blessing. If you will take it. It is not a thing the Abbey gives to many. I would give it with a very full heart.”

“No.”

The word came before she had thought. It was flat, and quiet, and quite final. She heard it go out among the herbs and stay there.

Caelia looked at her.

Riarhin closed her fingers on the bag. It was heavy, and warm from the old woman’s body. Caelia’s other hand had begun to rise towards the pendant. Riarhin stepped away before she could lift it.

She left her sitting there. She went down the path, through the little door in the wall, and out onto the shore, and stood at the water’s edge with the leather bag in her fist and the cold coming up. She stood there a long time. She had not known that she was capable of being so offended by a kindness. It was more than she had felt at the fee. It was more than she had felt at the man in the smokehouse. It was a slow, hot, sick contempt, and it was not, she thought, for Caelia.

She opened her hand. The bag was there. She counted it by the feel. It was right.

***

Cera came to her at the well.

It was in the courtyard behind the kitchens, at dark, and there was nobody about. The Temple’s sister had a shawl over her saffron, and she walked with her hands folded and her round face very grave. She did not sit. She stood on the other side of the well with her hands on the stone and looked at the black water.

“The Abbess is saying it was Providence,” she said.

“I’ve heard.”

“She has said it to the steward, and to two of the captains, and to a steward of the Hall. She believes it.” Her voice was level. “She believes that the Red Oar was taken in the nick of time, from a great danger, by the hand of a merciful heaven. She has said it in front of me.”

“And what did you say?”

“I said that I have recorded that he died on the morning before the sealing, of an illness whose cause is not known.” Cera did not look up. “I said that it is what I will write.”

Riarhin waited.

“I do not say that I know anything.” It was quite quiet. “I am a woman of the Temple. I have been in this country a year and I do not understand it. I have seen a man die who was in good health, in a room with a physician, and I have heard the physician say that it was not his heart.” She drew a breath. “I should say that I suspect. I should say that I have no evidence. I do not intend to invent any.”

“That’s very fair.”

“It is not fair. It is what I can do.” She lifted her head. Her eyes were quite steady, and there was in them an expression that Riarhin had not seen before: an odd, plain, unhappy resolve. “I will not tell Caelia that she is wrong. It is not my place. But I will not tell her that she is right. If she wishes it to be Providence, I will let her. I only wanted you to know that I have not agreed.”

“Why?”

“Because somebody should.” She almost smiled. “I am told it is what the Temple is for.”

She went. Her steps went away across the cobbles. And Riarhin stood beside the well with the smell of stone and old water in her nose, and found that she had, without meaning to, put her hand flat against her breast.

***

Sile she found last, in the Gray Liturgy’s lodging, at midnight, sitting on her bed with her bell in her lap and a piece of bread on her knee.

She had asked for her. She stood in the door. She did not know how to begin.

“You were with him at the end,” she said.

“I was.”

“I wondered—” She stopped. She made herself go on. “I wondered if he said anything. To you. At the last. When the rest had gone out.”

Sile looked at her.

It was a plain, broad, weathered look, quite without hostility. She turned the bread in her hand, and put it down.

“No,” she said.

“No, he didn’t?”

“No, I’ll not tell you,” said Sile. “It’s not mine to give. I sat with him because I was asked. It was for him and for the girl and for the woman at the door. It wasn’t for you.” She shook her head slowly. “I know why you ask. I’d ask too. But I’ll not be the person that the company comes to for a thing it wants to be told. I’d not be much of a Confessor.”

“I only want to know if he knew.”

“I know you do.”

“Then—”

“It would not help,” said Sile. “Whichever it was.”

I know what you did. Or worse, I forgive you. She had come hoping for words she could bear to hear.

“Thank you,” she said.

“For what?”

“For not.”

“It’s not a great deal,” said Sile. She took up the bread. “Go to bed.”

She went out into the yard. There were stars over the Hall and the beast’s ribs stood black against them. In the north bay, a single lamp was burning on the trestle, where somebody had left it, and Muirenn sat beside it, quite alone, in the leather jerkin, with the silver clasp in her hand.

She did not look up. She said, in a low rough voice, without turning her head: “It’s the sealing tomorrow.”

“Yes.”

“It won’t be.”

“No.”

“I don’t know what it’ll be.” She turned the clasp over. “I’d like to say I’d go through with it. But my father’s waiting to be buried. I’ve a room of people who think I killed him and a man who’s afraid to look at me. I don’t know what I’m to do.”

“You’ll do what you do.”

“Which is?”

“What’s in front of you.”

“It’s not much of a plan.”

“No,” said Riarhin. She sat down on the trestle beside her, a foot away. In the small yellow light Muirenn kept turning the clasp.

Chapter 29 — Holding the Hall

By seven the next morning the nave had begun to sort itself, as a crowd does before it decides what it is.

She stood on the steps of the north bay and watched it happen. It was not a riot. It was a great many people, in a great many small knots, each with a face turned inward and a hand somewhere it had not been the night before. The Red Oar’s captains stood in a tight grey group by the third rib. The Whiterick lay-brothers had put their trestles against the wall. Brannoc’s household had pulled together round the old mother’s chair, and Lorcan stood at the front of it with his jaw working. A man she did not know had climbed on a bench and was saying, in a high carrying voice, that a wedding could not be held over a corpse.

“It’s beginning,” said Amari, at her shoulder.

“Yes.”

“I’ve seen it start like this. Not at once. You get a sort of drift.” He rubbed his neck. “In an hour they’ll be standing where they’ll fight from.”

She did not answer. She was looking at the far end of the hall, where the door of the Everflame’s wing stood open. Halla had come out of it. She stood on the step in her leather jerkin, quite alone, with a plain long bundle in her arms wrapped in a sheet, and behind her four of the household came out carrying a bier.

They were taking him. It was as plain as a written sentence. They meant to lift the Red Oar and his people and everything that was theirs, and take them out of a gathering that she no longer trusted, and away to Dunmarrow, before anything else could be decided. It was what Riarhin would have done. She could not have said that it was wrong.

“It’s not going to be that easy,” said Amari.

“No.”

It was not. Halfway down the nave a big red-bearded man in a captain’s coat had stepped out in front of the bier, and put up his hand, and said something she could not hear. It was low. It was very clear. It had the sound of a question that is not a question, and Halla’s square face went still.

“That’s Corran,” said Amari. “He’s the second hull.”

“What does he want?”

“The boats. There’s six of them at the jetty, and four of them are his master’s, and he’s the one who can sail them.” He let out a breath. “He’ll be saying that a household in mourning can’t take a fleet. That it ought to be put in safe hands till the Hall’s decided.”

“His hands.”

“Aye.”

“And the others?”

“They’ll say the same, each in his own mouth.” He looked at her. “What do you want done?”

She stood on the step. It was a sharp cold morning and her breath went up white. She could feel the whole hall lean, slightly, as a boat leans before it is over.

I could have this done in a quarter of an hour. She knew the names. She knew the six or seven who would start it. There was a way of making a few of them stop. It was quite clear, laid out in her head like a route on a map. She had done it on the scouting line, with a good reason. She could have kept the Hall by killing the men whose loyalty had become inconvenient, and it would have been quick.

And the Hall would not have been a place where a marriage could be made.

She let the map go. She put it back in the drawer where she kept it, and shut the drawer.

“Amari,” she said. “You’ll go to the jetty. Take Berent, if he’ll go. Don’t do anything. Stand where you can be seen.”

“That’s all?”

“It’s what we’re for.”

He gave her a long, steady, faintly rueful look. Then he nodded, and went.

***

She gave the rest of it in ten minutes, quietly, in the north bay, to the ones who were there.

She did not use a great many words. She had learned that. She told Cadan to stand at the door of the boat-shed on the shore, where the captains would come to argue, and let nobody go in with a drawn blade, and touch nobody who did not draw. If someone drew, he was to take the blade out of the hand. He was to break nothing. He was to say what he was doing before he did it.

“That is very exact,” said Cadan.

“I mean it to be.”

“I shall say it aloud.”

“You’ll say it clearly.”

“Yes.” He bowed. “I should like to be told, afterwards, whether I did it correctly.”

“I’ll tell you.”

She turned to Nessa. She did not have to ask. The singer stood at the edge of the bay with her shawl round her shoulders and her plain hands folded, and she was looking, over Riarhin’s head, at the corner where they had put Fenra in a chair, with her leg on a stool, and the boy Ket beside her on a bench, and three old women from Brannoc’s household who could not walk fast.

“There’s a west door,” said Nessa.

“I know.”

“It’s clear. It’s quite easy. I could take them through it in five minutes, and be out on the road.” She spoke quite lightly. “It would be a good thing to do. It would be sensible. The fewer people who are slow in a hall like this, the better.”

“Yes.”

“I’m not going to.”

“No.”

“I’ll stay with them.” She said it as a woman says that she will have a second cup of tea. “If it comes to it, I can see them out. I’ll not have them left.”

“You could go with them.”

“I could. That’s the thing.” Nessa smiled. “I’m told that it’s the only way to tell whether one has decided.”

She went across to the corner and sat down beside the old woman, with her back very straight, and took Fenra’s hand.

***

It was Tavin, in the end, who brought the strongbox.

He did it at half past eight, in the passage behind the scriptorium, and he did it in a way that made her heart stop. He came through the door at the end with the bird on his shoulder and a small black iron chest under one arm, no bigger than a loaf, very old, with a hasp and three wards. He set it on the trestle in front of her. He did not say anything.

She looked at it.

“Whose?”

“The Red Oar’s.” He sat down on the edge of a stool. His face was pale and rather cross. “It was in the room. It was under the bed. In all the confusion nobody thought of it. It’s the only one he’d bring, I’m told, and it’s the one that has the Dunmarrow deeds and the four hulls and a purse of Viterran gold that I could feel through the lid.” He swallowed. “I had it out of that room in about four minutes. It’s not been missed.”

“Tavin.”

“I know.”

“You’ve taken it.”

“I did.” He put both hands on his knees. “I stood there with it in my arms. It would be a claim. It would be a claim on the whole estate. If I’d put my name to the deeds in the right place, in the right week, before anyone knew, I’d have had a share of four ships and a house. I could have gone to Ash and been a gentleman.” He shook his head. “I’d a very clear view of it.”

“Why did you bring it?”

“The Abbey’s paid. The landing hasn’t, and its promise is worth nothing if the marriage falls apart. Muirenn still owes us the boats to Davra. I take this, there’s a hue and cry before lunch.” He rubbed a thumb over the chest’s brass corner. “Four ships. I was choosing the curtains.”

“Choose the pantry.”

“Good.” He pushed the chest across. “Put it back. Or give it to Halla. Or throw it in the lake. But not to me.” He rose. “I’m going to go and stand in front of the pantry, and be very honest, for about an hour. I find it quite a strain.”

He went. The magpie, on his shoulder, looked back once at the chest with one bright eye, and then she too was gone.

***

She did not see the jetty. She was told about it afterwards, and put it together as she put things together, from five accounts.

It began at nine. Corran had brought forty men down to the shore and sent twenty into the boats, and Amari, who had gone down at seven with Berent, stood in the shingle in front of the nearest hull with his hands at his sides and did nothing at all. He did it very well. He was a large, calm, unthreatening man in a plain coat, and after a while it became clear to every person on the beach that he was not going to move.

“Stand aside,” said Corran.

“I’ll not.”

“Then we’ll go round.”

“You’ll be welcome to. You’ll be doing it in front of me.” Amari said it mildly. “I’d like it seen who took the boats.”

There was a pause. It was a bad one. Then, at his elbow, a voice said something different.

“Alderman,” said Berent.

Nobody had heard him come. He was leaning on the stick, at the edge of the shingle, thin and grey and very tall, with Na’ar’s sword in the crook of his arm. He was looking, not at Corran, but at a heavy grizzled man standing in the second rank, with a great scar on his neck and a bad shoulder.

“Cormac,” he said.

The man turned his head.

“It’s the Bishop’s off foreleg I wanted to ask you about,” said Berent. “You said it was sound, at the supper. I’d a look at it in the yard. It’s not.” He spoke quite quietly. “I was going to say. I thought you’d want to know.”

“Is it?”

“It’s hot at the knee.”

“Ah, Lady,” said Cormac. He said it with a kind of groan. “I’ve been at it a fortnight.”

“Put a wet cloth.”

“I did.”

“Put another.”

For a moment nobody said anything. The wind stirred the reeds along the shingle. The forty men stood with their hands on their belts, and the twenty in the boats held their oars, and a big, grey, sunburned veteran had turned away from his captain to look at a lame man on a stick. He said, in a rough low voice, “You’d know. You told me you’d a grey mare once, with a wind-gall.”

“Aye.”

“Is she well?”

“She’s dead,” said Berent. “A while ago.”

“I’m sorry.”

“It’s all right.”

They talked for perhaps ten minutes, in low voices, about two horses and a farrier at Killen and a drover neither of them had known well and both had liked. At the end of it Cormac put his hand on his sword, and took it off again, and turned to the red-bearded captain, and said in a flat quiet voice: “Corran. It’s not our fight.”

“The boats—”

“The boats are the master’s. He’s not in his grave.” He gestured at the shingle. “I’d as soon not be the first to move them.”

“That’s—”

“I’ll say it to the others.”

Corran stood. He looked at the line of men, and at the tall grey figure with the stick, and at Amari. Then, with a face like a shut door, he turned away.

It took a very little time, after that. The twenty came out of the boats. The forty broke into knots. And Berent, who had stood without moving for the whole of it, took one step backwards, and his bad leg went, and he sat down on the shingle quite suddenly with a look of mild surprise.

Amari was there in two strides. He took him under the arm, and lifted, and Tavin, who had come running from somewhere with a face like a sheet, took the other. They carried him up the beach between them. He did not say anything. He was very white. But she was told that he was smiling, a little, in a peculiar fixed way, as a man smiles who has been holding a heavy thing and has just put it down.

“I couldn’t stand,” he said, when they set him in the boat-shed. “I couldn’t have gone another minute.”

“You didn’t need to,” said Amari.

“I’d not have got through it without.”

“No,” said Amari. “You wouldn’t. That’s what the rest of us are for.”

***

While the men were still at the boats, Riarhin went down to the boat-shed. Three of Corran’s men were at its door.

She had come down with a message for Amari and found the path blocked by their backs. Inside, two of the Red Oar’s captains had drawn a bench across the earth floor to argue over a boat-hook laid between them. Cadan stood at the threshold. He was listening to the man in front of him with the mild attention he gave to complaints about supper.

“I’ve a right to speak,” the man said.

“You may speak.”

“I’ll speak in there.”

“Yes. Put the knife away first.”

It was small, scarcely more than a working blade, held down against the man’s thigh. Riarhin saw it when he shifted. He saw her looking and brought it up, as though showing it were better than being found with it.

“Tell him,” he said to her. “I’ve carried this since I was a boy.”

“I’m not asking for it,” said Cadan. “I’m asking you to put it away.”

One of the two men behind touched his companion’s shoulder. He shrugged the hand off. In the shed the argument stopped. Riarhin could see the boat-hook and the narrow space between the bench and the door, and Berent farther down the beach, still upright on his stick. She took a step towards Cadan.

The man pushed forward.

“I am going to take that from you,” Cadan said.

There was a scuffle at the threshold, a heel scraping the stone. The man came back out with his empty hand against his chest. Cadan held the knife low at his own side. A little blood ran over one knuckle.

“You may go in now.”

The man stared at him. His companions had moved apart. He could have passed between them and gone down the beach; instead he reached towards the knife, stopped, and looked at Riarhin.

“It’s mine.”

“Yes,” she said. “You can collect it when you leave.”

He went in. His companions followed. After a while the voices started again, lower, and someone moved the bench to let a fourth man sit. Cadan laid the knife on the window ledge inside the door.

“You kept it narrow,” Riarhin said.

He looked pleased. The expression was immediate, almost boyish, and she felt herself answer it before she had thought.

“You said to.”

“There was room to hurt him.”

“There was.” He looked at the blood on his hand. “It would have been quicker. I should have missed the next part.”

“What part?”

“He went in. He is speaking. I had thought he would go away.”

From inside came an angry question about an oar, followed by a much angrier answer. Cadan turned to listen, interested.

“Will you wash that?” Riarhin asked.

“When someone takes the door.”

“I’ll take it.”

He bowed and went to the water-butt. She stood in his place. The knife lay within reach on the ledge. She moved it farther in, then left it where its owner could see it.

***

The hearing was held in the weighing-house at three, because it was the only room in the Hall with a door that locked from the inside.

There were nine present. Eogan sat at the table with his slate. Halla stood at the end in her leather jerkin, with Heber at her shoulder, grey and silent, and the steward Piran behind them, and behind him a captain whose name she did not know. Muirenn stood on the other side, with Brannoc at her elbow, and Caelia in the corner on a stool. Riarhin had been asked to stay. She stood by the great iron beam with her back to the wall.

Halla spoke first. She did it in a flat hard level voice, with her thumbs in her belt.

“I make a claim of death against the household of Muirenn Varr. I claim that Donnan Varr, my master, died not by God’s will but by hands. I don’t say whose. I say it wasn’t natural.” She drew a breath. “I want it in the roll. I want it heard.”

There was a small silence.

“It is a very serious claim,” said Eogan.

“I know it.”

“It is one that must be answered.”

“It will be.” Muirenn’s voice was quite steady. “I’ve said I’ll stand for it. I’ve said I’ll answer any question the Hall puts. I’ve nothing to hide from the brehons.” She did not look at Riarhin. “It’s a fair claim. I don’t deny it’s fair.”

“Muirenn—” began Brannoc.

“Let her.”

Eogan looked from one to the other. He was, she thought, a tired man. His face was grey with lack of sleep. He had ink on both cuffs, and there was in the plain bright eyes an expression of something that was not quite fear and not quite sorrow. He had held the Hall together for five days by main force, with a slate and a pen, and he knew perfectly well what was to be done.

“I will enter it,” he said. “I will enter it as a claim brought and accepted, against the household of Muirenn Varr, for consideration at a hearing after the sealing, or at such later time as the brehons appoint.” He wrote. “I will not say that it is proved. I will not say that it is refused. I will say that it is held.”

“That’s all?”

“It is all I can do. It is a good deal.” He lifted his head. “Master Halla, I should like to know what you require, in return for standing down.”

She was silent for a moment.

She looked, in the yellow light, suddenly quite old. She had a broken nose and a stiff back, and there was a small hard muscle jumping in her jaw. She said, in a low voice:

“The body’s ours. It goes to Dunmarrow in the household’s hands, and is buried by the household. No one else touches it.”

“Granted.”

“Heber has the run of the Hall. He’s to see what he wishes to see, and speak to whom he likes. He’s not to be stopped at any door.”

“Granted.”

“And the household’s standing is acknowledged.” She lifted her chin. “We’re not a rabble. We’re not a fleet without a master. We’re the Red Oar’s people and we’ve rights under the Charter and I’ll hear no other word.”

“It is acknowledged.”

“Then I’ll stand down,” said Halla.

She did not sound as though she meant it as a favour. She did not look at Muirenn. She did not say that she believed it natural, or that she did not. She stood at the end of the table for a moment longer, and her eyes went once, quite briefly, to the woman by the beam, and moved on.

She turned and went out. Heber followed. At the door he stopped, and looked back over his shoulder at Riarhin, with his long grey face quite empty, and it was not, she thought, at all unfriendly. It was the look of a man who is not going to ask.

The door closed.

“Well,” said Eogan. He put down his pen. He looked, all at once, very old. “That’s the Hall.”

“It’s standing,” said Muirenn.

“It is standing. It has a cost.” He rubbed his eyes with the heel of his hand. “There is a claim on the roll that will not go away, and there are three or four people with a good deal of standing who have every reason to remember it. I would not like to say what will become of it.”

“No.”

“But it is a marriage that can be made.”

“Yes,” said Muirenn. She drew a long breath. “That’s all I wanted to know.”

Riarhin stood by the beam. She had not moved through the whole of it. She looked at the door where the woman had gone, and at the small black chest on the trestle that someone had put there, and at the great scales hanging over them all in the lamplight, still.

She took the chest from the trestle and carried it after Halla. At the door the woman turned, received it in both hands, and checked the hasp before she looked at Riarhin.

“The key was in his coat,” she said.

“I haven’t touched the key.”

Halla held her gaze a moment, then went on down the passage.

Interlude VII — The Proposal to Burn

Veralyn

The council met in a granary, because the town hall at Serrat had been used for something else in the spring and no one had yet been brave enough to scrub it.

It was a long low stone building with a slate roof and a great many windows, and the light in it was clean and cold. They sat at trestles down the middle, nine of them, with maps pinned to the walls behind and the smell of old grain still in the beams. Veralyn had the chair at the head. It was an ordinary chair. The sword lay along the table at her right hand in its plain sheath, and she had long since ceased to notice that people looked at it before they looked at her.

“Marshal Hale,” she said. “You have the floor.”

He rose. He was a heavy grey man of fifty-five, with a scarred face and a way of standing that suggested that the floor had once tried to move. He had commanded the left at Marrenta and been right about everything, and he did not have the temperament to be told so twice. He put his fingers on the nearest map and drew them down it, slowly, across a great blotch of green.

“The valleys,” he said. “The upper Tarne, the Orl, the three sisters. Some four hundred villages.”

“Yes.”

“They have not declared. They had the fortnight and did not use it. They have been given a second, and a third, and there have been three parleys, and in each the headmen said they would consider.” His voice was flat. “I do not say it is treason. I say it is a delay. It is a great deal of the country, Majesty, lying in the hills behind us, full of men who have been told it is over and have decided to wait.”

“I know.”

“Every one of them is a man who can walk to Marrenta with a knife.”

“I know.”

“Then you will forgive me for saying that we have been very patient.” He moved his hand. “I propose that we should stop. I propose that a line be drawn from the Tarne to the pass, and that all within it that has not declared by the first of the month be cleared. Fields, stores, roofs. What cannot be brought to obedience in a season can be brought to silence in a week.”

He sat down.

Nobody moved. It was a quiet room. She heard a sparrow somewhere in the beams, and the small dry sound of the secretary’s pen stopping. She had thought she would feel something at the word. She listened for it, calmly, as one listens for a bell.

It was merely a proposal. It was in the same voice as the others. It had been laid on the table beside the questions of wagons and rations, and she would treat it as such.

“Colonel Venn,” she said.

He was on his feet before she had finished. He was leaner than he had been in the winter, and he had a careful, unhappy, rather formal expression, and he spoke without notes.

“I should like to say, Majesty, that I do not object on grounds of feeling.” He said it at once, as a man clears his throat. “I know what is at stake. I have seen the reports from the passes. I do not ask that the Council take it as a matter beyond consideration. I ask that it be considered as a matter of work.”

“Go on.”

“Four hundred villages produce some fifteen per cent of the grain of the Acasian plain. They pay a tithe. They keep six thousand horses. They supply, in ordinary years, a third of the wool and most of the iron.” He counted it on his fingers. “If they are burned, they produce nothing. If they produce nothing, the army must eat something else. It will not be Acasian. There is not the transport. We shall be feeding the whole of the plain from our own stores, for a year, perhaps two. It is a very great deal to ask of a supply line that we have not yet made safe.”

“There will be no one to feed.”

“There will be the people who live there, Majesty. They do not vanish. They go down the roads.” He said it quite gently. “Ten, twenty thousand. Without homes. Into the towns we have taken. Into the camps. We shall have to garrison them. It is not a matter of pity. It is a matter of the men who will be needed to stand over them.”

“I have considered that.”

“I do not say you have not.”

“I say that it is a cost, and that it is a smaller one than the alternative.” She spoke without heat. “A war which drags for three years costs a great deal more than one that is ended in one. The people in those valleys will be fighting in the spring, whatever we do. There will be raids. There will be burnings of our own. There will be a winter in which every convoy has to be escorted and every sentry is doubled. They will die in the hills, of cold, of hunger, in a hundred small skirmishes, over a very long time.” She turned her head. “I have seen it. I have seen a country that would not end. I would rather have it over.”

“Majesty—”

“I am not speaking of vengeance.” Her voice was quite level. “I am speaking of what is kinder. It is a very hard question, and I do not think that anyone at this table has the right to say that it is easy. But it seems to me that the one who has the whole of the picture ought to be the one to say what shortens it.”

She looked round the granary. She saw the faces turn to her, one after another, in the clean light. She saw Marshal Hale nod, once, heavily. She saw the young officer at the end look at the table. She saw Tylan, at the foot, put down his cup and turn it a quarter round.

They were waiting for her to explain. She knew that look; they expected her to put it right.

***

A letter had come that morning, and it lay on the table under her left hand.

She had read it in the corridor. It was very short, and it was written in a strong, plain, upright script that she remembered. It said that the writer had received Her Majesty’s offer of terms with the respect that it deserved. It said that the writer was not in a position to accept them. It said that she had four thousand people and sixty barges, and a river, and that she did not think it was Her Majesty’s business to tell her where to put them.

I do not ask for anything, it said. I have never been in the habit. I remain, as I was, in my own place.

It was signed Aryka Everblood.

Veralyn had read it twice. She had felt, on the second reading, a small hard bright irritation, entirely disproportionate to the words. She remembered her aunt at a family table, speaking across the adults to ask her a question and waiting for her answer. Aryka could be kind in that offhand way, as though nothing in the room required courage. She had thought her a very fine person. She had, in a corner of her mind, hoped she would be reasonable.

She was not. She had sat on her river in the rain, having lost a bridge, and written a note.

Of course she would, thought Veralyn. It was, she found, a rather comforting thought. Of course she would not come. There was a kind of person who could not be brought in, who preferred to be right on the outside. It was not a defect of policy. It was a defect of character. It was very difficult to be angry with, and she was.

She laid her hand on the letter. It was only a piece of paper. It was, she thought, the whole argument in a single page: a woman who would not be governed, in a country that could not be finished.

“Marshal,” she said.

He looked up.

“I do not decide today. I say that at once. I have heard the proposal and the objection, and both are sound.” She was quite calm. “I should like to be told, by the end of the week, what the four hundred villages actually produce, in every particular. What could be saved of it if a certain number were spared. What the line from the Tarne to the pass would cost, in men and in time, and where the smoke would go.” She looked at Venn. “I should like a plan of the roads on which the people would travel, and where they could be housed. And I should like to know how many of the headmen have not been asked in person.”

“In person?”

“I don’t consider a message a question.” She smiled slightly. “I had an order put out, I recall, some weeks ago. It seems to me to have been well drafted.”

There was a small, grateful stir. It went round the table. She saw it, and understood it, and did not correct it. She saw the officers relax, one by one, into the belief that the matter had been shelved.

She had not shelved it. She had merely asked to be told the whole of it. It was what a good ruler did, before she acted: she gathered every fact.

“That is all,” she said. “Thank you, gentlemen.”

They rose. They went out, in ones and twos, into the clean cold light. Tylan stayed. He came round the table and put a hand on her shoulder, and did not say anything, and she covered it with her own.

“It’s a great deal to weigh,” he said.

“It is.”

“You’ll be fair.”

“I shall try to be right,” said Veralyn. She looked down the long empty table, at the maps on the wall, and the green blotch, and the small pinned flags. “It’s very nearly the same thing.”

Chapter 30 — The Bride-Charter

They chose it again on the morning of the third day, in the empty nave, under the ribs, with nobody watching but a broom.

She had come in to fetch a coat. It was very early. The lamps were out and the vault of pale bone went up in the grey light like the inside of a great slow lung, and the earth of the floor was cold and trampled and smelled of last night’s ox. At the far end, by the second rib, two people were standing a yard apart. She would have gone out again. But the acoustics of the place were not made for privacy, and by the time she had thought of it she had already heard the first sentence.

“I don’t forgive it,” said Muirenn.

She stood with her arms folded, in the leather jerkin, with a strip of black cloth tied round her left arm above the elbow. She was not looking at him. She was looking at the floor.

“I know,” said Brannoc.

“I’m saying it so you’ll know. I’m not going to let it lie because there’s a wedding. I’ll not say it’s a small thing.” Her voice was gruff and quite low. “You hid a thing from me and you meant to keep hiding it. I’d have married you and found it in the spring. I’ll never not know that.”

“No.”

“I don’t say I can’t. I say I’ll not pretend.”

“I’d not want you to.”

“Then say what you’ll do.”

He was silent for a moment. He stood with his big hands hanging, in the mended brown coat that his mother had buttoned for him, and she saw in the grey light how much thinner his face had grown. He did not look like a man making an offer. He looked like a man reading out a list of what was left.

“I’ll say in front of the Hall what I may pledge,” he said. “The landing’s dues, and no more. Not the boats. Not the stalls, without your leave. Not a barrel of what’s been brought in by your crews. It’ll be written, and stitched, and read in the hearing of the households, and if I break it, it’s broken in front of them.” He drew a breath. “I’ll give up the seat.”

“What seat?”

“The council’s. The head of the households’ table. I’ve sat there since my father. I’d thought that with the marriage, it’d be the two of us.” He moved his shoulders. “It’ll be yours. It’ll be Muirenn Varr’s, with a place for me at the side, if I’m asked. I’ll not have it said I got it with the boats.”

“That’s a great deal to give.”

“It’s not. It’s what I’d thought I’d gain.” He tried to smile. “I find I’d like to be a man who has a landing and a wife, and not a man who has a great deal more.”

She looked up at last.

It was a long look. It was as level and searching as she had ever seen her give. She stood there in the grey dawn, in the smell of trampled rushes, and looked at a big red-eyed man in a mended coat, and Riarhin saw her decide that she could not tell what she saw, and would find out.

“It’ll not be easy,” she said.

“No.”

“It’ll be a poor marriage for a year.”

“I know.”

“It’ll be a marriage where I’ve a lot to say and you’ve a lot to be silent about.” She let out her breath. “I’ll do it. Not because it’s what we planned. Because it’s a thing that could work.”

“That’s all I’d ask.”

“Then that’s two.” She unfolded her arms. “Go and wash. You’ve got egg on your coat.”

He looked down. He had. He laughed, quite suddenly, an odd sound, half a sob. She did not smile. But she put out a hand, and took hold of the front of the coat, and rubbed at the yolk with her thumb, hard, once, as a woman rubs a child’s face; and he stood still and let her.

Riarhin found her coat. She went out by the west door, in silence, and did not look back.

***

The sealing was held at noon, and the hall was so full that the beast’s ribs seemed to lean.

The mourners lingered, and the wedding guests had not yet found their voices. There were people in black. There were people in the households’ blue. The children had been kept at the back. The three harpers sat in a row on a bench and did not play, and the man who sold ribbons had shut his stall.

The linen lay across the trestle at the top of the nave. Someone had ironed it. The holes where the fourth clause had been unpicked were still there, in a neat row, and above them, in black, a stitcher’s needle had gone in and out, and it now said, in the flat courtly hand of the Hall: the flotilla of the bride shall remain of the household of Muirenn Varr, and in alliance with that of Brannoc of Rossan. Beside it, sewn to the cloth with four small stitches, lay a silver clasp.

Muirenn had put it there. She had done it herself, at seven that morning, without saying anything. It was plain, and old, and slightly bent, and it looked, against the pale linen, like a small heavy bird that had come down to rest.

The stitchers had done the rest. There were fourteen new clauses. They had been added in an afternoon and a night, and each was worked in its own colour: brown for the landing’s limits, black for the boats, white for the Abbey’s supply, and, in a plain dull grey that had been used only once before, the Confessor’s.

“Read it,” said Eogan.

He was standing at the head of the table with his slate. He was tired, and very neat. There was a clean cuff on one wrist and an inky one on the other, as if he had been called away half-dressed.

He read. It took a quarter of an hour. She stood at the back, between Amari and Nessa, and listened to the country made small. Beginning with the second season, a fixed proportion of the landing’s dues would go to Whiterick over three seasons, up to the stated sum. The Abbey should have no lien on the boats, no seat at the sealing but a guest’s, no voice in the observances of the households. Brannoc of Rossan should pledge no more of the landing’s dues than those set down, and no part of the flotilla’s takings, without the consent of its master. The seat at the head of the households’ table was to be held by Muirenn Varr.

It was read plainly, and each clause was answered. Brannoc said aye to his in a clear cracked voice. Caelia, seated on a stool at the side with her hands folded, said the Abbey accepts, and bowed her head slightly, and did not look at anyone. She did not look at Riarhin. It was as if a door had been quietly shut between them, and both had heard it.

“Matron Eithne,” said Eogan. “You have a matter.”

She stood up.

She did it briskly, like a woman coming to the end of a long list. She was small and grey and very upright in her good dress, with the keys at her belt, and she held up a slip of paper.

“I said before, in the north bay, that there are two names that ought to be on this cloth and are not.” She did not raise her voice. “I said it to the steward and to the brehons and to a great many people who agreed with me. I’ll say it once more, in front of the Hall.” She read. “Daire, drover, of Killen. He died on the Whiterick road in the service of the landing at Rossan, carrying stores for this gathering. His sister Sorcha has four children. Ket, teamster, of the same household, whose shoulder and leg are broken. He cannot lift a barrel till the spring.” She lowered the slip. “I said that they ought not to be left to somebody’s generosity. I said it was a thing that should be stitched.”

“It has been agreed,” said Eogan.

“It has been agreed. I should like to see it done.”

There was a pause. Then the youngest stitcher, a sallow boy of fifteen, came up with a needle and a spool of brown thread. He knelt at the end of the cloth. He worked slowly, with his tongue between his teeth, and every eye in the hall was on his hand, and it took a long time. When it was done he sat back on his heels and held up the linen, and she saw the words: Daire of Killen. Ket. They were small and rather crooked, and they were in the same brown as the rest.

“Thank you,” said Eithne, and sat down.

Riarhin stood still. Beside her, Amari had bowed his head. She did not look at him.

***

Cera spoke for the Temple, and did it as she did everything, with a composed and careful face.

She stood on the left of the cloth in her saffron robe with her carved sun-disc in her hands. She said that the Temple of Light, in the person of its sister at this Hall, recognised the marriage of Muirenn Varr and Brannoc of Rossan as a union lawfully made under the custom of the country. She said that it did so as a witness and not as an authority. She said that it did not thereby express any opinion on the qualities of the persons who had sponsored it, or the means by which it had been made possible.

“The Sister will not say that we are righteous,” said Caelia, from her stool, dryly.

“I will not.”

“That is very correct.”

“I hope so.” Cera did not turn her head. “I am not qualified to say what anyone is.”

Sile came last. She came up from the back of the nave, heavily, in her grey dress, with the little bronze bell on its grey thread swinging at her wrist, and stood at the right of the table. She did not look at the crowd. She looked at the linen.

“I’ve been asked to witness,” she said. “I’ll do it. I’ll witness what they can promise.” Her voice was plain and quite unhurried. “They can promise to keep the peace for a season. They can promise to pay what they’ve written and to not do what they’ve said they won’t. They can promise to try. I’ll stand at the back of that. It’s a good thing to stand at.”

“And the rest?” said Eogan.

“What rest?”

“There is a feeling,” he said carefully, “that the death of Donnan Varr has settled a good many older matters. That the coast will now be quiet. That there is an end.”

“Is there?”

“I do not say so.”

“I do,” said Sile. “I say it’s not so.” “A man is dead. It’s a sad thing. It didn’t undo a single feud. It didn’t give back the eleven lost between the households. It didn’t make Halla’s claim less, or Lorcan’s temper. It isn’t a verdict. It isn’t any sort of a finding.” She lifted her hand from the bell. “I’ll not stand at the back of that. I’d be lying.”

There was a small murmur. It went through the crowd like a wind through barley. And then, at the very back of the nave, in the last of the doors, someone said in a flat hard voice, “That’s so.”

It was Halla.

She stood alone against the west door, in her leather jerkin, with her thumbs in her belt and her face still. She had come without a word. She had, Riarhin saw, not come to interrupt. She had come to be counted. She stood there through the rest of it, a square weather-tanned woman at the edge of the light, and nobody looked at her, and nobody forgot that she was there.

***

They stitched the last of it in silence.

It was a single line, in white, along the bottom of the cloth. It said let the veil be made. The stitcher who did it was Whiterick’s, a placid round-faced sister of forty, and she drew the thread through very slowly, and bit it off, and stood.

Then they lifted the linen.

It was eleven feet long and quite heavy. Four of them took it, two on each side, and raised it over the heads of the couple, and let it fall, gently, so that it hung between their shoulders like a tent. Muirenn stood on the left, with the black strip round her arm and her hair uncovered. Brannoc stood on the right. The light came through the pale stuff and made them both the colour of milk. She could not see their faces. She saw only two shapes side by side, and the small dark row of clauses hanging over them like a script.

Sile came forward with a length of grey thread.

She did not say a blessing. She took Muirenn’s left wrist, and Brannoc’s right, and drew them together, and wound the thread round both, three times, and tied it in a plain knot. She did it without ceremony. She did it as a woman ties up a parcel.

“That’s all,” she said. “It’ll last as long as it lasts.”

“Is that a rite?” said someone, from the crowd.

“It’s a thread.”

“Does it bind them?”

“It says they’re tied,” said Sile. “What they do about it’s theirs.”

And under the linen, in the milky light, two hands closed on each other, a big scarred woman’s and a big square man’s. She could not see it. She only knew it from the way the cloth shifted, slightly, and stopped.

It was over.

She did not hear the cheer. She heard a sound that was like one, and not quite: the sound of a great many people who have been afraid for a long time letting out a breath. It went up into the ribs. It went round the hall. A harper began, very softly, on a bench, and another took it up. She saw Brannoc’s mother, in her black shawl, put her hand to her mouth. She saw Lorcan, at the front, stare at the couple with an expression she could not read, and turn slowly, and take off his cap.

Halla was gone. The door was empty.

Nobody had made a move to stop her. It had been said, in the roll and in the cloth, that her claim was held, and that the feud should not begin that night. The truce held for that night.

***

She stood outside in the wind afterwards, on the step of the west door, with her hands in her sleeves.

It was a bright cold afternoon. The bay was blue, and small boats were coming in over it, and on the green ground below the hall the households were sitting down to eat. There was a dog, and three children, and a woman selling pies. She watched a very small boy run in a circle, shrieking, with a whistle. He fell over. He got up and did it again.

Nessa came out and stood beside her.

She did not say anything. She had the shawl round her shoulders, and the white streaks in her hair were very bright in the sun, and she stood with her hands folded and her face turned to the sea. After a moment she began, quietly, to hum. It was the song for a door. It went up and turned and came down. Riarhin listened.

It went all the way to the end.

“You found it,” she said.

“I did.” Nessa did not look round. “It came back this morning. I don’t know why. I was washing my face.” She smiled. “It’s not the same as it was. It’s a little different at the end. But I think it’s mine.”

“That’s good.”

“It’s very good.” She drew a breath. “I said I’d not stay. Do you remember?”

“Yes.”

“I’ve not decided. I’ve decided to decide.” She laughed a little. “It’s a very poor sort of resolve.”

“It’s what I’ve got.”

“Then we’re well matched.”

They stood together on the step, and neither said anything more. The wind came in off the bay and blew the smoke of the cooking fires along the ground. She thought that a season’s peace had been made, in a hall, out of a great deal of ordinary work by people who did not like one another, and that it would hold as long as it held. She thought that it was very small.

She thought that it was enough to have been part of.

She did not think of Veralyn. She noticed that, some time later, with a small slow surprise, as one notices that a pain has stopped. She had come to this country for a queen, and she had spent months among these islands, and for the length of an afternoon she had not once looked over the water.

She looked now. There was nothing there but the wind, and a boat, and a long grey line of hills.

Chapter 31 — The Share and the Account

They divided the money on a trestle in the north bay, on the fifth day after the sealing, with the beast’s ribs going up over them into an afternoon light the colour of weak tea.

It was not a great heap. She had thought it would be, in the spring, when she was making the plan. She had imagined a leather bag and a table and a company round it, watching. What lay on the wood was mostly paper. There was a purse of silver from Whiterick, the Abbess’s second half, counted and found right. There was a letter of credit on the Abbey’s correspondents at Ash, in a clerk’s clean hand, which could be turned to coin in a fortnight if one knew whom to ask. There was a note from Muirenn, in her big scrawl, promising the crossing and the boats on a named day. And there was a note confirming Saikou’s reserve: four hundred and sixty marks, still sealed in the carting-yard at Coracle Harbor.

Amari kept the book. He sat at the end with a pencil behind his ear and a ruled sheet under his hand, and read it out in the plain flat voice of a man who had once run a pay-tent.

“Received,” he said. “From the Abbey, the second half, in silver, so much. The first half already spent on the road and the loft. From Mistress Muirenn, a promise, in writing, of the crossing. No silver, since she’s not got any. From the landing, nothing till the first season. Against that—” He turned a page. “Daire’s remaining pay, including the third for his sister. Ket’s keep, at the steading, for the fortnight, and the woman’s bread. The wheel. The barrels. A great many small items, for which see the second sheet.”

“The boat money?” said Riarhin.

“Half paid from his travelling credit,” said Amari, nodding at Saikou. “The rest Muirenn takes against what she owes us. Both halves on her receipt.” He laid it beside the note from Coracle. “This stays sealed. The wedding hasn’t borrowed from it.”

“What is the share?” said Saikou.

“An eighth apiece, after costs.” Amari looked up. “I’ve ruled it in eighths. Riarhin’s name isn’t on the sheet.”

“I’ll take what’s left after the rest.”

“You’ll take a share.”

“I’ll take a half-share.”

“You’ll take one,” said Amari, without heat. “It’s a small enough thing. I’ll not have a company where the one who says where to go is the one who’s not paid.”

Amari added R., 1/8 at the bottom of the sheet. She watched him finish the figure.

“Very well,” she said.

They took it in turn. It was odd how quiet it was. Tavin took his in silver, all of it, with a broad happy smile, and counted it twice, and put it in four different pockets. Cadan took his in paper and folded it into his sleeve without looking at it. Nessa took hers and held it a moment in her palm, and then put half in a small cloth bag and gave it to Amari without a word. Berent looked at his for a long time.

“What is it?” said Riarhin.

“It’s more than I’ve had in a year.” He turned it over. “It’s Daire’s, near enough. I’d like Sorcha to have a tenth of mine.”

“That’s—”

“I know it’s not a thing that’s asked. I’d like to.”

“Then do.”

He pushed a small pile across the trestle to Amari. He did not look at anyone. His hand, she noticed, was steady.

Amari reached for a scrap to wrap it. Riarhin put out her hand.

“Keep his name on that part.”

“I was going to.”

She drew her hand back. Berent watched until the packet was tied, then picked up what remained of his share.

Tavin had three pockets buttoned and the fourth open. He took two coins out of it, laid them on the table, and took one back.

“Ket’s boots,” he said.

“We’ve paid for his keep,” said Amari.

“His boots. One of them won’t go on.” Tavin turned the coin with a nail. “He kept trying it at the steading. Wouldn’t let me cut the seam. Said he’d need them when he was up.”

“Send it to the woman?”

“Send it to him.”

Riarhin looked at the coin. For a moment she wanted to add enough for a coat, a horse, a different road. She could hear herself making the offer.

“One pair,” Tavin said. “He can choose them. And it’s not to go in the company’s costs.”

Amari wrote on a second scrap. Tavin peered at it.

“Don’t put my name.”

“He’ll ask.”

“Tell him I owe him.”

“Do you?”

“I do now.” He buttoned the last pocket. “And leave out anything about gratitude. He’ll spend the money on drink just to spite us.”

Berent laughed once, through his nose. Amari sanded the note and tipped the loose grains back into their box. Riarhin waited with her hands in her lap until he had finished both packets.

***

Aurine did not take hers at once. She sat at the end of the trestle with her hands folded and her eyes on the sheet.

“I should like to say something,” she said.

“Please.”

“I said I would choose at the end. It’s the end.” She looked up. Her narrow dark face was quite composed, and quite tired, and there was a sort of plain bright clearness in it that had not been there in the spring. “I’ve thought about it for eleven days. I thought about it in a tent with an old man, while a woman was being delivered of a child, and again on a bench in the nave.”

“And?”

“I’ll go on.”

Nobody spoke.

“Under the terms. All four. I’ll write nothing to her that Saikou does not read. I’ll keep no second door. If I think a thing should be done for him, I’ll say it at a table.” She drew a breath. “Heber let me hold the child while he saw to the mother.” She rubbed at a mark on her cuff. “He thought he had found someone he could work beside. I could go on to another town and find that again.”

She looked along the table, stopping at Riarhin.

“You know what else I did that night. I find I’d rather not begin again with somebody who doesn’t.” She drew her hand away from the money. “I’m not asking you to tell me it was well done.”

“Thank you,” said Riarhin.

“You’re not to thank me.”

“No,” said Riarhin. “I’ll say that I’m glad.”

“That’s better.” The dry smile came and went. “I’d like to put in one condition.”

“Say it.”

“If I go, I go on my own feet. Not with a rope on my waist, or a word in my ear, or a bag of silver that’s to be earned back.” She looked round the ring. “It’s all I want. It’s in the fourth already. I’d only like to hear it said twice.”

“It’s said,” said Riarhin. “It’ll be said as often as you like.”

She took her share. She put it away quietly, in the front of her dress.

Saikou had been watching her the whole time. He sat with his long hands folded and his tired, gentle, clever face still.

“I shall write to my mother today,” he said.

“You said so.”

“I shall do it in front of you.” He drew a sheet towards him. “I should like the company to see it. It is a short letter.”

He wrote. She watched his hand go across the paper, quick and fine and exact, and did not read it. He held it up at the end and read it aloud, in a flat dry voice, without inflexion.

Mother. I am alive, and in the company of persons I have chosen, and I am eating. I know that you have had reports. I know how, and from whom. I have taken account of it. I do not ask you to stop caring what becomes of me. I ask that you do not arrange a door. I shall not use it. When I am ready to be brought home, I shall write and say so, in my own hand. Until then I decline. I am, as ever, your son. S.

He folded it. He put it in an envelope, and sealed it with a plain black wax, and handed it to Aurine.

“You will send it,” he said. “By the Abbey’s post. It says what I have authorised and no more.”

“Yes.”

“It does not reconcile us.”

“No,” said Aurine. She took it. “It’s not meant to.”

“I should not like it thought that it did.” He rubbed his eyes. “She will be very angry. She will be angry for a year. She is a woman who can make anger last.” A faint dry smile. “I find I am rather looking forward to it.”

***

She left them at the table and went out to see the Abbess off.

It was raining a little. The Whiterick carts were drawn up in the yard, and the lay-brothers were putting the last of the empty sacks into them, and Caelia stood at the tail of the foremost with her hands in her sleeves and a small black hood over her cowl. She had come out to say goodbye to the Hall’s steward. She had said it, and been thanked, and was now waiting for a cart to be turned.

She looked, as she always looked, upright and small and calm. But there was something else about her. Riarhin had noticed it in the sealing. It was not a shrinking. It was a kind of settledness, like that of a woman who has spent a great deal of her fortune on a small piece of land and found, on inspection, that it will grow barley.

She had got most of what she wanted. She had lost the court, and the arbitration, and the observances. She had her grain, over three seasons, and a seat at the wedding-feast, and a great many people in three parishes who now knew her name and had eaten her bread. She was stronger than she had been. She was not as strong as she had hoped. And she stood there, at the tail of a cart, in the rain, quite plainly aware of both.

“I will not offer you my hand,” said Caelia.

“No.”

“I do not think you would take it.”

“I’d not know what to do with it.”

“That is honest.” The old woman almost smiled. “I said that the Abbey would keep its door open, till the Hall. I do not think I shall shut it now. You are welcome at Whiterick. You have only to ask. I should ask nothing.”

“I know you wouldn’t.”

“That is not a kindness.”

“No.” Riarhin met her eyes. “It’s not. I’d like you to know I know it.”

“Then we understand each other,” said Caelia. She drew up her hood. “I have found that it is the most one can hope for, at my age.” She lifted her hand, briefly, in the sign of the Everflame. She did not say the words. She let the hand fall, and turned away, and was helped up onto the cart by a lay-brother. It creaked. It rolled out of the yard.

Cera was in the gateway.

She stood in her saffron robe with her carved disc in her hands, and a rolled letter under one arm, and she was looking after the carts with a quiet, drawn, very young face. She had written to the Temple. She had said that she had recognised a marriage in which she could not approve the sponsors, and had recorded a death of unknown cause. She had said it plainly. She expected, she told Riarhin, to be recalled by the spring.

“It will not be a punishment,” she said. “It will be an inconvenience.”

“Will you mind?”

“I shall mind a great deal.” She smiled, very slightly. “I shall not take any of it back.”

Sile did not say goodbye. She was not the sort. She stood a while in the door of her lodging with a shawl round her shoulders and a bundle on her back and a stout stick, and said, to no one in particular, that there was a woman at Dunmarrow who had been asking for a shore-rite for a drowned husband since the harvest began, and that she would as soon get to her before the weather turned.

“Will you come back?” said Riarhin.

“If I’m sent for.”

“Will you—”

“I don’t need to know,” said Sile. She said it kindly. “Whatever it is. I don’t need it. It’s a thing I’ve found. It makes one a much better company.” She hitched the bundle. “Keep the thread.”

“I haven’t any.”

“You have,” said Sile, and went off down the road with her stick, at a slow heavy walk, and the little bell swinging silently at her wrist.

***

She wrote to Sorcha that night, in the north bay, by the lamp.

She had put it off. She had thought it should be done when the money was in her hand and there was a plain thing to say. But there was no such day. She sat with a sheet of Saikou’s paper in front of her and did not know how to start, and after a long time she began.

Mistress Sorcha of Killen.

You will have had a letter from Amari, who was with your brother on the road. I am the one who hired him. I am writing so that you may have from me, in one place, what can be said.

He died on the night we crossed the moss beyond the raised way, some hours after the party was separated. He was found the next morning in a shallow pool. He had no mark on him. He had not been robbed. The mare he was leading came out alone, and was well. He was buried on the third day in the ground behind the turf-cutter’s house, with a Confessor of the Gray Liturgy to say his name.

Fenra advised us to wait for daylight. We could still have reached the Hall in time. Amari questioned me too. I ordered us to go on because I wanted more time there before the gathering. Your brother entered the causeway with the party I sent forward.

We do not know what happened after the lights appeared. Some of us followed them. We did not find him until the morning. I cannot tell you that it was quick, or that he was not afraid. We brought him back. We sat with him, and Sile spoke his name before we buried him.

The Charter of the landing at Rossan now provides that your household shall have half your brother’s wages for as long as your children are under twelve. It is written, and stitched, and read in the Hall. Ket, who was his companion, will bring you the mare. The company has paid the rest of what he was promised, and I enclose it. It is not a payment for what was lost. I do not think that it could be.

I am sorry that I have no more to give you.

R.

She did not sign it with the name she had given. She wrote it as it stood. She read it over, and it was quite short, and she found that she could not improve it, and did not try.

She folded it. She put it in the packet with the silver.

***

Late that night, when the lamp had burned low and the Hall was silent, Saikou found her still sitting there.

He did not say anything at first. He drew up a stool on the other side of the trestle and sat down, with his hands in his lap, and looked at the papers.

“You are thinking,” he said, “of what you will do about the company.”

“Yes.”

“You have not decided.”

“There are eight places in the boats,” she said. “I’m keeping them.”

“All eight?”

“Aurine’s too. If she still wants it in the morning.”

Saikou drew the stool closer. “Aurine has the letter for the post. I kept the copy. I should like you to read it.”

“Tonight?”

“If you can bear another.”

She moved Sorcha’s packet aside to make room. He laid the sheet down with its folded edge towards her and waited while she read.

“It says what you meant,” she said.

He took it back and smoothed the crease against his knee.

“She may send someone anyway,” he said.

“The western office.”

“Yes.”

“What will you do?”

“Ask their name. Read their instructions, if they will show me.” He folded the copy along its old crease. “You should be there.”

“And if they’re only offering a room?”

“I may want a room. I should like to choose who keeps the key.”

He got up. He said goodnight, with grave courtesy, and went off to his blanket. She sat on with the lamp. She counted them in her head, one by one, as she had once counted them in a room in Theona: Berent, and Saikou, and Amari, and Nessa, and Cadan, and Tavin, and Aurine. Seven. And herself.

She turned the lamp down and went to join them.

Interlude VIII — The Next Question

Jaennis

It took repeated requests to be allowed to see it, and she thought afterwards that it was the most honest negotiation she had ever conducted.

She did it by argument. She did not use her seal, or her position, or the Queen’s name, although all three were available and the Chancellor knew it. She went to the west wing every Thursday, since her first lesson, and did the exercises Master Hesse set her. She drew the pattern. She kept the flame upright. She watched the small dark line at the north-west tack and wrote down, each week, what it did. It did the same thing. She wrote that down too. And every Thursday, at the end of the lesson, she asked the same question in a different form: what is the rest of this?

Hesse would look at her with her plain grey face and say that there was no rest, and Jaennis would say that there was, and the argument would go on until the tea came.

It was the Chancellor who gave way. He did it on a wet Thursday, in his panelled room above the court, with a fire lit and a very old book open in front of him, and he did it as a man does who has found that a thing he had hoped to put off has arrived at the door.

“I do not want to show you,” he said.

“I know.”

“I wish that to be understood as something other than politeness. It is not a courtesy that I say it.” His pale eyes were quite steady. “There is a reason that it is kept apart. It is not that it is secret. It is that the people who have looked at it are, in general, not the people one would choose.”

“Markus.”

“Yes. And others.” He closed the book. “I taught Markus Fenwood for a season. He was the best I ever had. He asked for work beyond his instruction, and I did not stop him, because he asked as you ask. It seemed to me that a man of that quality had earned an answer. I was wrong.” He said it quite simply. “I have been wrong in a great many small ways. That was not small.”

“I know what happened to him.”

“He knew other people’s histories too.”

“Then I won’t offer you that as a safeguard.” She sat with her hands in her lap, in her plain dark dress, and looked at him. “I’m going to say what I know. I’m not young. I have a city to govern, a family to think of, and a great many years of not being told what I could do. I am not in a hurry. I don’t wish to be brilliant. I only wish to know what the edge is.”

“It is a very natural wish.”

“Then you’ll not stop me by saying it’s natural.”

He smiled. It was small and sad, and he looked for a moment about a hundred.

“No,” he said. “I do not think that I shall.”

***

It was kept in a case in the cellar of the west wing, under three locks, and it was smaller than she had expected.

A strip of grey vellum, about the length of her forearm, lay on black felt under glass. Closely set marks bordered it from end to end without repeating. She knew no such script. For a long time she searched for a pattern, losing it whenever she tried to follow a particular mark.

“It is the continuation,” said Hesse. She stood at her shoulder with the lamp. She had not wanted to come. She had come because the Chancellor had asked her and because she had, in her dry way, become fond of the woman. “It is what comes after. Your ring is the first line of a page that has a good many more. This is the second.”

“What does it do?”

“I do not know.”

“You must have some idea.”

“I have a very great number of ideas. I have written them down.” Hesse’s voice was flat. “I have never once been right about it. I have been wrong in twelve different directions. It does what the ring does, in the way that a man walking does what a man sitting does, and it is not the same thing.”

“You’ve tried it.”

“I have not.”

“You’ve not tried it.”

“No one in this University has done so, in the last forty years, who has been in a position to be asked about it.” She looked at the glass. “I say that with some care.”

Jaennis said nothing. She looked at the strip. She thought of a bowl of water, and a candle, and a small dark line on a stone.

“I must say the warning,” said Hesse. “The Chancellor has asked. It’s to be in the room.”

“Say it.”

“It is not like the ring. It is not a thing that you can do a little of. If you take this up, it will ask you for more time than you have, and it will ask you for it in the middle of things that you had meant to be doing. It will not come to harm you. It is not that sort of thing. It will merely be very interesting.” Her plain face was quite grave. “I have known three people who found it so. One is dead. One is in a house at Ashmere and does not speak. One is myself, and I am the least of them, and I have given it up.”

“Why?”

“Because I was afraid.” She said it as a woman states a fact. “Not of dying. I was afraid that I would want to go on.”

“And do you?”

“I do,” said Hesse. “That is why I have not looked.”

There was a silence. The lamp hissed.

“Thank you,” said Jaennis.

“It is not a thing to be thanked for.”

“It’s the truest thing anyone’s said to me in a year.” She turned from the glass. “I shall think about it.”

“You will not.”

“I shall,” said Jaennis, “for a great deal less time than I ought.”

Hesse gave a dry short laugh, and turned the lamp down, and said that she would lock up.

***

She had a matter to see to that afternoon, which was a dispute between two grain-factors over the terms of a contract, and she did it thoroughly.

It took two hours. She sat at the counting-house desk with the papers, and the men in front of her, and listened to each, and asked what each had done, and pointed out, quite courteously, in the second hour, the line that the clerk had failed to read. It was the usual thing. One of them had misdated a delivery. It was quite honest. She fined him a small sum and made him pay the other’s costs, and they went out, chastened, together, arguing about lunch.

It was a good day’s work. She was aware of it. She gave a small nod to the clerk, and put the papers in the drawer, and shut it.

She did not go home. She went, instead, down through the dark court to the west wing, with a lamp, in her plain coat, as she had every night since beginning her lessons. She did not stop at the small round room where the ring lay on its slab. She went past it, down the stair, into the cellar, and stood in front of the case with the three locks.

Hesse came down behind her, carrying the key-ring.

“I thought you might come,” she said.

“I want an hour. Looking, and a written account of what I see. No working of the pattern.”

“You’ll ask for that next.”

“Then refuse me next.”

Hesse held the keys between them without letting go when Jaennis reached. “I stay. You stop when I say. Your notes remain here.”

Jaennis looked at the hand on the ring. “Will you look?”

“At you.”

She released the keys. Jaennis set her lamp on the floor, leaving the place beside the case clear for her teacher.

She stood with the keys in her hand and Hesse waiting at her shoulder. When she first arrived, she could have said this was an answer to the Queen: she had work of her own now, and could not always be sent for. But Veralyn seemed distant tonight. The strip of vellum was under the glass, close enough to touch. She wanted to see what it said next.

She unlocked the first lock. It turned easily. The second key stuck; she eased it back and tried again. Then the third.

It’s only a look, she thought.

She opened the case.

Chapter 32 — What Goes Aboard

They went down to Scarrow on a grey morning in the last week of the harvest, with the wind out of the north and the eight boats drawn up on the shingle like the bodies of sleeping animals.

She counted them from the cart. She did not say the number. She had not said it to Muirenn’s crews, and she saw now that she would not need to; it had become, in the course of the season, a thing she carried without looking at, as one carries the weight of one’s own hand. They were where they had always been, black and bright with grease, with their oars laid across them in neat rows and their stems bare. On each bow-ring, through the ring, hung a short length of grey thread. It was wet. The morning was dry.

The crews were there. There were about ninety of them, standing on the shingle in a long loose ring, with their sea-bags at their feet and their hands in their belts. They had not been ordered to come. They had come because a woman with a black strip on her arm had said, on the day of the sealing, that she was going to make a crossing, and had asked whether any of them would care to. Riarhin had thought some would refuse. They had accepted her orders on the eve of the gathering, before the marriage, when she was a bride with a father, and they had accepted them again in the week after, when she was an orphan with a husband and a claim against her house.

Muirenn stood at the head of the slip with her arms folded. She wore the leather jerkin and the blue cloth, and there was a grey thread still round her left wrist, quite frayed, where someone had tied it. She did not look like a bride. She looked like a woman at a job.

“You’re late,” she said.

“Berent’s slow on the road.”

“I’d not have him hurried.” She jerked her chin at the boats. “They’re loaded. There’s bread for the crossing and fish for two days, and a barrel of water for each boat. Brannoc sent it down yesterday.” A short pause. “It’s all sound. Fenra went through every cask.”

“How is she?”

“She’s cross. It’s a good sign.” Muirenn’s mouth twisted. “She’s at the landing, in a chair, telling the lads how to stack. She’ll be keeper there till she’s ninety.”

“And Brannoc?”

“He’s at the jetty. He’s counting.” She said it with a kind of rough tenderness. “He’ll not be told he needn’t.”

They stood for a moment side by side and looked at the sea. It was very wide and very grey, and there was a long low swell coming in from the north, and out beyond the point, where the water turned dark, the Dolmis lay quiet and enormous under a sky the colour of a slate. Riarhin looked at it and thought that it did not look like a place where anything would answer. She knew that it was.

“I’ll not thank you,” said Muirenn.

“Don’t.”

“I thought about it. I thought I ought. It seemed a poor thing to send a person off without.” She drew a breath. “But I’d not be telling the truth. I don’t know what I’m grateful for. I’m alive, and he’s not, and I’ve a husband who doesn’t look at me across a table, yet, and eight boats.” She shook her head. “I’ll say it’s the best I could have had. I’ll not say it’s good.”

“It’s what I’d have said.”

“I know it is.” She held out her hand. It was a big, scarred, calloused hand, and it was not quite steady. “It’s a hard trade.”

“It’s the only one I’ve got.”

Riarhin took it. It was warm. It gripped hers, hard, once, and let go.

***

She finished the letter on a barrel-head, on the quay, with the wind pulling at the paper.

Ailsa’s last letter lay under her wrist to keep it from blowing away. There was a grease mark beside the news of Iona, and half a sentence up the margin about cloth that Tavish had promised to pay for. Riarhin had read it often enough to see Ailsa putting the work down, clearing a place to write, and going back to it when she had finished. She wanted to promise that she would come back to that room.

She did not say that.

Ailsa, she wrote.

I am going away again. It is not the last time. I am not going to say that it is, because I have found that I cannot stop, and I would not like you to be waiting for a day that I cannot promise.

I am well. I have people with me, and they are better than I had any right to. I have had some bad nights. I did a thing that was hard, and it is done, and I am not sorry that it is finished. I do not think I shall ever be able to say that I am sorry it was done. I want you to know that I know what that means.

Mara has work in Eilis’s household here. She is staying because she chooses to. Maelis is with the players. I have enclosed what Mara asked me to tell Tavish; she would rather he heard it from someone who knows her.

I cannot say when I shall write again. It may be some time. But I shall.

R.

She read it through. It was not a good letter. It was plain, and a little stiff, and it said nothing that would comfort. She read the opening again. Ailsa had never asked her to promise the last journey. It was Riarhin who kept trying to offer one.

She folded it. She put it in the packet with the other, to Tavish, that had been written in a single evening and did not need changing. She gave both to Deirdre’s carter, who was waiting with his cart at the head of the quay.

“It’ll go by Thursday,” he said.

“Thank you.”

“Sorley,” he said. It was, she saw, his name. He had told her twice before, and she had not been listening. “I’d not want it forgotten.”

“I won’t forget it,” said Riarhin.

***

The report came down the road as they were loading.

It was in a small square packet of Deirdre’s, wrapped in oiled cloth, brought at a gallop by a boy on a pony who had ridden all night. She recognised the hand on the outside. It was Saikou’s correspondent at Ash. The packet was dated eleven days ago. It had gone through four pairs of hands. It said very little, and it said it in a clerk’s flat prose, and every line of it was a plain fact.

Saikou read it on the shingle, standing, with his long hands steady. Then he handed it to her without a word.

Thepyrgos is quiet and held. Governor appointed: a woman of the Queen’s household, a widow, one Jaennis Fenwood. The University has not been disturbed. Acasia is in the Queen’s hands, save the upper valleys and the river country west of Cann, which are contested. The Queen is at Serrat. She is said to be well. A general muster has been called for the spring. It is understood, from persons in the commissariat, that it is directed at Ecktoria.

Of the object, nothing.

She read it twice. She stopped at the name.

She saw Jaennis at Valora, listening while another woman spoke, her attention apparently at ease. Riarhin had spent evenings in rooms arranged by her. Even now the title on the sheet looked too small for the person she remembered.

She folded the sheet. She held it a moment.

“Well?” said Saikou.

“Ecktoria.”
