“Yes.” He was looking at the sea. “The Abbess will be pleased to hear that she was right.”

“About what?”

“That the queen’s captains have a great many places to put their thumbs.” His mouth twisted. “I should like to say that it fits my thesis. It does. It fits it very well. I have, I find, no wish to say so.”

“Consistent with.”

“Consistent with.” He almost smiled. “I need the fourth thing. I need the campaign that fails. I have not had it. I do not think that I shall until she meets a country that will not be finished.” He took the paper back. “It may be Ecktoria. It may not. It is a very old country and it has a great many walls.”

“And the object?”

“Has not been recovered.” He folded it into his coat. “I am going to Ecktoria, I think. I am going to walk into a very large city and ask a series of impertinent questions, and see what turns up.” He looked at her. “It is not much of a plan.”

“It’s the one we’ve got.”

Saikou looked down at the folded report, then put it away.

***

They began boarding at noon.

Muirenn sent the first boat back up the slip before anyone had sat down. A water-cask had been set where the stern oarsman needed his heel. The man who had stowed it protested that he had crossed with worse. Muirenn asked whether he meant to row with his knee, and he moved it.

Riarhin waited with Berent’s bag. The strap was twisted; she straightened it and found one of his stockings caught in the buckle. It had been mended in the north bay, in brown wool because there had been no grey. She pushed it back inside.

Behind her somebody called for another pair of hands. She turned before she knew she had, expecting a large man to complain that all his hands were already in use.

Tavin was trying to lift a chest without bending his knees. Amari watched him for a moment, then took the other end. The complaint came from Tavin, who said that it had been lighter when it belonged to someone else.

She stood with the bag against her leg until they had passed.

The boarding took an hour. Amari took the first boat’s stern and stood in the water to the knee, handing up the bags. Aurine went aboard with a small leather satchel and a bundle of dry herbs, very upright, with her plain dark hair covered by a scarf, and sat in the bow with her hands on her knees and did not speak. Cadan followed, courteous, in his sleeves, and said good morning to the boatman, who did not answer. Tavin came last, with Penny on his shoulder, and a great many pockets, and a very great air of having been there before. He did not look at the sea.

Nessa stopped at the edge of the shingle. She stood a moment, with her shawl round her and her hair blowing, and her white streaks bright in the grey light. She did not look afraid. She looked like a woman with her hand on a door.

“I’ve decided,” she said.

“To come?”

“To come as far as the crossing.” She smiled. “It’s another leg. It’s not a life. I’ll say when it’s enough.”

“That’s all I ask.”

“I know.” She stepped down into the water. She began, softly, as she went, to hum. It was the song for the door. It went up and turned and came down, and it went all the way to the end.

Berent was carried.

He did not like it. He said so, several times, in a low fierce voice, and was ignored. Amari took him under one arm, and Tavin under the other, and between them they got him across the shingle, in the wet, with the stick swinging, and up the plank into the third boat. They set him on a thwart amidships with a folded cloak behind him and Na’ar’s sword across his knees. He was grey to the lips. He sat with his eyes on the horizon.

She went across to him.

He did not look at her. He said, without turning his head, in a rough low voice: “It didn’t come.”

“The book.”

“Aye. I’d hoped—” He stopped. “I asked Deirdre’s man at the last. He said there’d been nothing from Master Ridow. Not a line.”

“It’s only been a month.”

“I know.” He rubbed his face. “I know how long it takes. It’s only that I’d thought, if she were free, she’d have said.” He looked at her then. His eyes were red-rimmed and quite steady. “She’s not free.”

“No.”

“I’m not going to say she is.”

“I’d not ask you.”

“I want to say her name,” said Berent. “Every day. On the boat. I don’t want to be a man who’s forgotten.”

“Then say it.”

He said it. It was quiet. It was a single plain word, and it went out over the water and was lost.

She stood a moment with her hand on the gunwale, and did not say anything. She could not have said what would have been kind.

***

She checked the boats herself.

She went down the line, from the first to the last, in the wet, with her boots soaked and the cold coming up her legs. She looked at each. She looked at the bare place on the stem. She looked at the grey thread on the bow-ring. She counted, in each, the persons and the bags, and the water-barrels, and the oars, and she did not say a word aloud.

The last boat rode higher than the others. A bundle of cloaks lay on the stern thwart, leaving a space for her. She set her hand on the gunwale and felt it move against her palm. Beyond the point there would be Davra’s vessel, if the weather and the arrangement held. There would be another person asking whom she had brought.

She looked back down the line. Aurine was tying her satchel where spray would not reach it. Cadan had made room for an oarsman without being asked. Nessa sat turned towards the shore, listening to something the woman beside her said. None of them was watching for Riarhin’s approval.

Her mouth had formed the beginning of an instruction. She let it go. Muirenn’s people were at their work, and she was holding the boat against their shove.

“Not yet,” the man at the bow said. “We’ve one more thing.”

The shore-hand was a young man, twenty perhaps, with a sunburned face and a tarred cap, and he was standing knee-deep at the bow with a coil of rope over his shoulder. He had, she thought, been told to do it and had not known how. He looked at her, and his eyes went to the bare stem, and to the thread, and he opened his mouth and shut it.

“Which boat is it?” he said. “For the load. Which one’s the one for—” He glanced at the crew. “I’ve to tell the master which. He’ll want to know.”

She looked at him.

She could have said a number. She could have said the first, the last, the fourth. She could have named a thing. She had, in the course of a season, a great many words. She thought of a man on a shingle saying eight and— and stopping, and of the way a person can put a foot on a step and find it there.

She pointed.

She did not say anything. She lifted her arm, and put out one finger, and pointed at the boat. The young man looked. He nodded. He said aye and went round to the stern, and she saw the crew of the boat move, and lift the oars, and set them.

“And who’s to stand for the party?” he called, over his shoulder. “If there’s a question, at the meeting. I’ll have to say. Who’s the one who’s answerable?”

The wind had risen a little. It blew her hair across her face, and she put it back with her left hand. She looked at the grey sea. She looked at the seven of them, in the boats, with their bags and their faces, waiting, not looking at her, as people wait who have been told that the person in front will speak when she is ready.

She had said a name at a well. It had been a small easy thing, and she had thought it a kindness, to a woman who asked. She had used it since. She had signed it on a paper, and let it stand on a slate, and had heard it said in a hall by a great many people who did not know that it was hers.

“I am,” she said.

“And you’re—”

“Riarhin.”

It was quite clear. It went out over the shingle and the water, in her own voice, without any weight, and nothing answered it. Nothing came out of the sea or the sky. The gulls went on crying. The young man nodded, and made a small mark in the air with his rope, as if he were writing.

“That’ll do,” he said.

She stood a moment longer, in the wind. Then she turned, and went up the plank into the last boat, and sat down at the stern with her hands on her knees, and the crew leaned on the oars.

They went out over the water. The Dolmis lay black and wide ahead of them, and behind, the shore drew off, and the green ground, and the small grey houses, and a very small tall figure on a stone jetty with a slate in his hand. On the bow of each boat, through the ring, a length of grey thread stood out straight in the wind. It was wet. She looked at it a while.

She did not count. She did not need to. She knew who was aboard.
